# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 27

## Page 13089

VOLUME TWENTY-SEVEN
THE BOOK OF JURISPRUDENCE
PART SEVEN:
VISITATION

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.

All praise is due to Allah. We praise Him and seek His help, guidance, and forgiveness. We seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray; and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. I bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah alone, without partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bestow blessings and abundant peace upon him, his family, and his Companions.

SECTION:
VISITING JERUSALEM

It is established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Journeys are not to be undertaken except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque, al-Aqsā Mosque, and this mosque of mine.”

It is recorded in the two Sahīh collections on the authority of Abū Saʿīd and Abū Hurayrah, and has also been transmitted through other chains. It is a widely transmitted hadith

---

## Page 13090

that has been widely accepted. The scholars unanimously agree on its authenticity and have accepted it as true.

Muslim scholars agree that travelling to Jerusalem for the acts of worship prescribed there is recommended, such as prayer, supplication, remembrance of Allah, recitation of the Quran, and devotional retreat. Al-Hākim relates in his Sahīh:

“Solomon, peace be upon him, asked his Lord for three things: a kingdom such as no one after him would possess; judgment that accords with His judgment; and that anyone who came to this House intending nothing other than to pray there should be forgiven.”

For this reason, Ibn ʿUmar, may Allah be pleased with him, would go there and pray without even drinking water there, so that Solomon’s supplication might apply to him, because of the words, “intending nothing other than to pray there.” This requires that one’s intention in travelling there be devoted solely to worship, without going there for a worldly purpose or a religious innovation.

The scholars differed concerning someone who vows to travel there to pray or undertake a devotional retreat: must he fulfil his vow? There are two well-known opinions, both of which are reported from al-Shāfiʿī.

The first is that this vow must be fulfilled. This is the opinion of the majority, including Mālik, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and others.

The second is that it need not be fulfilled. This is the opinion of Abū Hanīfah, whose governing principle is that only an act of a kind prescribed as obligatory by Islamic law can become binding through a vow. For this reason, he regards as binding vows to perform

---

## Page 13091

prayer, fasting, charity, Hajj, and ‘umrah, since these are acts of a kind prescribed as obligatory by Islamic law. He does not, however, regard a vow to undertake a devotional retreat as binding, for he holds that a devotional retreat is valid only when accompanied by fasting. This is also the position of Mālik and, according to one of the two reports from him, Ahmad.

The majority cite as evidence the report narrated by al-Bukhārī in his Sahīh from ‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah, let him obey Him; and whoever vows to disobey Allah, let him not disobey Him.”

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, thus commanded everyone who vows to obey Allah to fulfil that vow. He did not stipulate that the act of obedience must be of a kind prescribed as obligatory by Islamic law. This opinion is more correct.

The same disagreement applies if someone vows to travel to the Prophet’s Mosque, even though it is superior to al-Aqsā Mosque. If, however, someone vows to visit the Sacred Mosque for Hajj or ‘umrah, the scholars unanimously agree that he must fulfil his vow.

The Sacred Mosque is the best of all mosques, followed by the Prophet’s Mosque, then al-Aqsā Mosque. It is established in both Sahīh collections that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“One prayer in this mosque of mine is better than a thousand prayers in any other mosque, except the Sacred Mosque.”

The majority of scholars hold that prayer in the Sacred Mosque is superior to prayer in the Prophet’s Mosque, peace and blessings be upon him. Ahmad, al-Nasā’ī, and others have narrated

---

## Page 13092

from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him:

“A prayer in the Sacred Mosque is equivalent to a hundred thousand prayers.”

As for al-Aqsā Mosque, it has been reported that “a prayer there is equivalent to fifty prayers,” and it has also been said, “five hundred prayers.” The latter is more plausible.

If someone vows to travel to “the grave of Allah’s close friend, peace be upon him,” or to the grave of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, or to “Mount Sinai,” where Allah spoke to Moses, peace be upon him, or to “Mount Hirā’,” where the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, used to worship and received revelation, or to the cave mentioned in the Quran, or to other graves, stations, and shrines associated with certain prophets and shaykhs, or to certain caves or mountains, fulfilling this vow is not obligatory, by agreement of the four imams. Travel to these places is prohibited, because of the Prophet’s prohibition, peace and blessings be upon him:

“Do not undertake a journey except to three mosques.”

Thus, if travel has been prohibited even to mosques—which are among the houses of Allah, in which He has commanded the five daily prayers—even to Qubā’ Mosque, which those in Medina are encouraged to visit, as established in both Sahīh collections through Ibn ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, who reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, “used to visit Qubā’ every Saturday, sometimes riding and sometimes walking.” Al-Tirmidhī and others narrated that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Whoever purifies himself at home, doing so thoroughly, then comes to Qubā’ Mosque intending only to pray there, will have a reward like that of an ‘umrah.”

Al-Tirmidhī said: “A good, authentic hadith.”

---

## Page 13093

Thus, if travel to such a place is prohibited, as is travel to Mount Sinai mentioned in the Quran, the same applies—as Mālik mentioned—to places not built for the five daily prayers; indeed, taking them as places of worship is prohibited. It is established in both Sahīh collections that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said during his final illness:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians: they took the sites associated with their prophets as places of worship.”

He was warning against what they had done. ‘Ā’ishah said: “Were it not for that, his grave would have been left in the open, but it was deemed undesirable that it be made a place of worship.”

In Sahīh Muslim and elsewhere, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, is reported to have said:

“Those before you used to take graves as places of worship. Beware! Do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.”

For this reason, the Companions did not travel to any of the shrines associated with the prophets—not the shrine of Abraham, Allah’s close friend, peace be upon him, nor any other. On the night of his ascension, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, prayed two units of prayer in Jerusalem, as established in the authentic hadith, and did not pray anywhere else. As for the accounts of the ascension narrated by some people, stating that “he prayed in Medina, prayed at the grave of Moses, peace be upon him, and prayed at the grave of Allah’s close friend,” all these reports are false and fabricated.

Some later scholars permitted travel to shrines, but they neither transmitted this position from any of the imams nor supported it with any evidence from Islamic revelation.

---

## Page 13094

Section

The acts of worship prescribed in al-Aqsā Mosque are of the same kind as those prescribed in the Prophet’s Mosque, peace and blessings be upon him, and all other mosques, except the Sacred Mosque. There, in addition to the acts prescribed in other mosques, circumambulating the Kaaba, touching its two Yemeni corners, and kissing the Black Stone are prescribed. As for the Prophet’s Mosque, peace and blessings be upon him, al-Aqsā Mosque, and all other mosques, there is nothing in them to be circumambulated, rubbed, or kissed.

No one may therefore circumambulate the chamber of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, any other graves of prophets or righteous people, the Rock in Jerusalem, or anything else, such as the dome atop Mount ‘Arafāt and similar structures. Indeed, nowhere on earth is circumambulation prescribed as it is around the Kaaba. Whoever believes that circumambulating anything else is prescribed is worse than someone who believes it permissible to pray facing something other than the Kaaba. For when the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, emigrated from Mecca to Medina, he led the Muslims in prayer facing Jerusalem for eighteen months. It was the Muslims’ direction of prayer throughout that period. Then Allah changed the direction of prayer to the Kaaba, and Allah revealed verses of the Quran concerning this

---

## Page 13095

as mentioned in “Surah al-Baqarah.” The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and the Muslims then prayed facing the Kaaba, which became their direction of prayer. It was also the direction of prayer of Abraham and other prophets.

Anyone who today adopts the Rock as his direction of prayer and prays facing it is an unbeliever and apostate. He is to be called upon to repent and, unless he does so, put to death. This is despite its having once been the direction of prayer, for that was abrogated. What, then, of someone who makes it a place to circumambulate as the Kaaba is circumambulated? Allah has never prescribed circumambulation around anything other than the Kaaba.

The same applies to someone who intends to drive sheep or cattle there to slaughter them, believing that offering the Eid sacrifice there is better; to shave his head there on Eid; or to travel there to perform the ‘Arafah observance on the afternoon of the Day of ‘Arafah. These practices—imitating the pilgrimage rites in Jerusalem through standing, circumambulation, sacrifice, and shaving the head—are religious innovations and deviations. Anyone who does any of these things believing it to be a means of drawing closer to Allah is to be called upon to repent and, unless he does so, put to death. This is just as if he prayed facing the Rock, believing that facing it in prayer was a means of drawing closer to Allah, like facing the Kaaba.

For this reason, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb built the Muslims’ prayer hall at the front of al-Aqsā Mosque. The name “al-Aqsā Mosque” refers to the entire mosque built by Solomon, peace be upon him. Some people have come to use the name al-Aqsā for the prayer hall that ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, built at its front. Prayer in this hall, which ‘Umar built for the Muslims, is better than prayer elsewhere in the mosque. For when ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb

---

## Page 13096

conquered Jerusalem, a great heap of rubbish lay on the Rock, because the Christians had deliberately sought to dishonor it in opposition to the Jews who prayed facing it. ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, ordered that the filth be removed and asked Ka‘b al-Ahbār, “Where do you think we should build the Muslims’ prayer hall?”

“Behind the Rock,” he replied.

‘Umar said, “Son of a Jewish woman! Judaism has influenced you. Rather, I shall build it in front of the Rock, for the front portions of mosques are ours.”

For this reason, when the leading scholars of the Muslim community entered the mosque, they would seek to pray in the prayer hall built by ‘Umar. It has also been reported that ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, prayed in David’s prayer chamber.

As for “the Rock,” neither ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, nor the Companions prayed beside it. Nor was there a dome over it during the time of the Rightly Guided Caliphs. Rather, it remained uncovered throughout the caliphates of ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān, ‘Alī, Mu‘āwiyah, Yazīd, and Marwān. However, when Marwān’s son ‘Abd al-Malik took control of Syria and conflict arose between him and Ibn al-Zubayr, people would perform Hajj and meet with Ibn al-Zubayr. ‘Abd al-Malik wanted to divert people away from Ibn al-Zubayr, so he built the dome over the Rock and draped it in winter and summer, to encourage people to undertake “visits to Jerusalem” and occupy them with that instead of meeting with Ibn al-Zubayr.

The scholars among the Companions and those who followed them in righteousness, however, did not venerate the Rock, for it is a direction of prayer that has been abrogated. Likewise, Saturday was a festival day under the law of Moses, peace be upon him, but was then replaced by Friday under the law of Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him. Muslims therefore must not single out Saturday and Sunday for worship as the Jews

---

## Page 13097

and Christians do. Likewise, the Rock is venerated only by the Jews and some Christians.

What some ignorant people claim about it—that it bears the imprint of the Prophet’s foot, his turban, and so forth—is all false. Still more false is the notion that it marks the place of the Lord’s foot. Likewise, the claim that a certain place is the cradle of Jesus, peace be upon him, is false; it was merely a Christian baptistery. Equally false are the claims that the Bridge and the Balance are there, or that the wall which will be erected between Paradise and Hell is the wall built to the east of the mosque. Likewise, venerating the chain or its location is not prescribed in Islam.

Section

There is no place in Jerusalem to be sought out for worship other than al-Aqsā Mosque. However, it is good to visit the graves of the dead, greet them, and pray for mercy upon them, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, taught his Companions. For the Prophet, peace be upon him, “used to teach his Companions, when they visited graves, that each should say: ‘Peace be upon you, inhabitants of these dwellings, believing men and women. We shall, if Allah wills, join you. May Allah have mercy on those among us and you who have gone before and those who come after. We ask Allah for well-being for ourselves and for you. O Allah, do not deprive us of the reward for our loss of them, do not put us to trial after them, and forgive us and them.’”

---

## Page 13098

Section

As for visiting “the places of worship of the disbelievers,” such as the place called “al-Qumāma,” “Bethlehem,” “Zion,” or other places such as “Christian churches,” this is forbidden. Whoever visits any of these places believing that visiting it is recommended and that worshipping there is better than worshipping in his own home is misguided and has departed from the law of Islam. He must be called upon to repent; if he does not repent, he is to be put to death.

If, however, someone enters such a place for a need and the time for prayer comes while he is there, scholars have held three views on praying there, both within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere. One view is that prayer there is disliked in all cases. Ibn ʿAqīl favoured this view, which is also reported from Mālik. Another view is that it is permissible in all cases. A third holds that if there are images inside, prayer there is prohibited; otherwise, it is not. This is explicitly stated by Ahmad and others, and is reported from ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, and others. For the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Angels do not enter a house in which there is an image.”

When the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, conquered Mecca, there were images in the Kaaba, and he did not enter it until those images had been effaced. Allah knows best.

Section

There is no place in Jerusalem designated a “sanctuary,” nor at the burial place of Abraham, nor

---

## Page 13099

in any other place, except for three locations:

The first is a sanctuary by the consensus of the Muslims: the sanctuary of Mecca—may Allah Most High honour it. The second is a sanctuary according to the majority of scholars: the sanctuary of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, extending from ‘Ayr to Thawr, an area one barīd (postal stage) long and one barīd wide. This is a sanctuary according to the majority of scholars, including Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad, and numerous authentic hadiths concerning it have been widely transmitted from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him.

The third is “Wajj,” a valley in Tā’if. A hadith concerning it was reported by Ahmad in his Musnad, but it is not found in the collections of authentic hadiths. Al-Shāfi‘ī regards it as a sanctuary because he considered the hadith authentic, whereas most scholars do not regard it as a sanctuary. Ahmad judged the hadith reported concerning it to be weak and therefore did not act upon it.

Apart from these three places, no location is regarded as a sanctuary by any Muslim scholar. A sanctuary is a place where Allah has forbidden hunting its game and cutting its vegetation, and Allah has not forbidden hunting game and cutting vegetation anywhere outside these three places.

Section

“Visiting Jerusalem” is sanctioned in Islamic law at all times. However, one should not go there at the times chosen by misguided people, such as the time of the Festival of Sacrifice. Many misguided people travel there to perform a ritual standing. Travelling there to observe the Day of ‘Arafah, believing this to be an act of devotion, is unquestionably forbidden. One should neither imitate them nor swell their numbers.

---

## Page 13100

Nor is travelling there in conjunction with Hajj an act of devotion. The expression, “May Allah sanctify your pilgrimage,” is baseless and invalid, as is the report:

“Whoever visits me and my father in a single year, I guarantee him Paradise.”

This is a lie according to the consensus of hadith experts. Likewise, every hadith narrated concerning visiting the grave of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, is weak—indeed, fabricated. The compilers of the Sahīh and Sunan collections and the Musnads, such as Ahmad’s Musnad and others, did not narrate any such report. What is found in the Sunan collections, however, is the report narrated by Abū Dāwūd from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, who said:

“No man greets me with peace but Allah restores my soul to me so that I may return his greeting.”

Thus, he returns the greeting of anyone who greets him at his grave, while the greetings of those who greet him from afar are conveyed to him. As reported by al-Nasā’ī, he said:

“Allah has appointed angels at my grave who convey to me the greetings of peace from my community.”

It is also reported from him in the Sunan collections that he said:

“Send abundant blessings upon me on Friday and the night preceding it, for your blessings are presented to me.” They asked, “How will our blessings be presented to you when you have decayed?” He replied, “Allah has forbidden the earth to consume the bodies of the prophets.”

He thus explained, peace and blessings be upon him, that blessings and greetings of peace reach him from afar. Allah has commanded us to send blessings upon him and greet him with peace. It is authentically reported in the Sahīh that he said:

“Whoever sends blessings upon me once, Allah will bestow blessings upon him ten times in return.”

May Allah bestow blessings and abundant peace upon him.

---

## Page 13101

Section

As for travelling to Ascalon in these times, it is not prescribed—neither obligatory nor recommended. However, residing in Ascalon and travelling there once had merit, when it was a Muslim frontier post where guards were stationed in Allah’s cause. It is authentically reported in Sahīh Muslim, from Salmān, that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Guarding the frontier for a day and a night in Allah’s cause is better than fasting for a month and spending its nights in prayer. Whoever dies guarding the frontier dies fighting in Allah’s cause; the reward for his deeds continues, his provision from Paradise continues, and he is safe from the one who tests him in the grave.”

Abū Hurayrah said: “I would rather guard the frontier for one night in Allah’s cause than spend the Night of Decree in prayer beside the Black Stone.”

People of goodness and religious devotion used to travel to the Muslim frontier posts to stand guard there: the frontier posts of Syria, such as Ascalon, Acre, Tarsus, Mount Lebanon and elsewhere; those of Egypt, such as Alexandria and elsewhere; and those of Iraq, such as Abadan and elsewhere.

Those places that have fallen into ruin and no longer contain dwellings, such as Ascalon, are no longer frontier posts, nor is there any merit in travelling to them. Likewise, travelling to Mount Lebanon and similar mountains is not recommended. There is no righteous person there who follows the law of Islam. Rather, there are many jinn there; these are the “men of the unseen” who are sometimes seen in these places. Allah Most High says:

“And there were men from among

---

## Page 13102

mankind who sought refuge with men from among the jinn, but they only increased them in burden.”

Likewise, those who sometimes see al-Khidr have actually seen a jinn. More than one person whom I know has seen him, and he said, “I am al-Khidr.” This was a jinn deceiving the Muslims who saw him. In fact, al-Khidr who accompanied Moses, peace be upon him, has died. Had he been alive during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, he would have been obliged to come to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, believe in him and fight alongside him. Allah required everyone who lived to see Muhammad—even a prophet—to believe in him and fight alongside him, as Allah Most High says:

“And when Allah took the covenant of the prophets: ‘Whatever I give you of Scripture and wisdom, should a messenger then come to you confirming what you have, you must believe in him and support him.’ He said, ‘Do you acknowledge this and accept My binding covenant?’ They said, ‘We acknowledge it.’ He said, ‘Then bear witness, and I am with you among the witnesses.’”

Ibn ‘Abbās, may Allah be pleased with him, said: “Allah never sent a prophet without taking his covenant that, if Muhammad were sent during his lifetime, he would certainly believe in him and support him. He also commanded him to take the covenant of his people that, if Muhammad were sent during their lifetimes, they would certainly believe in him and support him.”

None of the Companions mentioned having seen al-Khidr or that he came to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. The Companions were too knowledgeable and too elevated in rank for Satan to deceive them. He did, however, deceive many who came after them: he would appear to one of them in the form of the Prophet and say, “I am al-Khidr,” when he was only a devil.

Similarly, many people see one of their dead come forth, visit them, speak to them about various matters and attend to their needs. They suppose it is the deceased person himself, but it is only a devil who has assumed his form. Many people seek deliverance from a created being, whether a Christian such as George or someone who is not

---

## Page 13103

a Christian—and then see that being come to them, perhaps even speaking to them. Yet it is only a devil who has assumed the form of the being whose aid they sought. When the supplicant associated that being with Allah, the devil appeared to him in its form, just as devils used to enter idols and speak to people. Such things occur frequently in many lands in these times.

Some of these people are carried by devils, who fly with them through the air to distant places. Some are carried to ‘Arafah, but they do not perform Hajj as prescribed: they neither enter the consecrated state of pilgrimage, nor recite the pilgrim’s invocation, nor circumambulate the Kaaba, nor perform the ritual walk between al-Safā and al-Marwah. Rather, they stand among the people in their ordinary clothes, and then the devils carry them back to their homeland. This is one way in which devils toy with many people, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

Allah knows best what is correct. May Allah bless our Prophet Muhammad, his family and his Companions, and grant them peace.

---

## Page 13104

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About visiting “Jerusalem” and “the grave of Allah’s intimate friend, peace be upon him”; about the blessing associated with eating bread and lentils and taking this food from one land to another for blessing; and about what in these practices accords with the Sunnah and what constitutes innovation.

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Travelling to Jerusalem to pray there, observe a devotional retreat, recite the Quran, remember Allah, or supplicate is lawful and recommended by the consensus of Muslim scholars. It is established in the two Sahīh collections, through the reports of Abū Hurayrah and Abū Sa‘īd, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Journeys should not be undertaken except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque, al-Aqsā Mosque, and this mosque of mine.”

The Sacred Mosque and the mosque of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, are superior to it. It is also reported in the two Sahīh collections that he said:

“One prayer in this mosque of mine is better than a thousand prayers elsewhere, except in the Sacred Mosque.”

As for travelling solely to visit “the grave of Allah’s intimate friend,” or any other graves of the prophets and the righteous, their shrines, or sites associated with them, none of the leading Muslim scholars has recommended it—neither the four imams nor anyone else. Indeed, if someone vowed to do so, he would not be obliged to fulfil this

---

## Page 13105

vow, according to the four imams and others. The three mosques, however, are different: if someone vows to travel to the Sacred Mosque for Hajj or ‘umrah, he must do so by agreement of the imams. If he vows to travel to either of the other two mosques, he must undertake the journey according to most of them, including Mālik, Ahmad, and al-Shāfi‘ī according to the stronger of his two opinions. This is based on the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah, let him obey Him; and whoever vows to disobey Allah, let him not disobey Him.”

Narrated by al-Bukhārī.

A vow must be fulfilled only when it concerns an act of obedience, such as a vow to pray, fast, observe a devotional retreat, give charity for Allah’s sake, or perform Hajj. Thus, a vow to travel to somewhere other than the three mosques does not make that journey obligatory, because it is not an act of obedience. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Journeys should not be undertaken except to three mosques.”

He thus prohibited travelling to any mosque other than the three mosques, so travelling to places other than mosques is even more deserving of prohibition. Worship in mosques is undoubtedly superior to worship in places that are neither mosques nor homes. Moreover, it is authentically reported that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The places most beloved to Allah are the mosques.”

Furthermore, his words, “Journeys should not be undertaken except to three mosques,” encompass the prohibition of travelling to any place sought out for its own sake. This differs from travelling for trade, seeking knowledge, and similar purposes, since such journeys are undertaken to obtain what is needed, wherever it may be found. The same applies to travelling to visit a brother in faith: the person himself is the purpose of the journey, wherever he may be.

Some later scholars have stated that there is no harm in travelling

---

## Page 13106

to tomb-shrines. They cited as evidence the report that:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to visit Qubā’ every Saturday, sometimes riding and sometimes walking.”

Al-Bukhārī and Muslim both recorded it in their Sahīh collections. Yet it provides no evidence for their position, because Qubā’ is not a tomb-shrine but a mosque. Moreover, travelling to it is prohibited by agreement of the imams, since this is not a prescribed journey. Indeed, even travelling to Qubā’ from one’s own home would not be permissible. However, if someone travels to the Prophet’s Mosque and then goes from there to Qubā’, this is recommended, just as visiting the graves of those buried in al-Baqī‘ and the martyrs of Uhud is recommended.

As for eating the bread and lentils prepared at “the grave of Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, peace be upon him,” no scholar, early or later, has recommended this. Nor was such food prepared during the time of the Companions, those who followed them in righteousness, or thereafter, for five hundred years after the beginning of the Prophet’s mission, until the Christians seized that land. The domed structure over his grave was not open; rather, it was sealed shut. Nor did the early generations—the Companions and their successors—travel to his grave or anyone else’s.

When the Christians seized that land, however, they remodelled his burial chamber and turned it into a church. When the Muslims subsequently recaptured the land, some people turned it into a mosque. This is a prohibited innovation, for it is authentically reported in the Sahīh that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

He was warning against what they had done.

It is also reported in the Sahīh that, five days before his death, he said:

“Indeed, those who came before you used to

---

## Page 13107

take graves as places of worship. Beware! Do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.”

Later, some people established an endowment to provide lentils and bread. This endowment was not established by Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, nor by any of the Children of Israel, nor by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor by his caliphs. Indeed, it has been reported that “the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, granted that village to the Dārimīs.” He did not instruct them to serve bread, lentils, or anything else at the tomb-shrine of Abraham, peace be upon him.

Anyone who believes that eating this bread and these lentils is a recommended practice prescribed by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is a misguided innovator. Indeed, anyone who believes that lentils in general possess any special virtue is ignorant. The report:

“Eat lentils, for they soften the heart, and seventy prophets were sanctified through them,”

is a fabricated, invented hadith, by the agreement of the scholars. Lentils, however, were among the foods the Jews craved. Allah Most High said to them:

“Would you exchange what is better for what is worse?”

Some people seek to draw close to the jinn through lentils: they cook lentils and place them in latrines or send them away, asking the devils for various favors. They do similar things in bathhouses and elsewhere. This is a form of belief in al-jibt and al-tāghūt (false powers and objects of worship).

“The essence of the religion of Islam” is that Allah alone be worshipped, without any partner, and that He be worshipped

---

## Page 13108

through the obligatory, recommended, and encouraged acts that He, glorified and exalted be He, has prescribed through His Prophet Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Whoever worships through an act that is neither obligatory nor recommended is misguided. And Allah knows best.

The shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Which is better: residing in Mecca, near the Prophet’s Mosque, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, near al-Aqsā Mosque, or at a frontier post to take part in military campaigns? And what of the reports attributed to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Whoever visits my grave is assured of my intercession,”

and:

“Whoever visits the House but does not visit me has treated me with disregard”?

Is visiting the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, recommended or not? Please give us a ruling; may you be rewarded.

The Answer

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. Guarding the frontiers is better than residing near the three mosques, as the imams of Islam have generally stated explicitly. Indeed, they differed concerning residence near these mosques: Abū Hanīfah considered it disliked, while Mālik, Ahmad, and others considered it recommended. Yet guarding the frontiers is, in their view, better than residing near these mosques. The early generations agreed on this, to the extent that Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, said: “Spending one night guarding the frontier in Allah’s cause is dearer to me than spending the Night of Decree in prayer beside the Black Stone.”

This is because guarding the frontiers is a form of jihad, and jihad as a category takes precedence over Hajj as a category, as recorded in the two Sahīh collections: “It is reported from the Prophet

---

## Page 13109

—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—that he was asked, ‘Which deed is best?’ He replied, ‘Faith in Allah and His Messenger.’ He was asked, ‘Then what?’ He replied, ‘Jihad in Allah’s cause.’ He was asked, ‘Then what?’ He replied, ‘An accepted Hajj.’”

Allah Most High has said:

“Do you consider providing water for pilgrims and maintaining the Sacred Mosque equal to believing in Allah and the Last Day and striving in Allah’s cause? They are not equal in Allah’s sight. And Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people.”

“Those who believe, emigrate, and strive in Allah’s cause with their wealth and their lives are greater in rank in Allah’s sight,” up to His words: “Indeed, with Allah is a great reward.”

As for the saying, “Whoever visits my grave is assured of my intercession,” this hadith was reportedly narrated by al-Dāraqutnī with a weak chain of transmission. For this reason, more than one scholar included it among fabricated reports. None of the compilers of the recognized Sahīh, Sunan, or Musnad collections narrated it.

As for the other hadith, “Whoever performs Hajj to the House but does not visit me has treated me with disregard,” no scholar of hadith has narrated it. Rather, it is a fabrication attributed to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Its meaning contradicts scholarly consensus, for treating the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with disregard is a major sin—indeed, it is disbelief and hypocrisy. Rather, he must be dearer to us than our families and our wealth, as he said, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“By Him in whose hand is my soul, none of you believes until I am dearer to him than his father, his child, and all people.”

---

## Page 13110

As for “visiting him,” it is not obligatory, by the agreement of the Muslims. Indeed, neither the Quran nor the Sunnah contains a command to do so. Rather, what the Quran and Sunnah command is to invoke blessings and peace upon him. May Allah bless him, his family and his Companions, and grant them abundant peace.

The principal evidence upon which scholars have relied concerning “visitation” is his saying in the hadith narrated by Abū Dāwūd:

“No Muslim greets me with peace without Allah restoring my soul to me so that I may return his greeting.”

Mālik and others disapproved of saying, “I visited the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.” The Companions, such as Ibn ʿUmar, Anas and others, would greet him—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and his two companions with peace. Thus, al-Muwattaʾ records that whenever Ibn ʿUmar entered the mosque, he would say: “Peace be upon you, O Messenger of Allah. Peace be upon you, O Abū Bakr. Peace be upon you, my father.”

Travelling to his mosque is prescribed, by the agreement of the Muslims, as stated in his saying recorded in the two Sahīh collections:

“No journey should be undertaken except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque, al-Aqsā Mosque and this mosque of mine.”

The two Sahīh collections also record that he said:

“One prayer in this mosque of mine is better than a thousand prayers in any other mosque, except the Sacred Mosque.”

When someone comes to the Prophet’s mosque, he greets him—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and his two companions with peace, as the Companions used to do. But if his purpose in travelling is to visit the Prophet’s grave rather than to pray in his mosque, then this is a matter of scholarly disagreement. The position held by the imams and most scholars is that this is not

---

## Page 13111

prescribed or commanded, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No journey should be undertaken except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque, this mosque of mine and al-Aqsā Mosque.”

For this reason, scholars have not stated that someone who vows to undertake such a journey must fulfil that vow. This contrasts with travelling to the three mosques—not for prayer and devotional retreat in them—for scholars have stated that fulfilling such a vow is obligatory in the case of one of them—the Sacred Mosque—while disagreeing concerning the other two.

The majority, including Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, hold that such a vow must also be fulfilled concerning the other two mosques, since travelling to the more virtuous mosque does not dispense with travelling to the less virtuous one. Abū Hanīfah holds that only a vow to travel to the Sacred Mosque must be fulfilled. This rests on his view that a vow makes an act obligatory only if acts of its kind are already obligatory under Islamic law. The majority, however, hold that every vow to perform an act of obedience must be fulfilled, on the basis of the report in Sahīh al-Bukhārī from ʿĀʾishah, may Allah be pleased with her, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah, let him obey Him; and whoever vows to disobey Allah, let him not disobey Him.”

Indeed, a number of scholars, including Ibn ʿAqīl and others, have explicitly stated that someone travelling to visit the graves of the prophets, peace be upon them, or other graves may not shorten the prayers on that journey. This is because the journey is sinful: he believes it to be an act of obedience when it is not, and seeking nearness to Allah, Mighty and Majestic, through something that is not an act of obedience is itself sinful. Moreover, the Prophet forbade this, and a prohibition entails that the act is unlawful. Some later scholars, however, permitted travelling to visit graves, as mentioned by Abū

---

## Page 13112

Hāmid in al-Ihyāʾ, Abū al-Hasan ibn ʿAbdūs, and Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī. The latter related a hadith also narrated by al-Tabarānī from Ibn ʿUmar, who reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever comes to visit me, with nothing prompting him except visiting me, it is incumbent upon me to intercede for him on the Day of Resurrection.”

However, this report is transmitted through ʿAbd Allāh ibn ibn ʿUmar al-ʿUmarī, who has been judged weak. For this reason, none of the early generations or the leading imams relied upon this hadith as evidence. By the consensus of Muslim scholars, a report of this kind cannot establish a ruling in Islamic law. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 13113

The shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

As for the saying:

“Whoever visits my grave, my intercession becomes due to him,”

and similar hadiths narrated concerning visiting the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, none is authentic. None of the compilers of the authoritative collections transmitted any of them: neither the compilers of the Sahīh collections, such as al-Bukhārī and Muslim; nor the compilers of the Sunan collections, such as Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasā’ī; nor the leading compilers of Musnad collections, such as Imam Ahmad and others like him. Nor did any of the leading jurists rely on them, such as Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh, Abū Hanīfah, al-Thawrī, al-Awzā‘ī, al-Layth ibn Sa‘d, and others like them.

Rather, most of these hadiths are known to be lies and fabrications, such as the saying:

“Whoever visits me and visits my father in the same year, I guarantee him Paradise from Allah,”

and the saying:

“Whoever performs Hajj but does not visit me has slighted me.”

These hadiths and others like them are lies. The first hadith was narrated by al-Dāraqutnī and by al-Bazzār in his Musnad, and its transmission hinges on

---

## Page 13114

‘Abd Allāh ibn ibn ‘Umar al-‘Umarī, who is a weak narrator. There is no established hadith whatsoever from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concerning visiting his grave or the grave of Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend. Rather, the scholars relied only on the hadiths concerning greeting him with peace and sending blessings upon him, such as his saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“No man greets me with peace without Allah returning my soul to me so that I may return his greeting.”

This was narrated by Abū Dāwūd and others. And his saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Allah has appointed angels at my grave to convey to me the greetings of peace from my community.”

This was narrated by al-Nasā’ī. And his saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Send abundant blessings upon me on Friday and Friday eve, for your blessings are presented to me.” They asked, “How will our blessings be presented to you when you have decayed?” He replied, “Allah has forbidden the earth to consume the flesh of the prophets.”

This was narrated by Abū Dāwūd and others.

Mālik disapproved of a man saying, “I visited the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.” They explained that, in common usage, the word “visiting” had come to encompass prohibited practices. Visiting graves takes two forms: one prescribed by the Sharia and the other an innovation.

The purpose of “the prescribed visit” is to greet the deceased with peace and supplicate for him, whether he is a prophet or not. Thus, when the Companions visited the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, they would greet him with peace, supplicate for him, and then leave. None of them would stand at his grave to supplicate for himself. For this reason, Mālik and others disapproved of this and said that it was among the newly introduced religious innovations.

For this reason, the jurists said:

“When a Muslim greets him with peace

---

## Page 13115

and wishes to supplicate for himself, he should not face the grave, but rather the qiblah (direction of prayer).” They differed, however, over whether he should face the qiblah or the grave while greeting him with peace. Abū Hanīfah said that he should face the qiblah, while Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad said that he should face the grave.

This is based on his saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“O Allah, do not make my grave an idol that is worshipped.”

And his saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Do not make my grave a place of recurring gatherings.”

And his saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of prayer”—warning against what they had done.

And his saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Those before you used to take graves as places of prayer. Beware! Do not take graves as places of prayer, for I forbid you to do so.”

For this reason, the early generations agreed that one should not touch any grave, whether that of a prophet or anyone else, or rub oneself against it. Nor is it recommended to pray beside it or deliberately seek it out to supplicate beside it or through it. These practices were among the causes of associating partners with Allah and worshipping idols. As Allah Most High says:

“And they said, ‘Never abandon your gods; never abandon Wadd, Suwā‘, Yaghūth, Ya‘ūq, or Nasr.’”

A group of the early generations said: These were righteous men among Noah’s people. When they died, the people remained in devotion at their graves, then fashioned statues of them and worshipped them.

These and similar practices constitute the “innovative visit” and are of the same nature as the religion of the Christians and the polytheists. This is when the visitor’s purpose is to have his supplication answered at the grave, or to supplicate to the deceased, seek his aid, and ask him for something, or

---

## Page 13116

adjure Allah by him to fulfil his needs and relieve his distress. All these are innovations that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, neither prescribed nor his Companions practised. The imams explicitly prohibited such practices, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

For this reason, none of the Companions would go specifically to visit “the grave of Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend.” Rather, they would go only to Jerusalem, in obedience to the hadith authentically transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, through several chains of transmission:

“No journey should be undertaken except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque, al-Aqsā Mosque, and this mosque of mine.”

Accordingly, the leading scholars of Islam agreed that if a person vowed to travel to visit “the grave of Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend,” “the Mount” where Allah spoke to Moses, peace be upon him, “Mount Hirā,” or similar places, he would not be obliged to fulfil his vow. Whether he must offer the expiation for an oath is a matter on which there are two views. For the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah, let him obey Him; and whoever vows to disobey Allah, let him not disobey Him.”

Travel to these places is sinful according to the stronger of the two views. Indeed, those who hold that prayers may not be shortened on a sinful journey have explicitly stated that someone undertaking such a journey may not shorten his prayers.

If, however, a person vowed to go to the Sacred Mosque, he would be obliged to fulfil his vow, by unanimous agreement. If he vowed to go to the mosque in Medina or to Jerusalem, the scholars have two views. The stronger is that he must fulfil his vow, as held by Mālik, Ahmad, and al-Shāfiʿī in one of his two opinions. The second view

---

## Page 13117

is that he is not obliged to fulfil it, as held by Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfi‘ī in his other opinion. This rests on the principle that a vow makes an act obligatory only if acts of the same kind are already obligatory under religious law. The correct view, however, is that every vow to perform an act of obedience must be fulfilled, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah, let him obey Him.”

He did not exclude any act of obedience.

The point here is that the Companions did not regard travelling to visit any such places as recommended—whether sites associated with the prophets, their graves, or their mosques—with the exception of the three mosques. Rather, when some people did such things, others rebuked them, just as they rebuked those who visited the Mount where Allah spoke to Moses. Even the “Cave of Hirā,” where the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to worship before his prophetic mission, was not visited by him after his mission began, nor by any of his Companions. The same applies to the supplication recorded in the Quran.

It is established that, during one of his journeys, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—saw people taking turns praying at a particular place. He asked, “What is this?”

They replied, “A place where the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed.”

He said, “A place where the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed! Do you want to turn the sites associated with the prophets into places of worship for yourselves? This is precisely what caused those before you to perish. Whoever is there when prayer falls due should pray; otherwise, let him move on.”

This is because Allah has not prescribed any places for Muslims to frequent for worship except mosques alone. As for a place that is not a mosque, He has not prescribed deliberately going there

---

## Page 13118

for worship, even if it is a site associated with a prophet or a prophet’s grave.

Moreover, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade establishing mosques over the graves of the prophets and the righteous, saying:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship. Beware! Do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.”

These are two hadiths recorded in al-Sahīh.

In al-Musnad and Sahīh Abī Hātim, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“Among the worst of people are those who are still alive when the Hour overtakes them, and those who take graves as places of worship.”

Indeed, he disapproved of praying in graveyards in general, because this involves resembling those who take graves as places of worship. As reported in the Sunan collections, he said:

“The whole earth is a place of prayer, except graveyards and bathhouses.”

These points have been expressly stated by the leading religious scholars among the followers of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad, the scholars of Iraq, and others. Indeed, this is also reported from Anas.

---

## Page 13119

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked about the saying:

“Whoever performs Hajj but does not visit me has shunned me.”

He replied:

The saying, “Whoever performs Hajj and does not visit me has shunned me,” is a fabrication. Shunning the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is forbidden, whereas visiting his grave is not obligatory, by the unanimous agreement of the Muslims. No hadith concerning visiting his grave has been authentically established from him. Rather, reports circulated such as:

“Whoever visits me and visits my father in the same year, I guarantee that Allah will grant him Paradise,”

and others like it are fabrications by scholarly consensus. Al-Dāraqutnī and others have narrated hadiths concerning visiting his grave, but they are weak.

Mālik—one of those most knowledgeable about the rights of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the sunnah followed by the people of his city among the Companions, their Followers, and the generation after them—disapproved of saying, “I visited the grave of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.” Had this wording been authentically established from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and been known to the scholars of Medina, Mālik would not have disapproved of it.

As for saying, “I greeted the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with peace,” there is unanimous agreement that this is not disapproved of. As reported from him in the Sunan collections, he said:

“No man greets me with peace but Allah restores my soul to me so that I may return his greeting.”

It was also the practice of

---

## Page 13120

Ibn ʿUmar to say:

“Peace be upon you, O Messenger of Allah! Peace be upon you, O Abū Bakr! Peace be upon you, my father!”

In Sunan Abī Dāwūd, it is reported that the Prophet said:

“Send abundant blessings upon me on Friday and the night preceding it, for your blessings are presented to me.” They asked, “How will our blessings be presented to you when you have decomposed?” He replied, “Allah has forbidden the earth to consume the flesh of the prophets.”

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

“Is Mecca superior to Medina, or is it the other way round?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Mecca is superior, because it is authentically reported from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAdī ibn al-Hamrāʾ that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, addressed Mecca while standing at al-Hazwarah, saying:

“By Allah, you are indeed the best of Allah’s lands and the most beloved of Allah’s lands to Him. Had my people not driven me out of you, I would not have left.”

Al-Tirmidhī said, “This is an authentic hadith.”

Another version reads:

“You are indeed the best of Allah’s lands and the most beloved of Allah’s lands to Him.”

Thus, it is established that Mecca is the best of Allah’s lands and the most beloved of Allah’s lands to Allah and His Messenger. This explicitly establishes its superiority.

As for the report that is narrated:

“You have brought me out of the place most beloved to me, so settle me in the place most beloved to You,”

this is a fabricated, false hadith that none of the scholars has narrated. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 13121

He was asked:

“Is the soil in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was buried superior to the Sacred Mosque?”

He replied:

As for the “soil” in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was buried, I know of no one who has said that it is superior to the Sacred Mosque, the Prophet’s Mosque, or al-Aqsā Mosque except al-Qādī 'Iyād. He stated that there was consensus on this, yet, as far as we know, no one before him had expressed this view. There is no evidence to support it. Rather, the body of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is superior to the mosques.

As for that in which he was created or buried, his being superior does not necessarily mean that what he was created from is superior. No one says that the body of his father, 'Abd Allāh, is superior to the bodies of the prophets, for Allah brings the living out of the dead and the dead out of the living. Noah was a noble prophet, whereas his son who drowned was a disbeliever. Abraham was the intimate friend of the Most Merciful, whereas his father Āzar was a disbeliever.

The texts indicating the superiority of mosques are unrestricted; no exception is made in them for the graves of

---

## Page 13122

the prophets or the righteous. If what he said were true, the burial place of every prophet—indeed, of every righteous person—would be superior to the mosques, which are the houses of Allah. Thus, the houses of created beings would be superior to the houses of the Creator, which Allah has ordained to be raised and His name remembered therein. This is an innovated view in religion, contrary to the foundations of Islam.

He was also asked:

“Two men disputed. One said, ‘The soil of the grave of Muhammad the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is superior to the heavens and the earth.’ The other said, ‘The Kaaba is superior.’ Which of them is correct?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. As for Muhammad himself, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, Allah has created no being more honoured in His sight than him. As for the soil itself, it is not superior to the Kaaba, the Sacred House; rather, the Kaaba is superior to it. No scholar is known to have held the soil of the grave to be superior to the Kaaba except al-Qādī 'Iyād. No one preceded him in this view, nor did anyone agree with him on it. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 13123

He was also asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

“What do the esteemed jurists, the leading scholars of religion, say: Is residing in al-Shām preferable to residing in other lands? Is there any text in the Quran or the hadiths concerning this, or not? Please answer us, and may you be rewarded.”

The Shaykh of Islam and the Muslims, defender of the Sunnah, Taqī al-Dīn, replied:

Praise be to Allah. Residing wherever circumstances are more conducive to obedience to Allah and His Messenger and to performing good and righteous deeds—where one has greater knowledge of these deeds, greater ability to perform them, and greater enthusiasm for them—is preferable to residing somewhere one’s obedience to Allah and His Messenger would be less. This is the overarching principle, for the most honoured of creation in Allah’s sight are those with the greatest piety.

“Piety” is as Allah Most High explains it in His words:

“But righteousness is in one who believes in Allah and the Last Day,”

through to His words:

“Those are the ones who have been true, and it is those who are the righteous.”

Its essence is to do what Allah and His Messenger have commanded and to refrain from what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden. Since this is the governing principle, its application varies according to a person’s circumstances.

Thus, a man’s residence in a land of disbelief and disobedience, with its various forms of religious innovation and wickedness, may be preferable if he is striving in Allah’s cause with his hand or his tongue, enjoining

---

## Page 13124

what is right and forbidding what is wrong, such that, were he to move from there to a land of faith and obedience, his good deeds would diminish and he would no longer be striving in Allah’s cause, even though his heart would be more at ease. The same applies if moving would deprive him of the good he used to do in places of wickedness and religious innovation. For this reason, residing at the frontiers with the intention of keeping guard in the cause of Allah Most High is preferable to residing near the three mosques, by scholarly agreement.

Jihad, as a category of deeds, is superior to Hajj, as Allah Most High says:

“Do you regard providing water for pilgrims and maintaining the Sacred Mosque as equal to the deeds of one who believes in Allah and the Last Day and strives in Allah’s cause? They are not equal in Allah’s sight, and Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people.”

“Those who believe, emigrate, and strive in Allah’s cause”—to the end of the verse.

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was asked, ‘Which deeds are best?’ He replied, ‘Faith in Allah and His Messenger, and jihad in His cause.’ He was asked, ‘Then what?’ He replied, ‘An accepted Hajj.’”

The same applies if a person is unable to emigrate and move to a better place where obedience would be easier for him, while his obedience to Allah and His Messenger is the same in both places, but more arduous where he is. When two acts of obedience are equal, the more arduous of them is superior.

This was the argument advanced by those who had emigrated to Abyssinia and lived among the disbelievers against someone who claimed to be superior to them. They said: “We were among hostile strangers, while you were with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who taught those of you who lacked knowledge and fed your hungry. We endured that for Allah’s sake.”

---

## Page 13125

But if his religious commitment is weaker there, moving is better for him. This is the case with most people, for most do not resist, but instead follow the religion of the majority.

Since this is so, Islam and its prescribed practices are more manifest in al-Shām (Greater Syria) at this time than elsewhere. This is known through observation and reason, and is virtually agreed upon among discerning Muslims endowed with knowledge and faith. The scriptural texts also indicate this.

For example, Abū Dāwūd relates in his Sunan that ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Amr reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There will be an emigration after an emigration, and the best people on earth will be those who remain closest to the land to which Abraham emigrated.”

Also in his Sunan, ‘Abd Allāh ibn Khawla reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“You will form armies: an army in al-Shām, an army in Yemen, and an army in Iraq.” Ibn Khawla said, “O Messenger of Allah, choose for me.” He replied, “Go to al-Shām, for it is Allah’s chosen portion of His earth, to which He draws the choicest of His creation. Whoever refuses should go to Yemen and beware of its treachery. Allah has undertaken for me to care for al-Shām and its people.”

Al-Khawālī used to say: “Whoever Allah undertakes to care for will not be neglected.” These are two explicit texts establishing the superiority of al-Shām.

In Muslim, Abū Hurayra, may Allah be pleased with him, reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The people of the west will remain triumphant, unharmed by those who oppose or abandon them, until the Hour comes.”

Imam Ahmad said: “The people of the west are the people of al-Shām.” It is as he said, for this was the usage of the people of the Prophet’s city at that

---

## Page 13126

time. They called the people of Najd and Iraq “the people of the east,” and the people of al-Shām “the people of the west.” East and west are relative: every place has its own east and west. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, spoke these words in Medina, so whatever lay west of it was its west, and whatever lay east of it was its east.

Anyone familiar with the geographical coordinates of lands—their longitudes and latitudes—knows that the fortresses on the banks of the Euphrates, such as al-Bīra and others, are aligned with Medina. Likewise, places farther east by approximately the distance permitting the shortening of prayers, such as Harrān and places in line with it, including al-Raqqa and Sumaysāt, are aligned with Umm al-Qurā (the Mother of Cities), Mecca—may Allah ennoble it. Hence, the direction of prayer there is the most direct. Whatever lies east of the line corresponding to Medina is to its east, and whatever lies west of that is to its west.

Reliable works, such as Ahmad’s Musnad and others, contain several reports from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concerning this principle. These include his description of the people of al-Shām:

“Their hypocrites do not prevail over their believers.”

And his saying:

“I saw what seemed to be the pillar of the Book—in another narration, the pillar of Islam—being taken from beneath my head. I followed it with my eyes as it was carried to al-Shām.”

The pillar of the Book and Islam is that upon which they rest; these people are its bearers and upholders.

Another example is his saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“The heartland of the believers is al-Shām.”

Another is the report in the two Sahīh collections from Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal, who related from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

---

## Page 13127

that he said:

“A group of my community will continue to prevail in upholding the truth, unharmed by those who oppose them or abandon them, until the Hour comes.”

The two collections also relate that Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal said:

“They are in al-Shām.”

In al-Bukhārī’s History, he said:

“They are in Damascus.”

It is also narrated:

“They are in the environs of Jerusalem.”

The two Sahīh collections also relate from Ibn ‘Umar that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said that the angels of the Most Merciful shade al-Shām with their outspread wings. The reports supporting this meaning reinforce one another. However, this reply—not an impromptu one—is being given in haste.

The Book, the Sunnah, and what has been narrated from the earlier prophets, peace be upon them, together with what is known through sensory perception, reason, and the spiritual unveilings of those who know Allah, indicate that creation and command began in Mecca, the Mother of Cities. It is thus the mother of creation, and there began Muhammad’s prophetic mission, whose light encompassed the earth. Allah has made it a support for mankind: towards it they pray, to it they make pilgrimage, and through it are sustained whatever benefits Allah wills for their religious and worldly lives.

Thus, in the earliest period, Islam’s prominence was greatest in the Hijāz. The evidence already mentioned indicates that the “dominion of prophethood” is in al-Shām and that the gathering will be there. Creation and command will therefore return to Jerusalem and its environs, and there all creation will be gathered. At the end of time, Islam will be most prominent in al-Shām. Just as Mecca is superior to Jerusalem, the first members of this community are better than its last. And just as, at the end of time, the command returns to

---

## Page 13128

al-Shām, just as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was taken by night from the Sacred Mosque to the Farthest Mosque. Thus, the best people on earth at the end of time will be those who remain most firmly in the land to which Abraham, peace be upon him, migrated, namely al-Shām. Thus, the matter’s bearing is as observed and known.

The glorious Quran indicates the blessedness of al-Shām in five verses. Allah says:

“And We caused the people who had been oppressed to inherit the eastern and western regions of the land which We had blessed.”

The land that Allah Most High caused the Children of Israel to inherit was none other than al-Shām.

He also says:

“Glory be to Him who took His servant by night from the Sacred Mosque to the Farthest Mosque, whose surroundings We have blessed.”

And He says:

“And We delivered him and Lot to the land which We had blessed.”

And He says:

“And for Solomon We subjected the wind, blowing forcefully at his command towards the land which We had blessed.”

And He, Most High, says:

“And We placed between them and the towns which We had blessed other towns in plain sight”—to the end of the verse.

These are five explicit scriptural statements. “Blessing” encompasses blessings in religion and in worldly life. Both are known beyond doubt.

This, however, concerns the general and predominant situation. For many people, residing somewhere other than al-Shām may be better, as explained earlier. Likewise, many inhabitants of al-Shām would be better off leaving it for a place where they would be more obedient to Allah and His Messenger.

Abū al-Dardā’ wrote to Salmān al-Fārisī—may Allah be pleased with them both—saying:

“Come to the land

---

## Page 13129

that is holy.” Salmān wrote back:

“Land does not sanctify anyone; it is a person’s deeds that sanctify him.”

It is just as Salmān al-Fārisī said. Mecca—may Allah Most High protect it—is the noblest of places. Yet when Islam was a stranger, it was an abode of unbelief and war where residence was forbidden. After the emigration, the emigrants were forbidden to return there to settle. Likewise, in the time of Moses, peace be upon him, before he led the Children of Israel out, al-Shām was the abode of the polytheistic Sabians, who were tyrannical and wicked. Concerning it, Allah Most High said to the Children of Israel:

“I will show you the abode of the wicked.”

Descriptions of a land as an “abode of unbelief,” an “abode of Islam or faith,” an “abode of peace” or “war,” an “abode of obedience” or “disobedience,” or an “abode of believers” or “the wicked” are contingent, not inherent. A land may pass from one description to another, just as a person may pass from unbelief to faith and knowledge, or the reverse.

As for the excellence that endures at every time and in every place, it lies in faith and righteous deeds. As Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, those who have believed, the Jews, the Christians, and the Sabians—whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day and does righteous deeds—will have their reward with their Lord”—to the end of the verse.

And Allah Most High says:

“They say, ‘None will enter Paradise unless he is a Jew or a Christian.’ Those are their wishes. Say, ‘Produce your proof, if you are truthful.’”

“Rather, whoever submits himself to Allah while doing good will have his reward with his Lord”—to the end of the verse.

And Allah Most High says:

“And who is better in religion than one who submits himself to Allah while doing good and follows the religion of Abraham

---

## Page 13130

, upright in faith? And Allah took Abraham as a close friend.”

Submitting oneself to Allah Most High means devoting one’s intention and deeds sincerely to Him and placing one’s trust in Him. As Allah Most High says:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

He also says:

“So worship Him and put your trust in Him.”

And Allah Most High says:

“In Him I put my trust, and to Him I turn.”

Ever since Allah established His proof against the people of the earth through the last of His messengers, Muhammad, His servant and Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—all people on earth have been obliged to believe in him, obey him, and follow his law and way.

The best of people are therefore those who know most about what he brought and follow it most closely—in knowledge, inward state, speech, and action. They are the most mindful of Allah. Whatever place or activity best helps a person achieve this aim is best for him, even if something else is best for another person. Then, if each person does what is best for him, and the good deeds and benefits he attains equal those attained by the other, the two are equal. Otherwise, whichever of them attains more in this regard is superior.

In these times, the decline and desolation of the “three mosques” with respect to knowledge and faith are such that the superiority of many people in the farthest western lands over most of their inhabitants becomes clear. One should therefore not regard the excellence of a place as an unqualified indication of the excellence of its inhabitants. Rather, everyone should be given their due. What counts, however, is a person’s excellence in faith, righteous deeds, and good speech.

Some places may nevertheless be more conducive to certain deeds, as Mecca—may Allah Most High protect it—is conducive to circumambulation, prayer whose reward is multiplied, and similar

---

## Page 13131

acts.

Yet a weightier countervailing consideration may make what is ordinarily preferable less preferable. An example is someone who resides in Mecca while begging, looking expectantly to others for support, and neglecting many righteous deeds. The same applies to someone who seeks to reside in Greater Syria to safeguard his wealth and person, rather than to perform righteous deeds. Actions are judged by intentions.

The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—uttered this noble hadith in connection with migration, saying:

“Actions are judged only by intentions, and each person will have only what he intended. Whoever migrates to Allah and His Messenger, his migration is to Allah and His Messenger. Whoever migrates for worldly gain or to marry a woman, his migration is for that for which he migrated.”

He said this because a man had migrated to marry a woman called Umm Qays, and he became known as ‘the migrant for Umm Qays.’

Judging one group superior to another does not entail that every individual in the former is superior to every individual in the latter. Examples include the superiority of the second generation over the third, of the Arabs over others, and of Quraysh over others. The same principle applies here. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 13132

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Two men disagreed about prayer in the Umayyad Mosque: is it equivalent to ninety prayers, as some claim, or not?

They have also stated that “three hundred prophets are buried there.” Is this true or not?

They have stated that “one who sleeps in Greater Syria is like one who spends the night in prayer in Iraq,” and that “one who observes a voluntary fast in Iraq is like one who does not fast in Greater Syria.” They have also stated that “Allah created blessing in seventy-one portions: one portion in Iraq and seventy in Greater Syria.” Is this true or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. No hadith has been reported from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—stating that the reward for prayer in the congregational mosque of Damascus is multiplied. It is, however, among the mosques in which Allah Most High is remembered most abundantly. Nor has it been established that the aforementioned number of prophets are buried there.

As for someone residing in Greater Syria or elsewhere, actions are judged by intentions. If he resides there with a righteous intention, he is rewarded for that. Wherever a servant is more obedient to Allah, it is better for him to reside there. [Authentic hadiths have been reported concerning the virtues of Greater Syria and its people, and it is indicated in

---

## Page 13133

the Quran, in four passages, that there is blessing.] (*)

There is no doubt that Islam and its supporters have a stronger presence there—in heart, action, and speech—than elsewhere. The ascendancy of faith and the suppression of disbelief and hypocrisy there have no equivalent elsewhere.

As for the aforementioned reports about fasting and not fasting, and about blessing being divided into seventy-one portions between Greater Syria and Iraq in the manner described, we have not heard this from any of the scholars. And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

Did ʿĀʾishah, the wife of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—enter Damascus and relate hadiths to the people in its congregational mosque, or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. None of the wives of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—entered Damascus, neither ʿĀʾishah nor any other wife. And Allah knows best.

> **Footnotes:**
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd said (p. 211):
> Two points should be noted here:
> First: Some text has been omitted. The correct wording is “and the Quran indicates that blessing is found there,” or something similar.
> Second: The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—states in 27/44 and 27/505 that the blessing of al-Shām (Greater Syria) is mentioned in five passages of the Quran. This is correct and accords with the Quran. What appears here may therefore be a slip of the pen. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 13134

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked about “Mount Lebanon”:

Is there any text in the Book of Allah Most High affirming its virtue, or any hadith from the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace? Is it permissible in the religion of Allah Most High for people to gesture toward it with their heads when they see it, even to the point that someone who sees it in the morning or evening considers this a tremendous blessing?

Has it been established among the scholars that forty of the abdāl reside there, or that there were men there covered with hair like that of goats? Is this a characteristic of the righteous? Is it permissible to form the intention of visiting it, or to believe that whoever sets foot on its ground has trodden upon part of a mountain singled out for mercy? Has it been established that any prophet is buried on it or in its foothills?

Has any scholar said that the “men of the unseen” reside there? What are these “men of the unseen” in whom ordinary people place their faith supposed to be like? Is it permissible in the religion of Allah Most High for Muslims to believe any of this? Is everyone who obstinately defends it and presents it as good, or compromises over it, mistaken and sinful? Under these circumstances, is someone who denounces all of this counted among those who enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong, or not?

He answered:

There is no text affirming the virtue of “Mount Lebanon” and similar mountains, neither from Allah

---

## Page 13135

nor from His Messenger. Rather, it and similar mountains are among those Allah created and made pegs for the earth and signs of His power. They contain benefits for His creation that are blessings from Allah upon His servants.

He will eventually do to them what He has declared in His words:

“They ask you about the mountains. Say, ‘My Lord will blast them into scattered dust,
leaving the earth a level plain,
in which you will see neither depression nor elevation.’”

As for accounts of people meeting devout worshippers on Mount Lebanon, Mount al-Lukkām, and similar places, and the various words and deeds reported from some of these worshippers, their origin is that these places were frontier regions where Muslims were stationed to wage jihad against the enemy. After the Muslims had conquered all of Greater Syria and lands beyond it, Gaza, Ashkelon, Acre, Beirut, Mount Lebanon, Tripoli, al-Massīsa, Sīs, Tarsus, Adana, Mount al-Lukkām, Malatya, Āmid, and Mount Laysūn, extending as far as Qazwīn and al-Shāsh, and other such places, were frontier regions, just as Alexandria and similar places were. The same was true of Abadan and similar places in Iraq.

The righteous would take turns serving on the frontiers, standing guard in Allah’s cause. Remaining on the frontiers for jihad in Allah’s cause is better than residing in Mecca or Medina. I know of no disagreement among the scholars concerning this.

It is authentically reported in Sahīh Muslim from Salmān al-Fārisī—may Allah be pleased with him—who related what was said by

---

## Page 13136

the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Standing guard for a day and a night in Allah’s cause is better than fasting and praying at night for a month. Whoever dies while stationed on the frontier dies engaged in jihad; his deeds continue to accrue to his credit, his provision from Paradise continues to reach him, and he is safe from the one who puts him to trial.”

The Sunan collections report from ‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān—may Allah be pleased with him—that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Standing guard for one day in Allah’s cause is better than a thousand days spent anywhere else.”

Abū Hurayrah—may Allah be pleased with him—said: “I would rather stand guard for one night in Allah’s cause than spend the Night of Decree in prayer beside the Black Stone.”

This is because guarding the frontiers is a form of jihad, whereas residing near the sacred sanctuaries is a form of ritual worship. Jihad in Allah’s cause, as a category, is superior to ritual worship, as established by Allah’s Book, the Sunnah of His Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and the consensus of the Muslims. As Allah Most High says:

“Do you regard providing water to pilgrims and maintaining the Sacred Mosque as equal to the deeds of one who believes in Allah and the Last Day and strives in Allah’s cause? They are not equal in Allah’s sight, and Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people.

Those who believe, emigrate, and strive in Allah’s cause with their wealth and their lives are greater in rank in Allah’s sight. It is they who will triumph.

Their Lord gives them glad tidings of mercy from Him, His pleasure, and Gardens in which they will enjoy lasting bliss,

remaining there forever. Indeed, with Allah is an immense reward.”

The virtues of jihad and guarding the frontiers are many.

For this reason, righteous believers would serve on the frontiers, as did al-Awzā‘ī, Abū Ishāq al-Fazārī, Mukhallad ibn al-Husayn, and Ibrāhīm ibn

---

## Page 13137
