A hadith relates that one of the Muslims saw someone saying, “What excellent people you are, were it not that you set up rivals!” That is, they set up a rival to Allah by saying, “Whatever Allah wills and Muhammad wills.” The Prophet, may Allah bless him and his family and grant him peace, therefore forbade them to say this.

In the authentic collection, Zayd ibn Khālid reported:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family and grant him peace, led us in the dawn prayer at al-Hudaybiyah following rainfall during the night. He said, ‘Do you know what your Lord said last night?’

“We replied, ‘Allah and His Messenger know best.’

“He said, ‘He said: “This morning, some of My servants believe in Me and others disbelieve. As for whoever says, ‘We were given rain by Allah’s grace and mercy,’ he believes in Me and disbelieves in the stars. But whoever says, ‘We were given rain because of such-and-such a star,’ he disbelieves in Me and believes in the stars.”’”

The means Allah has established as such must not be treated as partners, equals, or helpers alongside Him.

---

## Page 13180

And when someone says, “Through the shaykh’s blessing,” he may mean the shaykh’s supplication. The supplication most swiftly answered is that of one person for another in his absence. He may also mean the blessing of the good that the shaykh instructed him to do and taught him, or the blessing of his assistance in upholding the truth, his religious fellowship, and similar things. All these meanings are sound.

He may also mean the shaykh’s supplication for the deceased and the absent. For, as regards the shaykh’s independently producing such an effect, or doing something he is incapable of doing, has no power to do, or does not intend—following him or complying with him in this is among reprehensible innovations and other such false notions.

What is beyond doubt is that obeying Allah Most High, believers supplicating for one another, and similar deeds bring benefit in this world and the Hereafter, through Allah’s grace and mercy.

As for the questioner’s inquiry about “the Pole, the Succour, the Unique, the All-Encompassing,” various groups use this expression and explain it in ways that are false according to the religion of Islam. Some, for example, explain “the Succour” as the one through whom assistance reaches all creatures, bringing them help and provision. They go so far as to say that even the angels and the fish of the sea receive assistance through him. This is akin to what Christians say about the Messiah, peace be upon him, and what the extremists say about ʿAlī, may Allah be pleased with him.

This is explicit disbelief. Whoever professes it must be called upon to repent; if he repents, well and good; otherwise, he is put to death. No created being, whether angel or human, is the intermediary through whom all creatures receive assistance. For this reason, what the philosophers say about the “ten intellects,” which they claim are the angels, and what Christians say about the Messiah

---

## Page 13181

and similar claims, is explicit disbelief by the consensus of the Muslims. The same applies to what he meant by “the Succour,” namely the claim made by some of them that there are three hundred and ten-odd men on earth whom they call “the Noble Ones.” From among them, seventy are selected as “the Overseers”; from among these, forty are “the Substitutes”; from among these, seven are “the Poles”; from among these, four are “the Pegs”; and from among these, one is “the Succour.” They claim that he resides in Mecca, and that whenever the inhabitants of the earth suffer a calamity affecting their provision and support, they turn to the three hundred and ten-odd men. These in turn seek help from the seventy, the seventy from the forty, the forty from the seven, the seven from the four, and the four from the one.

Some of them add to or subtract from these numbers, titles, and ranks, for they hold various views concerning them. Some even say that a green sheet descends from heaven onto the Kaaba, bearing the name of the Succour of the age and the name of its Khidr—according to those among them who say that Khidr is a rank and that every age has its own Khidr, for they hold two views on this matter.

All of this is false. It has no basis in the Book of Allah or the Sunnah of His Messenger, nor was it stated by any of the early generations of this nation, its imams, or the great shaykhs of earlier times who are worthy of being followed. It is well known that our master, the Messenger of the Lord of all worlds, and Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿUthmān, and ʿAlī—may Allah be pleased with them—were the best of creation in their time. Yet they lived in Medina, not Mecca.

Some of them have narrated a hadith concerning “Hilāl,” the slave of al-Mughīra ibn Shuʿba

---

## Page 13182

stating that he was one of the seven. This hadith is false by the agreement of the experts. Although Abū Nuʿaym narrated some of these reports in Hilyat al-Awliyāʾ, and Shaykh Abū ʿAbd al-Rahmān al-Sulamī did so in some of his works, do not be misled by that. Such material includes sound, good, and weak reports, as well as fabricated reports and lies which scholars unanimously agree are false and fabricated. Sometimes he transmits them in the manner of certain hadith scholars who narrate what they have heard without distinguishing the sound from the false. Hadith scholars would not narrate reports of this kind, because it is authentically reported that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and his family and grant them peace—said:

“Whoever narrates a report from me, believing it to be false, is one of the liars.”

In short, all Muslims know that when circumstances arise involving hope or fear—such as their supplications when seeking rain for provision to descend, or their supplications during eclipses and when preparing to ward off affliction, and similar occasions—they call upon Allah alone, without partner, associating nothing with Him. Muslims were never to turn to anyone other than Allah, Mighty and Majestic, for their needs. Indeed, even the polytheists in their days of ignorance would call upon Him without an intermediary, and Allah would answer them. Do you then suppose that, after they have embraced monotheism and Islam, He will answer their supplications only through this intermediary for which Allah has sent down no authority?

The Exalted said:

“When affliction touches man, he calls upon Us, lying on his side, sitting, or standing. But when We remove his affliction, he goes on his way as though he had never called upon Us concerning an affliction that had touched him.”

And the Exalted said:

---

## Page 13183

“When distress befalls you at sea, all those you invoke are lost to you except Him.”

And the Exalted said:

“Say, ‘Tell me: if Allah’s punishment came upon you, or the Hour came upon you, would you call upon anyone other than Allah, if you are truthful?’”

“Rather, Him alone you would invoke, and He would remove the distress for which you invoked Him, if He willed, and you would forget whatever you associate with Him.”

And He said:

“We certainly sent messengers to nations before you, then afflicted them with poverty and hardship so that they might humble themselves.”

“Why, then, did they not humble themselves when Our punishment came upon them? Instead, their hearts hardened, and Satan made their deeds appear attractive to them.”

The Prophet—may Allah bless him and his family and grant them peace—supplicated for rain for his Companions, both with and without a formal prayer. He led them in the prayer for rain and the eclipse prayer. He would also offer qunūt supplications during his prayers, seeking victory over the polytheists. His rightly guided caliphs followed the same course after him, as did the leading religious scholars and the shaykhs of the Muslims. They have continued upon this path.

Hence the saying: “Three things have no foundation: the Bāb (Gate) of the Nusayrīs, the Awaited One of the Rāfidah, and the Ghawth (Succour-Giver) of the ignorant.” The Nusayrīs make claims of this kind about their Bāb, asserting that he sustains the world. That individual does exist, but the Nusayrīs’ claim about him is false.

As for the awaited Muhammad ibn al-Hasan, the Ghawth residing in Mecca, and the like, these are fictitious and do not exist. Likewise, some claim that the Qutb (Pole), the all-encompassing Ghawth, supplies Allah’s allies with aid and knows them all, and make similar claims. This is false. For Abū Bakr and Umar

---

## Page 13184

—may Allah be pleased with them both—did not know all of Allah’s allies, nor did they supply them with aid. How, then, could these misguided, deluded liars do so? Allah’s Messenger—may Allah bless him and his family and grant them peace—the master of the children of Adam, recognized those of his nation whom he had never seen only by the marks of ablution: the radiance of their faces, hands, and feet. Among these are allies of Allah whom none but Allah, Mighty and Majestic, can number. Nor did he know most of Allah’s prophets, though he is their leader and spokesman. Indeed, Allah Most High said:

“We certainly sent messengers before you. Some We have told you about, and others We have not told you about.”

Moses did not know al-Khidr, nor did al-Khidr know Moses. Indeed, when Moses greeted him, al-Khidr said to him, “How is the greeting of peace known in your land?”

He replied, “I am Moses.”

He asked, “Moses of the Children of Israel?”

He replied, “Yes.”

He had heard his name and reports about him, but did not recognize him in person. Whoever says that he is the chief of Allah’s allies, or that he teaches them all, has spoken falsehood.

The correct view, held by the discerning scholars, is that he is dead and did not live to see Islam. Had he been alive in the time of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and his family and grant them peace—he would have been obliged to believe in him and strive alongside him, as Allah made this obligatory for him and others. He would have been in Mecca and Medina. Being present with the Companions to strive alongside them and support them in upholding the religion would have been more fitting for him than being present among a group of unbelievers to patch their boat for them. He would not have concealed himself from the best nation brought forth for mankind, although he

---

## Page 13185

had been among the polytheists and had not concealed himself from them. Moreover, Muslims have no need of him or others like him, whether in their religious or worldly affairs. They received their religion from the Messenger, the unlettered Prophet—may Allah bless him and his family and grant them peace—who taught them the Book and wisdom. Their Prophet said to them:

“If Moses were alive, and you then followed him and abandoned me, you would go astray.”

When Jesus son of Mary—peace be upon him—descends from heaven, he will judge among them only according to their Lord’s Book and their Prophet’s Sunnah. Given this, what need have they of al-Khidr or anyone else? The Prophet—may Allah bless him and his family and grant them peace—told them that Jesus would descend from heaven and be present among the Muslims. He said:

“How could a nation perish when I am at its beginning and Jesus at its end?”

If, then, these two noble prophets—who, together with Abraham, Moses, and Noah, are the best of the messengers, with Muhammad—may Allah bless him and his family and grant them peace—being the master of the children of Adam—have not concealed themselves from this nation, neither from its ordinary members nor from its elite, how could someone who is not their equal conceal himself from them? If al-Khidr has remained alive throughout, why did neither the Prophet—may Allah bless him and his family and grant them peace—nor his rightly guided caliphs ever mention this or inform his nation of it?

As for the claim, “He is the chief of Allah’s allies,” the claimant should be asked: “Who appointed him chief, when the best of Allah’s allies are the Companions of Muhammad—may Allah bless him and his family and grant them peace?” Al-Khidr is not among them.

Of the accounts commonly related on this subject, some are lies, while others rest on someone’s supposition—for example, someone who saw a man and assumed that he was al-Khidr

---

## Page 13186

and said, “He is al-Khidr.” Similarly, the Rāfidah see someone whom they suppose to be the awaited, infallible imam, or claim that he is. It is related that when al-Khidr was mentioned to Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal, he said:

“Whoever refers you to someone absent has not treated you fairly. None but Satan has put this on people’s tongues.”

We have discussed this at length elsewhere.

If, however, by “the Pole, the Succour, the Unique One, the All-Encompassing One,” the speaker means a man who is the best person of his age, that is possible. Yet it is also possible for two, three, or four people in an age to be equal in excellence. One cannot categorically assert that in every age only one person ranks highest in excellence. There may be a group whose members surpass one another in some respects but not others, with their respective merits being closely matched or equal.

Even if there is a man who is the best person of his age, calling him “the Pole, the Succour, the All-Encompassing One” is an innovation for which Allah has sent down no authority. None of this nation’s early generations or imams ever used such language. The early generations would consider certain individuals to be the best, or among the best, of their age, without applying to them these titles for which Allah has sent down no authority. This is especially so since some who adopt this title claim that the first of the Poles was al-Hasan ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib—may Allah be pleased with them both—and that this position then passes down to those below him, reaching some of the shaykhs of

---

## Page 13187

later generations. This is untenable according to both Ahl al-Sunnah and the Rāfidah. Where, then, do Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿUthmān, ʿAlī, and the earliest forerunners among the Emigrants and the Helpers stand? At the Prophet’s death—may Allah bless him and his family and grant them peace—al-Hasan had only approached the age of discernment and puberty.

It has been related of one of the leading shaykhs who espouse this notion that the knowledge of “the Pole, the Unique One, the Succour, the All-Encompassing One” corresponds exactly to Allah’s knowledge, and his power to Allah’s power: he knows everything Allah knows and can do everything Allah can do. He claimed that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and his family and grant them peace—was likewise, and that this passed from him to al-Hasan and then through a succession down to his own shaykh. I explained that this is explicit disbelief and reprehensible ignorance, and that making such a claim about the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and his family and grant them peace—is disbelief, let alone making it about anyone else. Allah Most High has said:

“Say: I do not tell you that I possess Allah’s treasures, nor do I know the unseen, nor do I tell you that I am an angel.”

And He Most High said:

“Say: I have no power to benefit or harm myself, except as Allah wills. Had I known the unseen, I would have acquired much good, and no harm would have touched me.”

To the end of the verse. And He Most High said:

“They say: Had we had any say in the matter, we would not have been killed here.”

To the end of the verse. And He Most High said:

“They say: Do we have any say in the matter? Say: The matter belongs entirely to Allah.”

And He Most High said:

“So that He might cut off a section of the disbelievers or humiliate them, so that they turn back disappointed. You have no say in the matter—whether He turns to them in mercy or punishes them, for they are wrongdoers.”

And He said:

---

## Page 13188

Most High:

“You cannot guide whom you love, but Allah guides whom He wills, and He knows best those who are guided.”

Allah, glorified and exalted, has commanded us to obey His Messenger—may Allah bless him and his family and grant them peace—saying:

“Whoever obeys the Messenger has indeed obeyed Allah.”

He has commanded us to follow him, saying, Most High:

“Say: If you love Allah, then follow me; Allah will love you.”

He has commanded us to honour him, revere him, and support him. He has established rights for him which He has explained in His Book and the Sunnah of His Messenger. Indeed, He has obliged us to love him more than anyone else, including ourselves and our families. He Most High said:

“The Prophet has a greater claim on the believers than they have on themselves.”

And He Most High said:

“Say: If your fathers, your sons, your brothers, your spouses, your relatives, the wealth you have acquired, the trade whose decline you fear, and the homes you cherish are dearer to you than Allah and His Messenger and striving in His cause, then wait until Allah brings about His command.”

He—may Allah bless him and his family and grant them peace—said:

“By Him in whose hand is my soul, none of you believes until I am dearer to him than his children, his parents, and all people.”

Umar—may Allah be pleased with him—said to him, “O Messenger of Allah, you are dearer to me than everything except myself.” He replied, “No, Umar, not until I am dearer to you than yourself.” Umar said, “Then you are indeed dearer to me than myself.” He replied, “Now, Umar.”

He also said:

“Three qualities, if present in a person, will enable him to taste the sweetness of faith: that Allah and His Messenger are dearer to him than all else; that he loves someone only for Allah’s sake; and that he hates to

---

## Page 13189

return to disbelief after Allah has saved him from it, just as he hates to be cast into the Fire.”

In His Book, He has explained the rights that belong exclusively to Him, the rights of His messengers, and the rights believers have over one another, as we have discussed at length elsewhere. An example is His saying, Most High:

“Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger, fears Allah, and is mindful of Him—it is they who will triumph.”

Obedience is thus due to Allah and the Messenger, whereas reverent fear and mindful devotion are due to Allah alone. He Most High also said:

“If only they had been content with what Allah and His Messenger gave them and had said, ‘Allah is sufficient for us. Allah will give us of His bounty, and so will His Messenger. To Allah alone we turn in hope.’”

Giving is thus attributed to Allah and the Messenger, whereas hope is directed to Allah alone. He Most High said:

“Whatever the Messenger gives you, take it; and whatever he forbids you, refrain from it.”

For the lawful is what Allah and His Messenger have made lawful, and the unlawful is what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden. As for sufficiency, it belongs to Allah alone, as He said:

“And they said, ‘Allah is sufficient for us.’”

He did not say, “Allah and His Messenger are sufficient for us.” He Most High also said:

“O Prophet, Allah is sufficient for you and for those believers who follow you.”

That is, Allah suffices you and suffices those believers who follow you. This is the unquestionably correct interpretation of this verse. Hence the words of Abraham and Muhammad—may blessings and peace be upon them both—were: “Allah is sufficient for us, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.”

Allah, glorified and exalted, knows best and is Most Wise. May Allah bless the best of His creation, our master Muhammad, and his family and Companions, and grant them peace.

---

## Page 13190

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

Concerning “those who visit the graves of prophets and righteous people,” such as the grave of Abraham and others: they approach the tomb and kiss it. The custodians of the site approach whoever arrives and bring him to the tomb, teaching visitors this practice and approving of their doing it.

Is this something that Allah Most High and His Messenger have commanded, or not? Is there any reward or recompense for it, or not? Is it part of the religion with which Allah, glorified be He, sent His Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—or not? If it is not, yet some people believe it to be part of the religion and practise it on that basis, must they be forbidden from doing so, or not? Did any of the four imams consider this recommended, or not? Did the Companions and their successors practise it, or not?

If any of the custodians or others engage in this practice, instruct others to do it, or approve of it in return for a payment they receive or for some other reason, will the ruler be rewarded for preventing them from doing so, or not? If they do not desist, may the ruler remove from office those among them who refuse to desist, or not?

Are the earnings people derive from such practices wholesome or tainted? Are they entitled to such earnings, or should these be taken from them and spent on

---

## Page 13191

the interests of the Muslims? Is it permissible to hold beside the “Mosque of Abraham” the musical performance they call “al-Nawba al-Khalīliyya,” and on that occasion to hold a listening gathering attended by the fuqarā’ and others, featuring the flute, or not? Does the man who plays the flute, while serving as a muezzin at the aforementioned place, thereby become openly sinful, or not? If he does not desist, should the ruler dismiss him, or not? If the ruler cannot put an end to this, may he move this performance to a place too cramped for dancing, or not?

He—may Allah be pleased with him—replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. Neither Allah nor His Messenger nor the imams of the Muslims have commanded kissing any part of the graves of prophets and righteous people or rubbing oneself against them—whether the grave of our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the grave of Abraham, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or anyone else’s grave. Nor have they commanded kissing or touching the Rock in Jerusalem or the two northern corners of the Ancient House. Rather, only its two southern corners are to be touched, in accordance with the Sunnah of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: he touched only the two southern corners and kissed only the Black Stone.

They agree that the two northern corners are neither to be touched nor kissed. They agree that the two southern corners are to be touched, and they agree that the Black Stone is to be kissed. As for kissing the Yemeni Corner, they differed, holding three well-known views.

One view is:

---

## Page 13192

it is to be kissed. Another view is that it is to be touched and the hand then kissed. A third view is that it is to be touched without kissing the hand. This is the correct view, for it is established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, touched it but neither kissed it nor kissed his hand after touching it. There is no reward or recompense for anything that is neither obligatory nor recommended. Reward and recompense are only for righteous deeds, and righteous deeds are either obligatory or recommended.

Since touching and kissing these objects is neither obligatory nor recommended, there is no reward or recompense for doing so. Whoever believes that he will be rewarded and recompensed for this is ignorant, misguided and mistaken. He is like someone who believes that he will be rewarded and recompensed for prostrating to the graves of the prophets and righteous people; someone who believes that he will be rewarded and recompensed for calling upon them besides Allah; or someone who believes that he will be rewarded and recompensed for making images of them—as the Christians do—calling upon those images and prostrating to them. The same applies to similar innovations, which are neither obligatory nor recommended, but rather constitute either disbelief or ignorance and misguidance.

By the consensus of the Muslims, none of this belongs to the religion with which Allah sent Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Anyone who believes that this is part of the religion and practises it must be prohibited from doing so. None of the four imams deemed this recommended, nor did any of the Companions or those who followed them in righteousness practise it. Anyone who commands people to do any of this, encourages them to do it, or helps them do so

---

## Page 13193

whether a caretaker or otherwise, must be forbidden to do so and prevented from doing it. The ruler is rewarded for restraining such people. Whoever does not desist must receive a discretionary punishment sufficient to deter him. At the very least, he must be removed from his position as caretaker. No one should be left to command people to do what is not part of the Muslims’ religion.

Earnings acquired through such practices are tainted, like the earnings of those who lie about Allah and His Messenger and receive payment for doing so, or those of the custodians of idols who command people to associate partners with Allah and receive payment for doing so. These practices are among the prohibited means, inducements and constituent elements of associating partners with Allah. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“O Allah, do not let my grave become an idol that is worshipped.”

Reported by Mālik in al-Muwatta’ and elsewhere. He also said:

“Do not make my grave a place of recurring observance. Send blessings upon me wherever you may be, for your blessings reach me.”

Reported by Abū Dāwūd and others.

The two Sahīh collections report that he said:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of prayer.”

He was warning against what they had done. ‘Ā’ishah said: “Were it not for that, his grave would have been left exposed, but he was averse to its being made a place of prayer.”

The Sahīh also reports that, five days before his death, he said:

“Those who came before you used to take graves as places of prayer. Beware! Do not take graves as places of prayer, for I forbid you to do so.”

The Musnad and the Sahīh of Abū Hātim report that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “Among the worst of people are those who

---

## Page 13194

are overtaken by the Hour while still alive, and those who take graves as places of prayer.” There are many hadiths and reports on this subject.

For this reason, the Companions did not travel to the grave of Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, or to the graves of other righteous people. Nor did they travel to visit Mount Sinai, the blessed spot and sacred valley mentioned by Allah in His Book, where He spoke directly to Moses. Indeed, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not visit Mount Hirā’, nor did his Companions, either during his lifetime or after his death, although it was there that revelation descended upon the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. During Hajj, they visited no places in Mecca other than the sacred sites, such as the Sacred Mosque, Minā, Muzdalifah and ‘Arafah.

Likewise, none of the Prophet’s Companions would specifically seek to supplicate at the grave of any prophet: neither the grave of our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor the grave of Abraham, nor any other prophet’s grave. For this reason, leading scholars such as Mālik and others stated that this is a religious innovation. Rather, when they came to the Prophet’s grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, they would greet him with peace and send blessings upon him. As Mālik mentioned in al-Muwatta’, when Ibn ‘Umar came to the Prophet’s grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he would send blessings upon him and upon Abū Bakr and ‘Umar.

Another report relates that he would say:

“Peace be upon you, O Messenger of Allah. Peace be upon you, O Abū Bakr. Peace be upon you, my father.”

Then he would leave.

Anyone who acquires tainted wealth, such as this man who commands people to practise religious innovations

---

## Page 13195

and takes payment for doing so, has no rightful ownership of that wealth. If it cannot be returned to its owner, the authorities shall take it from this man who has wrongfully consumed people’s wealth and obstructed Allah’s path, and spend it on the interests of the Muslims that Allah and His Messenger love. Thus, money spent in obedience to Satan is taken and spent in obedience to the Most Merciful.

As for the musical gathering they call Nawbat al-Khalīl, it is a baseless and invalid religious innovation. Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—never did anything of this kind. Nor did the Companions hold anything of this kind at Abraham’s burial place when they conquered those lands. Neither the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor his caliphs ever did anything of this kind. Rather, this practice originated either with the Christians—for they were the ones who broke an opening into Abraham’s chamber after it had been sealed, with no one entering it—or with some ignorant Muslims.

It is not permissible to hold dancing, pipe-playing, or anything similar there. Rather, these practices must be prohibited. Anyone who persists in attending such gatherings, whether a muezzin or anyone else, thereby impugns his moral integrity. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 13196

He was also asked—may Allah sanctify his soul:

What is the ruling on the assertion made by some scholars and Sufi devotees that supplications are answered at the graves of four followers of the four imams: “the grave of al-Fandalāwī,” a follower of Mālik; “the grave of al-Burhān al-Balkhī,” a follower of Abū Hanīfah; “the grave of Shaykh Nasr al-Maqdisī,” a follower of al-Shāfiʿī; and “the grave of Shaykh Abū al-Faraj,” a follower of Ahmad—may Allah be pleased with them—and that whoever faces the qiblah beside their graves and supplicates will have his supplication answered?

And what of the advice that some scholars report having received from certain shaykhs: “If a calamity or something you fear befalls you, call upon me, and the distress you experience will be relieved, whether I am alive or dead”?

And what of the claim that whoever recites Āyat al-Kursī (the Verse of the Throne), faces in the direction of Shaykh ʿAbd al-Qādir al-Jīlānī, and greets him seven times, taking one step towards his grave with each greeting, will have his need fulfilled; or, if he is attending a devotional listening gathering, he will feel uplifted and expressions of ecstasy will increase?

And what of the Sufi devotees’ assertion that Allah Most High looks upon them, manifesting Himself to them on three occasions: when the meal is spread out; when they rise to seek forgiveness or engage in discussions among themselves; and during devotional listening?

And what of the practice of certain worshippers who offer supplications at the graves of Zechariah and Hūd, perform prayers beside them, and stand in the space by the eastern side of the mosque’s portico, at the Gate of Purification in Damascus

---

## Page 13197

and supplicating beside the ʿUthmānic Quran manuscript; and of someone who presses his aching back against the column at the head of Muʿāwiyah’s grave, beside the martyrs’ graves at Bāb al-Saghīr?

Does supplication have any special prospect of acceptance or of being answered swiftly at a particular time or in a particular place—at the grave of a prophet or a righteous friend of Allah? Is it permissible, when supplicating, to seek help from Allah Most High through a prophet sent by Him, an angel brought near to Him, His speech, the Kaʿbah, the supplication known as Ihtiyāt Qāf, or the supplication of Umm Dāwūd or al-Khidr?

Is it permissible, when making a request, to adjure Allah Most High by someone’s right, someone’s sanctity, the standing of those brought near to Him, or those of His creation closest to Him; or to adjure Him by their acts and deeds?

Is it permissible to venerate a place containing perfume, saffron, and a saddle because someone saw the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, there in a dream? Or is it permissible to venerate a tree with rags hanging from it, of which people say, “This tree is blessed; righteous friends of Allah gather around it”?

Is it permissible to venerate a mountain or visit it, or to visit the shrines and relics it contains and supplicate and pray there—for example, the Cave of Blood, Adam’s Cave and the relics, the Cave of Hunger, the graves of Seth, Abel, Noah, Elijah, Ezekiel, Shībāl the Shepherd, and Ibrāhīm ibn Adham in Jibillah, the Crow’s Nest at Baalbek, the Cave of the Forty, and the bath at Tiberias? And what of visiting Ashkelon and the Mosque of Sālih in Acre, which is renowned for the sanctity accorded to it, the veneration shown to it, and the visits paid to it?

---

## Page 13198

And is it permissible to seek out graves as places for supplication, to kiss them, or to light lanterns and lamps beside them? Do these acts performed by the living bring any benefit or harm to the dead?

And what of supplication at the “Prophet’s Footprint” in Dār al-Hadīth al-Ashrafiyyah in Damascus and elsewhere; at the footprint of Moses, the cradle of Jesus, the Station of Abraham, and the head of al-Husayn; at sites associated with Suhayb al-Rūmī, Bilāl al-Habashī, and Uways al-Qaranī; and at similar places throughout the lands, villages, coasts, mountains, shrines, mosques, and congregational mosques?

Likewise, what of their claim that supplication is answered at the tower of Bāb Kaysān, between Bāb al-Saghīr and Bāb al-Sharqī, when one turns one’s back to it and faces the qiblah; and what of supplication just inside Bāb al-Farrādīn? Has anything been established concerning supplications being answered at these places, or not?

Is it permissible to seek help from someone other than Allah Most High by saying, “O rank of Muhammad!” or “O Lady Nafīsah!” or “O my master Ahmad!”; or, when someone stumbles, encounters difficulty, or jumps from one place to another, to say, “O Ali!” or “O Shaykh so-and-so!”—or not?

Is it permissible to make vows to prophets or shaykhs, such as Shaykh Jākīr, Abū al-Wafā’, Nūr al-Dīn the Martyr, or others—or not? Likewise, is it permissible to make vows to the graves of any members of the Prophet’s household, Mudrikah, the four imams, or the shaykhs of Iraq, Persia, Egypt, the Hijaz, Yemen, India, the Maghreb, and throughout the earth, or to Mount Qān and other places—or not?

---

## Page 13199

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds.

As for the claim that supplications are answered at the graves of the four aforementioned shaykhs—may Allah be pleased with them—it is of the same kind as someone else’s statement, “So-and-so’s grave is the tried-and-tested antidote,” and similar claims that supplications are answered at the graves of particular individuals.

Many people make such statements about certain graves. The grave in question may be known to belong to a righteous man from among the Companions, the Prophet’s household, or other righteous people. Alternatively, its attribution to that person may be false or unverified, as is the case with most alleged graves of prophets. The attribution may also be correct, yet the man himself may not have been righteous. All these cases occur in connection with people who make such claims, or who say that supplications are answered at a particular grave and that their own supplications were answered there, when in fact it is either the grave of someone known for sinfulness and religious innovation or the grave of a disbeliever. We have seen someone who supplicated and was then astonished when the truth about the graves was disclosed to him. We have witnessed various instances of this.

The fundamental point is this: the claim that supplications are answered at the graves of prophets and righteous people has no basis in the Book of Allah or the Sunnah of His Messenger. Nor was it stated by any of the Companions, those who followed them in righteousness, or any of the Muslim imams renowned for their leadership in religion, such as Mālik, al-Thawrī, al-Awzā'ī, al-Layth ibn Sa'd, Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfi'ī, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and Ishāq

---

## Page 13200

ibn Rāhawayh and Abū 'Ubaydah, nor by their shaykhs who served as exemplars, such as al-Fudayl ibn 'Iyād, Ibrāhīm ibn Adham, Abū Sulaymān al-Dārānī, and others like them.

None of the Companions, the Successors, or the early imams and shaykhs said that supplications are answered at the graves of prophets and righteous people, whether as a general claim or concerning particular graves. Nor did any of them say that supplicating at the graves of prophets and righteous people is better than supplicating elsewhere, or that prayer in such a place is better than prayer elsewhere. None of them deliberately sought out these graves for supplication or prayer.

Indeed, the best of all creation and their master is the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. There is no grave on earth unanimously acknowledged to be that of a prophet except his; people have differed concerning the grave of Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, and those of other prophets. The imams agree that, when visiting him, one should greet him and his two Companions with peace, in accordance with the report in the Sunan collections from Abū Hurayrah—may Allah be pleased with him—that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“No man greets me with peace without Allah restoring my soul to me so that I may return his greeting.”

This is a good hadith. Ibn Abī Shaybah and al-Dāraqutnī also narrated from him:

“Whoever greets me with peace at my grave, I hear him; and whoever invokes blessings upon me from afar, it is conveyed to me.”

Its chain of transmission contains some weakness, but it has established corroborating reports. The compilers of the Sunan collections have narrated through several routes that blessings and greetings of peace invoked upon him from afar are conveyed to him. Thus, the Sunan collections report that he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Invoke blessings upon me frequently on Friday and on the night preceding Friday,

---

## Page 13201

for your invocations of blessings are presented to me.”

They asked, “How will our invocations of blessings be presented to you when you have crumbled away?”—that is, decayed.

He replied, “Allah Most High has forbidden the earth to consume the flesh of the prophets.”

Al-Nasā’ī and others also narrated that he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Allah has assigned angels to my grave who convey to me the greetings of peace from my community.”

Despite this, none of them said that supplications are answered at his grave, or that it is recommended to make a point of supplicating while facing his grave. Rather, they explicitly stated the opposite, and all agreed that one should not supplicate while facing the grave. They differed, however, concerning greeting him with peace.

The majority, including Mālik, Ahmad, and others, said that one should greet him with peace while facing the grave. This is also what al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers stated, and I believe it is reported from him as well. Abū Hanīfah and his followers, however, said that one should greet him with peace while facing the qiblah.

Indeed, the imams of the early generations explicitly stated that one should not stand at his grave to supplicate at all, as Ismā‘īl ibn Ishāq mentioned in his book al-Mabsūt, and as al-Qādī ‘Iyād also related. Mālik said:

“I do not consider it appropriate for someone to stand at the grave of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and supplicate. Rather, he should greet him with peace and move on.”

He also said in al-Mabsūt:

“There is no harm in someone returning from a journey or setting out on one standing at the grave of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—invoking blessings upon him, and supplicating for him and for Abū Bakr and ‘Umar.”

He was then asked:

“But some people of Medina who have neither returned from a journey nor intend to undertake one do this once a day or more. Sometimes they stand there once or twice in a week or a day, or

---

## Page 13202

more often at the grave, greeting him with peace and supplicating for a while.” He replied:

“I have not heard of this from any of the jurists of our city. Nothing will set right the later generations of this community except what set right its earliest generations. I have received no report that the earliest and foremost generations of this community used to do this, except those who had returned from a journey or intended to undertake one.”

Ibn al-Qāsim said:

“I saw that when the people of Medina departed from it or entered it, they would come to the grave and offer greetings of peace.”

He said:

“That is also my practice.”

Thus, standing to supplicate after greeting him with peace was disapproved of by Mālik, the most knowledgeable scholar of his time—that is, the generation following the Successors in the Prophet’s city, whose inhabitants during the eras of the Companions, the Successors, and their followers were the most knowledgeable people about what is prescribed at the grave of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He explained that what is recommended is to supplicate for him and his two Companions through the prescribed invocations of blessings and peace. Even this, however, is not recommended for the people of Medina at all times, but rather when returning from a journey or intending to undertake one. This is because it is a greeting to him, and a person’s home is not sought out at all times merely to greet him, unlike when one returns from a journey.

Mālik said, in the report transmitted by Abū Wahb:

“When greeting the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—one should stand facing the grave, not the qiblah. One should draw near and offer greetings of peace, but should not touch the grave with one’s hand.”

Mālik also disapproved of saying:

“We visited the grave of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

Al-Qādī ‘Iyād said:

“Mālik disapproved of this because it applied that expression to the Prophet’s grave—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—in view of his words: ‘O Allah, do not make my grave an idol that is worshipped. Great was Allah’s wrath against a people

---

## Page 13203

who took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.’ He forbade applying this expression to the grave and imitating that practice, so as to block the means to wrongdoing and close the door to it.”

I say:

The numerous hadiths reported concerning visiting his grave are all weak—indeed, fabricated. Neither the leading hadith scholars nor the compilers of the recognized Sunan collections, such as those of Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasā’ī, transmitted anything on this subject. However, the expression “visiting graves” occurs in other hadiths, such as his words—may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“I had forbidden you to visit graves. Now visit them, for they remind you of the Hereafter.”

He—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—used to teach his Companions that, when visiting graves, each of them should say:

“Peace be upon you, inhabitants of these dwellings, believers and Muslims. We shall, if Allah wills, join you. May Allah have mercy on those among us and you who have gone before and those who come later. We ask Allah for well-being for ourselves and for you.”

Yet, in the customary usage of many later generations, the expression “visiting graves” has come to encompass both innovated visits and visits prescribed by the religion. Most of them use it only in the innovated sense, not the prescribed one. This is why using the expression without qualification was considered objectionable.

As for visiting graves in the prescribed manner, it is akin to the funeral prayer over the deceased: its purpose is to supplicate for the deceased, just as that is the purpose of the funeral prayer. Thus Allah said concerning the hypocrites:

“Never pray over any of them who dies, nor stand at his grave.”

Thus, when He prohibited

---

## Page 13204

praying over the hypocrites and standing at their graves, this indicated—through both the implication of the wording and the rationale underlying the ruling—that these practices are prescribed for believers. Standing at a believer’s grave after burial is akin to praying over him before burial: its purpose is to supplicate for him. This is the practice established by the Sunnah and recommended by the early generations when visiting the graves of prophets and righteous people.

As for “innovated visits,” they belong to the category of polytheism and the means leading to it, as exemplified by what the Jews and Christians did at the graves of prophets and righteous people. He—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said in hadiths widely transmitted in the collections of authentic hadith, the Sunan collections, and the Musnad collections:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians, who took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

He was warning against what they had done. He also said:

“Those before you used to take graves as places of worship. Take heed: do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.”

He also said:

“Among the worst of people are those who are alive when the Hour overtakes them, and those who take graves as places of worship.”

He also said:

“Allah has cursed women who visit graves and those who establish places of worship over them and place lamps upon them.”

Since he cursed those who take the graves of prophets and righteous people as places of worship, deliberately choosing these graves for supplication cannot be recommended. Wherever supplication is recommended, prayer is also recommended, since supplication following prayer is more likely to be answered. There is no place in Islamic law where prayer is prohibited yet supplication is recommended. The leading scholars, such as al-Shāfiʿī and others, have explicitly stated that this prohibition is motivated

---

## Page 13205

by fear that the grave may lead people astray, not merely by its impurity, as some people suppose. For this reason, the early generations instructed that graves be levelled and that the traces of those liable to lead people astray be effaced. Thus, when Daniel’s grave was discovered in Tustar, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb ordered that its traces be effaced. Abū Mūsā had written to him, mentioning that Daniel’s grave had been discovered and that people used to seek rain through him. ‘Umar wrote back, instructing him to dig thirteen graves during the day, then bury him in one of them at night and efface its traces, so that people would not be led astray through him.

What we have mentioned from Mālik and other leading scholars was well known among the early generations. Abū Ya‘lā al-Mawsilī narrated in his Musnad, and the hadith master Abū ‘Abd Allāh al-Maqdisī included in his selected collection, a report from ‘Alī ibn al-Husayn ibn ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib, known as Zayn al-‘Ābidīn. He saw a man coming to an opening beside the Prophet’s grave—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—entering it and supplicating there. He forbade him from doing so, saying:

“Shall I not relate to you a hadith that I heard from my father, from my grandfather, from the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace? He said: ‘Do not make my grave a place of recurring observance, nor make your houses graves, for your greetings of peace reach me wherever you may be.’”

This hadith also appears in Sunan Abī Dāwūd, on the authority of Abū Hurayrah, who said: “The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

‘Do not make your houses graves, nor make my grave a place of recurring observance. Send blessings upon me, for your blessings reach me wherever you may be.’”

In the Sunan of Sa‘īd ibn Mansūr: ‘Abd al-‘Azīz ibn Muhammad narrated to us: Suhayl ibn Abī Suhayl informed me, saying: “I was seen by al-Hasan ibn

---

## Page 13206

al-Husayn ibn ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib at the grave. He called to me while eating supper in Fātimah’s house, saying, ‘Come to supper.’ I replied, ‘I do not want any.’ He asked, ‘Why were you at the grave?’ I replied, ‘I greeted the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.’

He said, ‘When you enter the mosque, offer your greeting of peace.’ Then he said, ‘The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said: “Do not make my house a place of recurring observance, nor make your houses graves. May Allah curse the Jews: they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship. Send blessings upon me, for your blessings reach me wherever you may be. You and those in al-Andalus are alike.”’”

This principle has been discussed at length elsewhere. If this is what is prescribed concerning the grave of the foremost of Adam’s descendants, the best of creation and the most honoured of them before Allah, then what can be said concerning anyone else’s grave?

It has been transmitted through numerous independent reports from the Companions that whenever hardships befell them—as in times of drought and when seeking rain, or during battle and when seeking victory—they would supplicate to Allah and seek His aid in mosques and houses. They did not deliberately seek to supplicate at the grave of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—or at any other graves of prophets and righteous people.

Indeed, it is established in the Sahīh that ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb said:

“O Allah, when drought afflicted us, we used to seek a means of approach to You through our Prophet, and You would grant us rain. Now we seek a means of approach to You through our Prophet’s uncle, so grant us rain.” They would then be granted rain.

Thus, they sought a means of approach to Allah through al-‘Abbās, just as they had done through the Prophet—that is, through his supplication and intercession. Likewise, they sought a means of approach through the supplication and intercession of al-‘Abbās. They did not deliberately seek to supplicate at the grave of

---

## Page 13207

the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—nor did they adjure Allah by any of His creatures. Rather, they sought a means of approach to Him through the means He prescribed: righteous deeds and the supplications of believers. Thus, a servant seeks a means of approach to Allah through faith in His Prophet, love and allegiance to him, and sending blessings and peace upon him. Just as they sought a means of approach through his supplication and intercession during his lifetime, so too will people seek a means of approach through his supplication and intercession in the Hereafter.

One may also seek a means of approach through the supplications of the righteous, as the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Are you granted victory and provision except through the weak among you—through their supplications, prayers and seeking forgiveness?”

It is self-evident that if supplicating at graves were better than supplicating elsewhere, more beloved to Allah and more readily answered, the early generations would have known this better than later generations and would have hastened to it. They knew best what Allah loves and is pleased with, and were foremost in obeying Him and seeking His pleasure. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—would also have explained this and encouraged it. For he commanded everything good and forbade everything evil. He left nothing that brings one closer to Paradise without telling his community about it, nor anything that distances one from the Fire without warning his community against it. He left his community upon a clear path, whose night is as bright as its day; none turns away from it after him except one who is doomed.

How could that be, when he forbade this kind of practice and cut it off at its root through his curse and his prohibition against taking graves as places of worship? He thus forbade praying to Allah while facing graves, even if the person praying neither worships the dead nor invokes them—just as prayer was forbidden at sunrise and sunset, because these are

---

## Page 13208

the times when the polytheists prostrate to the sun—even though the person praying prostrates only to Allah—as a safeguard against what leads to such wrongdoing. How much more so when the harm actually occurs, with a person invoking the deceased and supplicating through him, just as it occurs when someone prostrates to the sun at sunrise or sunset!

Idol worship originally arose from the veneration of graves, as Allah Most High says:

“And they said, ‘Never abandon your gods; never abandon Wadd, Suwā‘, Yaghūth, Ya‘ūq, or Nasr.’”

The early generations, including Ibn ‘Abbās and others, said: These were righteous men among the people of Noah. When they died, people devoted themselves to their graves, then made images of them, and then worshipped them.

Furthermore, it is well known that among those buried in the Bāb al-Saghīr cemetery are Companions, their Successors, and the generation after them who are superior to these four shaykhs. Why, then, should these four be singled out for supplication at their graves rather than those who are superior to them? Moreover, each of these shaykhs and others like them has people who love him and honour him through supplication, rather than another shaykh. Has Allah commanded that supplication be offered at the grave of one rather than another, as the polytheists do with them? These people resemble those who

“took their rabbis and monks as lords besides Allah, and the Messiah, son of Mary, though they were commanded only to worship one God. There is no deity except Him. Exalted is He above whatever they associate with Him.”

---

## Page 13209

Section

As for the statement attributed to some shaykhs:

“If a calamity or something you fear befalls you, turn to me, and your distress will be relieved, whether I am alive or dead.”

Such words and others like them are either a lie on the part of the transmitter or an error on the part of the speaker. This is a report of unknown authenticity attributed to someone who is not infallible. Whoever abandons an authenticated report from an infallible speaker and follows an unauthenticated report from someone who is not infallible has indeed strayed far astray. It is well known that neither Allah nor His messengers commanded anything of this kind. Rather, Allah Most High said:

“So when you have finished, strive diligently, and to your Lord direct your longing.”

He did not say: “Direct your longing to the prophets and angels.” And He Most High said:

“Say, ‘Call upon those whom you claim besides Him. They have no power to remove harm from you or transfer it.’ Those whom they invoke themselves seek a means of drawing near to their Lord, competing to be nearest; they hope for His mercy and fear His punishment. Indeed, your Lord’s punishment is to be feared.”

A group of the early generations said: Some people used to invoke Ezra, the Messiah, and the angels, so Allah revealed this verse.

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, never said to any of his Companions:

“If

---

## Page 13210

a calamity befalls you, turn to me.” Rather, he advised his cousin, ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Abbās:

“Be mindful of Allah, and He will protect you. Be mindful of Allah, and you will find Him before you. Know Allah in times of ease, and He will know you in times of hardship. When you ask, ask Allah; when you seek help, seek help from Allah.”

As for the report circulated by some ordinary people that he said:

“When you ask Allah, ask Him by virtue of my standing, for my standing with Allah is great.”

This is a false, fabricated hadith. No scholar has transmitted it, nor does it appear in any of the Muslims’ authoritative religious books. If a deceased person possesses any virtue, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has the greatest claim to every virtue, followed by his Companions. And if the living can benefit through the dead, his Companions would have been the people most entitled to benefit through him, both during his life and after his death.

It is thus clear that this is a form of misguidance. Even if one of the shaykhs did say this, it was an error on his part. Allah will forgive him if he exercised independent scholarly judgment and erred. He is not a prophet whose words must be followed, nor is he infallible in what he commands and forbids. Allah Most High has said:

“If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day.”

Section

As for the claim that whoever recites the “Verse of the Throne” and faces in the direction of Shaykh

---

## Page 13211

‘Abd al-Qādir al-Jīlānī—may Allah be pleased with him—and greets him, taking seven steps toward his grave, one step with each greeting, will have his need fulfilled; or, if he is attending a session of devotional listening, he will experience delight and his expressions of ecstasy will increase.

Seeking nearness through such an act constitutes associating partners with the Lord of all worlds. There is no doubt that Shaykh ‘Abd al-Qādir neither said this nor commanded it. Whoever attributes such a statement to him has lied about him. Such innovations are introduced only by people given to excess and associating partners with Allah: innovators who resemble the Christians, such as the Rāfidah who go to extremes concerning the imams, and those who resemble them in going to extremes concerning the shaykhs.

It is established in the Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not sit on graves or pray facing them.”

If he forbade facing a grave when praying to Allah, how could it be permissible to turn toward it and supplicate to someone other than Allah, even from a distant dwelling? Is this not the same kind of conduct as that of the Christians toward Jesus, his mother, their religious scholars, and their monks, taking them as lords and gods whom they invoke, whose aid they seek in pursuing their needs, whom they ask, and through whom they make requests?

Section

As for the statement that Allah looks upon the poor on three occasions—when they eat, exchange sincere counsel, and engage in devotional listening—something similar has been related from one of the shaykhs, who said:

“Allah looks upon them when they eat, for they eat selflessly;

---

## Page 13212

and when they engage in scholarly discussion, for they intend to offer one another sincere counsel; and during devotional listening, for they listen for Allah’s sake.” Or words to that effect.

The overarching principle here is that whoever performs a deed beloved to Allah and His Messenger—one done for Allah’s sake and with His permission—Allah loves him and looks upon him with love as he performs it. A righteous deed is both sincere and correct: sincere means done for Allah’s sake, and correct means in accordance with Allah’s command. Undoubtedly, sharing meals, conversing, and listening each include things Allah loves, things He does not love, and things combining good and evil, truth and falsehood, benefit and harm. Each is judged accordingly.

Section

Some people deliberately choose to pray and supplicate at what is said to be the grave of a prophet, one of the Companions, or a member of the Prophet’s family, or at similar sites. Others press their bodies, or some part of their bodies, against a grave or against an adjacent piece of wood or other object. This includes someone who deliberately chooses to pray and supplicate on the southeastern side of the Great Mosque of Damascus, at the place said to be the grave of Hūd—although the scholars hold that it is the grave of Mu‘āwiyah ibn Abī Sufyān—or beside the wooden representation beneath which the head of John son of Zechariah is said to lie, and similar practices. Such a person is mistaken, introducing an innovation and acting contrary to the Sunnah; for

---

## Page 13213

prayer and supplication at these places have no special merit according to any of the early generations of the Muslim community or its imams, nor did they engage in such practices. Rather, they forbade them, just as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade the means and motives leading to them—even when people did not intend to invoke the grave or supplicate through it. How much more so, then, if they did intend that!

Section

As for his question:

“Is supplication especially likely to be accepted or answered swiftly at a particular time or place—at the grave of a prophet or a friend of Allah?”

There is no doubt that supplication is more readily answered at some times and in some circumstances than others. The depths of the night are the time when supplication is most readily answered, as established in the two Sahīh collections from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“Our Lord descends to the lowest heaven when the last third of the night remains—in another narration, half the night—and says: ‘Who will call upon Me, that I may answer him? Who will ask Me, that I may give him? Who will seek My forgiveness, that I may forgive him?’—until dawn breaks.”

Another hadith states:

“The Lord is closest to His servant in the latter part of the night.”

Supplication is answered when rain falls, when battle is joined, at the adhān and iqāmah, at the close of prayers, and while prostrating. Likewise, the supplications of a fasting person, a traveller, and someone who has been wronged are answered, as are other supplications of this kind. All of this is attested by

---

## Page 13214

the well-known hadiths in the Sahīh and Sunan collections. The same applies to supplication at the sacred pilgrimage sites, such as ʿArafah, Muzdalifah, Minā, al-Multazam and other sacred sites in Mecca, and to supplication in mosques generally. The greater a mosque’s merit—as with the three mosques—the greater the merit of prayer and supplication within it.

As for supplicating at a place because it contains the grave of a prophet or a friend of Allah, none of the early generations of the Muslim community or its imams ever said that supplication there is better than elsewhere. Rather, this is something introduced by some of those who pray towards the qiblah, in imitation of Christians and other polytheists. Its origin lies in the religion of the polytheists, not in the religion of Allah’s sincere servants. The same applies to taking graves as places of worship: none of the early generations of the Muslim community or its imams considered this recommended. Rather, some of those who pray towards the qiblah introduced it in imitation of those Jews and Christians whom the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, cursed.

Section

As for the questioner’s words:

“Is it permissible to seek Allah’s help in supplication through a prophet sent by Him, an angel brought near to Him, His speech—exalted is He—the Kaaba, the supplication known as Ihtiyāt Qāf, or the supplication of Umm Dāwūd or al-Khidr? Or is it permissible, when petitioning Allah, to adjure Him by the right of so-and-so, the sanctity of so-and-so, or the standing of those brought near to Him

---

## Page 13215

by those creatures nearest to Him, or to adjure Him by their deeds and actions?”

The answer is:

This question involves several distinct issues.

The supplications transmitted in the Sunnah include asking Allah by His names and attributes and seeking refuge in His speech. Among the supplications recorded in the Sunan collections are his words:

“O Allah, I ask You by the fact that all praise belongs to You. You are Allah, Originator of the heavens and the earth. O Possessor of Majesty and Honour, O Ever-Living, O Sustainer of all!”

And his words:

“O Allah, I ask You by the fact that You are Allah, the One, the Eternal Refuge, who neither begets nor is born, and to whom none is equal.”

Another example is the supplication recorded in the Musnad:

“O Allah, I ask You by every name belonging to You with which You have named Yourself, which You have revealed in Your Book, which You have taught to any of Your creation, or which You have kept to Yourself in the knowledge of the unseen with You.”

As for the supplications recited by some ordinary people and written out by amulet sellers among the Turuqiyya, which contain such words as:

“I ask You by Ihtiyāt Qāf—which is Yūfi al-Mukhāf—and by Mount Sinai, the Throne, the Footstool, Zamzam, the Station of Abraham, and the Sacred City,”

neither these nor similar supplications have been transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, from the Companions, or from the imams of the Muslims. No one may swear by these things under any circumstances. Rather, it is authentically established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever wishes to swear an oath must swear by Allah or remain silent.”

He also said:

“Whoever swears by anything other than Allah has committed shirk.”

Thus, no one may swear by created things at all. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also said:

---

## Page 13216

“Among Allah’s servants are those who, if they adjure Allah, He will fulfil their oath.”

He said this when Anas ibn al-Nadr said: “Will al-Rubayyiʿ’s front tooth be broken? No, by Him who sent you with the truth, al-Rubayyiʿ’s front tooth will not be broken!”

Likewise, al-Barā’ ibn Mālik said: “I adjure You, my Lord, to do such and such.” Both were among those whose oaths Allah would fulfil.

A servant asks his Lord through the means that bring about what he seeks: righteous deeds for which Allah has promised reward, and the supplications of His believing servants whom He has promised to answer. Thus, the Companions would seek a means of approach to Allah Most High through His Prophet, then through his uncle and other righteous men among them. They did so through his supplication and intercession, as recorded in the Sahīh: ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—sought rain through al-ʿAbbās, saying:

“O Allah, we used to seek a means of approach to You through our Prophet, and You would grant us rain. Now we seek a means of approach to You through our Prophet’s uncle, so grant us rain.”

They would then be granted rain. After the Prophet’s death, they sought a means of approach through al-ʿAbbās, just as they had done through the Prophet; this was through his supplication and intercession.

Another example is the report transmitted by the compilers of the Sunan and graded authentic by al-Tirmidhī:

“A man said to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: ‘Supplicate to Allah to restore my sight.’ He instructed him to perform ablution, pray two units of prayer, and say: ‘O Allah, I ask You and turn to You through Your Prophet Muhammad, the Prophet of mercy. O Muhammad, O Messenger of Allah, I turn through you to my Lord concerning this need of mine, that He may fulfil it. O Allah, accept his intercession on my behalf.’”

This man made his request of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who instructed him to ask Allah to accept the Prophet’s intercession on his behalf. His turning to Allah through His Prophet was like the other Companions’ seeking a means of approach to Allah through him, for

---

## Page 13217

this turning to Allah and seeking a means of approach is through his supplication and intercession.

As for someone saying:

“I ask You, or adjure You, by the right of Your angels, or by the right of Your prophets, or by Your prophet so-and-so, or by Your messenger so-and-so, or by the Sacred House, or by Zamzam and the Station, or by Mount Sinai and the Frequented House,”

or similar expressions, this kind of supplication has not been transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, his Companions, or those who followed them in righteousness. Indeed, a number of scholars, including Abū Hanīfah and his companions—such as Abū Yūsuf—and other scholars, explicitly stated that such supplication is impermissible. For the person has adjured Allah by a created being, and an oath by anything other than Allah is not valid, even if he asks Him through it on the grounds that it is a cause and a means of having his need fulfilled.

But when someone asks Allah through righteous deeds and through the supplication of His Prophet and His righteous servants, righteous deeds are a cause of reward, and supplication is a cause of receiving an answer. Asking Him through these is therefore asking through something that is a cause of attaining what is sought. This is the meaning of what is reported in the supplication said when going out to prayer:

“O Allah, I ask You by the right that those who ask You have upon You, and by the right of this walk of mine.”

Likewise, the people in the cave supplicated to Allah through their righteous deeds. Seeking a means of approach to Allah through the prophets therefore means doing so through faith in them and obedience to them—such as invoking blessings and peace upon them, loving them, and being loyal to them—or through their supplication and intercession.

As for their very persons, there is nothing in them that entails the fulfilment of what the servant seeks, even though they possess great standing and an exalted rank with Allah because of His honouring them, His kindness to them, and His bounty toward them. Nor is there

---

## Page 13218

anything in that which entails an answer to another person’s supplication, unless there is a cause linking him to them—such as faith in them and obedience to them—or linking them to him, such as their supplication for him and intercession on his behalf. These two things may be used as means of approach to Allah, but adjuring Him by a created being may not.

As for the saying that some of the common people attribute to him:

“When you ask Allah, ask Him by my standing, for my standing with Allah is great,”

it is a false, fabricated hadith.

Section

As for the questioner’s words:

“Is it permissible to venerate a place containing khalūq (perfume) and saffron because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was seen there?”

The answer is: Rather, venerating such places and turning them into mosques and places of visitation for that reason is among the practices of the People of the Book, whom we have been forbidden to imitate in these matters.

It is established that, while travelling, ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb saw some people hastening towards a place. He asked, “What is this?” They replied, “A place where the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed.”

He said, “And what if it is a place where the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed? Do you intend to turn the sites associated with your prophets into mosques? Whoever is there when the prayer falls due should pray; otherwise, he should continue on his way.”

ʿUmar said this in the presence of the Companions. It is well known that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would pray during his journeys

---

## Page 13219

in various places. Believers would also see him in dreams in various places, yet the early generations did not turn any of these into a mosque or a place of visitation. Were this door opened, many—or most—Muslim homes would become mosques and places of visitation, for people continue to see the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in dreams, coming to their homes. Some see him many times. Perfuming these places with saffron is a disapproved innovation.

As for the further claims that liars invent—for example, when a footprint is seen somewhere and people say, “This is his footprint,” and similar claims—all of this is false. The stone footprints that people transport, claiming that they mark where he placed his foot, are a fabricated lie. Were they genuine, it would have been prescribed for Muslims to make such a place a mosque and a place of visitation. Yet Allah has not commanded that the standing-place of any prophet be made a place of prayer except the standing-place of Abraham, saying:

“And take the standing-place of Abraham as a place of prayer.”

Likewise, He has not commanded the touching and kissing of any stone except the Black Stone, nor prayer towards any house except the Sacred House. By the agreement of the Muslims, it is impermissible to extend this by analogy to anything else. Rather, that would be like instituting a pilgrimage for people to somewhere other than the Ancient House, or imposing a month of fasting other than the month of Ramadan, and similar things.

Thus, by the agreement of the Muslims, it is not prescribed to touch or kiss the Rock in Jerusalem. Nor does prayer or supplication beside it have any special merit over other parts of the mosque. Prayer and supplication in the qiblah-facing part of the mosque that Umar ibn al-Khattāb built for the Muslims

---

## Page 13220

are better than prayer and supplication beside it. When Umar ibn al-Khattāb conquered the city, he said to Kaʿb al-Ahbār, “Where do you think I should build the Muslims’ prayer hall?”

He replied, “Build it behind the Rock.”

Umar said, “Judaism has influenced you, son of a Jewish woman! Rather, I shall build it in front of the Rock, for the foremost parts of mosques belong to us.” He then built this prayer hall, which ordinary people call “al-Aqsā.” He neither rubbed the Rock nor kissed it nor prayed beside it. How could he have done so, when it is established in the Sahīh that, upon kissing the Black Stone, he said, “By Allah, I know that you are a stone that can neither harm nor benefit. Had I not seen the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, kissing you, I would not have kissed you.” When Abdullah ibn Umar came to al-Aqsā Mosque, he would pray there without going to the Rock. The same was true of others among the early generations.

Likewise, kissing or rubbing the burial chamber of our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the burial chamber of Abraham, the Close Friend of Allah, or other burial places containing a prophet or a righteous man is not recommended, by agreement of the imams. Rather, it is forbidden. Prostrating to any of these is disbelief, as is addressing the deceased in terms with which the Lord is addressed, such as saying, “Forgive me my sins,” or “Grant me victory over my enemy,” and the like.

Section

As for trees, stones, springs, and similar things to which some of the common people make vows

---

## Page 13221

or on which they hang rags or other things, or whose leaves they take to seek blessings through them, or beside which they pray, or do anything similar—all of this constitutes reprehensible religious innovations. These are practices of the people of pre-Islamic ignorance and among the causes of associating partners with Allah Most High. It is related:

“The polytheists had a tree on which they hung their weapons, which they called Dhāt Anwāt. Some people said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, appoint for us a Dhāt Anwāt just as they have a Dhāt Anwāt.’ He replied, ‘Allah is Most Great! You have said what the people of Moses said to Moses: “Make for us a god just as they have gods.” These are the same old ways! You will surely follow the ways of those before you, span for span and cubit for cubit, so that even if one of them entered a lizard’s hole, you would enter it, and even if one of them had intercourse with his wife in the road, you would do the same.’”

Umar ibn al-Khattāb learned that some people were making a point of praying beside “the tree” beneath which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had received the people’s Pledge of Divine Pleasure. He ordered that tree to be cut down, and it was cut down. The scholars of religion have agreed that if someone vows to perform an act of worship at any such place, this is not a vow that must be fulfilled, nor is there any special merit in worshipping there.

Section

The fundamental principle in this matter is that, in Islamic law, no place is to be singled out for the worship of

---

## Page 13222

Allah through prayer, supplication, remembrance, recitation, and similar acts, except the mosques of the Muslims and the sacred sites of Hajj.

As for shrines over graves, whether or not they have been made into mosques; sites associated with particular prophets or righteous people; grottoes, caves, and other such places—these include “the Mount,” where Allah spoke to Moses; “the Cave of Hirāʾ,” where the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to worship before revelation came to him; “the cave” mentioned by Allah in His words, “one of two, when they were in the cave”; the cave on Mount Qāsiyūn in Damascus known as “the Cave of Blood”; and the two sites on its eastern and western sides, one called “the Station of Abraham” and the other “the Station of Jesus.” These and similar places and shrines throughout the east and west are not places to which travel for the purpose of visiting is prescribed. If someone vows to travel to them, he is not obliged to fulfil his vow, by agreement of the leading scholars of the Muslims.

Indeed, it is established in the two Sahīh collections, through the reports of Abū Hurayrah and Abū Saʿīd—and it is also narrated from others—that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Journeys are not to be undertaken except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque, al-Aqsā Mosque, and this mosque of mine.”

When the Companions of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, conquered these lands—Greater Syria, Iraq, Egypt, Khurāsān, the Maghreb, and elsewhere—they did not seek out these places or visit them, nor did they deliberately seek to pray or supplicate there. Rather, they were

---

## Page 13223

holding firmly to the law of their Prophet, maintaining the mosques concerning which Allah said:

“Who is more unjust than those who prevent Allah’s name from being mentioned in His mosques?”

And He said:

“Allah’s mosques are maintained only by those who believe in Allah and the Last Day, establish prayer, give zakāh, and fear none but Allah.”

And He, the Exalted, said:

“Say, ‘My Lord has commanded justice.’ And direct your faces to Him at every place of worship.”

And He, the Exalted, said:

“The mosques belong to Allah, so do not invoke anyone alongside Allah.”

There are other texts of this kind.

In the two Sahīh collections, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“A man’s prayer in the mosque surpasses his prayer at home or in the marketplace by twenty-five degrees. That is because, when a man performs ablution thoroughly and then comes to the mosque, prompted by nothing but prayer there, one of his two steps raises him a degree and the other removes a sin. When he sits awaiting prayer, he is counted as being in prayer for as long as he awaits it. When he has finished praying, the angels continue to pray for him as long as he remains in his place of prayer, saying, ‘O Allah, forgive him. O Allah, have mercy on him.’”

The later scholars differed concerning someone who travels to visit a prophet’s grave or other such shrines.
