Those among them who critically examined the matter held that this is a journey undertaken in disobedience to Allah, during which he is not to shorten his prayers, just as he is not to shorten them on any journey undertaken in disobedience to Allah. This was stated by Ibn ʿAqīl and others. Abū ʿAbd Allāh ibn Battah likewise stated that this is among the innovations introduced into Islam.

Indeed, deliberately seeking out these places to pray and supplicate there has no basis in Islamic law. Nor has it been transmitted from the earliest forerunners—may Allah be pleased

---

## Page 13224

with them and grant them His pleasure—that they deliberately sought out these places for supplication and prayer. Rather, they went only to Allah’s mosques. Indeed, they did not even seek out mosques built contrary to Islamic law, such as the mosque built to cause harm, concerning which Allah said:

“And those who established a mosque to cause harm, promote disbelief, divide the believers, and provide an outpost for those who had previously fought against Allah and His Messenger—they will surely swear, ‘We intended nothing but good.’ But Allah bears witness that they are certainly liars.”

“Never stand in prayer there. A mosque founded on piety from the first day is more worthy of your standing in prayer there. In it are men who love to purify themselves, and Allah loves those who purify themselves.”

Furthermore, prayer is not permissible in mosques built over the graves of prophets and righteous people, and building them is forbidden, as several leading scholars have explicitly stated. This is because it is widely reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the Sahīh, Sunan, and Musnad collections that he said:

“Those before you used to take graves as places of worship. Beware! Do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.”

During his final illness, he said:

“May Allah’s curse be upon the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

He was warning against what they had done. ʿĀ’ishah said, “Were it not for that, his grave would have been left exposed.” But he disliked the prospect of its being made a place of worship.

The chamber of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was outside his mosque. When al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik was ruler, he wrote to ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz

---

## Page 13225

his governor in the Prophet’s city, instructing him to enlarge the mosque. He purchased the chambers of the Prophet’s wives, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, which lay to the east of the mosque and on its qiblah side, and incorporated them into the mosque. The chamber thus became part of the mosque. They built its enclosure in a pointed shape, out of alignment with the qiblah, so that no one would pray towards it.

Likewise, when the Muslims conquered the land, the “grave of Abraham, the Friend of Allah” was surrounded by the Solomonic wall. No one entered it or prayed beside it. Rather, the Muslims prayed in a mosque in the village of Hebron. This remained the case during the time of the Rightly Guided Caliphs and their successors, until that wall was breached and then fitted with a doorway.

It is said that the Christians were the ones who breached it and made it a church. Then, when the Muslims took the land from them, it was made into a mosque. For this reason, the righteous Muslim scholars would not pray in that place.

This is so even when the grave is genuine. How much more so when most graves attributed to the prophets are falsely identified? One example is the grave said to be the “grave of Noah”: this is undoubtedly a false attribution, put forward by ignorant people only relatively recently. The same applies to other such graves.

Section

As for “Ascalon,” it was one of the Muslims’ frontier strongholds. The righteous among

---

## Page 13226

the Muslims would reside there to guard the frontier in Allah’s cause. The same was true of other places of this kind, such as Mount Lebanon, Alexandria, ‘Abbādān and similar places in Iraq, and Qazwīn and other towns that were frontier strongholds. The righteous would travel to these places to guard the frontier in Allah’s cause. It is established in Sahīh Muslim, on the authority of Salmān al-Fārisī, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Guarding the frontier for a day and a night in Allah’s cause is better than fasting and praying at night for a month. Whoever dies while guarding the frontier dies as one striving in Allah’s cause. The reward for his deeds continues to accrue to him, his provision from Paradise continues to reach him, and he is kept safe from the one who puts him to trial.”

In Sunan Abī Dāwūd and elsewhere, ‘Uthmān reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Guarding the frontier for one day in Allah’s cause is better than a thousand days spent in any other place.”

Abū Hurayrah said: “I would rather spend one night guarding the frontier in Allah’s cause than spend the Night of Decree in prayer beside the Black Stone.”

For this reason, the scholars said that guarding the frontier strongholds is better than residing near the Two Noble Sanctuaries, because guarding the frontier is a form of jihad, whereas residing near the sanctuaries is a form of Hajj. By the consensus of the Muslims, jihad as a category is superior to Hajj as a category. As Allah Most High says:

“Do you consider providing water to pilgrims and maintaining the Sacred Mosque equal to the deeds of one who believes in Allah and the Last Day and strives in Allah’s cause? They are not equal in Allah’s sight, and Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people.”

“Those who believe, emigrate, and strive in Allah’s cause with their wealth

---

## Page 13227

and their lives are greater in rank in Allah’s sight, and it is they who will triumph.”

“Their Lord gives them glad tidings of mercy from Him, His good pleasure, and gardens where lasting bliss awaits them.”

“They will remain there forever. Indeed, with Allah is a tremendous reward.”

This, then, is the basis for holding these places in esteem. Some of them were subsequently inhabited by disbelievers and people of religious innovation and wickedness. Others fell into ruin, and places other than these became frontier strongholds. The rulings applying to places change as the circumstances of their inhabitants change. A place may be an abode of unbelief when its inhabitants are disbelievers, then become an abode of Islam when they embrace Islam. Thus Mecca—may Allah ennoble it—was initially an abode of unbelief and war. Allah said of it:

“How many a town was mightier than your town which drove you out!”

Then, when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, conquered it, it became an abode of Islam, although in itself it is the Mother of Towns and the land most beloved to Allah. Likewise, the Holy Land was inhabited by the mighty people whom Allah Most High mentioned. As He Most High said:

“And when Moses said to his people, ‘O my people, remember Allah’s favor upon you when He raised prophets among you, made you kings, and gave you what He had not given anyone else in all the worlds.’”

“‘O my people, enter the Holy Land which Allah has assigned to you, and do not turn back, lest you become losers.’”

“They said, ‘O Moses, there are mighty people in it. We will never enter it until they leave. If they leave, then we will enter.’”

And the verses that follow. Allah Most High also said, when He saved Moses and his people from drowning:

“I will show you the abode of the defiantly disobedient.”

Those lands were the lands of

---

## Page 13228

the defiantly disobedient, because such people inhabited them at that time. Then, when the righteous inhabited them, they became the abode of the righteous. This is a principle that must be understood. A town may be praised or condemned at a particular time because of the condition of its inhabitants. Their condition may then change, and the judgment concerning them changes accordingly. For praise and blame, reward and punishment, depend solely on faith and righteous deeds, or their opposites: unbelief, wickedness, and disobedience.

Allah Most High said:

“O mankind, fear your Lord, who created you from a single soul, created from it its mate, and spread from the two of them many men and women. And fear Allah, in whose name you appeal to one another, and honor the ties of kinship.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“An Arab has no superiority over a non-Arab, nor a non-Arab over an Arab; neither a white person over a black person, nor a black person over a white person, except through piety. All people are children of Adam, and Adam was created from dust.”

Abū al-Dardā’ wrote to Salmān al-Fārisī, “Come to the Holy Land.” The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had established a bond of brotherhood between them when he established brotherhood between the Emigrants and the Helpers. Abū al-Dardā’ was in Syria, while Salmān was in Iraq as a deputy of Umar ibn al-Khattāb.

Salmān wrote back to him:

“The land sanctifies no one; it is a man’s deeds that sanctify him.”

---

## Page 13229

Section

The answer to all the other matters mentioned has now become clear: deliberately choosing to pray and supplicate at what is said to be a prophet’s footprint, a trace left by a prophet, or the grave of a prophet, a Companion, a shaykh, or a member of the Prophet’s household—or at towers or caves—is among the reprehensible innovations introduced into Islam. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not prescribe this. Neither the foremost early Muslims nor those who followed them in righteousness practised it, nor did any of the leading Muslim scholars consider it recommended. Rather, it is among the causes of associating partners with Allah and the avenues leading to falsehood. This subject is discussed at length elsewhere, outside this response.

Section

As for someone saying when he stumbles, “O rank of Muhammad!” or “O Lady Nafīsah!” or “O my master, Shaykh so-and-so!” or similar expressions involving appeals to him for rescue and requests addressed to him, this is forbidden and is of the nature of associating partners with Allah. A deceased person, whether a prophet or anyone else, must not be invoked, petitioned, or called upon for rescue, whether at his grave or far from it. Rather, this is of the same nature as the religion of the Christians who:

“Have taken their rabbis and monks as lords besides Allah, and also the Messiah, son of Mary, although they were commanded to worship only one God. There is no deity except Him. Exalted is He above whatever they associate with Him.”

It is also of the same nature as the practice of those concerning whom He said:

---

## Page 13230

“Say, ‘Call upon those whom you claim to be gods besides Him. They have no power to relieve your suffering or shift it elsewhere.’”

“Those whom they invoke themselves seek a means of drawing near to their Lord, striving to be the nearest. They hope for His mercy and fear His punishment. Indeed, your Lord’s punishment is something to beware of.”

The Most High has also said:

“It is not for any human being whom Allah has given the Scripture, wisdom, and prophethood to then tell people, ‘Be my worshippers rather than Allah’s.’ Rather, ‘Be devoted to the Lord through your teaching of the Scripture and your study of it.’

“Nor would he command you to take the angels and prophets as lords. Would he command you to disbelieve after you have submitted to Allah?”

This has been discussed at length elsewhere.

Section

Likewise, vows made to graves or to any of their occupants—such as vows to Abraham, the intimate friend of Allah, to shaykh so-and-so, to a member of the Prophet’s household, or to anyone else—are vows involving disobedience. By agreement of the leading scholars of the religion, fulfilling them is not obligatory; indeed, fulfilling them is not even permissible. For it is established in the Sahīh collection that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah, let him obey Him; and whoever vows to disobey Allah, let him not disobey Him.”

It is also reported from him in the Sunan collections that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has cursed women who visit graves and those who establish mosques and lamps over them.”

Thus, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, cursed those who build mosques over graves and light lamps there:

---

## Page 13231

such as lanterns, candles, and the like. If someone who does this is cursed, then whoever places gold and silver lanterns and candlesticks there, beside the graves, is all the more deserving of the curse.

Anyone who vows to provide oil, candles, gold, silver, a covering, or anything else to be placed at the grave of a prophet, a Companion, a relative of the Prophet, or a shaykh has made a vow involving disobedience, which it is not permissible to fulfil. Must he offer the expiation for a broken oath? The scholars have two opinions on this. If he gives what he vowed as charity to deserving members of the Prophet’s household, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or to other righteous poor people, that would be better for him in Allah’s sight and more beneficial to him. This is a righteous deed for which Allah will reward him, for Allah rewards those who give charity and does not let the reward of those who do good go to waste.

One who gives charity does so seeking Allah’s Countenance, seeking his reward not from created beings but from Allah Most High. As the Most High says:

“But the most righteous will be kept away from it—he who gives his wealth to purify himself, not repaying a favour owed to anyone, but only seeking the Countenance of his Lord, the Most High. And he will surely be pleased.”

The Most High also says:

“And the example of those who spend their wealth seeking Allah’s pleasure and out of firm conviction within themselves is that of a garden on high ground”—to the end of the verse.

And He says of His righteous servants:

“We feed you only for the sake of Allah. We desire neither reward nor thanks from you.”

For this reason, no one should ask in the name of anyone other than Allah, as when someone says, “In honour of Abū Bakr and ʿAlī, or shaykh so-and-so, or shaykh so-and-so.” Rather, one should give only to someone who asks

---

## Page 13232

for Allah’s sake. No one may ask for the sake of anyone other than Allah, for sincere devotion to Allah alone is obligatory in every physical and financial act of worship, such as prayer, charity, fasting, and Hajj. Bowing and prostration are proper only for Allah; fasting is only for Allah; Hajj is only to the House of Allah; and supplication is only to Allah. The Most High says:

“Fight them until there is no more persecution and all worship is devoted to Allah.”

The Most High also says:

“Ask those of Our messengers whom We sent before you: Did We appoint any gods besides the Most Merciful to be worshipped?”

And the Most High says:

“The revelation of the Book is from Allah, the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

“Indeed, We have sent down the Book to you in truth, so worship Allah, devoting your religion sincerely to Him.”

This is the foundation of Islam: that we worship none but Allah and worship Him only as He has prescribed, not through religious innovations. As the Most High says:

“So whoever hopes to meet his Lord, let him do righteous deeds and associate no one in the worship of his Lord.”

The Most High also says:

“To test you as to which of you is best in deeds.”

Al-Fudayl ibn ‘Iyād said: “That means the most sincere and the most correct.” They asked, “O Abū ‘Alī, what do you mean by the most sincere and the most correct?”

He replied: “If a deed is sincere but not correct, it will not be accepted. If it is correct but not sincere, it will not be accepted until it is both sincere and correct. Sincerity means that it is done for Allah, and correctness means that it accords with the Sunnah and the Book.”

All of this is because Allah’s Messenger conveyed His religion on His behalf. Nothing is forbidden except what Allah has forbidden, and religion consists only of what Allah has prescribed. Allah Most High condemned the polytheists because they prescribed

---

## Page 13233

in religion what Allah had not authorized, declaring forbidden things that Allah had not forbidden, such as livestock designated as bahīrah, sā’ibah, wasīlah, and hām. They also instituted religious practices that Allah had not authorized, such as calling upon and worshipping others besides Him, and the monasticism that the Christians invented.

Islam is the religion of all the messengers, from the first to the last; all of them were sent with Islam. As Noah, peace be upon him, said:

“My people, if my presence among you and my reminding you of Allah’s signs have become too much for you, then in Allah I have put my trust. So settle on your plan together with your gods, leaving no uncertainty about it; then carry it out against me and give me no respite.”

“But if you turn away, I have asked you for no reward. My reward is with Allah alone, and I have been commanded to be among the Muslims.”

The Most High says:

“Who would turn away from the faith of Abraham except one who makes a fool of himself? We certainly chose him in this world, and in the Hereafter he will surely be among the righteous.”

“When his Lord said to him, ‘Submit,’ he replied, ‘I have submitted to the Lord of all worlds.’”

“Abraham enjoined this upon his sons, as did Jacob: ‘My sons, Allah has chosen this religion for you, so do not die except as Muslims.’”

The Most High says:

“Moses said, ‘My people, if you believe in Allah, then put your trust in Him, if you are Muslims.’”

The Most High says:

“And when I inspired the disciples to believe in Me and My messenger, they said, ‘We believe, so bear witness that we are Muslims.’”

It is established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“We prophets all have one religion.”

Thus, the religion of all the messengers is one and the same: Islam, which is to worship Allah alone, with no partner, in accordance with what He has commanded and prescribed

---

## Page 13234

as He says:

“He has ordained for you the religion He enjoined upon Noah, and that which We have revealed to you, and what We enjoined upon Abraham, Moses, and Jesus: uphold the religion and do not be divided within it. What you call the polytheists to is intolerable to them.”

What varies within this religion is the law and the prescribed way, as He says:

“For each of you We have appointed a law and a way.”

Likewise, the law prescribed for a single messenger may vary. In the early days of Islam, Allah commanded Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to pray facing Jerusalem. Then, in the second year after the Hijrah, He commanded him to pray facing the Kaaba, the Sacred House. This, in its own time, was part of the religion of Islam. Likewise, the law of the Torah, in its time, was part of the religion of Islam, and the law of the Gospel, in its time, was part of the religion of Islam.

Whoever believed in the Torah but then rejected the Gospel departed from the religion of Islam and became a disbeliever. Likewise, whoever believed in the two earlier scriptures but rejected the Quran was a disbeliever outside the religion of Islam. For the religion of Islam entails belief in all the scriptures and all the messengers, as the Most High says:

“Say, ‘We believe in Allah and what has been revealed to us, and what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and the Tribes, and what was given to Moses and Jesus, and what was given to the prophets by their Lord. We make no distinction between any of them, and to Him we submit.’” (The verse.)

---

## Page 13235

What do the esteemed scholars, the leading authorities in religion, say about the following?

A person faces a need relating to this world or the Hereafter, then goes to the grave of a prophet or another righteous person and supplicates there for relief from his distress. Is this Sunnah or an innovation? Is it prescribed in Islam or not? If it is not prescribed, their needs may nevertheless sometimes be fulfilled. Does that make it permissible for them to do this? What accounts for their needs being fulfilled? Please give us a ruling.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. This is not Sunnah; rather, it is an innovation. Neither the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor any of his Companions, nor any of the leading authorities whom Muslims follow in their religion ever did this. Nor was it commanded or recommended by the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, any of his Companions, or the leading authorities in religion.

Indeed, this is not known to have been practised by any person of knowledge and religion among the favoured generations praised by the Messenger of Allah—the Companions, the Successors, and their successors—whether in the Hijāz, Yemen, Syria, Iraq, Egypt, the Maghreb, or Khurāsān. It was only introduced later.

---

## Page 13236

It is well known that anything neither prescribed nor recommended by the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or by any of these authorities whom Muslims follow in their religion, is a reprehensible innovation. No one would call such a thing a good innovation. For a good innovation—according to those who divide innovations into good and bad—must be recommended by a scholar whose example is followed, and there must be evidence from Islamic law establishing that it is recommended.

Likewise, those who maintain that every innovation in the religious sense is blameworthy, citing the Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the authentic hadith:

“Every innovation is misguidance,”

explain that when ʿUmar said of the Tarāwīh prayers:

“What an excellent innovation this is!”

he called it an innovation only in the linguistic sense. According to them, an innovation in the religious sense is something for which no evidence from Islamic law establishes that it is recommended.

The two positions ultimately amount to the same thing, for they agree that whatever Islamic law has not made recommended or obligatory is neither obligatory nor recommended. Thus, anyone who adopts an act as worship and religion when it is neither obligatory nor recommended in Islamic law is misguided, by the agreement of the Muslims.

Going to graves in order to supplicate there, hoping that one’s supplication will be answered, falls into this category. It is not prescribed in Islamic law, whether as an obligation or a recommendation. It is therefore neither a religious practice nor something good, neither an act of obedience to Allah nor something He loves and is pleased with, neither a righteous deed nor a means of drawing near to Him. Whoever regards it as any of these things is misguided, by the agreement of the Muslims.

---

## Page 13237

For this reason, when hardships befell the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and they wished to supplicate to Allah for relief from distress or to seek His mercy, they did not go to any graves, whether those of prophets or anyone else. Indeed, they did not even go to the Prophet’s grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, intending to supplicate there. Rather, it is established in Sahīh al-Bukhārī, on the authority of Anas, that whenever they suffered drought, Umar ibn al-Khattāb would seek rain through al-Abbās ibn Abd al-Muttalib, saying:

“O Allah, we used to seek to draw near to You through our Prophet, and You would grant us rain. Now we seek to draw near to You through our Prophet’s uncle, so grant us rain.”

And they would be granted rain.

Sahīh al-Bukhārī also relates that Abd Allāh ibn Dīnār said: “I heard Ibn Umar reciting these lines of Abū Tālib:

‘A fair-faced man, by whose countenance rain is sought from the clouds—
A refuge for orphans, a protector of widows.’”

It also relates, through Sālim ibn Abd Allāh ibn Umar, that his father said:

“At times I would recall the poet’s words while looking at the face of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as he sought rain, while it came down until a gutter gushed with water for him:

‘A fair-faced man, by whose countenance rain is sought from the clouds—
A refuge for orphans, a protector of widows.’”

These are the words of Abū Tālib.

Likewise, Mu‘āwiyah and those with him in Syria sought rain through Yazīd ibn al-Aswad al-Jurashī. During the lifetime of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, they would “come to him and ask

---

## Page 13238

him to supplicate, seeking to draw near to Allah through him and seeking his intercession with Allah, just as all creation will come to him on the Day of Resurrection and ask him to intercede for them with Allah. Then, after his death, when drought afflicted them in the Year of Ashes during Umar’s caliphate—a time of tremendous hardship—they brought al-Abbās and sought to draw near to Allah through him and sought rain through him instead of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. They did not come to the Prophet’s grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to supplicate there, nor did they seek rain through him or seek to draw near to Allah through him.

Likewise, in Syria, they did not go to the graves there; rather, they sought rain through the righteous among them. It is well known that if supplicating at graves and seeking to draw near to Allah through the dead had been recommended for them, then doing so through the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would have been better than doing so through al-Abbās or anyone else.

They used to seek rain in “three ways”: sometimes they supplicated after the prayers; sometimes they went out to the prayer ground and supplicated without performing a prayer; and sometimes they performed a prayer and supplicated. The first two ways are prescribed by the consensus of the Muslim community. The third is prescribed according to the majority, including Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad. Abū Hanīfah did not know of it.

When seeking rain, they were instructed to seek it through righteous people, especially the relatives of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as the Companions did. They were also instructed to invoke blessings upon the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, when seeking rain. None of them instructed people to seek rain at any grave, whether that of a prophet or anyone else, or to seek help

---

## Page 13239

from a dead person, or to seek to draw near to Allah through him, or to engage in similar practices that some people regard as acts of religion and devotion. This shows the believer that these are innovations which the Companions regarded not as approved practices, but as reprehensible ones.

Section

This would suffice even if no prohibition indicating that these practices are forbidden had been transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions. How much more so when his mass-transmitted Sunnah indicates that they are forbidden.

For example, in the two Sahīh collections, Ā’ishah said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said during the illness from which he never recovered: ‘May Allah curse the Jews and Christians; they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.’ Were it not for that, his grave would have been left exposed; however, he feared—or it was feared—that it would be taken as a place of worship.”

This is part of al-Bukhārī’s wording. Also in the two Sahīh collections, Ā’ishah said:

“During the illness of the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, some of his wives mentioned a church they had seen in Abyssinia called Māriyah. They spoke of its beauty and the images within it. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, raised his head and said: ‘When a righteous man among those people died, they would build a place of worship over his grave and then make those images within it. Those are the worst of creation in Allah’s sight.’”

---

## Page 13240

This meaning is widely reported from him through several chains in the Sahīh, Sunan, and Musnad collections.

In Sahīh Muslim, Jundub reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said five days before his death:

“Those before you used to take graves—or he said, the graves of their prophets—as places of worship. Beware! Do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.”

The same report includes:

“If I were to take anyone on earth as an intimate friend, I would take Abū Bakr as an intimate friend. But your companion is Allah’s intimate friend.”

This meaning is reported through several chains in the two Sahīh collections. The report also includes:

“Let every small doorway opening into the mosque be sealed, except Abū Bakr’s doorway.”

He clarified these two matters, both of which have been mass-transmitted from him, and brought them together five days before his death: mentioning the excellence of Abū Bakr al-Siddīq and forbidding the taking of graves as places of worship. Together, they cut off the very source of the polytheism through which religion was corrupted and the religion of the polytheists came to prevail.

For Allah said in His Book concerning the people of Noah:

“And they said, ‘Never abandon your gods; never abandon Wadd, Suwāʿ, Yaghūth, Yaʿūq, or Nasr.’ And they have already led many astray.”

Al-Bukhārī narrated in his Sahīh, through his chain of transmission, that Ibn ʿAbbās said:

“The idols that had existed among the people of Noah came to be worshipped among the Arabs. Wadd belonged to Kalb at Dūmat al-Jandal; Suwāʿ belonged to Hudhayl; Yaghūth belonged to Murād, then to Banū Ghutayf at al-Jarf near Sabaʾ; Yaʿūq belonged to Hamdān; and Nasr belonged to Himyar, to the clan of Dhī

---

## Page 13241

al-Kalām. These were the names of righteous men from the people of Noah. When they died, Satan prompted their people: ‘Set up memorials at the places where they used to sit, and name them after them.’ They did so, but these were not worshipped until those people had died and knowledge had disappeared. Then they were worshipped.”

A number of the early generations related much the same meaning in works of Quran commentary, accounts of the prophets, and other works: these had been righteous people. Some related that people would keep vigil at their graves and later make images of them. Others related that they would take their images along when travelling and supplicate beside them, without worshipping them. Later, however, the idols came to be worshipped.

For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, mentioned graves and images together in several hadiths. For example, Sahīh Muslim records that Abū al-Hayyāj al-Asadī said:

“Alī ibn Abī Tālib said to me: ‘Shall I not send you on the same mission on which the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, sent me? He commanded me to leave no elevated grave without levelling it, and no image without obliterating it.’”

Thus, he commanded him to erase images and level graves, just as he said in another authentic hadith:

“When a righteous man among those people died, they would build a place of worship over his grave and make those images within it. Those people will be the worst of creation before Allah on the Day of Resurrection.”

The hadiths from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbidding the taking of

---

## Page 13242

graves as places of worship and praying in graveyards are very numerous. For example, the two Sahīh collections and the Sunan collections report from Abū Hurayrah that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah destroy the Jews! They took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

Abdullāh ibn Masʿūd said: I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say:

“Among the worst of people are those who are still alive when the Hour overtakes them, and those who take graves as places of worship.”

This was narrated by Ahmad in al-Musnad and by Abū Hātim ibn Hibbān in his Sahīh.

Ibn ʿAbbās said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, cursed women who visit graves and those who establish places of worship and lamps over them.”

This was narrated by Ahmad in al-Musnad, the compilers of the four Sunan collections, and Abū Hātim ibn Hibbān in his Sahīh.

He also narrated in his Sahīh that ʿĀʾishah said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah curse those who took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

The two Sahīh collections report that Ibn ʿUmar said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Offer some of your prayers in your homes, and do not turn them into graves.”

Sahīh Muslim reports from Abū Marthad al-Ghanawī that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not pray facing graves, and do not sit on them.”

Abdullāh ibn ʿAmr said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade praying in graveyards.”

Abū Hātim narrated this in his Sahīh. He also narrated from Anas:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade praying among graves.”

Abū Saʿīd reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

---

## Page 13243

“The whole earth is a place of prayer, except graveyards and bathhouses.”

This was narrated by Ahmad, the compilers of the four Sunan collections, and Ibn Hibbān in his Sahīh. Al-Tirmidhī said that its transmission contains inconsistency, because Sufyān al-Thawrī reported it with a disconnected chain. Other scholars, however, declared it authentic because other trustworthy narrators transmitted it with connected chains. Ibn Hazm also graded it authentic.

Sunan Abī Dāwūd reports that ʿAlī said:

“My beloved friend forbade me to pray in graveyards and forbade me to pray in the land of Babylon.”

The reports concerning this are very numerous.

Some scholars have supposed that praying in graveyards was forbidden because of ritual impurity, since their soil becomes mixed with the pus and flesh of the dead. These scholars may distinguish between new and old graveyards, and between cases where a barrier is present and those where it is not.

Yet this explanation is not mentioned in the hadith, nor does the hadith indicate it, either explicitly or by its apparent meaning. It is merely a reason they have conjectured. According to other scholars, the correct reason is what a number of earlier and later scholars—in the time of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others—have explained: this practice involves imitating the polytheists and can become a means leading to associating partners with Allah. This is why he forbade taking the graves of prophets as places of worship. He said:

“When a righteous man among those people died, they would build a place of worship over his grave and make those images in it.”

He also said:

“Those who came before you used to take graves as places of worship. Beware! Do not take graves as places of worship.”

He also forbade praying facing them.

---

## Page 13244

And, supposing the prohibition were solely due to ritual impurity, it is well known that the graves of the prophets do not become foul-smelling. Indeed, the prophets’ bodies do not decay, and the soil of their graves is pure. Impurity in front of someone praying does not invalidate his prayer. Those who took graves as places of worship would spread clean coverings beside them, so they did not come into contact with impurity. Moreover, those who explain the prohibition in terms of impurity do not deny this other reason. Al-Shāfiʿī and others mentioned the prohibition against establishing mosques over graves and explained it as a precaution against imitating such practices.

Several scholars of the legal schools—including followers of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad, as well as jurists of Kufa—expressly stated the prohibition against building mosques over graves. Several of them explicitly declared it unlawful. There can be no doubt about this in view of the curse pronounced by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his emphatic prohibition of it.

Taking graves as places of worship encompasses two things: building a mosque over them, or praying beside them without any building. The latter is what he himself and his Companions feared would happen if they buried him in the open: they feared that people would pray beside him, thereby turning his grave into a place of worship.

Mālik’s Muwattaʾ reports that he said:

“O Allah, do not let my grave become an idol that is worshipped.”

This has been transmitted both with connected chains and with chains omitting the Companion. Sunan Abī Dāwūd reports that he said:

“Do not make my grave a place of recurring observance. Send blessings upon me wherever you may be, for your blessings reach me.”

As for the report some people transmit that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed in Abraham’s mosque or beside Abraham’s grave, this hadith is not regarded as established by

---

## Page 13245

the scholars, even though some people described as righteous have mentioned it. Rather, what is reported in the two Sahīh collections is that “he prayed in Jerusalem.”

This is a broad subject. It is well known that if supplication beside the graves of prophets and righteous people were better than supplication elsewhere, then praying in those places and taking them as places of worship ought to be recommended, for prayer is accompanied by supplication. Hence no Muslim says that supplication in a place where prayer is prohibited—such as camel resting places, graveyards, or places contaminated by ritual impurity—is better than supplication elsewhere. Whoever says this has defied the Messenger and made the polytheism and means leading to it that he prohibited equal or preferable to the affirmation of Allah’s oneness and worship of Him alone that he commanded.

It was through this opening that the hypocrites introduced into Islam what they did. The man who invented the religion of the Rāfidah was a Jewish heretic who outwardly professed Islam while concealing unbelief, scheming to corrupt the Muslims’ religion—just as Paul schemed to corrupt the Christians’ religion. He worked to stir up discord among the Muslims until ʿUthmān was killed. Among the believers are some who respond to the hypocrites, as Allah Most High says:

“Had they gone forth among you, they would only have added to your disorder and hurried about in your midst, seeking to stir up discord among you; and among you are those who would listen to them.”

Then, when the Muslim community became divided, he introduced his claims concerning designation and infallibility in the imamate, and began openly disparaging Abū Bakr and ʿUmar. These claims found receptive hearts marked by ignorance and injustice, even though they were not unbelieving hearts. Thus emerged the innovation of Shiism, which is the key to the door of polytheism.

---

## Page 13246

Then, when the heretics gained power, they ordered shrines to be built and mosques to be left unused, arguing that Friday and congregational prayers could only be performed behind the infallible imam. They transmitted lies concerning the lighting and veneration of shrines and supplication beside them, the like of which I have not encountered among the fabrications of the People of the Book that I have examined. Their leader, Ibn al-Nu'mān, even wrote a book entitled “The Rites of Pilgrimage to Shrines.” In it they fabricated lies about the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his household, thereby altering his religion and changing his faith.

They introduced polytheism, which contradicts the affirmation of Allah’s oneness, thus combining polytheism with lying, just as Allah has linked the two in several passages, including His words:

“And shun false speech, being true to Allah, associating no partners with Him.”

In an authentic report, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “False testimony is equivalent to associating partners with Allah.” He said this twice, then recited this verse.

Allah Most High also says:

“Those who took the calf for worship will incur wrath from their Lord and humiliation in this worldly life. Thus do We recompense those who fabricate lies.”

And Allah Most High says:

“On the Day He calls them and says, ‘Where are those you claimed were My partners?’”

“And We will bring forth a witness from every community and say, ‘Produce your proof.’ Then they will know that the truth belongs to Allah, and whatever they used to fabricate will fail them.”

This right belongs to Allah, as is established in an authentic report that the Prophet said to Mu'ādh ibn Jabal:

“O Mu'ādh, do you know what Allah’s right over His servants is?”

He replied, “Allah and His Messenger know best.”

He said, “His right over His servants is that they worship Him and associate nothing with Him. O Mu'ādh, do you know what

---

## Page 13247

the servants’ right over Allah is if they do so?”

He replied, “Allah and His Messenger know best.” He said, “Their right over Him is that He not punish them.”

Allah Most High says:

“And to ‘Ād We sent their brother Hūd. He said, ‘O my people, worship Allah; you have no god other than Him. You are merely fabricating lies.’”

There are many such passages in the Quran in which He describes those who associate partners with Him as fabricators of lies. Hence He demands proof and authoritative evidence from them, as in His words:

“Whoever invokes another god alongside Allah, for which he has no proof, his reckoning rests only with his Lord.”

And His words:

“Say, ‘Have you considered those you invoke besides Allah? Show me what they have created of the earth. Or do they have a share in the heavens? Bring me a scripture preceding this, or some remnant of knowledge, if you are truthful.’”

He also says:

“So devote yourself to the religion, inclining to truth—the natural disposition Allah has instilled in mankind. There is no altering Allah’s creation. That is the upright religion, but most people do not know.

“Turn to Him in repentance, fear Him, establish prayer, and do not be among those who associate partners with Him—those who divided their religion and became sects, each faction rejoicing in what it has.

“When adversity touches people, they call upon their Lord, turning to Him in repentance. Then, when He lets them taste mercy from Him, a group of them associate partners with their Lord, showing ingratitude for what We have given them. So enjoy yourselves; soon you will know.

“Or have We sent down to them any authority that speaks in support of what they associate with Him?”

Allah Most High says, “Do not be among those who associate partners with Him—those who divided their religion and became sects,” because affirming Allah’s oneness is the religion of Allah with which He sent the messengers of earlier and later times. As Allah Most High says:

“We sent no messenger before you without revealing to him that

---

## Page 13248

there is no god but Me, so worship Me.”

Allah Most High also says:

“And ask those of Our messengers whom We sent before you: Have We appointed gods besides the Most Merciful to be worshipped?”

And He says:

“We certainly sent a messenger to every nation, saying, ‘Worship Allah and shun false gods.’”

It is established in the authentic hadith collection that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah is pleased with three things for you: that you worship Him without associating anything with Him; that you all hold firmly to the rope of Allah and do not become divided; and that you offer sincere counsel to those whom Allah has placed in authority over you.”

Thus, those who take graves as places of worship, owing to the association of partners with Allah found among them, have divided their religion and become sects. You find each group venerating the person they follow or their prophet, saying, “Supplication at his grave is answered.” Their hearts are attached to his grave rather than to the graves of other prophets and righteous people, even if another is superior to him. Likewise, each worshipper of celestial bodies and idols has taken his own desire as his god: he worships whatever he takes as a god, even if something else is superior to it.

They then call this “visitation,” a term sanctioned by Islamic law that they have misapplied. It is well known that the “prescribed visitation” which the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instituted for his community entails greeting the deceased with peace and supplicating for him, much like offering the funeral prayer over him. The person offering the funeral prayer intends to supplicate for the deceased; Allah Most High shows the deceased mercy through his supplication and rewards the worshipper himself for his prayer. Likewise, when someone visits graves in the prescribed manner, greeting their occupants with peace and supplicating for them, they receive mercy through his supplication

---

## Page 13249

and he himself is rewarded for his kindness towards them. How different is the intention to benefit the deceased from the intention to associate him with Allah in worship! In Sahīh Muslim, Buraydah said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to teach them that, when they went out to the cemeteries, one of them should say: ‘Peace be upon you, inhabitants of these abodes, believers and Muslims. We shall, if Allah wills, join you. You have gone ahead of us, and we shall follow you. We ask Allah for well-being for ourselves and for you.’”

Also in Sahīh Muslim, ‘Ā’ishah said:

“I asked, ‘What should I say, O Messenger of Allah?’ He replied, ‘Say: “Peace be upon the inhabitants of these abodes, believers and Muslims. May Allah have mercy on those of us who have gone before and those who come later. We shall, if Allah wills, join you.”’”

It is permissible to visit the grave of a disbeliever to take a lesson, but not to seek forgiveness for him. Thus, in the two authentic hadith collections, Abū Hurayrah said:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, visited his mother’s grave and wept, causing those around him to weep. He said: ‘I asked my Lord for permission to seek forgiveness for her, but He did not permit me. I asked Him for permission to visit her, and He permitted me. So visit graves, for they remind you of death.’”

It is also authentically established from him, in a hadith narrated by Anas, that he said:

“I had forbidden you to visit graves; now visit them.”

As for visiting graves in order to supplicate there, to use them as a means of approaching Allah, or to seek intercession through them, Islamic law has not sanctioned this at all. All the reports narrated on this subject, such as the sayings:

“Whoever visits me and visits my father’s grave in a single year, I guarantee him Paradise with Allah,”

“Whoever performs Hajj but does not visit me has shunned me,”

and “Whoever

---

## Page 13250

visits me after my death is as though he visited me during my lifetime”—are weak hadiths; indeed, they are fabricated. The compilers of the authentic hadith collections, the well-known Sunan collections, and the Musnad collections transmitted none of them. At most, such reports are attributed to al-Dāraqutnī’s collection, which he intended to contain singular reports concerning the Sunnah. For this reason, he included weak and fabricated reports that others did not transmit. Hadith scholars agree that merely citing his collection does not justify relying on a report. Those hadith scholars who have written about the reports narrated on this subject explain that none of them is authentic.

Indeed, Mālik and others disapproved of saying, “I visited the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.” Mālik was the most knowledgeable authority on this subject, for the people of Medina knew more about it than the inhabitants of other cities, and Mālik was their imam. Had there been a sunnah transmitted from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, containing the expression “visiting his grave,” it would not have escaped the scholars of his city and those living near his grave—may my father and mother be his ransom.

Accordingly, the established practice among the Companions and the imams of the Muslims was that, when a person offered salutations to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his two Companions, he would then supplicate to Allah facing the direction of prayer, not the chamber. The account related about Mālik and al-Mansūr to the contrary is false and has no basis. I know of no disagreement among the imams that the Sunnah is to face the direction of prayer when supplicating, rather than the Prophet’s grave. Their disagreement concerned only the direction to face when offering salutations to him. The majority said that one should offer salutations to him while facing

---

## Page 13251

the grave. Abū Hanīfah said that one should offer salutations to him while facing the direction of prayer, with one’s back to the grave. ‘Abdullāh ibn ‘Umar would say, “Peace be upon you, O Messenger of Allah. Peace be upon you, O Abū Bakr. Peace be upon you, O my father,” and then leave.

If, even in the mosque of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the imams instructed people to face the direction of prayer when supplicating—as reported from the Companions—and disapproved of facing the grave, what, then, of anyone else’s grave? This makes clear to you that deliberately seeking out graves to supplicate there is not part of the religion of the Muslims. Any author of works on pilgrimage rites or other subjects who states something contrary to this has neither evidence to support it nor a transmitted statement from a recognized imam. It is merely something that people have taken from one another, based on hadiths they thought authentic but which are false, or on innovated customs they supposed to be sunnah, without any basis in Islamic law.

During the most virtuous generations, there were no “shrines” built over graves. These only appeared and became widespread under the rule of Banū Buwayh. When the Qarmatians emerged in the lands of the East and West, there were unbelieving heretics in those lands whose aim was to alter the religion of Islam. The extent to which Banū Buwayh agreed with them in some of this, and embraced the innovations of the Jahmiyyah, Mu‘tazilah and Rāfidah, is well known to scholars. They therefore built spurious shrines, “such as the shrine of ‘Alī”—may Allah be pleased with him—and others like it. Fabricators composed hadiths about visiting shrines, praying and supplicating at them, and similar practices. Thus, these heretics and the people of innovation who followed them came to venerate shrines and demean mosques.

---

## Page 13252

and this is contrary to the religion of the Muslims. They use Shiism as a cover. The previously cited, mass-transmitted hadiths from the Prophet concerning the honor due to al-Siddīq and the prohibition against taking graves as places of worship refute these two innovations, which are the root of associating partners with Allah and altering Islam.

Further evidence of this is that Allah did not mention “shrines” or command prayer in them; rather, His commands concern mosques. He, Exalted is He, said:

“Who is more unjust than one who prevents Allah’s name from being mentioned in His mosques and strives to destroy them?”

He did not say, “Allah’s shrines.” Indeed, “the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded ‘Alī not to leave any raised grave without leveling it, or any image without effacing it.” He also prohibited taking graves as places of worship and cursed those who did so. This is therefore a command to demolish shrines, not to cultivate them, whether through physical construction or devotional activity.

He, Exalted is He, said:

“Do not have sexual relations with them while you are in devotional retreat in the mosques.”

He did not say, “in the shrines.” He, Exalted is He, also said:

“Say, ‘My Lord has commanded justice. Direct your faces to Him at every mosque.’”

He did not say, “at every shrine.”

He, Exalted is He, said:

“It is not for the polytheists to maintain Allah’s mosques.”

He did not say, “Allah’s shrines,” for those who maintain shrines are polytheists or resemble the polytheists. The passage continues to His words:

“Only those who believe in Allah and the Last Day, establish prayer, give zakah, and fear none but Allah maintain Allah’s mosques.”

He did not say, “maintain Allah’s shrines.” Rather, those who maintain shrines fear others besides Allah: they fear the dead, but do not fear

---

## Page 13253

Allah; for they worship Him in a manner for which He has sent down no authority and which neither the Book nor the Sunnah has prescribed. As Allah’s close friend, peace be upon him, said when debating the polytheists, after they disputed with him and sought to frighten him with their gods:

“How should I fear what you associate with Allah, while you do not fear that you have associated with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority to you? Which of the two parties has more right to security, if you know?”

He, Exalted is He, said:

“Those who believe and do not mix their faith with injustice—it is they who will have security, and they are rightly guided.”

In the two Sahīh collections, Ibn Mas‘ūd reported:

“When this verse was revealed—‘Those who believe and do not mix their faith with injustice’—the Companions of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, found it distressing. They said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, which of us has not wronged himself?’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, replied, ‘It refers only to associating partners with Allah. Have you not heard the words of the righteous servant: “Indeed, associating partners with Allah is a tremendous injustice”?’”

He, Exalted is He, said:

“That was Our argument which We gave Abraham against his people. We raise in degrees whom We will.”

Zayd ibn Aslam and others said: “Through knowledge.”

He, Exalted is He, said:

“The mosques belong to Allah, so do not invoke anyone alongside Allah.”

He did not say, “The shrines belong to Allah.” Rather, those devoted to shrines invoke others alongside Allah. Thus, since building mosques over graves—known as “shrines”—and venerating them are not part of the religion of the Muslims, but rather of the religion of the polytheists, this has not been preserved. Allah has guaranteed us that He will preserve the Reminder He sent down, as He said:

“Indeed, it is We who sent down the Reminder, and indeed, We will preserve it.”

Thus, what Allah sent His Messenger with of the Book

---

## Page 13254

and wisdom is preserved. Matters relating to shrines, however, have not been preserved. Indeed, most graves over which mosques have been built are either of doubtful authenticity or certainly falsely attributed. Examples include the grave at Karak said to contain Noah; the one outside Damascus said to be the grave of Ubayy ibn Ka‘b; the one on the other side said to be the grave of Uways al-Qaranī; the graves there thought to belong to ‘Ā’ishah, Umm Salamah—the wife of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—or Umm Habībah; the grave within Najaf attributed to ‘Alī; the shrine in Cairo said to stand over al-Husayn; the shrine in Aleppo; and other such shrines. All these attributions are false, by the agreement of the scholars.

As for the grave in Homs said to be “the grave of Khālid ibn al-Walīd,” the one in Dārayyā said to be the grave of Abū Muslim al-Khawlānī, and similar graves, their attributions are doubtful. We may know in general that someone died in a particular region, without establishing that a particular spot is the location of his grave. Examples include the grave attributed to Bilāl and others outside Damascus, the grave attributed to Fātimah in Medina, and similar cases.

Most people who accept these attributions have learned of them through a dream, an unreliable report, or some other means. The identity of some of those buried in these graves may indeed be certain, but none of these innovated rulings follows from that.

For this reason, the early generations would close off this avenue. When the Muslims conquered Tustar, they found there a bier bearing a body still intact, which they said was “Daniel”

---

## Page 13255

and they found beside him a book recording events. The people of that region used to seek rain through him. Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī wrote to ʿUmar about this. ʿUmar wrote back instructing him to have thirteen graves dug during the day, then to bury him in one of them at night and erase all traces of his grave, lest people be led astray by him.

This resembles what has been reported about ʿUmar: when he learned that some people were visiting the tree beneath which the Pledge of Divine Pleasure had been given and praying there, he ordered the tree to be cut down.

It is authentically reported that, while travelling, he saw people frequenting a spot to pray there. He asked, “What is this?”

They replied, “This is a place where the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed.”

He said, “So it is a place where the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed! Do you wish to turn the sites associated with your prophets into mosques? This was precisely what brought about the destruction of the Children of Israel. Whoever happens to be there when prayer falls due should pray; otherwise, he should continue on his way.”

Know that none of these people has anything with which to counter this except an anecdote reporting that one of them said, “If you have a need to ask of Allah, call upon Him at my grave,” or, “The grave of so-and-so is the tried-and-tested antidote,” and similar anecdotes that may be true or false. Even supposing such an account is true, the person who made the statement is not infallible. Only those who are astray—the brothers of devils—would counter an established report from the infallible Prophet with an unsubstantiated report from someone who is not infallible. This is among the causes of associating partners with Allah and altering the religion.

---

## Page 13256

As for someone saying, “Their needs are sometimes fulfilled; does that justify their seeking out these graves?” the answer is that this does not justify seeking them out, for several reasons.

First, many needs of the polytheists and the People of the Book are fulfilled through supplication at idols, statues of saints, and places they venerate. Such veneration is forbidden in the era of Islam. Would any Muslim say that this justifies their engaging in conduct forbidden by the consensus of the Muslims?

Whatever factors lead the followers of whims and religious innovations to introduce their innovations, factors of the same kind—and worse—are found among the polytheists and the People of the Book, leading them into their disbelief. Whoever reflects on this will find it true of most matters, for religious innovations are derived from disbelief. Complete faith consists in doing what Allah and His Messenger have commanded and refraining from what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden. If a person neglects some of what is commanded and replaces it with something forbidden, his faith is diminished accordingly.

An innovation cannot be pure truth, for if it were, it would be religiously sanctioned. Nor can its benefit outweigh its harm, for if it did, it would be religiously sanctioned. Nor is it pure falsehood containing no truth, for if it were, no one would mistake it for truth. Rather, it contains some truth and some falsehood.

The same applies to the religion of the polytheists and the People of the Book: not everything they report is false, nor is everything they enjoin corrupt. Rather, there must be some truth in what they report

---

## Page 13257

and some measure of benefit in what they enjoin. Yet they are disbelievers because of the truth they have abandoned and the falsehood they have embraced.

The second reason:

Lying is exceedingly common in this matter. Lying is associated with polytheism, as the Quran indicates in several places, while truthfulness is associated with sincere devotion to Allah. Believers are people of truthfulness and sincere devotion, whereas disbelievers are people of lying and polytheism. Since these shrines contain the polytheism that they do, lying accompanies them in various forms.

One is the claim that a particular place is the grave of some venerated person or the burial place of his head. There is much falsehood in such claims.

Second, reports about him in which falsehood abounds.

Third, reports of needs fulfilled at his grave. How often those who venerate a grave resort to tricks, misleading people into believing that an extraordinary event has occurred or a need has been fulfilled through it! How many people report things that have no basis in reality! We have witnessed a great many such things.

Fourth, reports about the lineage of those connected to him. Many people claim a connection to the deceased in that grave, whether through descent or otherwise. I have even seen someone who claims

---

## Page 13258

that he is a descendant of Ibrāhīm ibn Adham, falsely claiming this so that he might become the custodian of his grave. As for lies about the Prophet’s family, they are too numerous to describe.

The Banū ʿUbayd—known as al-Qaddāh—claimed to be Fatimids, built Cairo, and remained kings for some two hundred years, claiming descent from ʿAlī. They gained control over half the Muslim realm, at times even taking control of Baghdad. They were as Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī described them:

“Outwardly, their doctrine is Rāfidī Shiism; inwardly, it is pure disbelief.”

Judge Abū Bakr ibn al-Tayyib wrote his book entitled “Uncovering the Secrets and Tearing Away the Veils” to expose their true character. Numerous other Muslim scholars did likewise, including Judge Abū Yaʿlā and Abū ʿAbd Allāh Muhammad ibn ʿAbd al-Karīm al-Shahrastānī.

All scholars know that they were not descendants of Fātimah. Rather, they were descended from Magians; it has also been said that they descended from a Jew. They were among those furthest removed from the Messenger of Allah—may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon him—in their adherence to his Sunnah and religion. Their inward religion combines the religions of the Magians and the Sabians, while what they outwardly profess of the Muslims’ religion is Rāfidī Shiism. Thus, the best of their religious adherents are the Rāfidīs: their ignorant followers and common people, and everyone who joins them thinking himself a Muslim and believing Islam to be true.

Their elite, however—their kings and scholars—know that they stand outside all these religions: those of the Muslims, Jews, and Christians. And those closest

---

## Page 13259

to them are the philosophers, although they too do not follow the principles of any particular philosopher. For this reason, various groups of philosophizers affiliated themselves with them. Ibn Sīnā and his household were their followers, as were Ibn al-Haytham and others like him, and Mubashshir ibn Fātik and the like. The authors of the “Epistles of the Brethren of Purity” composed their epistles somewhat along their lines. Among them are the Ismāʿīlīs and those belonging to the House of the Mission in Muslim lands.

This is not the place to describe their condition. The point is simply that they were among the greatest liars and those most deeply steeped in associating partners with Allah, and that they lied about their lineage and other matters. Thus, you find that most of those associated with shrines who claim descent from ʿAlī are liars. One may be a client of Banū Hāshim, or have neither kinship nor clientage ties with them, yet say, “I am an ʿAlawī,” meaning an adherent of ʿAlī’s religious doctrine. He portrays ʿAlī—may Allah be pleased with him and his pure household—as having followed the religion of the Rāfidīs. Not content with this slur against ʿAlī, he also passes himself off as a member of his household. The lies concerning graves are too numerous to record in this fatwa.

Fifth:

The Rāfidīs are, without exception, the most untruthful of all the groups within the Muslim community. Among all groups professing Islam, they are the most extreme in religious excess and in associating partners with Allah. From among them came the first person to claim divinity for Quran reciters and prophethood for someone other than the Prophet—may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon him—

---

## Page 13260

such as those who claimed prophethood for ʿAlī, and al-Mukhtār ibn Abī ʿUbayd, who claimed prophethood for himself. Next after them come the ignorant, such as the extremists among misguided worshippers and the followers of shaykhs. After the Rāfidīs, they are the people who most revere graves, the most extreme in religious excess, and the most given to falsehood. Both groups resemble the Christians in some respects. The falsehood, association of partners with Allah, and religious excess of the Christians are well known to learned and ordinary people alike. These groups engage in such things to an extent that Allah alone can reckon.

The third consideration:

If a Muslim’s need is fulfilled after he has offered a supplication at a grave, how does he know that the grave had any influence on the fulfillment of that need? The same applies to the vows they make at graves or elsewhere when their needs are fulfilled.

It is established in the two Sahihs that the Prophet—may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon him—forbade making vows and said:

“It brings no good; it merely extracts something from the miser.”

Another wording states:

“A vow brings the son of Adam nothing that was not already decreed for him. Rather, the vow brings him to the decree I have ordained.”

This authentic hadith establishes that a vow does not bring about the outcome upon which it is made conditional, whether securing a benefit or averting harm—even though the vow is a recompense for the fulfillment of that need and is conditional upon it, and despite the many people whose needs are fulfilled after they have made vows conditional upon their fulfillment. Graves are therefore even less likely to be a cause of such fulfillment.

Furthermore, that need must have been fulfilled either without his supplication or through his supplication. If the former is the case, there is nothing further to discuss; but if

---

## Page 13261

it is the latter, then he has supplicated with such earnestness that, had he done so somewhere other than that spot, or even beside a cross, his need would have been fulfilled. The cause, therefore, is his earnestness in supplication, not anything particular to the grave.

The fourth consideration:

Even supposing that graves have some influence in this regard—whether through the graves themselves, as the would-be philosophers and those who follow their approach maintain, claiming that the departed soul connects with the supplicant’s soul, thereby strengthening him, as Ibn Sīnā, Abū Hāmid, and others like them allege concerning visits to graves, or through some other cause—the response is:

Not every means by which a person obtains what he needs is prescribed by Islamic law, or even permissible. A means is prescribed only when its benefit outweighs its harm. If its harm predominates, however, it is not prescribed; rather, it is forbidden, even if it yields some benefit.

This includes the prohibition of sorcery, despite its effects and its fulfillment of certain needs, and the practices it encompasses, such as worshipping the heavenly bodies, invoking them, and summoning the jinn. The same applies to soothsaying, divination by arrows, and the various practices forbidden by Islamic law, even though they sometimes involve some disclosure of hidden matters or produce certain effects. This provides a general indication of the means by which their needs are fulfilled. As for

---

## Page 13262

a detailed account of this, it would require extensive discussion, as would an explanation of the kinds of sorcery, the reason for its effects, and the sīmiyā (a form of sorcery) it involves, along with the kinds of associating partners with Allah and what prompted the polytheists to worship idols.

Any rational person knows that no nation has ever unanimously adopted a practice without a reason. Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, peace be upon him, says:

“And keep me and my sons away from worshipping idols.”

“My Lord, indeed they have led many people astray.”

Anyone who imagines that idol-worshippers believed their idols created the world, sent down rain, caused plants to grow, created animals, or anything of that kind is ignorant of their beliefs. Rather, their purpose in turning to their idols was of the same kind as that of those who associate partners with Allah through graves when they turn to the graves they venerate, or that of Christians when they turn to the graves of saints: they take them as intercessors, intermediaries, and means of approach. Indeed, reliable reports have established for us that some of those who worship at graves do more there than many idol-worshippers do. It is enough for a Muslim to know that Allah has forbidden nothing unless its harm is either unmixed or predominant.

As for anything whose benefit is unmixed or outweighs its harm, Allah has prescribed it. For the messengers were sent to secure and perfect benefits, and to eliminate and reduce harms. Just as associating partners with Allah is linked with falsehood, it is also linked with sorcery, as in His words, Exalted is He:

“Have you not seen those who were given a portion of the Scripture? They believe in sorcery and false gods, and say of those who disbelieve, ‘These are better guided than the believers as to the way.’”

“Those

---

## Page 13263

are the ones whom Allah has cursed. And whoever Allah curses—you will never find anyone to help him.”

Jibt means sorcery, and tāghūt means Satan and idols. Such is the condition of many who profess adherence to Islam: they venerate sorcery and associating partners with Allah, and regard disbelievers more highly than many believers who adhere to the Sharia. There is no room on this sheet for more than this. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 13264

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Is supplication at graves, such as those of the righteous and the friends of Allah, permissible or not? Is it more likely to be answered there than at the graves of others? And which places are best for supplication?”

He replied:

Supplication at graves is no better than supplication in mosques or elsewhere. None of the early generations or the imams said that it is recommended to go to graves specifically to supplicate there, whether the graves of prophets or anyone else. Rather, it is established in Sahīh al-Bukhārī that ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb sought rain through al-ʿAbbās—the uncle of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him—and said:

“O Allah, we used to seek rain from You through our Prophet, and You would grant us rain. Now we seek a means of drawing near to You through our Prophet’s uncle, so grant us rain.”

And they would be granted rain. Thus, they sought rain through al-ʿAbbās just as they had sought it through the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, because he was the Prophet’s uncle. They did not seek rain at his grave or supplicate there. Indeed, it is established in the authentic hadith collections that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

He was warning against what they had done. He also said, before he

---

## Page 13265

died—five days beforehand:

“Those before you used to take graves as places of worship. Beware! Do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.”

The Sunan collections also report that he, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“May Allah curse women who visit graves and those who set up places of worship and lamps over them.”

Since he forbade taking graves as places of worship and lighting lamps over them, it is clear that he did not designate them as places for worshipping Allah and supplicating. Rather, he prescribed that those who visit graves should greet the deceased and supplicate for them, just as he prescribed offering the funeral prayer over them before burial and supplicating for them. Thus, the purpose of what he, peace and blessings be upon him, prescribed is to supplicate for the deceased, not to invoke them. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 13266

Shaykh Muhammad ibn Abd al-Hādī also said:

(*)
In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful.

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds.

To proceed: This is a fatwa issued by the shaykh and imam, Taqī al-Dīn Abū al-Abbās Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah, may Allah be pleased with him. Then, some seventeen years later, certain people denounced it, and a group used it to vilify him before some of those in authority. It was presented in inflammatory terms, leading some people to misunderstand its actual meaning. Other matters compounded this denunciation, vilification, and alteration of its wording. All of this prompted correspondence with the Sultan—the Sultan of Islam in Egypt—may Allah Most High support him. He assembled the judges of his land, and it was subsequently decided to imprison the shaykh. He was therefore imprisoned in the citadel of Damascus, may Allah protect it, pursuant to a written order that arrived on the seventh of the blessed month of Shaʿbān, 726 AH.

Throughout all of this, the aforementioned shaykh had not appeared before a court, nor had he seen the written text of his that was denounced, nor had any formal claim been brought against him. Some men from his town who were away from home then copied out this fatwa and showed it to some of the scholars of Baghdad. After carefully considering it and reading its wording, they wrote their responses to it.

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (pp. 211–213):
> The following points should be noted:
> First: The Shaykh’s fatwa—may Allah have mercy on him—and the supporting fatwas of other scholars subsequently quoted by Ibn 'Abd al-Hādī appear verbatim in Ibn 'Abd al-Hādī’s al-'Uqūd al-Durriyya min Manāqib Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyya, pp. 346–376.
> Second: Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—also reproduced the text of this fatwa in al-Radd 'alā al-Ikhnā'ī, pp. 40–49. This passage from al-Radd 'alā al-Ikhnā'ī also appears in its abridgment, published in al-Fatāwā, 27/214–228.
> Third: There are a few minor differences—approximately eight—between the fatwa transmitted by Ibn 'Abd al-Hādī—may Allah have mercy on him—and published here, and the fatwa reproduced by the Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—in al-Ikhnā'iyya and its abridgment. The most significant differences are:
> 1 — 27/185: “Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī argued that travelling to visit graves is permissible, citing the fact that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, used to visit the mosque of Qubā' [and that he used to visit graves].” The bracketed words appear in al-Ikhnā'iyya, p. 44, and its abridgment in al-Fatāwā, 27/219, but are missing here.
> 27/188 — “If a man vows to travel to them for a permissible purpose, this is permissible.” In al-Ikhnā'iyya, p. 45, and its abridgment, 27/221, the wording is: “If, however, it is supposed that a man travelled to them…” This is the more plausible reading.
> 3 — 27/189: “In the Sunan of Sa'īd ibn Mansūr, it is reported that 'Abd Allāh ibn Hasan ibn al-Husayn ibn 'Alī ibn Abī Tālib saw a man repeatedly visiting the Prophet’s grave [and supplicating there]. He said: ‘O man, the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said: “Do not make my grave a place of celebration. Send blessings upon me, for your blessings reach me wherever you may be.” Thus, you and a man in al-Andalus are on an equal footing in relation to him.’”
> The bracketed words are absent from al-Ikhnā'iyya, p. 46, and its abridgment, 27/222. Their omission accords with the transmitted wording of the hadith.
> 4 — 27/191: “The first to fabricate these hadiths concerning travel to visit shrines erected over graves were the people of innovation, such as the Rāfida and others like them, who leave mosques unused and venerate shrines. [They abandon the houses of Allah, in which He has commanded that His name be mentioned and that He alone be worshipped, without partner, and venerate shrines] where partners are associated with Him, lies are told, and a religion is invented for which Allah has sent down no authority. For the Book and the Sunnah mention mosques, not shrines.”
> The bracketed words were omitted from al-Ikhnā'iyya, p. 48, and its abridgment, 27/224, because the copyist’s eye skipped from the first occurrence of “venerate shrines” to the second. And Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 13267

Some Mālikī scholars in Damascus were also asked about it, and they wrote similarly. We have heard that someone in Egypt examined it and concurred. We shall now present the question on which the scholars of Baghdad wrote, the request for a ruling and the aforementioned shaykh’s answer to it, followed by the jurists’ responses. Here is the text of the question and the answers.

Our esteemed scholars and virtuous guides, the imams of the religion and guides of the Muslims—may Allah grant them success in attaining His pleasure and perpetuate guidance through them—are kindly requested to examine the request for a ruling and its answer, attached to this inquiry and transcribed below. Its wording is as follows:

What do the esteemed scholars, the imams of the religion—may Allah benefit the Muslims through them—say concerning a man who intends to travel to “visit the graves of the prophets and the righteous,” such as our Prophet Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him, and others? May he shorten his prayers during his journey? Is such a visit prescribed by Islamic law or not?

It has been reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Whoever performs Hajj but does not visit me has slighted me.”

“And whoever visits me after my death is like one who visited me during my lifetime.”

---

## Page 13268

And it has also been reported that he, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Do not undertake a journey except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque, this mosque of mine, and al-Aqsā Mosque.”

Give us your ruling—may you be rewarded, and may Allah have mercy on you.

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds. As for someone who travels solely to visit the graves of the prophets and the righteous, may he shorten his prayers? There are two well-known opinions on this:
