The first is that shortening the prayers during such a journey is not permissible, because it is a prohibited journey. This is the view of earlier scholars who do not permit shortening the prayers on a sinful journey, such as Abū ‘Abd Allāh ibn Batta, Abū al-Wafā’ ibn ‘Aqīl, and many groups of earlier scholars. The position of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad is that prayers may not be shortened during a journey prohibited by Islamic law.

The second opinion is that he may shorten his prayers. This is held by those who permit shortening the prayers during a forbidden journey, such as Abū Hanīfa. It is also held by some later followers of al-Shāfi‘ī

---

## Page 13269

and Ahmad who permit travelling to visit the graves of the prophets and the righteous, such as Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī, Abū al-Hasan ibn ‘Abdūs al-Harrānī, and Abū Muhammad ibn Qudāma al-Maqdisī.

These scholars say that such travel is not forbidden, citing the general wording of the Prophet’s statement, peace and blessings be upon him:

“Visit the graves.”

Some who are unfamiliar with hadith may cite reports concerning visiting the Prophet’s grave, peace and blessings be upon him, such as his saying:

“Whoever visits me after my death, it is as though he visited me during my lifetime.”

This was narrated by al-Dāraqutnī and Ibn Mājah.

As for the saying mentioned by some people:

“Whoever performs Hajj but does not visit me has shunned me,”

none of the scholars has narrated it. It is like the saying:

“Whoever visits me and my father Abraham in the same year, I guarantee him Paradise from Allah.”

The scholars agree that no one has narrated this either, nor has anyone cited it as evidence. Rather, some of them cite the report narrated by al-Dāraqutnī and similar reports.

Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī argued that travelling to visit graves is permissible on the grounds that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, used to visit the mosque of Qubā’. He answered the hadith, “Do not undertake a journey,” by interpreting it as meaning that such travel is not recommended.

---

## Page 13270

As for the former group, they cite as evidence the Prophet’s saying, peace and blessings be upon him, recorded in the two Sahīh collections:

“No journey should be undertaken except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque, this mosque of mine, and al-Aqsā Mosque.”

The imams agree that this hadith is authentic and is to be acted upon. If a man vows to undertake a journey to pray or observe a devotional retreat at a mosque or shrine other than these three mosques, or to travel there, he is not obliged to fulfil that vow, by agreement of the imams. If he vows to travel to the Sacred Mosque for Hajj or ‘umrah, he is obliged to do so, by agreement of the scholars.

If he vows to visit the Prophet’s Mosque, peace and blessings be upon him, or al-Aqsā Mosque for prayer or a devotional retreat, he must fulfil this vow according to Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī in one of his two opinions, and Ahmad. According to Abū Hanīfah, however, he is not obliged to do so, because in his view a vow becomes binding only when the act belongs to a category already made obligatory by Islamic law.

The majority, however, hold that a vow to perform any act of obedience must be fulfilled, as established in Sahīh al-Bukhārī, on the authority of ‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah, let him obey Him; and whoever vows to disobey Allah, let him not disobey Him.”

Travelling to these two mosques is an act of obedience; therefore, a vow to do so must be fulfilled.

---

## Page 13271

As for travelling to a place other than the three mosques, no scholar has held that someone who vows to make such a journey is obliged to do so. Indeed, the scholars explicitly stated that one should not travel to the Mosque of Qubā’, since it is not one of the three mosques—even though visiting it is recommended for those in Medina, because this does not involve undertaking a journey. As stated in the authentic hadith:

“Whoever purifies himself at home, then comes to the Mosque of Qubā’ intending only to pray there, will receive the reward of an ‘umrah.”

They also argued that travelling to visit the graves of the prophets and the righteous is an innovation that none of the Companions or Successors practised, that the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, never commanded, and that none of the imams of the Muslims deemed recommended. Whoever believes this to be an act of worship and undertakes it therefore contravenes the Sunnah and the consensus of the imams. This is among the innovations contrary to the Sunnah and scholarly consensus that Abū ‘Abd Allāh ibn Battah mentioned in al-Ibānah al-Sughrā.

This makes clear the invalidity of Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī’s argument: the Prophet’s visits to the Mosque of Qubā’, peace and blessings be upon him, did not involve undertaking a journey, and al-Maqdisī concedes their point that a vow to travel there does not make that journey obligatory.

His statement that the hadith whose purport is “No journey should be undertaken” is to be understood as denying that such travel is recommended can be answered in two ways:

---

## Page 13272

First: this is an admission on his part that such travel is not a righteous deed, a means of drawing near to Allah, an act of obedience, or a good deed. Thus, whoever believes that travelling to visit the graves of the prophets and the righteous is a means of drawing near to Allah, an act of worship, and an act of obedience has contradicted scholarly consensus. If he travels in the belief that doing so is an act of obedience, this is forbidden by the consensus of the Muslims. The prohibition therefore arises from treating it as a means of drawing near to Allah, and it is well known that no one travels to these graves except for that purpose. If, however, a man vows to travel to them for a permissible purpose, that is permissible and does not fall into this category.

Second: this hadith entails a prohibition, and a prohibition entails that the act is forbidden. As for the hadiths they cite concerning visiting the Prophet’s grave, peace and blessings be upon him, they are all weak according to the consensus of hadith scholars; indeed, they are fabricated. None of the compilers of the authoritative Sunan collections narrated any of them, nor did any of the imams cite any of them as evidence. Rather, Mālik—the imam of the people of the Prophet’s city, who are the most knowledgeable about the ruling on this matter—disapproved of a man saying, “I visited his grave, peace and blessings be upon him.” Had this expression been familiar among them, sanctioned by the religion, or transmitted from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, the scholar of Medina would not have disapproved of it.

As for Imam Ahmad, the most knowledgeable person of his time concerning the Sunnah, when he was asked about this, he did not

---

## Page 13273

have any hadith on which to rely in this matter except the hadith of Abū Hurayrah, in which the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“No man greets me with peace without Allah restoring my soul to me so that I may return his greeting.”

This is also what Abū Dāwūd relied upon in his Sunan. Likewise, Mālik narrated in al-Muwatta’ that whenever Abd Allāh ibn Umar entered the mosque, he would say, “Peace be upon you, O Messenger of Allah. Peace be upon you, O Abū Bakr. Peace be upon you, my father,” and then leave.

In Sunan Abī Dāwūd, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, is reported to have said:

“Do not make my grave a place of recurring gathering. Send blessings upon me, for your blessings reach me wherever you may be.”

In the Sunan of Sa‘īd ibn Mansūr, it is reported that Abd Allāh ibn Hasan ibn Husayn ibn Alī ibn Abī Tālib saw a man repeatedly visiting the grave of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and supplicating there. He said, “O you! The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said: ‘Do not make my grave a place of recurring gathering. Send blessings upon me, for wherever you may be, your blessings reach me.’ In this respect, you and a man in al-Andalus are on an equal footing.”

In the two Sahīh collections, Ā’ishah reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said during his final illness:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians; they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

He was warning against what they had done. Were it not for that, his grave would have been left exposed, but he was averse

---

## Page 13274

to its being made a place of worship. They buried him, peace and blessings be upon him, in the chamber of Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, contrary to their usual practice of burial in open ground, so that no one would pray at his grave and make it a place of worship, thereby turning his grave into an idol.

While the Prophet’s chamber remained separate from the mosque—until the time of al-Walīd ibn Abd al-Malik—none of the Companions or Successors would enter it to pray there, rub against the grave, or supplicate there. Rather, they would do all of this only in the mosque. When the early generations of Companions and Successors greeted the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and wished to supplicate, they would supplicate facing the direction of prayer, not the grave.

As for standing to greet him, may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon him, Abū Hanīfah said:

“One should face the direction of prayer in this case too, not the grave.”

Most of the imams, however, said:

“Rather, one should face the grave specifically when offering the greeting.”

None of the imams said that one should face the grave when supplicating. The only report to that effect is a fabricated account attributed to Mālik, whose own position is contrary to it.

---

## Page 13275

The imams also agreed that one should neither rub against the grave of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, nor kiss it. All of this serves to safeguard monotheism.

One of the roots of associating partners with Allah is taking graves as places of worship. Thus, a number of the early generations commented on Allah’s words:

“They said, ‘Never abandon your gods, and never abandon Wadd, Suwā‘, Yaghūth, Ya‘ūq, or Nasr.’”

They said:

“These were righteous men among Noah’s people. When they died, people gathered devotedly at their graves, then fashioned statues in their likeness. After a long time had passed, they worshipped them.”

Al-Bukhārī reported the substance of this account from Ibn ‘Abbās in his Sahīh. Muhammad ibn Jarīr al-Tabarī and others also related it in their Quran commentaries from several of the early generations. Wathīmah and others related it through several chains of transmission in their accounts of the prophets. I have discussed the foundations of these matters at length elsewhere.

The first to fabricate these hadiths concerning travel to visit shrines erected over graves were adherents of religious innovation among the Rāfidah and others like them, who leave mosques unused and exalt shrines. They abandon the houses of Allah, in which He commanded that His name be remembered and that He alone be worshipped, without any partner. Instead, they exalt shrines where partners are associated with Him, truth is denied, and a religion is invented for which Allah has sent down no authority. The Quran and Sunnah mention mosques, not shrines, as Allah Most High says:

“Say: My Lord has commanded justice. And direct

---

## Page 13276

your faces toward Him at every mosque, and call upon Him, sincerely devoting your worship to Him.” Allah Most High also says:

“Only those who believe in Allah and the Last Day maintain Allah’s mosques.”

And He says:

“Do not have sexual relations with them while you are in devotional retreat in the mosques.”

And He says:

“The mosques belong to Allah, so do not invoke anyone alongside Allah.”

And He says:

“Who is more unjust than those who prevent Allah’s name from being mentioned in His mosques and strive to bring about their ruin?”

It is authentically reported in the Sahīh that he, peace and blessings be upon him, used to say:

“Those before you used to take graves as places of worship. Beware! Do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.”

And Allah knows best.

This concludes Shaykh al-Islām’s answer. Allah, glorified and exalted be He, knows best. He has discussed such matters extensively elsewhere, as he indicated in his answer.

When they obtained this answer in Damascus, they copied it and sent it to Egypt. The Shāfiʿī judge wrote upon it:

“I checked the answer to this question against the text in Ibn Taymiyyah’s handwriting and found it correct.”

Later in his statement, he said:

“The distortion lies in his declaring that visiting the grave of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and the graves of the other prophets—may Allah Most High be pleased with them all—is a sin conclusively established by consensus.”

These were his words.

Consider this misrepresentation of Shaykh al-Islām! His answer contains no prohibition against visiting the graves of the prophets and the righteous. He merely mentioned two opinions concerning undertaking travel solely to visit graves.

---

## Page 13277

And visiting graves without undertaking a journey to them is one matter; undertaking a journey solely to visit them is another. The Shaykh does not prohibit visits that involve no such journey; rather, he considers them recommended and encourages them. His books and manuals of pilgrimage rites bear witness to this. In his fatwa, the Shaykh did not address such visits, declare them sinful, or report a consensus prohibiting them. Nothing is hidden from Allah, glorified and exalted be He.

When the aforementioned judge’s written statement reached Egypt, much controversy arose and the turmoil intensified. The judges there were summoned, and they gathered and deliberated. Some recommended imprisoning the Shaykh, and the Sultan ordered it. What has already been recounted then took place. Afterwards, other events befell those involved in this case, which cannot be related here.

The Shaykh’s answer to this question reached the scholars of Baghdad. They rose to his defence and wrote expressing their agreement with him. I have seen their statements in their own handwriting.

The following is a copy of what they wrote:

---

## Page 13278

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.

The servant, ever in need of Allah Most High, declares, after praising Allah, whose blessings abound and whose favours are longstanding, and invoking blessings upon the noblest of the prophets and messengers, Muhammad—may Allah bless him and all his family and Companions:

Allah Most High has bestowed His favour upon His servants and graciously extended His mercy to His lands by entrusting the affairs of Muhammad’s community and the reins of the upright faith to one whom He has endowed with the finest qualities of soul and the fullest spiritual felicity: one who revives the ways of justice and manifests the ways of generosity; who holds fast to Allah’s rope, relies upon Allah, finds sufficiency in Allah’s blessings, upholds Allah’s commands, draws strength from Allah’s might, and seeks illumination through Allah’s light. May Allah strengthen his sovereignty and exalt his standing above all other kings. May the nations ever bow to his commands and the people submit to his decrees. May whoever supports his rule ever be strengthened through obedience to him, and whoever opposes his might be disgraced, condemned, and driven away.

It is therefore hoped, through the graciousness of the august court—may Allah Most High increase its eminence and honour—that the scholars, who are the heirs of the prophets and the choicest of the elect

---

## Page 13279

and the pillars of the faith and the mainstay of those endowed with certainty—may receive an ample share of the sovereign’s care and a measure of mercy and compassion. For this is a distinction unmatched by any other virtue, and a good deed that no evil can overshadow: it embodies true reverence for Allah Most High’s command and the essence of compassion for His creation.

I, your humble servant, have indeed examined the question put to the shaykh, imam, and erudite scholar, unequalled in his day and peerless in his age, Taqī al-Dīn Abū al-'Abbās Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah, together with his answer. I found it to be a distillation of what the scholars have said on this subject, suited to the circumstances, drawing upon his accurate citations and the implications established and accepted in the course of the discussion. It is neither tainted by prejudice nor marred by a wilful disregard of the facts. Nor does it contain—Allah forbid—anything that would disparage or diminish the Messenger’s standing, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

How could scholars allow partisanship to lead them to utter words disparaging or belittling the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace? Could anyone conceivably imagine that visiting his grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, enhances his stature, or that refraining from visiting it diminishes the reverence due to him? The Messenger is far above such a notion!

Admittedly, if someone were to make such a statement unprompted, and there were indications of disparagement and belittlement, it could be construed in that sense—though even then it would be an implicit allusion, not an explicit statement.

---

## Page 13280

How, then, could it be construed that way when he said it in response to a question and in the course of inquiry and debate?

Moreover, what is understood from the scholars’ statements and the reflections of discerning people is that visitation is not, in itself, an act of worship or obedience. Thus, if someone swore to perform an act of worship or obedience, he would not fulfil his oath through such a visit. However, Judge Ibn Kajj—one of the later scholars of our school—held that a vow to undertake this visitation constitutes an act of devotion binding upon the person making it. He stands alone in this view: neither an explicit textual authority nor a sound analogy supports him.

The unqualified wording of the prophetic report—his saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “Journeys are not to be undertaken,” and the remainder of the hadith—implies that undertaking journeys to places other than those he mentioned is not permissible—or that such journeys are not obligatory or recommended. Whoever does so contravenes the explicit prohibition. Contravening a prohibition is a sin—whether disbelief or something less—depending on the matter prohibited, its obligatory or forbidden status, and the nature of the prohibition. Visitation is more specific in one respect: visitation without undertaking a journey is not prohibited, whereas visitation involving such a journey is prohibited.

In short, what Shaykh Taqī al-Dīn stated, in the form described and examined, merits neither punishment nor reproach. The sovereign’s merciful benevolence is more fittingly expressed by granting him relief and regarding him with compassion and mercy. May the sovereign’s counsels rise ever higher.

Written by Ibn al-Kutubī al-Shāfi'ī, praising Allah for His blessings. End of quotation.

---

## Page 13281

Another Response

Allah is the One who grants success.

What the most eminent and peerless shaykh—a surviving exemplar of the early generations and a model for later generations, foremost among critical scholars and finest of meticulous investigators, Taqī al-Milla wa-l-Haqq wa-l-Dīn—stated in his response regarding the disagreement on this issue is correct and recorded in numerous scholarly works. There is no ground for objecting to him on this account, for it neither disparages the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor diminishes his standing, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Shaykh Abū Muhammad al-Juwaynī explicitly stated in his books that travelling to visit graves is forbidden. This is also the position adopted by the judge and imam, ‘Iyād ibn Mūsā ibn ‘Iyād, in his Ikmāl. He is among the most distinguished later scholars of our school.

Al-Mudawwana states:

“Whoever says, ‘I undertake to walk to Medina or Jerusalem,’ is not to go to either place at all unless he intends to pray in their two mosques; in that case, he is to go.”

Thus, he did not regard a vow to visit the Prophet’s grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as an act of obedience that must be fulfilled, since one of our fundamental principles is that whoever vows to perform an act of obedience is bound to fulfil it

---

## Page 13282

, whether an act of the same kind is obligatory under the Sacred Law—as stipulated in Abū Hanīfa’s school—or not.

The judge Abū Ishāq Ismā‘īl ibn Ishāq said after discussing this issue:

“Were it not for prayer in those two mosques, he would not be obliged to go to them. Even if he had vowed a visit as an act of obedience, he would not be obliged to do so.”

Al-Qayrawānī mentioned this in his Taqrīb, as did Shaykh Ibn Sīrīn in his Tanbīh.

In al-Mabsūt, Mālik said concerning someone who vows to walk to one of the mosques to pray there:

“I disapprove of that for him, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: ‘Mounts are not to be ridden on a journey except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque, the Mosque of Jerusalem, and this mosque of mine.’”

Muhammad ibn al-Mawwāz related in al-Mawwāziyya:

“Unless it is nearby, in which case he must fulfil his vow, since this does not constitute setting out on a journey.”

Shaykh Abū ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-Barr said in his book al-Tamhīd:

“It is forbidden for Muslims to take the graves of the prophets and the righteous as places of worship.”

Since this has been established, it is not permissible to accuse of disbelief someone who answers this question by stating that such travel is forbidden. Whoever declares him an unbeliever on this account without valid grounds is himself an unbeliever if he considers doing so lawful; otherwise, he is sinfully disobedient.

Imam Abū ‘Abd Allāh Muhammad ibn ‘Alī al-Māzarī said in his book al-Mu‘lim:

---

## Page 13283

“Whoever declares anyone who prays facing the qiblah an unbeliever has himself disbelieved if he considers doing so lawful; otherwise, he is sinfully disobedient.”

When his case is brought before the judge, the judge must discipline him and impose a discretionary punishment sufficient to deter others like him. If the judge leaves him unpunished despite having the power to act, he is sinful. And Allah Most High knows best.

Written by Muhammad ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān al-Baghdādī, serving the Mālikī community at the noble Mustansiriyya Madrasa. May Allah have mercy on its founder.

Another respondent answered as follows:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds, and may His blessings be upon our master Muhammad and his pure family.

In his statement, our master—the learned imam who acts upon his knowledge, a man of manifold virtues and beneficial learning, an ocean of knowledge and a fountain of excellence, Jamāl al-Dīn, whose handwritten statement precedes this writing of mine—has presented the plain and manifest truth and eschewed the indulgence shown by the shaykhs. May Allah adorn Islam through him and lavish His abundant favours upon him.

As for the question and answer that preceded his statement, no one possessed of discernment and reason can fail to see that he provided an answer corresponding to the question by relating the statements of earlier scholars. The only challenge remaining would be for someone to dispute the accuracy of his transmission, whereupon he would produce it

---

## Page 13284

to him from the books of the scholars whose statements he cited. Anyone who attacks him with denunciations is either ignorant, not knowing what he is saying, or feigning ignorance, driven by envy and the zealotry of the age of ignorance to reject what scholars accept. May Allah Most High protect us from the perils of envy and shield us from the portents of misery through Muhammad and his good and pure family. Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

Written by one in need of his Lord’s pardon and pleasure, ‘Abd al-Mu’min ibn ‘Abd al-Haqq al-Khatīb. May Allah forgive him and all Muslims.

Another respondent answered as follows:

After praising Allah, with whose praise every discourse begins, and invoking blessings and peace upon His Messenger Muhammad, the best of creation, and upon his family and his righteous, noble Companions, beacons of guidance and lamps in the darkness, the poorest of Allah’s servants and the most in need of His pardon says:

What the accomplished and noble shaykh and imam—the pride of mankind, the adornment of Islam, the pillar of the Sacred Law, the champion of the Sunnah, the suppressor of innovation, the possessor of manifold virtues, and the exemplar of eminent scholars—has cited in this answer from the statements of the scholars and noble imams—may Allah have mercy upon them

---

## Page 13285

all—is clear beyond dispute and manifest beyond concealment. Indeed, it is clearer than the sun and moon, and more evident than daybreak to anyone with eyes to see.

The principal basis in this matter is the hadith whose authenticity is agreed upon. The disagreement among the scholars arises from the two possible meanings of its wording.

The Prophet’s words—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—

“Journeys are not undertaken”

admit two interpretations: a statement of negation or a prohibition.

If understood as a negation, they negate the merit and recommended status of undertaking journeys and riding mounts to places other than the three mosques. For, supposing that these acts do occur, their occurrence cannot be denied. The negation must therefore concern their merit and recommended status, not the acts themselves. This applies generally to every destination to which travelling and riding mounts are believed to be acts of devotion and merit: mosques, visits to the graves of the righteous, and similar purposes. Indeed, its scope is broader still.

This is established by the necessity of affirming, for what follows “except,” the predicate implicitly negated at the beginning of the sentence. Otherwise, the ruling would not differ between what precedes “except” and what follows it, whereas it does differ. In that case, denying merit and recommended status does not entail denying permissibility. This is the reasoning relied upon by those who consider such travel permissible: they understand this wording as a negation and, on that basis, allow the shortening of prayers.

If, however, the wording is understood as a prohibition, it means that he forbade riding mounts and undertaking journeys to places other than the three mosques. For the generally accepted principle among scholars of legal theory is that a prohibition entails that an act is either forbidden or reprehensible, according to what the evidence requires.

---

## Page 13286

This, then, is the reasoning relied upon by those who hold that shortening the prayers during such a journey is impermissible, because the journey itself is prohibited.

Among those who declared it forbidden were Shaykh and Imam Abū Muhammad al-Juwaynī of the Shāfi‘ī school and Shaykh Abū al-Wafā’ Ibn ‘Aqīl of the Hanbalī school. This is also the position that al-Qādī ‘Iyād of the Mālikī school indicated he preferred. The hadiths recommending visits to graves are understood to concern visits that do not involve undertaking a journey or riding mounts, thereby reconciling the two sets of reports.

It might also be argued that no other hadith can stand in opposition to the hadith,

“Journeys are not undertaken,”

because it does not equal it in rank, the latter belonging to the highest category of authentic hadith. And Allah knows best.

I have been informed that the respondent has suffered harm and been subjected to pressure. This is something that bewilders the discerning, astonishes the intelligent, and fills them with troubling doubts. His answer to this question sets out the disagreement among the scholars; it does not entail any disparagement of the righteous or the prophets.

Acting according to the purport of his words—may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon him—in a hadith whose authentic attribution to him is agreed upon represents the utmost adherence to his commands and prohibitions. Departing from this is something to beware of, and there is no doubt about that. If this is so, what blame can attach to someone who, when asked about an issue, mentions

---

## Page 13287

the disagreement among the jurists and inclines towards one of the scholars’ views? Such has always been the practice throughout the passing ages and succeeding generations.

Can the critic’s conduct be explained by anything other than his mounting the emaciated steed of desire, which carries its rider to ruin? For anyone who draws upon his valuable insights and gathers his rare gems of learning surely deserves esteem and honour from those of sound understanding and sound judgment.

And, to all appearances, is not the one who joins forces against him aptly described by the familiar proverb:

“Barley is eaten, yet reviled.”

And by the poet’s words:

“His sons repaid Abū al-Ghaylān for his years
and kindly deeds as Sinimmār was repaid.”

And another poet’s words:

“Speech I relish, which those who describe it
say is weighed with care;
splendid speech, at times allusive—
the finest speech is that which hints.”

Allah Most High said:

“And do not let the hatred of a people prevent you from being just. Be just; that is nearer to righteousness. And fear Allah; indeed, Allah is Acquainted with what you do.”

---

## Page 13288

And He, Most High, said:

“Cooperate in righteousness and piety, but do not cooperate in sin and aggression. And fear Allah; indeed, Allah is severe in punishment.”

And He, Most High, said:

“O you who believe, fear Allah and speak words of truth and justice.”

“He will set right your deeds for you and forgive you your sins. Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger has indeed achieved a great triumph.”

And He, Most High, said:

“Allah will surely support those who support Him. Indeed, Allah is Powerful and Almighty.”

Were it not for fear of causing weariness, I would not have refrained from writing at greater length. We ask Allah, the Generous, to lead us and you along the path of guidance and to keep us and you away from the path of error. He has power over all things and is most worthy of answering our prayers. Allah is sufficient for us; He is the best disposer of affairs and the best helper. All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of the worlds. May Allah’s blessings and peace be upon the master of the messengers, Muhammad the Prophet, his pure family, and his noble, chosen Companions.

This is the response of the shaykh, imam and eminent scholar Jamāl al-Dīn Yūsuf ibn ʿAbd al-Mahmūd ibn ʿAbd al-Salām ibn al-Battī al-Hanbalī, may Allah Most High have mercy on him.

The author said:

I copied it from his own handwriting.

## Page 13289

Another Response
By a Mālikī Scholar of Syria

Praise be to Allah, who is sufficient for me.

Journeying to places other than the three mosques is not prescribed. As for someone who journeys to the Prophet’s Mosque to pray there and offer greetings of peace to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and to his two Companions, may Allah be pleased with them both, such a journey, as described, is prescribed by agreement of the scholars.

As for undertaking a journey to visit him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, without intending to pray: if a person mentions the scholarly disagreement concerning this journey—that some scholars have said it is prohibited, while others have said it is permissible; that, according to both views, it is neither an act of obedience nor a means of drawing near to Allah; and that, consequently, under these two views, treating it as an act of obedience and a means of drawing near to Allah is forbidden by consensus—and presents the evidence for each view or gives preference to one of them, he does not incur the same liability as someone who disparages the Prophet, since this involves neither disparagement nor disrespect toward the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

---

## Page 13290

Mālik, may Allah have mercy on him, was asked by a man who had vowed to visit the Prophet’s grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He replied:

“If he intended the Prophet’s Mosque, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, let him go there and pray in it. But if he intended the grave, he should not do so, because of the hadith: ‘Journeys are not to be undertaken except to three mosques.’”

Allah knows best.

Written by Abū ʿAmr ibn Abī al-Walīd al-Mālikī.
ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī al-Walīd al-Mālikī likewise affirms this.

The author, may Allah have mercy on him, said: “I transcribed all these responses from the handwriting of the muftis who issued them.”

He continued:

I also examined a letter that arrived with the responses from the scholars of Baghdad. Its text is as follows:

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Bestower of Mercy.

Praise be to Allah, who upholds the Islamic faith and strengthens the law brought by Muhammad through the enduring, blessed sultanic rule of al-Malik al-Nāsir. May Allah Most High clothe it in lasting might and adorn it with uninterrupted victory throughout the passing nights and days. Blessings and peace be upon the Prophet sent to all mankind; may Allah bless him and his righteous, noble family.

## Page 13291

O Allah, Your door has always been open to those who ask, and Your bounty has always been freely bestowed upon those who come seeking it. Whoever You have accustomed to asking You alone asks no one besides You; whoever You have granted gifts from Your bounty turns to no one else and seeks refuge only in Your protection. You are the Mighty Lord, the Generous, the Most Generous. Your servants are forbidden to seek any door other than Yours. You are He besides whom there is no god, and none is worthy of worship but You. Whoever is under Your protection is secure; exalted is Your praise, holy are Your names, and great is Your bounty. There is no god besides You.

Your established way with Your creation has always been to test Your friends and those You love, as a grace and kindness from You towards them, so that they may remember You more in every circumstance and thank You for Your blessings through every change of fortune. Yet most people do not know.

“These are the parables We set forth for people, but none will understand them except those of knowledge.”

O Allah, You are the Knower who is not known, and You are the Generous One who is never miserly. You know, O Knower of what is hidden and what is manifest, that our hearts have always sincerely offered earnest supplication, and our tongues have voiced it in private and in public: that You answer us by granting this blessed and auspicious sultanic rule of al-Nāsir still greater eminence, dignity and strength; and that You fulfil our hopes for it by making the word of faith supreme, strengthening the foundations that uphold religion, and thwarting the schemes of the heretics. For this is a rule untainted by partiality and injustice, and free from the excesses of pen and sword.

And what lies within the hearts of Muslims and fills the innermost souls of believers is:

---

## Page 13292

that Sultan al-Malik al-Nāsir li-l-Dīn is among those of whom the Lord of all worlds, the God of the heavens and the earth—through whose granting of authority on His earth its kings and mighty sultans have gained their power—speaks in His mighty Book, which is recited. Let whoever wishes reflect:

“Those who, if We establish them in the land, establish prayer, give zakah, enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong.”

He is indeed among those whom Allah Most High has established in authority on earth: this is certain, not conjectural. He is among those meant by His words, Most High:

“Allah has promised those of you who believe and do righteous deeds that He will surely grant them succession in the land, as He granted it to those before them; that He will surely establish for them their religion, which He has approved for them; and that He will surely replace their fear with security. Among the gracious acts of mercy and compassionate care that Muslims have known and believers have grown accustomed to are the honouring of religious people and the esteem shown to Muslim scholars. What prompted the presentation of these explicit supplications to your august presence—although they have always been raised to Allah, Glorified is He, with sincere intention—is the saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Religion is sincerity.” He was asked, “Towards whom, O Messenger of Allah?” He replied, “Towards Allah, His Messenger, the leaders of the Muslims and their common people.”

And his saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Actions are judged only by intentions.”

These two hadiths are well known to be authentic and are widely transmitted throughout the Muslim community.

## Page 13293

Now this revered and illustrious shaykh, this honoured and noble imam, is unrivalled in his age and unique in his time, the ornament of the royal realm and the standard of the sultan’s state. Were anyone to swear by Allah, the Almighty, the All-Powerful, that this great imam has neither equal nor peer in his age, his oath would be true and require no expiation. The seven regions are devoid of his like, save this region. Every fair-minded person endowed with a sound disposition agrees with this.

I am not exaggerating in praising him. Indeed, however extensively someone might praise and commend him, he would not encompass even some of his virtues. Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah is a peerless pearl for which people vie, one to be bought but never sold. No pearl in the treasuries of kings matches or resembles it. All hope of finding his equal has vanished.

Hearing that Abū al-ʿAbbās—Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—had been taken up to the citadels has deafened ears and sapped the strength of leaders and followers alike. Nothing deserving reproach could come from a man like him, unless it involved a matter that had been obscured for him and he had been accused of the sort of thing of which men like him are accused.

It would be unfitting to burden your august presence with a lengthy account. If there is a qutb (spiritual pole) in this world, he is unquestionably that pole. Allah has placed the Sultan—may Allah exalt his standing—in this age in the position occupied by Joseph the truthful—may Allah bless our Prophet and him—when Allah turned the people of the lands towards him after drought had afflicted their lands and they needed the food stored with him. As for people’s present need for the nourishment of souls referred to at that time, there is no doubt

---

## Page 13294

that this nourishment consists of noble learning and subtle meanings. Within the Sultan’s dominions—may Allah Most High protect them—it had been dispensed to us in abundance, without charge: a magnificent gift from Allah to the Sultan and an immense blessing, for He had distinguished his kingdom and the territory under his rule with something found in no other region or land. Visitors had come from every province to those lands,

only to find that the keeper of the king’s measuring cup had been taken up to the citadels. Such a bounty could not be found elsewhere to be bought or sold. This coincided with a drought throughout the land and its surrounding regions, a drought that devastated its inhabitants until their desperate need for sustenance left them as though dead. Whoever suggested to the king that he impose restrictions on the keeper of his measuring cup, despite the pressing need for nourishment of the soul, may not have realized that this imam is among the greatest friends of Allah and the foremost righteous men. This is one of Satan’s incitements. Allah, Glorified is He, says:

“And tell My servants to say that which is best. Indeed, Satan sows discord among them. Indeed, Satan is ever a clear enemy to mankind.”

As for certain scholars’ disparagement of his fatwa and his answer concerning undertaking journeys to graves, the response of the scholars of these lands was conveyed to their fellow scholars and learned peers. All of them ruled that the answer he had given was correct.

## Page 13295

And it is evident to all that honoring this imam and treating him with reverence and respect sustains the kingdom, preserves the order of the state, strengthens the faith, draws forth supplications, confounds enemies, and humbles the people of religious innovation and vain desires. The Muslims’ very condition speaks in the words of the Most Great, the Most Exalted:

“So when they entered his presence, they said, ‘O exalted one, hardship has afflicted us and our families, and we have brought goods of little worth. So give us full measure and be charitable toward us. Indeed, Allah rewards the charitable.’”

The goods of little worth are these sheets inscribed with ink, and the benefit sought is the release of Shaykh al-Islām. What prompted this bold step was his saying, peace be upon him:

“Religion is sincere counsel.”

And peace be upon you. May Allah bless our master Muhammad and his pure, noble family, and grant them abundant peace. Here ends this letter.

I also came across “another letter” from Baghdad. It reads as follows:

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds, and blessings and peace be upon the noblest of the messengers, Muhammad the Prophet

## Page 13296

and all his family and Companions. O Allah, just as You have supported the kings of Islam and those in authority with strength and might, established their renown, made them a source of succor for the oppressed who seek their protection, and a means of restoration for the broken who seek shelter at their gates, so strengthen them, O Allah, through Your gracious aid. Increase their prosperity, elevate their standing, augment their honor, grant them victory over Your enemies, and bestow upon them rightly guided success and enduring authority.

To proceed: when news of the restrictions imposed upon Shaykh al-Islām Taqī al-Dīn Abū al-Abbās “Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah”—may Allah preserve him—reached the people of the eastern lands and the regions of Iraq, the Muslims were deeply distressed, and the devout greatly troubled. The heretics raised their heads, while the followers of vain desires and religious innovators rejoiced.

When the scholars of this region saw the gravity of this calamity—the people of religious innovation and vain desires gloating over the foremost men of virtue and leading scholars—they reported this dreadful and outrageous affair to the noble court of the Sultan, may Allah increase its honor. They wrote their responses affirming the correctness of the answers the shaykh—may Allah preserve him—had given in his fatwas, and mentioned some of the knowledge and virtues he possesses. They presented this to our lord, the king of the emirs—may Allah strengthen his supporters and redouble his emulation—out of protective concern for this religion and sincere counsel for Islam and the rulers of the believers.

## Page 13298

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—also said:

Section

A brief account drawing attention to the ignorance and falsehood contained in this work, despite its extreme brevity.

Before that, we shall quote “the wording of the answer,” so that what is mistaken and what is correct in the objections to it may become clear. The wording of the answer follows that of the question.

The question comes from someone seeking guidance. He asks about travelling to the graves of the prophets and the differing opinions and conflicting hadiths reported concerning this. He has heard these differing opinions and conflicting hadiths but does not know which reports are authentic and which are weak.

He asked:

“What do the esteemed scholars say about a man who intends ‘to visit the graves of the prophets and the righteous,’ such as our Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and others: is it permissible for him, during

---

## Page 13299

his journey, to shorten his prayers? And is this visit prescribed in Islam or not?

It has been reported that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

‘Whoever performs Hajj but does not visit me has slighted me.’

And:

‘Whoever visits me after my death, it is as though he visited me during my lifetime.’

It has also been reported that he said:

‘No journey should be undertaken except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque, al-Aqsā Mosque, and this mosque of mine.’”

The wording of the answer:

Praise be to Allah. Regarding someone who travels solely to visit the graves of the prophets and the righteous, there are two well-known views on whether he may shorten his prayers.

The first is that shortening the prayers on such a journey is not permissible, because it is a prohibited journey. This is the view of the earlier scholars who do not permit shortening prayers on a sinful journey and who regard this journey as sinful, such as Abū ‘Abd Allāh ibn Battah, Abū al-Wafā’ ibn ‘Aqīl, and many groups of earlier scholars. The position of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad is that prayers may not be shortened on a journey prohibited by Islamic law.

The second view is that prayers may be shortened on such a journey. This is held by those who permit shortening prayers on a prohibited journey, such as Abū Hanīfah. It is also held by some later followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad who permit travelling to visit the graves of the prophets and the righteous, such as Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī, Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī, and Abū al-Hasan ibn ‘Abdūs

---

## Page 13300

al-Harrānī.

These scholars say that such travel is not prohibited, citing the general wording of his statement:

“So visit the graves.”

Some people unfamiliar with hadith may cite reports concerning visiting the Prophet’s grave—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—such as his reported statement:

“Whoever visits me after my death, it is as though he visited me during my lifetime.”

This was narrated by al-Dāraqutnī. As for the statement some people attribute to him:

“Whoever performs Hajj but does not visit me has slighted me,”

none of the scholars narrated it. It is like the statement attributed to him:

“Whoever visits me and my father in the same year, I guarantee him Paradise from Allah.”

This, too, is baseless by scholarly consensus. Ahmad did not narrate it, nor has anyone cited it as evidence. Some of them merely cite al-Dāraqutnī’s hadith as evidence—the respondent subsequently added a marginal note to it.

However, although none of the scholars narrated this report in “books of jurisprudence and hadith,” whether as proof or as supporting evidence, and although some later scholars mentioned it, Abū Ahmad ibn ‘Adī did narrate it in his “Book of Weak Narrators” to demonstrate the weakness of its transmission. He cited it through al-Nu‘mān ibn Shibl al-Bāhilī al-Misrī, from Mālik, from Nāfi‘, from Ibn ‘Umar, who reported that the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever performs Hajj but does not visit me has slighted me.”

Ibn ‘Adī said: “No one other than this man narrated it from Mālik.” That is, it is known that this is not among Mālik’s hadiths, so it is clear that the flaw originated with this narrator.

Yūnus ibn Hārūn said: “This al-Nu‘mān was under suspicion.” Abū Hātim ibn Hibbān said: “He transmits

---

## Page 13301

outrageous reports on the authority of trustworthy narrators.”

Abū al-Faraj ibn al-Jawzī included this hadith in his Book of Fabricated Reports and narrated it through Abū Hātim ibn Hibbān:

“Ahmad ibn ‘Ubayd narrated to us: Muhammad ibn al-Nu‘mān narrated to us: My grandfather narrated to us, from Mālik.”

Abū al-Faraj then said: “Abū Hātim said, ‘Al-Nu‘mān transmits outrageous reports on the authority of trustworthy narrators.’ Al-Dāraqutnī said, ‘The fault in this hadith lies with Muhammad ibn Muhammad, not with Nu‘mān.’”

As for the other report:

“Whoever visits me and my father in the same year, I guarantee him Paradise from Allah,”

it appears in none of the books, whether with a fabricated chain of transmission or otherwise. It has been said that this was unheard of among Muslims until they conquered Jerusalem in the time of Saladin. Thus, none of the scholars mentioned either of these two reports, whether as supporting evidence or as a basis for their conclusions. This is unlike the report mentioned earlier, which a number of scholars cited and transmitted. It is known through Hafs ibn Sulaymān al-Ghādirī, the associate of ‘Āsim, from Layth ibn Abī Sulaym, from Mujāhid, from Ibn ‘Umar, who said that the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever performs Hajj and then visits me after my death, it is as though he visited me during my lifetime.”

Hadith scholars agree in criticizing this Hafs’s transmission of hadith, but not his Quranic recitation. Al-Bayhaqī said in Shu‘ab al-Īmān (Branches of Faith):

“Hafs ibn Abī Dāwūd—who is weak—narrated from Layth ibn Abī Sulaym, from Mujāhid, from

---

## Page 13302

Ibn ‘Umar, who said that the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

‘Whoever performs Hajj and then visits me after my death, it is as though he visited me during my lifetime.’”

Yahyā ibn Ma‘īn said of Hafs: “He is not trustworthy. His Quranic recitation is more accurate than that of Abū Bakr ibn ‘Ayyāsh, but Abū Bakr is more trustworthy than he is.” In another report from him: “Hafs was the more accomplished Quran reciter, but Abū Bakr was truthful, whereas Hafs was a liar.”

Al-Bukhārī said: “They abandoned him.” Muslim ibn al-Hajjāj said: “His reports are abandoned.” ‘Alī ibn al-Madīnī said: “He is weak in hadith; I deliberately stopped narrating from him.” Al-Nasā’ī said: “He is not trustworthy, and his hadiths are not to be recorded.” On another occasion, he said: “His reports are abandoned.” Sālih ibn Muhammad al-Baghdādī said: “His hadiths are not to be recorded; all his hadiths are rejected reports.”

Abū Zur‘ah said: “He is weak in hadith.” Abū Hātim al-Rāzī said: “His hadiths are not to be recorded. He is weak in hadith, is not to be believed, and his reports are abandoned.” ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Khirāsh said: “He is a liar whose reports are abandoned; he fabricates hadiths.” Al-Hākim Abū Ahmad said: “His hadith transmission is worthless.” Ibn ‘Adī said: “Most of the hadiths he transmits from his sources are not reliably preserved.”

There is another hadith on this subject, transmitted by al-Bazzār, al-Dāraqutnī and others through Mūsā ibn Hilāl: “‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar narrated to us, from Nāfi‘, from Ibn ‘Umar, who said that the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

‘Whoever visits my grave is assured of my intercession.’”

Al-Bayhaqī, after narrating this hadith, said: “It has also been reported from Mūsā, from ‘Abd Allāh.” He said: “Whether it is ‘Abd Allāh or ‘Ubayd Allāh,

---

## Page 13303

it is a rejected report from Nāfi‘, from Ibn ‘Umar; no one else has transmitted it.”

Al-‘Uqaylī said of Mūsā ibn Hilāl: “His narration of this hadith is not corroborated by other transmitters.” Abū Hātim al-Rāzī said: “He is unknown.”

Abū Zakariyyā al-Nawawī, in Sharh al-Muhadhdhab, cited Abū Ishāq’s statement:

“It is recommended to visit the grave of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—because of the report from Ibn ‘Umar that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said: ‘Whoever visits my grave is assured of my intercession.’”

Al-Nawawī said: “As for the hadith of Ibn ‘Umar, Abū Bakr al-Rāzī, al-Dāraqutnī and al-Bayhaqī narrated it through two very weak chains of transmission.”

The respondent continued his answer:

Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī argued that travelling to visit graves and mosques is permissible, citing the fact that the Prophet used to visit Qubā’ and visit graves. He answered the hadith, “Journeys must not be undertaken,” by interpreting it as negating the recommendation to do so.

Those who hold the former view, however, cite the report in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Journeys must not be undertaken except to three mosques: al-Masjid al-Harām, al-Masjid al-Aqsā, and this mosque of mine.”

The imams agree that this hadith is authentic and is to be acted upon. Thus, if a man vows to pray in a mosque or at a shrine, to observe a devotional retreat there, or to travel there—other than these three mosques—he is not obliged to fulfil that vow, according to the consensus of the imams. If he vows to travel or go to al-Masjid al-Harām for Hajj or ‘umrah, he is obliged to do so, according to the consensus of the scholars. If he vows to go to the Prophet’s Mosque

---

## Page 13304

—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—or to al-Masjid al-Aqsā for prayer or a devotional retreat, he is obliged to fulfil this vow according to Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī in one of his two opinions, and Ahmad. According to Abū Hanīfah, however, he is not obliged to do so, because, in his view, a vow creates an obligation only when the act belongs to a category already made obligatory by Islamic law.

The majority, however, hold that a vow to perform any act of obedience must be fulfilled, as established in Sahīh al-Bukhārī, where ‘Ā’ishah reported that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah, let him obey Him; and whoever vows to disobey Allah, let him not disobey Him.”

Travelling to these two mosques is an act of obedience; hence a vow to do so must be fulfilled. As for travelling to any place other than the three mosques, no scholar has held that such a journey becomes obligatory through a vow. Indeed, the scholars explicitly stated that one should not travel to the Mosque of Qubā’, because it is not one of the three, even though visiting the Mosque of Qubā’ is recommended for someone already in Medina. That does not involve undertaking a journey, as stated in the authentic hadith:

“Whoever purifies himself at home, then comes to the Mosque of Qubā’ intending only to pray there, it is equivalent to an ‘umrah.”

—In the margin: This hadith was narrated by the compilers of the Sunan collections, such as al-Nasā’ī, Ibn Mājah and al-Tirmidhī, who graded it good.

He said:

They also said: Moreover, travelling to visit the graves of the prophets and the righteous is an innovation that none of the Companions or their successors practised. The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—did not command it, nor did any of the imams of the Muslims deem it recommended. Whoever regards this as an act of worship and performs it is acting contrary to the Sunnah and the consensus of the imams. This is among the innovations contrary to the Sunnah that Abū ‘Abd Allāh ibn Battah mentioned in al-Ibānah al-Sughrā.

---

## Page 13305

This makes clear the weakness of Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī’s argument, for the Prophet’s visits—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—to the Mosque of Qubā’ did not involve undertaking a journey, and travelling there does not become obligatory through a vow. His claim that the Prophet’s words—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—“No journeys are to be undertaken” should be understood as merely denying that such travel is recommended admits of two responses.

The first is that this amounts to an acknowledgement on his part that such travel is neither a righteous deed, nor a means of drawing near to Allah, nor an act of obedience, nor a good deed. Thus, anyone who believes that travelling to visit the graves of the prophets and the righteous is a means of drawing near to Allah, an act of worship and an act of obedience has contradicted the consensus. If he travels because he believes it to be an act of obedience, that is forbidden by the consensus of the Muslims. The prohibition thus arises on these grounds. It is well known that no one travels to these graves except for this purpose. If, however, we suppose that a man travels to them for a permissible purpose, that is permissible and does not fall within the matter under discussion.

The second response is that this hadith conveys a prohibition, and a prohibition entails unlawfulness. As for the hadiths mentioned by the questioner concerning visiting the Prophet’s grave—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—they are all weak by agreement of the hadith scholars; indeed, they are fabricated.

None of the compilers of the authoritative Sunan collections included any of them, nor did any of the imams cite any of them as evidence. Indeed, Mālik, the imam of the people of the Prophet’s city, who are the most knowledgeable concerning the ruling on this matter, disapproved of a man saying:

“I visited the grave of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

---

## Page 13306

And had this wording been familiar to them, prescribed in religion, or transmitted from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—the scholar of Medina would not have disapproved of it.

When Imām Ahmad, the most knowledgeable person of his time concerning the Sunnah, was asked about this, the only hadith he had on which he could rely was the report of Abū Hurayrah that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“No man greets me with peace without Allah restoring my soul to me so that I may return his greeting.”

This was also the report on which Abū Dāwūd relied in his Sunan. Likewise, Mālik related in al-Muwatta’ that whenever ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar entered the mosque, he would say: “Peace be upon you, O Messenger of Allah. Peace be upon you, O Abū Bakr. Peace be upon you, my father.” Then he would leave.

In the Sunan of Abū Dāwūd, it is reported that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Do not make my grave a place of recurring gatherings. Send blessings upon me wherever you are, for your blessings reach me.”

In the Sunan of Sa‘īd ibn Mansūr, it is related that ‘Abd Allāh ibn al-Hasan ibn al-Husayn saw a man repeatedly visiting the grave of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He said: “The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

‘Do not make my grave a place of recurring gatherings. Send blessings upon me wherever you are, for your blessings reach me.’

In this regard, you and those in al-Andalus are equal.”

In the two Sahīh collections, it is reported that during his final illness the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

He was warning against what they had done. ‘Ā’ishah said: “Were it not for that, his grave would have been in the open, but he did not want it to be made a place of worship.” They buried him in ‘Ā’ishah’s chamber, contrary to what

---

## Page 13307

they were accustomed to—burial in open ground—so that no one would pray at his grave and turn it into a place of worship, thereby making his grave an idol.

In the days of the Companions and Successors, while the “Prophet’s chamber” remained separate from the mosque—until the time of al-Walīd ibn ‘Abd al-Malik—no one would enter it to pray there, rub the grave, or supplicate there. Rather, they did all of this only in the mosque. When the early generations among the Companions and Successors greeted the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and wished to supplicate, they would face the qiblah, not the grave.

As for a Muslim standing at his grave, Abū Hanīfah said: “He should face the qiblah here too, not the grave.” Most of the imams, however, said: “Rather, he should face the grave only when greeting him with peace.” None of the imams said that one should face the grave when supplicating—that is, when supplicating for oneself—except in a fabricated account attributed to Mālik, whose school holds the contrary. The imams agreed that one should neither touch the Prophet’s grave—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—nor kiss it.

All of this is to safeguard monotheism, for turning graves into places of worship is among the origins of associating partners with Allah. Thus, concerning the words of Allah Most High:

“And they said, ‘Do not abandon your gods; do not abandon Wadd, Suwā‘, Yaghūth, Ya‘ūq, or Nasr.’”

A group of the early generations said: “These were righteous men

---

## Page 13308

among the people of Noah. When they died, the people devoted themselves to their graves, then made images of them. After a long time had passed, they began to worship them.”

Al-Bukhārī mentioned part of this account in his Sahīh, and also related Ibn ‘Abbās’s statement: “These idols passed to the Arabs.” Ibn Jarīr al-Tabarī and others related this in their Quran commentaries from several of the early generations. Others related it through several chains of transmission in “Stories of the Prophets.” I have discussed these matters at length elsewhere.

The first to fabricate these hadiths about travelling to visit shrines built over graves were the people of religious innovation—among the Rāfidah and others—who neglect mosques and venerate shrines, where partners are associated with Allah, truth is denied, and a religion for which Allah has granted no authority is invented. The Quran and Sunnah mention mosques, not shrines. As Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘My Lord has commanded justice. Direct your faces toward Him at every place of prayer, and call upon Him with sincere devotion.’”

And He says:

“And the mosques belong to Allah, so do not invoke anyone alongside Allah.”

And He says:

“The mosques of Allah are only to be maintained by those who believe in Allah and the Last Day, establish prayer, and give zakah.”

And Allah Most High says:

“And do not have sexual relations with them while you are in devotional retreat in the mosques.”

And Allah Most High says:

“And who is more unjust than one who prevents Allah’s name from being mentioned in His mosques and strives to destroy them?”

It is authentically reported that he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—used to say: “Indeed, those before you used to take graves as places of worship. Beware! Do not take graves as places of worship,

---

## Page 13309

for I forbid you to do so.” And Allah Most High knows best.

These are the respondent’s exact words. Let the reader reflect on what they contain, on what these opponents quoted from the answer in challenging it, and on what they claimed was false. Are they truthful and correct in either respect, or is the opposite the case?

The respondent gave this answer ten-odd years ago, according to the questioner’s circumstances and his request for guidance. He did not discuss the matter at length or name everyone who held each of the two views, but confined himself to what was possible at the time. Otherwise, these two views are found in many works on the schools of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad, as well as in hadith commentaries and elsewhere.

The view that travelling to any place other than the three mosques is forbidden—even if it is the grave of our Prophet Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—is the position of Mālik and the majority of his followers, and likewise of most of Ahmad’s followers. They understand the hadith as prohibiting travel to places other than the three mosques. Some of them, however, say that our Prophet’s grave does not fall within its general scope.

There are two grounds for this view. The first is that travelling to his grave is travelling to his mosque. This is the correct basis, and it accords with the position of Mālik and the majority of his followers.

The second basis is that our Prophet is not to be likened to other believers, as stated by

---

## Page 13310

a group of Ahmad’s followers, who held that one may swear by him even though swearing by created beings is forbidden. This is one view reported from Ahmad.

Some of his followers held that, in these two matters, the same ruling applies to the other prophets as to him: some said this concerning swearing by them, and others concerning visiting their graves. Likewise, Abū Muhammad al-Juwaynī and those Shāfiʿī scholars who agreed with him held that the hadith entails a prohibition on travelling to places other than the three mosques.

Other followers of al-Shāfiʿī, Mālik, and Ahmad said that the hadith means that such travel has no particular merit, is not recommended, and does not become obligatory through a vow—not that it is impermissible. This is the view of Shaykh Abū Hāmid, Abū ʿAlī, Abū al-Maʿālī, al-Ghazālī, and others. It is also the view of Ibn ʿAbd al-Barr, Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī, and those followers of Mālik and Ahmad who agreed with them.

These, then, are the two views found in Muslim scholarly works. The respondent mentioned both and knew of no recognized Muslim scholar who had stated in these works that travelling to visit the graves of the prophets and the righteous is recommended. Had he known of a third view on the matter, he would have reported it. But he knew of none, and to this day he knows of no one who has said this. Many scholars, however, have stated without qualification that visiting the Prophet’s grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is recommended, and some have reported a consensus on this. The respondent did not mention any disagreement over this in his answer, for it is well known, on the basis of explicit textual evidence and consensus, that travelling to the Prophet’s mosque, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is recommended. Thus, anyone travelling to his grave, if knowledgeable about Islamic law, must intend to travel to

---

## Page 13311

his mosque. Such travel therefore falls outside the scope of the answer to the question, for the answer concerned only those who travel solely to visit their graves. Someone knowledgeable about Islamic law would not do this: he knows that the Messenger recommended travelling to his mosque and praying there, and he is himself travelling to that mosque. How, then, could he not intend to travel there? Anyone who knowingly acts of his own free choice must necessarily intend what he does. Such an intention can be absent only through ignorance: either ignorance that travelling to his mosque is recommended because it is his mosque, not because of the grave, or ignorance that the traveller’s destination is in fact his mosque. But if he knows both these things, he must necessarily intend to travel to his mosque. Thus, visiting his grave differs in its rulings from visiting other graves in several respects, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

People of ignorance and misguidance, however, treat travelling to visit him like their customary journeys to visit the grave of someone they venerate. They travel there to invoke him, supplicate at his grave, go in to his grave, and sit beside it. A mosque may have been built over or beside the grave for its sake, and they pray in that mosque out of veneration for the person buried there. This is among the practices for which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, cursed the People of the Book and which he forbade his community to follow. During his final illness, he said:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians; they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

This is reported in both Sahīh collections through several routes of transmission. Five days before his death, he said:

“Those who came before you used to take the graves of their prophets and righteous people as places of worship. Beware! Do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you

---

## Page 13312

from doing so.” Reported by Muslim.

Whoever fails to distinguish between what is prescribed and what is forbidden in visiting graves does not understand the religion of Islam in this matter. The purpose here is to draw attention to the contents of this work, written by the objector against the aforementioned response, and to expose its ignorance and fabrications.

Among these is his statement concerning the response:

“What has become clear to me from the explicit wording of that discussion, its implications, its intended purpose and its thrust, is that he prohibits visiting the graves of the prophets and all other graves, and travelling to them, and claims that this is a forbidden act of disobedience whose prohibition is established by consensus.”

The reply is as follows:

Anyone who has read the response knows that it contains no prohibition on visiting graves—whether those of the prophets or anyone else—when no journey is involved. Nor does it claim a consensus on the prohibition of travelling; rather, it explicitly acknowledges the disagreement over that.

How, then, can he be reported as saying that visiting graves in itself, without qualification, is a forbidden act of disobedience whose prohibition is established by consensus? This is a blatant fabrication against the response. Moreover, the objector contradicts himself: after this, he reports that the respondent acknowledged the disagreement over the permissibility of travelling.

Then, at the end of his discussion, he says that the respondent claimed there was consensus on its prohibition, and that he contradicts himself in this. Yet it is the objector himself who contradicts himself in this account. As for the respondent,

---

## Page 13313

he reported their views on the permissibility of travelling and their agreement that it is neither an act of devotion nor an act of obedience to Allah. Whoever believes it to be an act of devotion has therefore opposed the consensus. If he undertakes it believing it to be an act of obedience, it is forbidden by consensus. Its prohibition thus arises from treating it as an act of devotion. This is the wording of the response.

It is well known that whenever Muslims disagree over whether an action is forbidden or merely permissible—not an act of devotion—whoever treats it as an act of devotion has opposed the consensus. If he performs it to draw nearer to Allah, doing so is forbidden by consensus. Examples include seeking nearness to Allah by playing backgammon or chess, exchanging one dirham for two, having anal intercourse with women, listening to singing and musical instruments, and similar actions concerning which people hold two views: prohibition and permissibility. No one has said that these are acts of devotion. Thus, whoever makes such an action an act of worship through which he seeks nearness to Allah, just as he does through prescribed acts of worship, has committed something forbidden by consensus.

This resembles seeking nearness to Allah through amusements and musical instruments. The majority of Muslims hold that these are forbidden, while some have permitted them; no one has said that they are acts of devotion. Whoever says this therefore opposes the consensus. Only a heretic would say such a thing—as in the account Abū Abd al-Rahmān al-Sulamī related from Ibn al-Rāwandī, who said: “The jurists disagree over whether singing is forbidden or lawful, but I say that it is obligatory.” It is well known that this is not one of the positions held by Muslim scholars.

Those who seek nearness to Allah by listening to poems, taghbīr, and the like are mistaken according to the great majority of the imams, even though none of these people says that singing is an act of devotion

---

## Page 13314

without qualification. Rather, they say this in a particular case, concerning certain religious people who use such listening to move their hearts towards obedience. Through it, they stir feelings of love and eagerness to obey Allah, as well as sorrow, fear, and dread of disobedience. This is what some people describe as an act of devotion. Nevertheless, the majority hold that they would be mistaken if they treated it as an act of devotion, because it is an innovation that is neither obligatory nor recommended, and because its harms outweigh the benefits they imagine it brings. The same applies to wine and gambling: although they contain benefits for people, their sin is greater than their benefit. The Sharia enjoins what is wholly or predominantly beneficial, such as faith and jihad. Faith is wholly beneficial; jihad, although it involves the taking of lives, brings benefits that outweigh its harms.

The tribulation of disbelief causes greater harm than killing, as Allah Most High says:

“Persecution is more grievous than killing.”

He has forbidden what is wholly or predominantly harmful, just as He has forbidden shameful deeds, whether open or concealed, and “sin, unjust aggression, associating with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority, and saying about Allah what you do not know.” He never permits these things under any circumstances or in any religious dispensation.

The prohibition of blood, carrion, pork, wine, and similar things concerns matters whose harm predominates. The Sharia permits this category in cases of necessity, because the harm of losing one’s life is greater than the harm of consuming them. The jurists disagreed over whether wine may be drunk to relieve thirst only because they disagreed over

---

## Page 13315

whether it quenches thirst. Those who prohibit it say that it only increases the drinker’s thirst and therefore does not sustain life. Those who permit it say that it may, on the contrary, provide enough hydration to sustain life. Whichever of these two premises accords with reality, the view based upon it is the more correct. A fuller discussion belongs elsewhere.

The point is that when scholars disagree over whether something is forbidden or permissible, anyone who regards it as an act of devotion contradicts their consensus. Likewise, when the Companions hold two positions on a matter, anyone who introduces a third contradicts their consensus.

For this reason, no Muslim has held that listening to singing is an act of devotion without qualification. Even if someone says that listening to words—subject to the conditions of a suitable place, opportunity, and companions, and consisting of encouragement to obey Allah and warnings against disobedience—is an act of devotion, he never says that everyone who listens to musical entertainment is thereby drawing closer to Allah. Yet someone similarly claims that travelling to the graves of prophets and righteous people is an act of devotion, and that anyone who vows to travel to visit their graves should fulfil that vow. Such a view is not known to have been held by any of the leading scholars of the Muslims.

Although they may state in general terms that travelling to visit the Prophet’s grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is an act of devotion, or say that it is an act of devotion established by consensus, this is true when their intended meaning is understood, as al-Qādī ‘Iyād, Ibn Battāl, and others have explained. They mean the prescribed journey to his mosque and the prescribed worship performed there, which is called visiting his grave. Mālik and others, however, dislike calling it a visit to his grave. Thus, this consensus

---

## Page 13316

in this sense is undoubtedly valid. But this is not a consensus in favour of what they expressly prohibited or declared not to be an act of devotion or obedience.

Mālik and others, such as al-Qādī Ismā‘īl and al-Qādī ‘Iyād, expressly stated that travelling to places other than the three mosques is prohibited: neither someone who has vowed to undertake it nor someone doing so voluntarily should do it. They also expressly stated that travelling to Medina or Jerusalem for a purpose other than praying in their two mosques falls within the prohibited journeys. A person may not undertake such a journey even if he has vowed to do so, whether he travels to visit any prophet, any of their graves, the graves of others, or a mosque other than the three. All of this, according to them, constitutes prohibited travel. How, then, could they call it an act of devotion?

However, consensus that it is forbidden to treat it as an act of devotion does not contradict disagreement over the act considered in itself. This consensus, reported from the early generations and the leading scholars, is not undermined by dissent from some later scholars, if any such dissent exists. If, however, any of the recognized righteous people of the early generations were found to have said that travelling solely to visit graves, or solely to visit the graves of prophets and righteous people, is recommended, this would undermine that consensus, and there would then be three positions on the matter. In that case, the scholar reporting the consensus would simply have been unaware of this view, as frequently happens to many scholars. Even so, this view must be referred back to the Quran and Sunnah for judgment. It is not permissible to compel people to follow it without proof, for doing so is contrary to the consensus of the Muslims.

---

## Page 13317

Section

Among these is his assumption that visiting the grave of the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is of the same kind as the customary visitation of other people’s graves. He therefore cites visits to al-Baqīʿ, to the martyrs of Uhud, and to his mother’s grave as evidence for it. Another is that he portrays anyone who prohibits travelling to visit his grave or other graves as openly declaring hostility to the prophets and displaying defiance towards them.

Yet it is well known that this is the position of most earlier scholars, including Mālik and most of his followers, Abū Muhammad al-Juwaynī and other followers of al-Shāfiʿī, and most of Ahmad’s earlier followers. His position thus entails that his own imam, Mālik, and other leading religious scholars openly declare hostility to the prophets and defy them.

Even if he had said this about matters in which they were mistaken, he would deserve severe punishment. How much more so when he says it about matters in which they followed the Messenger and his authentic Sunnah, prohibiting what he prohibited! He has thus portrayed someone who obeys Allah and His Messenger, and whose conduct Allah, His Messenger, and His prophets approve, as openly declaring hostility to them and defying them. Concerning the unbelief of someone whom Allah and His Messenger have judged to be a believer.

Someone like this should have the correct position explained to him, and be shown that this position is what was brought by

---

## Page 13318

the Messenger and upheld by the earliest Muslims and the community’s imams, as attested by the Book and the Sunnah. If it becomes clear to him that this is what the Messenger brought, yet he persists in opposing the Messenger and following a path other than that of the believers, he must be called upon to repent; if he does not repent, he is to be put to death.
