Likewise, if it becomes clear that this position does not constitute disbelief, but is one that Muslims agree is permissible to hold, and that its proponent exercises independent scholarly judgment and is rewarded for that effort, whether right or wrong—then, if he persists in declaring someone an unbeliever when the Book, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus establish that this person is not an unbeliever, and it becomes clear to him that he is committing disbelief, yet he persists in opposing the Messenger and following a path other than that of the believers, he must be called upon to repent; if he does not repent, he is to be put to death. This is like someone who considers belief that the Messiah is Allah’s servant to be hostility towards the Messiah, or believes that anyone who says, “Do not swear by the prophets,” has thereby shown hostility towards them and become an unbeliever. Such a person must be called upon to repent.

Another point is that his own imam, Mālik, and the majority of Mālik’s followers explicitly addressed this issue. It appears in their books, both large and small, yet he is unfamiliar with what they said. Rather, he declares an unbeliever, curses, and insults anyone who holds the very position they held. His position thus entails declaring them unbelievers, reviling them, and deeming their blood lawful to shed.

Another point is his statement:

“Authentic hadiths have been transmitted concerning visiting his grave, along with others that do not reach the degree of authenticity, but which may nevertheless be cited as evidence for rulings

---

## Page 13319

of Islamic law.” This is the statement of someone who knows neither what has been narrated on this subject nor what Muslim scholars have said about it. Rather, he is like a Rāfidī (rejectionist Shiite) who says:

“Authentic hadiths have been narrated explicitly designating Alī as the imam after the Messenger of Allah, along with others of a lesser grade.”

It is well known that none of the hadiths mentioning visitation to his grave were included by the compilers of the authentic hadith collections, the authoritative Sunan collections—such as those of Abū Dāwūd and al-Tirmidhī—or comparable Musnad collections, such as the Musnad of Ahmad. Nor has any imam cited any of them as evidence. Moreover, he has not mentioned even a single such hadith, let alone identified the book in which it appears.

As for his statement that those reports which do not reach the degree of authenticity may nevertheless be cited as evidence, this would apply only if they were graded good according to those who divide hadith into three categories. This depends on knowing that they merit that grade. Yet the leading hadith scholars have not judged them to be so, and neither he nor others like him possess such knowledge.

Such an assertion is among the gravest instances of speaking about religion without knowledge and presumptuously tampering with the Sunnah of the Messenger of the Lord of all worlds: introducing into it, through ignorance and misguidance, what does not belong to it. How much worse, then, when everything narrated on this subject has been judged weak by those knowledgeable in hadith—indeed, they have judged it to be fabricated falsehood—as has been discussed at length elsewhere than in this book.

Another point is that he has failed to distinguish between the “religiously prescribed visitation” which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to perform, and whose purpose is to supplicate for the deceased, as in the funeral prayer for him

---

## Page 13320

and what the misguided have innovated: associating the deceased with Allah in worship, making pilgrimage to his grave, and invoking him besides Allah. Such a person’s purpose in visiting the deceased and journeying to him is to invoke him besides Allah, not to supplicate for him. The Messenger never practiced this kind of visitation, nor did he ever permit it. How much worse, then, is journeying for this purpose, which is akin to making pilgrimage to false objects of worship!

Another point is that he equated visiting the deceased with visiting him during his lifetime, citing the hadith about “the man who visited a brother of his while the latter was alive” as evidence that visiting the deceased is recommended. No such equation or analogy is known to have been made by any Muslim scholar.

It is well known that no one, through virtuous deeds such as jihad and Hajj, can equal the Companions who journeyed to the Messenger, supported him, heard his words, spoke with him, and asked him questions which he answered; whom he taught and instructed in proper conduct; and whom he entrusted with messages for their people and commanded to convey his teachings. How, then, could someone equal them merely by seeing the exterior of his chamber? Or how could this visitation be compared with theirs?

The Sunnah and the consensus of the Muslim community establish that all the righteous deeds performed in the mosque beside his chamber—sending blessings and peace upon him, praising and honoring him, and recounting his admirable qualities and virtues—can be performed anywhere else, with those performing them receiving the reward they deserve. As he said:

“Do not make my house a place of recurring gatherings, and send blessings upon me, for your blessings reach me wherever you may be.”

Had deeds performed at the grave possessed any special merit, he would have opened the door of the chamber to the Muslims. Since, then, they were prevented from reaching the grave

---

## Page 13321

and were commanded to worship in the mosque, it is clear that the special merit of deeds performed there derives from their being performed in his mosque, just as one prayer in his mosque is worth a thousand prayers elsewhere. He—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—never commanded that any righteous deed be deliberately performed at his grave.

Another point is his blatant fabrications against the respondent in numerous passages. His reason for fabricating these allegations is that the respondent cited the views of Muslim scholars and favored the position of Mālik and others among the early generations, because the authentic, explicit Sunnah of Allah’s Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—supports their position. Such conduct entails enmity toward Allah and His Messenger, for whoever opposes his Sunnah, his law, and his religion has opposed him. Whoever bears enmity toward someone on that account is, in reality, bearing enmity toward the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—even if he does not intend this. How, then, can repeated lies and fabrications be permissible when their falsehood is plain? Even if the respondent were mistaken, this would not be permissible, for lying and fabrication are forbidden without exception. Allah has required everyone to be truthful and just toward everyone else, in every circumstance.

How much worse, then, when the views cited by the respondent are those of people who follow the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—while the objector disparages them and their statements, insults those who believe in the Messenger, obey him, and follow him, and declares them unbelievers for the very things that constitute following the Messenger and believing in him! This statement of his entails enmity toward the Messenger, toward what he brought, and toward those who follow him, even if he is unaware of what his statement entails. Thus, despite his lack of knowledge, his statement is of the same kind as the statements of those who oppose Allah and His Messenger and ally themselves with the people of

---

## Page 13322

falsehood and polytheism who resemble Christians and their like, even though they do not realize that their statements entail this, owing to their limited knowledge, poor understanding, and lack of the qualifications needed for independent scholarly judgment, reasoning from scriptural evidence, and knowledge of what the leading religious scholars have said. Indeed, on a momentous issue such as this, they make statements whose speaker is in greater need of being called to repent, disciplined, taught, and helped to understand than of being refuted or engaged in debate. Similarly, among the ignorant adherents of religious innovations—the Rāfidah, the Kharijites, and others—are those who hasten to declare unbelievers those among the early generations who followed the Messenger, owing to their limited knowledge and poor understanding of what the Messenger brought. Thus, they adopt religious innovations out of ignorance and declare those who disagree with them unbelievers.

Those who adhere to the Sunnah and possess knowledge and faith, however, recognize the truth, follow the Messenger’s Sunnah, show mercy to people, treat them justly, and excuse those who strive to discover the truth but are unable to ascertain it. They condemn only those whom Allah and His Messenger condemn: those who neglect to seek the truth, thereby abandoning a duty, and those who transgress by following their desires without knowledge, thereby committing what is forbidden. Thus, they condemn anyone who neglects a duty or commits what is forbidden, but they do not punish him until the proof has been established against him, as the Most High says:

“We never punish until We have sent a messenger.”

This is especially so in matters over which scholars have differed and concerning which knowledge is obscure to most people. And anyone who does not speak in the manner of the people of

---

## Page 13323

knowledge, but instead speaks recklessly without knowledge, is unfit for debate except in the manner in which one debates ignorant laypeople who follow religious innovations and resemble the polytheists and Christians. They regard anyone who speaks the truth about a created being as reviling and insulting him, while they themselves revile, insult, and offend Allah. They do not fear reviling and insulting the Creator and associating partners with Him as they fear speaking the truth about a created being. As Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, said to them:

“How could I fear what you associate with Him, while you do not fear associating with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority to you? Which of the two parties has more right to security, if you know?”

“Those who believe and do not taint their faith with wrongdoing—it is they who will have security, and they are rightly guided.”

Likewise, the Most High says of the polytheists:

“When the disbelievers see you, they only ridicule you, saying, ‘Is this the one who speaks against your gods?’ Yet they themselves disbelieve in the mention of the Most Merciful.”

They are not as angered by false statements about the Most Merciful as they are by truthful statements about their gods.

The Most High also says:

“O People of the Scripture, do not go to extremes in your religion, and say nothing about Allah except the truth. The Messiah, Jesus son of Mary, was only a messenger of Allah, His word which He conveyed to Mary, and a spirit from Him. So believe in Allah and His messengers, and do not say, ‘Three.’ Desist—it is better for you. Allah is only One God. Exalted is He above having a son! To Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on earth. And Allah is sufficient as a Guardian.”

“The Messiah would never disdain to be a servant of Allah, nor would the angels brought near to Him.”

The Quran commentators have related: “The Christians—the Christians of Najrān—

---

## Page 13324

when they came to the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), said, ‘O Muhammad, have you not mentioned our companion?’ He asked, ‘Who is your companion?’ They replied, ‘Jesus.’ He said, ‘What should I say of him? He is Allah’s servant.’ They said, ‘Rather, he is Allah.’ He replied, ‘It is no disgrace for him to be a servant of Allah.’ They said, ‘Yes, it is.’ So Allah revealed this verse.”

In both Sahīh collections, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) is reported to have said:

“No one is more patient than Allah with the offensive things he hears: they ascribe to Him a child and a partner, yet He grants them well-being and provides for them.”

It is also reported in both Sahīh collections that he said:

“Allah says: ‘The son of Adam insults Me, though he has no right to do so, and the son of Adam calls Me a liar, though he has no right to do so. His insult against Me is his claim that I have taken a child, whereas I am the One, the Self-Sufficient, who neither begets nor was begotten, and none is equal to Me. His calling Me a liar is his statement, “He will not bring me back as He first created me,” although creating him the first time is no easier for Me than bringing him back.’”

Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal used to say of the Christians: “Do not show them mercy, for they have insulted Allah as no other human beings have.”

These people disparage the Creator, yet resent a created being being described as he deserves. They regard this as disparaging him, whereas it merely gives him his due and lowers him from the rank of divinity, which none but Allah deserves. Such is also the condition of those who resemble them in certain respects.

Another is his assumption that any act of devotion may be pursued by any means.

---

## Page 13325

and this is among the most obvious errors.

Another is his assumption that no scholar has held such travel to be forbidden; rather, that the respondent—if his report is accurate—has attributed this view only to someone who cannot be relied upon and whose dissent carries no weight. Yet this is Mālik’s explicit statement concerning the Messenger’s grave in particular, and it is the position of the majority of his followers and of the early generations and scholars.

Another is his claim that, apart from those whose views the respondent cited—al-Ghazālī, Ibn ‘Abdūs and Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī—no one’s dissent carries any weight, and no one else should be consulted on this matter. Such a statement cannot be made even about any of the great imams. Rather, everyone’s statements may be accepted or rejected, except those of the bearer of the revealed Law. How, then, can such a statement be justified concerning people like these?

Another is that, when he sought to establish that the Prophet hears invocations from nearby and is informed of blessings and greetings of peace sent from afar, he did not mention the good hadiths on this subject found in the Sunan collections. Instead, he relied solely on a fabricated hadith:

“Whoever invokes blessings upon me at my grave, I hear it; and whoever invokes blessings upon me from afar, I am informed of it.”

This is narrated only by Muhammad ibn Marwān al-Suddī from al-A‘mash. He is unanimously regarded as a liar, and they unanimously agree that this hadith was fabricated in al-A‘mash’s name. Moreover, he has altered its wording. For the copy I saw had the following wording, marked as correct:

“And whoever

---

## Page 13326

invokes blessings upon me from afar, I hear it.” Yet its actual wording is: “I am informed of it.” This is also how al-Qādī ‘Iyād cited it from the Musnad of Ibn Abī Shaybah, and it was from him that he took it. Anyone who relies on such a fabricated hadith while disregarding the good hadiths recorded by the compilers of the Sunan collections is far removed indeed from people of knowledge and understanding.

If he has also distorted its wording, this is darkness upon darkness, akin to what the heretics did with the report:

“When Allah first created the intellect, He said to it, ‘Come forward,’ and it came forward”—and so on.

This is a fabricated lie. Yet they also distorted its wording, reading awwalu, with the nominative ending, whereas its wording is awwala mā khalaqa (“when He first created”), with the accusative ending marking an adverbial expression, as in the other reported wording, “when He created.”

Another error is his appeal to the consensus of the early and later generations concerning visiting his grave. He assumed that the response prohibited something upon which there was consensus. Yet the response explicitly stated that travelling to his mosque is an act of obedience by consensus. The same applies to the recommended acts it discussed that fall under what is called visiting his grave: invoking blessings and peace upon him; praying that he be granted al-Wasīlah and other blessings; bearing witness in his favor; praising him for the distinctions Allah has bestowed upon him; loving him, maintaining allegiance to him, supporting him and honoring him; and other acts that may fall within the meaning of visiting. All of this is recommended, and the respondent explicitly affirms that it is recommended.

The scholars have differed over whether this is to be called a visit. He mentioned their disagreements concerning those matters over which they differed, and their consensus concerning those upon which they agreed. Thus, he stated the permissibility of travelling to his mosque and visiting his grave insofar as this is established by the revealed texts and consensus, and mentioned some of what

---

## Page 13327

was disputed in this regard. This man assumed that travelling to visit our Prophet is like travelling to visit other prophets and righteous people. This is mistaken for several reasons.

First: His mosque is beside his grave, and travelling to it is prescribed by the revealed texts and consensus, unlike the case of others.

Second: It is not possible to visit him as others are visited. A person can only reach his mosque and perform there the acts prescribed for him.

Third: If our Prophet’s grave were to be visited as other graves are visited, the residents of his city would be the people most entitled to do so, just as the residents of every city are most entitled to visit the righteous people buried among them. Yet the early generations and the leading scholars of the religion agreed that the residents of his city do not visit his grave, nor even stand beside it to offer greetings of peace when entering or leaving the mosque—even if this is not called a visit. Rather, doing so outside the context of travel is disliked for them, as Mālik stated, explaining that it is among the innovations that the earliest generations of this community did not practise. It follows that whoever considers visiting his grave prescribed in the same manner as visiting another person’s grave has contradicted the consensus of the Muslims.

Fourth: He forbade making his grave a place of recurring gatherings, and commanded his community to invoke blessings and peace upon him wherever they might be, informing them that this reaches him. Thus, singling out that place for supplication on his behalf is not prescribed. Rather, supplication is made on his behalf in all places and at every

---

## Page 13328

call to prayer, during every prayer, and upon entering and leaving any mosque, unlike anyone else. This is because of his exalted standing and elevated rank. Allah has distinguished him with an excellence shared by no one else, so that his grave should not be treated like other graves. Rather, a distinction is drawn between them in several respects, making clear his superiority over others and the blessings Allah has bestowed upon his community.

Another point is his statement:

“It does not follow from his claim that there is consensus on its prohibition that the noble Companions, together with the Successors and the subsequent scholars qualified to exercise independent judgment, were violating consensus, persisting in endorsing what is forbidden, committing through their own actions and legal rulings what it is impermissible to undertake, agreeing upon misguidance, and following the path of blindness and ignorance.”

These words display ignorance of the Sharia and of what the Muslims have agreed upon, and equate worship of the Most Merciful—which the believers unanimously uphold—with worship of idols—which they unanimously prohibit. These and other aspects demonstrate that this statement contains various forms of opposition to the religion of Islam. If its author understood what he said and its implications, he would be an apostate who must be put to death. But he is ignorant and may speak of what he does not grasp, while grasping its implications. Thus, he and others like him—who suppose that the answer contains something contrary to consensus—are to be told:

---

## Page 13329

What the Muslims, earlier and later generations alike, have unanimously agreed upon, century after century, is travelling to his mosque—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and invoking blessings and peace upon him there, and similar deeds beloved to Allah and His Messenger. These deeds entail worshipping Allah alone and fulfilling the rights of His Messenger, and are among the finest acts of worship offered to Allah, as are our testimony to his messengership and our praise of him. Invoking blessings and peace upon him is among the finest ways in which we worship Allah. This and similar practices are what is prescribed in his mosque, whether or not they are called visiting his grave.

Neither the expression “visiting his grave” nor the recommendation of doing so is known to have come from any of the Companions. Rather, what is transmitted from Ibn ʿUmar and those who followed his practice is greeting him there with peace and invoking blessings upon him. They did not call this visiting his grave. What, then, of those who never stood at the grave at all—the majority of the Companions?

As for the polytheistic practices and religious innovations that some people have introduced and called “visiting his grave,” these belong to the same category as the innovated visits made to other people’s graves, not to visits prescribed by the Sharia. As for practices that fall within what the Sharia prescribes, these are recommended by his authentically established Sunnah and the consensus of his community.

Yet some leading scholars do not call this “visiting his grave”; indeed, they dislike this designation, let alone describe it as travelling to his grave. Those who have expressed this view, such as Mālik and others, have explicitly stated that if a person travelling there

---

## Page 13330

has the grave as his intended destination, his journey is prohibited and falls within the Prophet’s saying:

“No journey is to be undertaken except to three mosques.”

They also state that a journey constituting obedience to Allah and a means of drawing near to Him is one undertaken for the purpose of praying in the mosque; and that if someone vows to travel to Medina for a purpose other than praying in the mosque, he is forbidden to fulfil his vow, because it is a vow involving disobedience. Since this position of theirs is well known in works both short and lengthy, how can anyone suppose that the leading scholars unanimously agree on travelling solely to visit graves?

Another group of scholars call this visiting his grave. They say, “It is recommended to visit his grave,” or, “to travel to visit his grave.” By visiting, they mean what the first group means: travelling to his mosque and performing there the prescribed acts of invoking blessings and peace upon him, supplicating for him, and praising him. This is what they call visiting his grave, although all agree that no one visits his grave in the manner familiar with other graves. Those graves are in the open and can be approached, and one can sit or stand beside them. It is possible to perform beside them both prescribed acts, such as supplicating for the deceased and seeking forgiveness for him, and prohibited acts, such as calling upon him, associating him with Allah in worship, wailing at his grave, and uttering laments. This is what is understood by “visiting graves.”

The Messenger was buried in his house, in his chamber, and he prohibited people from entering there and gaining access to his grave. Thus, no one can visit his grave as one visits another person’s grave, whether in a manner prescribed by the Sharia or an innovated manner. Rather, everyone can reach only

---

## Page 13331

his mosque, where they perform acts that are prescribed for them or disapproved. Travel to his mosque, being prescribed, is unanimously regarded as an act of obedience to Allah and a means of drawing near to Him. It is this on which the Muslims are unanimous. The respondent has stated in many places that this journey is recommended, and that its recommendation is established by scriptural evidence and consensus. He has also stated this in the present answer, explaining what is established by scriptural evidence and consensus concerning travel to his mosque and visiting him in the manner prescribed by the Sharia. He has also explained what is not prescribed concerning travel to visit the graves of others among the prophets and the righteous. For travelling to those places is not travelling to a mosque to which travel has been prescribed. Indeed, even if the mosques there are ones whose construction and use for prayer are prescribed—such as the Muslims’ congregational mosques in their cities—travelling to them is neither a means of drawing near to Allah nor an act of obedience, according to either the four imams or the Muslim imams generally.

All agree that travelling to them falls within his saying:

“No journey is to be undertaken except to three mosques.”

This is an exceptive construction in which the general term is left unstated. There are two possible ways of supplying it.

The first is to understand it as: “No journey is to be undertaken” to any mosque “except the three mosques.” Its explicit wording would thus prohibit journeys to other mosques, while its implication, its purport, and a fortiori reasoning would prohibit journeys to all other places believed to possess special merit. For, according to scriptural evidence and consensus, mosques and worship within them are dearer to Allah than worship in those places. If, then, journeys to places of greater merit have been prohibited, journeys to places of lesser merit

---

## Page 13332

are all the more deserving of prohibition.

The same applies to someone who understands the hadith to mean: “Travel is recommended only to the three mosques.” If he understands it to mean that travel is obligatory only to the three, intending obligation arising from a vow—as a group of scholars have stated—then such people say: “Travel to anywhere other than the three is neither recommended nor made obligatory by a vow.” Those who understand the hadith as denying either recommendation or obligation arising from a vow are saying the same thing in substance. If travel becomes obligatory by a vow only to these three, then travel to the other two mosques becomes obligatory by a vow, although it is not otherwise obligatory under the Sharia. It follows that it becomes obligatory because it is recommended under the Sharia. If, among journeys not otherwise obligatory under the Sharia, only journeys to these two become obligatory by a vow, it follows that only journeys to these two are recommended. This has been discussed at length elsewhere.

The other possibility is to say that the implied wording is: “Do not travel to any spot or place other than the three mosques.” Alternatively, the meaning is that travel to any place other than the three is not recommended. This is what everyone means who says: “Travel to anywhere other than the three does not become obligatory by a vow.” That is, do not travel for the sake of that particular place or spot, such that both the purpose and the worship are tied to that very spot, as with travel to the three mosques. This differs from travel to the frontier regions, where the purpose is to travel to a place of frontier duty. The “frontier” may be in one place; then the Muslims conquer the territory adjoining them, and it shifts

---

## Page 13333

to the boundary of Muslim territory. Thus, a place may at one time be a frontier and at another not, just as it may at one time be a land of Islam and righteousness and at another a land of unbelief and wickedness. Mecca was once a land of unbelief and war, while Medina was a land of faith and emigration and a place of frontier duty. When Mecca was conquered, it became a land of Islam, and Medina was no longer a place of emigration and frontier duty as it had been before the conquest of Mecca. Indeed, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There is no emigration after the conquest, but jihad and intention remain. When you are called to arms, go forth.”

The frontier regions then became the outer reaches of the Hijāz bordering the lands at war with the Muslims: Syria and Iraq. When the Muslims subsequently conquered Syria and Iraq, the frontier regions in Syria shifted to the seacoast, including Ashkelon, Acre, and the surrounding areas; in Iraq, they included Abbādān and similar places. This is why “Ashkelon” and “Abbādān” are frequently mentioned in the writings of earlier scholars: both were frontier towns. “Tartūsh” was also a frontier town when it belonged to the Muslims. When the unbelievers captured it, the frontier shifted to those parts of the Aleppo region bordering enemy territory.

Someone travelling to the frontier regions, to seek knowledge, to trade, or to visit a relative does not seek a particular place except incidentally, because he knows that what he seeks is there. Were it elsewhere, he would go there instead.

Scholars agree that travel of this kind is not covered by the hadith. Rather, it covers someone who travels to a particular place because of the virtue of that very place, such as someone travelling to mosques and sites associated with the prophets, including Mount Sinai, where Allah spoke to

---

## Page 13334

Moses, the cave of Hirā’, where revelation first came to the Messenger, and the cave of Thawr, mentioned in the Quran in His words:

“When the two were in the cave”

—as well as caves and mountains of lesser standing, as in travelling to Mount Lebanon, the Cave of Blood, and the like. Many people travel to mountains and caves that they believe possess special merit. If travelling to Mount Sinai, where Allah spoke to Moses and which He called the blessed spot and the sacred valley, is not recommended, then there is all the more reason not to travel to other mountains.

When I say “by consensus,” I mean the consensus of the early generations and the imams. The Companions, including Ibn ‘Umar, Abū Sa‘īd, Abū Basrah, and others, understood the Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Do not undertake a journey except to three mosques”

to include within the prohibition Mount Sinai, where Allah spoke to Moses and which He called the sacred valley and the blessed spot. They forbade people to travel there and did not restrict the prohibition to mosques. Accordingly, no one held that a vow makes such a journey obligatory. I know of no early disagreement concerning this, nor have I seen anyone explicitly state a contrary view except Ibn Hazm al-Zāhirī. Like the majority, he prohibits travelling to a mosque other than the three when one has vowed to do so. Yet if someone vows to travel to a site associated with the prophets, he considers fulfilling that vow obligatory, because he does not accept what an utterance implies or indicates beyond its literal wording. This is one of the two views reported from Dāwūd. Thus, he does not regard His words:

“Do not say to them even ‘ugh’”

as evidence of the prohibition of insulting and reviling

---

## Page 13335

and striking them; nor does he regard the prohibition against urinating in still water and then bathing in it as prohibiting pouring urine into it and then bathing in it. The majority of scholars consider such reasoning a deficiency in intellect and understanding, and a form of “sophistry” that denies the speaker’s intended meaning, as explained at length elsewhere.

If we consider the cave of Hirā’, the people of Mecca used to climb up to it to worship, and it is said that ‘Abd al-Muttalib instituted this practice among them. Before his prophethood, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to devote himself to worship there, and it was there that revelation first came to him. Yet once revelation had come to him, he never climbed up to it again or approached it—neither he nor his Companions. He remained in Mecca for ten-odd years after becoming a prophet without visiting it or climbing up to it, and the believers with him in Mecca did likewise. After the Hijrah, he came to Mecca several times: for the ‘umrah of al-Hudaybiyah; in the year of the conquest, when he stayed there for nearly twenty days; and for the ‘umrah of al-Ji‘rānah. Yet he neither went to the cave of Hirā’ nor visited it.

If, then, this cave is not to be travelled to or visited, there is all the more reason not to visit other caves, such as the Cave of Blood and the like. For, since the Messenger’s mission began, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, acts of worship such as prayer, remembrance of Allah, and supplication have been prescribed in every place: “The whole earth has been made a place of prayer and a means of purification for him and his community.”

The places possessing special merit are the mosques, which are the places most beloved to Allah, as authentically reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It is in them that devotional retreat is observed.

---

## Page 13336

so devotional retreat may be observed only in mosques, by scholarly agreement, as Allah Most High says:

“And do not have relations with them as long as you are staying for worship in the mosques.”

Devotional retreat, whether in seclusion or otherwise, is not to be observed in a cave, at a grave, or at any other place to which misguided people deliberately travel and where they remain in devotion, just as the polytheists remain devoted to their idols.

Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, said:

“What are these statues to which you are devoted?”

And Allah Most High says:

“We took the Children of Israel across the sea, and they came upon a people devoted to their idols. They said, ‘O Moses, make for us a god just as they have gods.’ He said, ‘You are indeed an ignorant people.’”

“What these people are engaged in is doomed to destruction, and all that they have been doing is in vain.”

A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

It is authentically reported that Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyib said:

“Whoever vows to observe devotional retreat in the mosque of Jerusalem, then observes it in the Prophet’s Mosque in Medina, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has fulfilled his vow. Whoever vows to observe devotional retreat in the mosque of Medina, then observes it in the Sacred Mosque, has fulfilled his vow. Whoever vows to observe devotional retreat on mountaintops should not do so; rather, let him observe it in a congregational mosque.”

The general body of scholars agrees on the prohibition stated by Sa‘īd. Even supposing that a person does not call this devotional retreat, anyone who does outside a mosque what someone observing devotional retreat does inside one is observing devotional retreat outside a mosque, and this is prohibited by scholarly agreement. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

The point here is that travelling to places other than the three mosques, whether a grave or a site associated with

---

## Page 13337

a prophet, or a mosque or any other place—is neither obligatory nor recommended, according to the revealed texts and scholarly consensus. Travelling to our Prophet’s Mosque, however, is recommended according to the revealed texts and scholarly consensus. This is what the scholars meant when they said, “Visiting his grave is recommended by consensus.” This, then, is what the Companions, the Successors, and the qualified jurists who followed them agreed upon. All praise belongs to Allah.

The respondent has stated that this is recommended according to the revealed texts and scholarly consensus. His words therefore show that he follows the Companions, the Successors, and the qualified scholars who came after them, and that they are above sanctioning the unlawful or violating consensus—above agreeing upon misguidance or following the path of blindness and ignorance. Yet this objector and others like him among the ignorant have equated this journey, whose recommended status is established by the Messenger’s explicit statement and the consensus of his community, with a journey which, by the Messenger’s explicit statement and the consensus of his community, is established not to be recommended.

They have drawn an analogy between the two, whereas the respondent mentioned the two views only concerning the second kind: someone who travels solely to visit the graves of the prophets and the righteous. He also stated that, for someone travelling to the Messenger’s Mosque and to visit him in the prescribed manner, travelling there is recommended according to the revealed texts and scholarly consensus.

They nevertheless attributed to the respondent the view that he prohibits visiting the Messenger’s grave and travelling to it, declares this unlawful, and forbids shortening the prayers during such a journey. Thus they portrayed him as prohibiting the journey that pilgrims make to his mosque and as holding that anyone who travels there may not shorten the prayers. All of this is fabrication and slander.

---

## Page 13338

This is because they have no argument for travelling to the graves of the other prophets except the journey to our Prophet. Since travelling to that place is prescribed in principle, they drew an analogy with it to justify travelling to other graves. Thus they went astray and led others astray, contradicting the Book of Allah, the Sunnah of His Messenger, and the consensus of the Muslims. They erred in many respects.

Among them is that the only prophet’s grave on earth known through mass transmission and consensus is the grave of our Prophet; all others are disputed.

Another is that those who recommended travelling to visit our Prophet’s grave meant travelling to his mosque, which is prescribed by consensus. Even if a traveller intends to visit the grave, he reaches only the mosque, and the mosque is the endpoint of his journey: he does not reach the grave itself. This differs from visiting another grave, where he does reach the grave.

The exception would be someone steeped in ignorance and misguidance who supposes that travelling to his mosque was prescribed only because of the grave; that this is why prayer there is equivalent to a thousand prayers; and that, were it not for the grave, the mosque would have no special merit over others. Or he might suppose that the mosque was built or designated as an adjunct to the grave, just as mosques are built over the graves of prophets and righteous people, and that praying in the mosque is secondary, while the grave is the real object of the visit. This is what those who travel to the graves of prophets and righteous people other than our Prophet suppose. It is like someone who goes to the Friday prayer and, upon entering, offers two units of prayer to greet the mosque: he came for the Friday prayer, not for those two units of prayer.

Anyone who thinks this about the mosque of our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is among the most misguided of people and the most ignorant of the religion of

---

## Page 13339

Islam, and the most ignorant of the lives of the Messenger and his Companions, and of his conduct, sayings, and deeds. Such a person needs to learn what he does not know of the religion of Islam, so that he may enter Islam rather than accept some of it and abandon the rest. His mosque was founded upon piety in the first year after the Hijrah, and it is the finest mosque on earth except for the Sacred Mosque. It has also been said that it is the finest without exception.

Would any rational person say that Muslim mosques—the congregational mosques in which Friday and other prayers are offered—derive their merit, and the recommendation to go there to pray, from a nearby grave? If this cannot be said of such mosques, how could it be said of one that is better and more excellent than them all?

According to the majority of scholars, the “Sacred Mosque” is the finest of all mosques without exception, and a prayer there is equivalent to a hundred thousand prayers, as reported in the Musnad and the Sunan collections. Would any rational person say that its merit is due to a grave there?

“Al-Aqsā Mosque” is the finest mosque after the Prophet’s Mosque, and Jerusalem contains so many graves of prophets that only Allah can count them. Would any rational person say that its merit is due to those graves? Indeed, this is what Christians believe: they believe that Jerusalem owes its merit to the “church” said to have been built over the grave of the crucified man, and they regard it as superior to Jerusalem’s sanctuary. These people are among the most misguided and ignorant of all.

---

## Page 13340

And this resembles the practice of the polytheists at the Sacred Mosque when it contained idols and they would go there for the sake of those idols. They did not pray there; rather, as Allah Most High said:

“Their prayer at the House was nothing but whistling and clapping.”

Yet they revered the House itself and circumambulated it, just as they performed pilgrimage every year, despite the changes they had made to the religious law of Abraham. This continued until Allah sent Muhammad with guidance and the religion of truth, and commanded that the faith of Abraham be followed. Muhammad proclaimed it, called people to it, established the pilgrimage according to what Allah had prescribed for Abraham, and purged the House of polytheism. Allah Most High revealed:

“It is not for the polytheists to maintain the mosques of Allah while bearing witness against themselves to their disbelief. Their deeds have come to nothing, and they will abide in the Fire forever.

“Only those who believe in Allah and the Last Day, establish prayer, give zakah, and fear none but Allah maintain the mosques of Allah. It is they who may be among the rightly guided.”

He thus made clear that those who maintain the mosques are those who fear none but Allah. Whoever fears none but Allah places his hope and trust in Him alone, for hope and fear are inseparable. Those who make pilgrimages to graves call upon their occupants, implore them, worship them, fear others besides Allah, and place their hope in others besides Allah—like the polytheists who fear their gods and place their hope in them. Thus, when they said to Hūd, peace be upon him, “We say only that some of our gods have afflicted you with evil,” he said, “I call Allah to witness—and you too bear witness—that I am free of what

---

## Page 13341

you worship besides Him. So plot against me, all of you, and give me no respite. I have put my trust in Allah, my Lord and your Lord. There is no living creature but He holds it by its forelock. Indeed, my Lord is on a straight path.”

When they disputed with Abraham, peace be upon him, he said to them:

“Do you dispute with me concerning Allah when He has guided me? I do not fear those you associate with Him, unless my Lord wills something. My Lord encompasses all things in knowledge. Will you not take heed? How could I fear those you associate with Him, while you do not fear associating with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority to you? Which of the two parties has more right to security, if you know? Those who believe and do not mix their faith with wrongdoing—it is they who will have security, and they are rightly guided.”

When they tried to frighten Muhammad—may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon him—with those besides Allah, Allah Most High said:

“Is Allah not sufficient for His servant? Yet they try to frighten you with those besides Him. Whoever Allah leaves astray has no guide, and whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray. Is Allah not Almighty, capable of retribution? If you ask them who created the heavens and the earth, they will surely say, ‘Allah.’ Say, ‘Have you considered those you call upon besides Allah? If Allah intends harm for me, could they remove the harm He inflicts? Or if He intends mercy for me, could they withhold His mercy?’ Say, ‘Allah is sufficient for me. In Him let those who trust put their trust.’”

And He Most High said:

“Say, ‘Call upon those you associate with Allah, then plot against me and give me no respite. Indeed, my Protector is Allah, who sent down the Book, and He protects the righteous.’”

---

## Page 13342

Section

As for “al-Aqsā Mosque,” the prophets prayed there from the time of Abraham, Allah’s close friend. As reported in the two Sahīh collections, Abū Dharr said:

“I asked, ‘O Messenger of Allah, which mosque was established first?’ He replied, ‘The Sacred Mosque.’ I asked, ‘Then which?’ He replied, ‘Al-Aqsā Mosque.’ I asked, ‘How much time was there between them?’ He replied, ‘Forty years. Thereafter, wherever you are when prayer falls due, pray, for it is a place of prayer.’ So many of Allah’s friends have prayed there that only Allah can count them. Solomon built this structure and asked his Lord for three things: a kingdom such as no one after him would have; judgment that accorded with His judgment; and that He forgive anyone who came to this mosque intending nothing other than to pray there.”

For this reason, Ibn ʿUmar would come from the Hijāz, enter the mosque and pray there, then leave without drinking any water there, so that Solomon’s supplication would apply to him. The Companions, and then the Successors, would come without seeking out any of the surrounding sites, nor would they travel to the town of Hebron or elsewhere.

Likewise, “our Prophet’s Mosque” was built by the best of the prophets, accompanied by the Emigrants

---

## Page 13343

and the Helpers. It was the first mosque in Islam where the call to prayer was made. There the Messenger led the Muslims in Friday and congregational prayers, taught them the Book and wisdom, and gave them instructions concerning military expeditions and other matters. There the Sunnah was established, and from there Islam spread. Prayer in it was worth a thousand prayers, and travelling to it was prescribed during the lifetime of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—when there was no grave there, neither his nor anyone else’s. Then, when the Messenger was buried, he was buried in his chamber, in his home; he was not buried in the mosque.

Every Muslim knows the distinction between a home and a mosque. A mosque is a place for devotional retreat, whereas a home is not. When he observed such a retreat, he would leave his home for the mosque and would enter his home only to attend to his bodily needs. Neither a person in a state of major ritual impurity nor a menstruating woman may remain in a mosque. Yet ‘Ā’ishah would remain in his home while menstruating, just as a woman remains in any ordinary home while menstruating. He would also enter a state of major ritual impurity there and remain there until he bathed. His clothes and food were there, and it was his dwelling and place of rest, as Allah has made homes to be.

Allah mentions “the Prophet’s homes” in His Book, attributing them sometimes to the Messenger and sometimes to his wives. Those homes do not possess the mosque’s sanctity or merit, nor the special merit of prayer within it. Journeys are not undertaken specifically to visit them, and prayer in any of them is not worth a thousand prayers. It is well known that he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—during his

---

## Page 13344

lifetime, he and his Companions were better than those who came after them, and their worship was better than the worship of those who came after them. When they died, their graves were not better than the homes in which they had lived, nor did their bodies worship and obey Allah more after death than they had during life. Allah Most High has told us that He made the earth a receptacle for the living and the dead: it holds people alive upon its surface and dead within it. Its holding them in death is no better than its holding them in life. Thus, it is recommended to visit those buried at al-Baqīʿ and Uhud, and other believers, to supplicate for them and ask forgiveness for them. It is not recommended, however, to seek out their graves for the purposes for which mosques are sought out, such as prayer, devotional retreat, and the like. It is authentically reported that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“The places most beloved to Allah are the mosques.”

No places, then, are better than mosques. The dwellings of the prophets, whether during their lives or after their deaths, are not better than mosques. This is established by the explicit statement of the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and by the agreement of the scholars of his community.

Some have claimed that the graves of the prophets and the righteous are better than mosques, and that supplication at those graves is better than supplication in mosques, even the Sacred Mosque and the Prophet’s Mosque. The falsehood of this claim is self-evident from the religion brought by the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The scholars of the Muslim community are known with certainty to agree unanimously that it is false, just as they agree that devotional retreat in mosques is better than at graves. What is intended

---

## Page 13345

by devotional retreat is worship, prayer, recitation, remembrance of Allah, and supplication.

As for the consensus some have claimed that a particular grave is superior to all mosques, this is a claim newly introduced into Islam. It is not known to have been held by any of the early generations. Rather, one of the later scholars mentioned it, and another took it from him and supposed it to be a consensus, on the grounds that the bodies of the prophets themselves are superior to mosques. Yet their argument applies to all believers: their bodies are superior to any earth in the world. The superiority of their bodies does not entail that their dwellings, whether in life or in death, are superior. Indeed, it is self-evident from their religion that their mosques are superior to their dwellings.

Some may appeal to the report:

“Every newborn is sprinkled with some earth from his burial pit.”

Thus, they argue, he was created from the earth of his grave. This argument is invalid for two reasons.

The first is that this is not established, and all the reports concerning it are weak. It is known with certainty that no earth has been sprinkled over the fetus in its mother’s womb. Rather, it was Adam himself who was created from earth; his descendants were then created from an extract of a lowly fluid. It is also known that no portion of that earth is distinguishable as belonging to one person, and another portion to someone else. For when it was transformed into a living body, as the spirit was breathed into Adam, it no longer remained earth. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

---

## Page 13346

The purpose here is to draw attention to such claims of consensus, which some people cite and use to support conclusions contrary to the religion of the Muslims: the Book, the Sunnah, and consensus.

The second reason is that, even if it were established that the deceased was created from that earth, it is well known that a person’s creation from his parents’ reproductive fluid is more immediate than his creation from earth. Yet Allah brings forth the living from the dead and the dead from the living: He brings forth a believer from a disbeliever and a disbeliever from a believer. Thus, He creates from a disbelieving person a believer, whether a prophet or otherwise, just as He created Abraham, His intimate friend, from Āzar. Abraham, the best of created beings, is the greatest of the prophets after Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, whereas Āzar is among the people of the Fire. As reported in the Sahīh, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Abraham will meet his father Āzar on the Day of Resurrection. Abraham will say, ‘Did I not tell you not to disobey me?’ His father will reply, ‘Today I will not disobey you.’ Abraham will then say, ‘My Lord, did You not promise me that You would not disgrace me? What greater disgrace could there be than my wretched father?’ He will be told, ‘Turn around.’ He will turn and see a huge dhīkh—a male hyena. Āzar will be transformed into that form, seized by his legs, and cast into the Fire, so that he will not be recognized as Abraham’s father.”

Likewise, our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was created from his parents, yet he was forbidden to ask forgiveness for his mother. It is also reported in the Sahīh:

“A man asked him, ‘Where is my father?’ He replied, ‘Your father is in the Fire.’ When the man turned away, he called him back and said, ‘My father and your father are in the Fire.’”

And He brought forth from Noah, who was

---

## Page 13347

a noble messenger, his disbelieving son, against whom the decree had been pronounced. He drowned him and forbade Noah to intercede for him. The Emigrants and the Helpers were created from their disbelieving fathers and mothers.

If the immediate substance from which the prophets and the righteous are created need not equal their bodies in excellence—for Allah brings forth the living from the dead, bringing forth a believer’s body from a disbeliever’s semen—then the more remote substance, namely earth, has even less claim to equal the bodies of the prophets and the righteous. These bodies worshipped Allah and strove in His cause, and their final abode is Paradise.

As for the substances from which these bodies were created, whatever was transformed into the body shares the body’s status, whereas whatever remained over has the same status as other matter of its kind. Herein lies the error of those who fail to distinguish between substances that were transformed into a body and those that underwent no transformation, but remained earth or lifeless matter.

Thus, regarding the earth of graves, if we suppose that the deceased was created from that earth, then whatever of it was transformed into his body is his body, whose excellence is known. As for what remains in the grave, it has the same status as other earth of its kind. Indeed, the earth that touched their foreheads during prostration—when the servant is closest to his Lord, whom he worships—is superior to the earth of graves and burial niches. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

---

## Page 13348

The point here is that the excellence of the Messenger’s Mosque and other mosques lies in their being houses of Allah, built for His worship. Allah Most High says:

“The mosques belong to Allah, so do not invoke anyone alongside Allah.”

And He says:

“Say, ‘My Lord has commanded justice. Direct your faces toward Him at every place of prayer.’”

And He says:

“It is not for the polytheists to maintain Allah’s mosques while bearing witness against themselves to disbelief”—up to His words—“Only those who believe in Allah and the Last Day, establish prayer, give zakat, and fear none but Allah maintain Allah’s mosques. It is they who may be among the rightly guided.”

And He says:

“In houses which Allah has ordained to be raised and His name remembered, He is glorified morning and evening by men whom neither trade nor sale distracts from remembering Allah, establishing prayer, and giving zakat. They fear a Day when hearts and eyes will be overturned, so that Allah may reward them according to the best of what they did and grant them still more out of His bounty. Allah provides for whomever He wills without measure.”

The three mosques possess an excellence above all others, for prophets built them and called people to travel to them. Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, called people to the Sacred Mosque; Solomon called them to the mosque in Jerusalem; and our Prophet called them to all three: his own mosque and the other two. However, he made travel to the Sacred Mosque obligatory and travel to the other two voluntary. Abraham and Solomon did not make any of this obligatory, nor did Allah’s intimate friend make Hajj obligatory. Thus, the Children of Israel did not perform Hajj, although Moses, Jonah, and others did. For this reason, it was not

---

## Page 13349

obligatory to perform Hajj in the early days of Islam; it became obligatory only through Allah Most High’s words in Sūrat Āl ‘Imrān:

“Pilgrimage to the House is a duty owed to Allah by mankind.”

Muslims agree that this establishes its obligation. As for His words:

“Complete Hajj and ‘umrah for Allah,”

some have said that this makes both undertaking them in the first place and completing them once begun obligatory. Others have said that it establishes only the obligation to complete them once begun, not an obligation to undertake them in the first place. This is the correct view, for everyone agrees that this verse was revealed in the year of al-Hudaybiyah, after the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, had begun his ‘umrah—the ‘umrah of al-Hudaybiyah—when the polytheists prevented him from proceeding. The verse permitted anyone prevented from proceeding to leave the state of ritual consecration. Thus, when the polytheists barred the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and his Companions, they left that state and returned.

The imams agree that anyone who begins Hajj or ‘umrah must complete it. They differed, however, concerning fasting, prayer, and spiritual retreat, holding two well-known positions. The position of al-Shāfi‘ī and, according to the well-known report from him, Ahmad is that completion is not obligatory. The position of Mālik and Abū Hanīfah is that it is obligatory, as explained at length elsewhere.

The point is that the merit of travelling to the Messenger’s Mosque lies in worshipping there, and that a prayer there is worth a thousand prayers. None of this is on account of the grave, by the consensus of the Muslims. This is among the distinctions between the Mosque of the Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, and other mosques, and between his grave and other graves. The difference has thus become clear in several respects.

---

## Page 13350

This objector and others like him have treated journeys to the graves of the prophets as a single category. Then, when they saw that scholars had spoken of the recommendation to visit our Prophet’s grave, they assumed that journeys could be made to all other graves just as to his.

They thus went astray in several respects:

First: Travelling to him is, in fact, travelling to his Mosque, which is recommended by explicit textual evidence and consensus.

Second: This journey is to the Mosque, whether during the Messenger’s lifetime or after his burial, and whether before or after the chamber was incorporated into it. It is therefore a journey to a mosque, whether the grave is there or not. Travelling to a grave alone cannot therefore be treated as analogous to it.

Third: Some scholars disapprove of calling this a visit to his grave. Those who do not disapprove of that expression agree with those scholars on the ruling; their disagreement concerns only the designation. In the case of anyone else, however, it is indisputably a visit to his grave.

Thus, one who prohibits such travel may say: “I do not concede that travelling to visit his grave is even possible in the first place. No journey is to be undertaken for anything termed a visit to a grave. Travelling to our Prophet’s Mosque is not travelling to visit his grave; rather, it is travelling to worship in his Mosque.”

Fourth: This journey is recommended by explicit textual evidence and consensus, whereas travelling to the graves of the other prophets and the righteous is not recommended by either explicit textual evidence or consensus; rather,

---

## Page 13351

it is prohibited according to the leading imams, as the textual evidence indicates.

Fifth: The mosque beside his grave is his Mosque, which was founded upon piety. It is the best of all mosques apart from the Sacred Mosque, and a prayer in it is equivalent to a thousand prayers. By contrast, establishing mosques over the graves of the prophets and the righteous, and praying in them, have been forbidden, as explained earlier. How much more, then, does this apply to travelling to them?

Sixth: Travelling to his Mosque—which is called travelling to visit his grave—is something upon which Muslims have agreed generation after generation. Travelling to other graves, however, is not known to have been practised by any of the Companions or the Successors who followed them in righteousness, or even by the Successors’ followers. Nor did any of the four imams or other imams recommend it. How, then, can the two be treated as analogous?

From his time until the present, Muslims have continued to travel to his Mosque, either in conjunction with Hajj or independently of it. During the Companions’ time, they did not come to it in conjunction with Hajj—as they travelled to Mecca—for the roads were safe, and undertaking a journey specifically to it was better than making it subsidiary to the Hajj journey.

Umar ibn al-Khattāb instructed them to devote one journey to ‘umrah and another to Hajj. By agreement of the four imams and others, this is better than tamattu‘ (performing ‘umrah followed by Hajj, leaving the state of consecration between them) or qirān (combining ‘umrah and Hajj in a single state of consecration). For those who preferred tamattu‘ and qirān—as Ahmad preferred tamattu‘ for someone who had not brought a sacrificial animal and qirān for someone who had, according to his expressly recorded position, and explicitly stated in more than one place that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed qirān—

---

## Page 13352

nevertheless maintain that devoting one journey to ‘umrah and another to Hajj is better than tamattu‘ and qirān. Likewise, Abū Hanīfah’s position—as reported by Muhammad ibn al-Hasan—is that an ‘umrah undertaken from Kufa is better than tamattu‘ and qirān. A detailed discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

The point is that Muslims have continually travelled to his Mosque, but have not travelled to the graves of the prophets, such as the graves of Moses and Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, peace be upon him. No Companion is known to have travelled to Abraham’s grave, despite their frequent visits to Greater Syria and Jerusalem. How, then, can travelling to the Messenger’s Mosque—which some people call visiting his grave—be treated as equivalent to travelling to the graves of the prophets?

Seventh: The prescribed journey to his Mosque entails doing there what was done during his lifetime and that of his rightly guided caliphs: prayer, salutations of peace upon him, praise, and supplication. These are also performed in other mosques and elsewhere, although his Mosque is superior. What is prescribed there is worship of Allah that He has commanded. By contrast, what those who travel to other people’s graves do falls within the category of associating partners with Allah: invoking them, asking them to fulfil needs, and taking their graves as mosques, sites of recurrent observances, and idols. This is prohibited by explicit textual evidence and scholarly consensus.

If you say: “But some people do such things at his grave.”

---

## Page 13353

I reply:

At the grave itself, no one is able to do that, for Allah answered his supplication when he said:

“O Allah, do not make my grave an idol that is worshipped.”

As for his Mosque, only some ignorant people do such things there. Those who know the teachings of Islam do only what is prescribed, and they restrain the others as far as they can. Thus, the visitors are not united in misguidance. As for anyone else’s grave, those who travel to it are all ignorant, misguided people who associate partners with Allah. They reach the grave itself, and no one there censures them.

Eighth: His grave is well known and established through mass transmission, unlike the graves of others.

It should be understood that Allah Most High has protected the graves of the prophets in general through the blessing of Muhammad’s mission, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. With the ascendancy of his religion, people have been unable to take the prophets’ graves as mosques. Likewise, He has made manifest faith in the prophets’ prophethood and in what they brought—through publicly remembering them, loving them, showing them allegiance, believing their words, and following their deeds—to an extent unmatched in any other community.

This is how people benefit from the prophets: by believing what they reported, obeying what they commanded, following their example in what they did, loving what they loved, hating what they hated, showing allegiance to those to whom they showed allegiance, and opposing those whom they opposed. These and similar things can only be attained through knowledge of the accounts of their lives. The Quran and Sunnah are full of accounts of the prophets. This is firmly established in hearts and spoken of by tongues. As for the grave itself, there is no

---

## Page 13354

such benefit in seeing it. Rather, the misguided take graves as idols, just as the Jews and Christians used to take the graves of prophets and righteous people as mosques. Through the blessing of Muhammad’s mission, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, Allah has made known accounts of them and their lives that must be believed and that benefit His servants. He has abolished the practices that harm people: associating them as partners with Allah and taking their graves as mosques, as people before us used to do.

In the time of the Companions, no prophet’s grave was openly known and visited, whether people travelled to it or not—neither Abraham’s grave nor any other. When “Daniel’s grave” was discovered at Tustar, and people were seeking rain through him, Abū Mūsā al-Ash‘arī wrote about it to ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb. ‘Umar wrote back, ordering him to dig thirteen graves during the day, bury him in one of them at night, and erase the traces of all the graves, lest people be led astray through him. This has been mentioned by several people.

Among those who narrated it was Yūnus ibn Bakr, in Additions to Ibn Ishāq’s Military Expeditions, from Abū Khalda Khālid ibn Dīnār, who related:

Abū al-‘Āliya narrated to us, saying: “When we conquered Tustar, we found in al-Hurmuzān’s treasury a bier bearing a dead man, with a manuscript of scripture belonging to him beside his head. We took the manuscript and carried it to ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb. He summoned Ka‘b to deal with it, and Ka‘b transcribed it in Arabic. I was the first Arab to read it; I read it just as I read this Quran.”

I asked Abū al-‘Āliya, “What did it contain?”

He replied, “Your ways of life, your affairs, the forms of your speech, and what is yet to come.”

---

## Page 13355

I asked, “Then what did you do with the man?”

He replied, “During the day, we dug thirteen graves in separate places. When night came, we buried him and levelled all the graves to conceal his whereabouts from people so that they would not dig him up.”

I asked, “What did they hope to gain through him?”

He replied, “Whenever rain was withheld from them, they would bring out his bier, and rain would fall upon them.”

I asked, “Who did you think the man was?”

He replied, “A man called Daniel.”

I asked, “How long had he been dead when you found him?”

He replied, “Three hundred years.”

I asked, “Had anything about him changed?”

He replied, “No, except for a few hairs at the back of his head. The earth does not cause the flesh of prophets to decay, nor do wild beasts devour it.”

The Companions left no prophet’s grave openly known in Islam for people to be led astray through it, travel to it, invoke it, or take it as a mosque. Indeed, they screened off even the grave of our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, within the chamber and prevented people from accessing it as far as possible. They erased the traces of other graves as far as possible if people were being led astray through them. If people were not being led astray through a grave, however, there was no harm in knowing its location, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, related when describing how the Angel of Death came to Moses, peace be upon him:

“The angel said, ‘Answer your Lord’s call.’ Moses slapped him and put out his eye. The angel returned to Allah and said, ‘You sent me to a servant of Yours who does not want to die, and he has put out my eye.’ Allah restored his eye and said, ‘Go back to Moses and say to him: “Do you wish to live? If you wish to live, place your hand on the back of a bull. For every hair your hand covers, you will live one year.”’ Moses said, ‘Then what?’

---

## Page 13356

He replied, ‘Death.’ Moses said, ‘Then let it be now, my Lord, but bring me within a stone’s throw of the Holy Land.’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Were I there, I would show you his grave beside the road, by the red sandhill.’”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, passed by Moses on the night of the Night Journey and saw him standing in prayer in his grave. Despite this, none of the Companions or their successors would travel to it, nor did they go there when they entered Syria during the time of Abū Bakr and ʿUmar. Likewise, they did not travel to the grave of Abraham, the Friend of Allah, or to other graves. This was how they dealt with the graves of prophets and righteous people.

As for the grave of “Daniel,” it is said that they could smell musk emanating from it, so they effaced its traces lest people be led astray through it. “Abraham’s grave,” peace be upon him, was enclosed by a structure. It is said that Solomon, peace be upon him, built it, so no one could reach the grave. An opening was only made in the structure much later, after the first three generations had passed away. It has also been said that it was the Christians who made the opening when they gained control of the land. Even so, no one was able to reach Abraham’s grave, may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon him.

Thus, travelling to visit the graves of prophets and righteous people was not possible during the time of the Companions and their successors; it arose only after them. The prophets were very numerous, whereas the graves attributed to them are very few, and none of these attributions is established by common knowledge.

Some graves attributed to them are known to be falsely attributed, such as “Noah’s grave” at the foot of Mount Lebanon. Among them are also

---

## Page 13357

those whose authenticity is not established by consensus—apart from the graves of our Prophet, Abraham the Friend of Allah, and Moses. This is an honor bestowed upon Muhammad and his community. Allah has safeguarded the prophets’ graves against being made places of worship in a way unparalleled among earlier nations, for Muhammad and his community upheld monotheism as no others had done. They subdued the worshippers of idols, crosses, and fire.

Just as Allah suppressed polytheism through them, so through Muhammad and his community He manifested faith in the prophets, reverence for them and for what they brought, and public remembrance of them in the finest manner, to a degree unparalleled in any other nation. In the Quran, He commands that they be remembered, as in His words, Exalted is He:

“Mention in the Book Abraham. Indeed, he was a man of truth and a prophet.”

“Mention in the Book Moses. Indeed, he was chosen, and he was a messenger and a prophet.”

And the subsequent verses. He also says:

“Be patient with what they say, and remember Our servant David, a man of strength. Indeed, he constantly turned to Allah.”

After him, He mentions Solomon, continuing to His words:

“And remember Our servant Job, when he called upon his Lord.”

Then to His words:

“And remember Our servants Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, men of strength and insight.”

Then to His words:

“And remember Ishmael, Elisha, and Dhū al-Kifl.”

Thus, He commanded that these prophets be remembered. As for Moses and, before him, Noah, Hūd, and Sālih, they had already been mentioned in His words, Exalted is He:

“Before them, the people of Noah denied the truth, as did ʿĀd and Pharaoh of the stakes,

and Thamūd, the people of Lot, and the people of the thicket. Those were the confederates.

Every one of them rejected the messengers, so My punishment became due.”

He also commanded that Moses and others be remembered in a surah

---

## Page 13358

other than this one, as noted above.

What Allah has brought about through Muhammad and his community—remembering the prophets in the finest manner, recounting their histories, praising and extolling them, affirming the obligation to believe in what they brought, declaring anyone who disbelieves in any one of them an unbeliever and putting him to death, putting to death anyone who reviles any of them, and other such ways of honoring their standing—has no parallel in any other religion.

The “foundation of faith” is to worship Allah alone, without any partner, and to believe in His messengers, as He, Exalted is He, says:

“So by your Lord, We will surely question them all about what they used to do.”

Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: “There are two matters about which people will be questioned on the Day of Resurrection: what they used to do and how they responded to the messengers.”

For this reason, Allah establishes these two foundations in numerous places in the Quran. Indeed, He gives them precedence over everything else, for they are the most fundamental principles. This is illustrated by what He mentions in Surah al-Baqarah. He begins it by describing the three categories of people: believers, disbelievers, and hypocrites. This division arose when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, migrated to Medina. There had been no hypocrisy in Mecca; people were either believers or disbelievers. Al-Baqarah is a Medinan surah, among the earliest revelations sent down in Medina. Allah revealed four verses concerning the believers, two concerning the disbelievers, and more than ten describing the hypocrites. He opened it with faith in all the Books and prophets, placed the same theme at its midpoint, and ended it

---

## Page 13359

with that same theme.

He says at its beginning:

“Alif Lām Mīm.
This is the Book about which there is no doubt, a guidance for the God-fearing,
who believe in the unseen, establish prayer, and spend out of what We have provided for them,
and who believe in what has been revealed to you and what was revealed before you, and are certain of the Hereafter.
Those are upon guidance from their Lord, and those are the successful.”

The correct interpretation of His words,

“And who believe in what has been revealed to you and what was revealed before you,”

is that this passage and the one preceding it describe the same group. For one must believe both in what was revealed to him and in what was revealed before him. The conjunction joins distinct attributes, as in His words:

“He is the First and the Last, the Manifest and the Hidden.”

And His words:

“Who created and proportioned,
and who determined and guided,
and who brought forth the pasture.”

And His words:

“Successful indeed are the believers,
who are humble in their prayers,
and who turn away from idle talk”—

up to His words:

“Those are the inheritors,
who will inherit Paradise; they will abide therein forever.”

Whoever says that “who believe in the unseen” refers to the pagan Arabs, while “and who believe in what has been revealed to you and what was revealed before you” refers to the People of the Book, is mistaken. For the pagan Arabs did not believe in what was revealed to him and what was revealed before him, so they were not successful. Likewise, if the People of the Book did not believe in the unseen, establish prayer, and spend out of what We have provided for them, they would not be successful.

For this reason, Allah, Exalted is He, says:

“Those are upon guidance from their Lord, and those are the successful.”

This indicates that they are one and the same group.

---

## Page 13360

And in the middle of the surah He says:

“Say, ‘We believe in Allah and in what has been revealed to us, and what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and the tribes, and what was given to Moses and Jesus, and what was given to the prophets from their Lord. We make no distinction between any of them, and we submit to Him.’”

Thus, He commands belief in everything the prophets were given by their Lord. Elsewhere in the surah, He says:

“But righteousness is to believe in Allah, the Last Day, the angels, the Book, and the prophets.”

And He concludes it with His words:

“The Messenger believes in what has been revealed to him from his Lord, and so do the believers. All believe in Allah, His angels, His books, and His messengers. We make no distinction between any of His messengers.”

Then, after classifying humankind, He establishes the foundations of religion. He establishes Allah’s oneness first, then prophethood second, saying:

“O mankind, worship your Lord, who created you and those before you, so that you may become God-fearing;
who made the earth a resting place for you and the sky a canopy, and sent down rain from the sky, bringing forth fruits through it as provision for you. So do not set up rivals to Allah while you know.”

Then He establishes prophethood with His words:

“And if you are in doubt about what We have sent down upon Our servant, then produce a surah like it, and call upon your witnesses besides Allah, if you are truthful.
But if you do not—and you will never do so—”

He thus declares that they will not do so, just as He says:

“Say, ‘If mankind and the jinn gathered together to produce the like of this Quran, they could not produce its like.’”

Then He mentions Paradise. Thus, He establishes Allah’s oneness, prophethood, and resurrection. These are the foundations of faith.

---

## Page 13361

In Surah Āl ʿImrān, He says:

“Allah—there is no deity except Him, the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all. He has sent down to you the Book in truth, confirming what came before it, and He sent down the Torah and the Gospel previously, as guidance for mankind, and He sent down the Criterion.”

Thus, He mentions His oneness first, then belief in what the messengers brought second. He mentions that He sent down the Book and the Criterion, just as He says:

“And when We gave Moses the Book and the Criterion.”

The term “Criterion” encompasses whatever distinguishes truth from falsehood, such as the signs with which the prophets were sent: the serpent, the white hand, and the parting of the sea. The Quran is a criterion in this respect: it is a tremendous sign of the prophethood of Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and a source of great knowledge. It is also a criterion in that, through its explanations, it distinguishes truth from falsehood, as He says:

“Blessed is He who sent down the Criterion upon His servant.”

For this reason, a number of scholars have interpreted “the Criterion” here as referring to the Quran.
