The term “Criterion” also encompasses Allah’s granting victory to His prophets and His believing servants, and His destruction of their enemies. Through this, He distinguishes His allies from His enemies; this, too, is among the signs. Allah Most High says:

“If you believe in Allah and in what We sent down upon Our servant on the Day of the Criterion—the day the two armies met.”

The signs that Allah makes evidence of the prophets’ truthfulness are among the things He sends down, as He says:

“They say, ‘Why has no sign been sent down to him from his Lord?’ Say, ‘Allah is certainly able to send down a sign.’”

And He says:

“If We willed, We could send down to them a sign from heaven before which their necks would remain bowed in submission.”

And Allah Most High says:

“But those who did wrong substituted words other than those they had been told, so We sent down upon the wrongdoers a punishment from heaven because of their disobedience.”

A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

---

## Page 13362

The purpose here is simply to draw attention to this. Likewise, in Surah Yūnus, Allah Most High says:

“Do people find it astonishing that We have revealed to a man from among them, ‘Warn mankind and give good news to those who believe that they will have a sure standing with their Lord’?”

Then He says:

“Indeed, your Lord is Allah, who created the heavens and the earth in six days, then rose over the Throne, directing all affairs. No one can intercede except after His permission. That is Allah, your Lord, so worship Him. Will you not then take heed?”

In Surah Alif Lām Mīm al-Sajdah, Allah Most High says:

“Alif Lām Mīm.”

“The revelation of the Book, about which there is no doubt, is from the Lord of all worlds.”

“Or do they say, ‘He has fabricated it’? Rather, it is the truth from your Lord, so that you may warn a people to whom no warner came before you, that they may be guided.”

“Allah is the One who created the heavens and the earth and everything between them in six days, then rose over the Throne. You have no protector or intercessor besides Him. Will you not then take heed?”

And He says:

“The revelation of the Book is from Allah, the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

“Indeed, We have sent down the Book to you in truth, so worship Allah, devoting your religion sincerely to Him.”

“Surely, sincere devotion belongs to Allah alone. Those who take protectors besides Him say, ‘We worship them only so that they may bring us nearer to Allah.’”

Another example is His saying:

“A Book whose verses have been perfected and then explained in detail, from One who is All-Wise, All-Aware.”

“Worship none but Allah. Indeed, I am a warner and a bearer of good news to you from Him.”

And His saying:

“If they do not respond to you, then know that it has been sent down with Allah’s knowledge and that there is no deity except Him. Will you then submit?”

And His saying:

“He sends down the angels with revelation by His command upon whomever He wills of His servants: ‘Warn that there is no deity except Me, so fear Me.’”

And His saying:

“And on the Day He calls to them and says, ‘Where are My partners whom you used to claim?’”

Then He says:

---

## Page 13363

“And on the Day He calls to them and says, ‘How did you respond to the messengers?’”

And His saying:

“We certainly sent a messenger to every nation, saying, ‘Worship Allah and shun false gods.’”

In the two units of prayer before Fajr, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would sometimes recite the two surahs of sincere devotion, al-Kāfirūn and al-Ikhlās. At other times, he would recite the verses beginning with His saying, Most High:

“Say, ‘We believe in Allah and what has been sent down to us and what was sent down to Abraham.’”

And in the second:

“Say, ‘O People of the Scripture, come to a common word between us and you: that we worship none but Allah, associate nothing with Him, and do not take one another as lords besides Allah.’ But if they turn away, say, ‘Bear witness that we are Muslims.’”

This is a vast subject, for people have an indispensable need for these two foundations. Only through them can they escape punishment and attain happiness.

They must therefore believe in the prophets and what they brought. The foundation of what they brought is that people should worship Allah alone, as He says:

“We never sent a messenger before you without revealing to him: ‘There is no deity except Me, so worship Me.’”

And He, Most High, says:

“Ask those of Our messengers whom We sent before you: Did We appoint any gods besides the Most Merciful to be worshipped?”

And He, Most High, says:

“We certainly sent a messenger to every nation, saying, ‘Worship Allah and shun false gods.’”

The prophets—may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon them—are intermediaries between Allah and His creation in conveying His speech, His commands and prohibitions, His promises and warnings, and the accounts He has given concerning His names and attributes, His angels, His Throne, and what has happened and what will happen. They are not intermediaries in His creation of His servants, nor in His providing for them, giving them life, or causing them to die, nor

---

## Page 13364

in His recompensing them for their deeds with reward or punishment, nor in His answering their supplications and granting their requests. Rather, He alone is the Creator of all things. He is the One who answers the distressed when they call upon Him. All who are in the heavens and the earth ask of Him; every day He is attending to some affair.

“Whatever blessing you have is from Allah. Then, when adversity touches you, it is to Him that you cry for help.”

And He, Most High, says:

“Allah has said, ‘Do not take two gods. He is but One God, so fear Me alone.’”

“To Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth, and to Him alone is constant obedience due. Will you then fear anyone other than Allah?”

As He, Most High, says:

“Say, ‘Call upon those you claim to be gods besides Him. They have no power to remove your affliction or divert it.’”

“Those whom they invoke themselves seek a means of nearness to their Lord, striving to be nearest to Him. They hope for His mercy and fear His punishment. Indeed, your Lord’s punishment is something to beware of.”

And He, Most High, says:

“Say, ‘Call upon those you claim to be gods besides Allah. They do not possess even an atom’s weight in the heavens or on earth, nor do they have any share in either, nor does He have any helper among them.’”

“Intercession is of no avail with Him except for one whom He permits.”

Thus, He makes clear that all those invoked besides Allah—angels, prophets and others—do not possess even an atom’s weight. None has any share with Him, nor does He have any helper among them. Only intercession remains:

“Intercession is of no avail with Him except for one whom He permits.”

The matter of intercession therefore rests with Him alone, as He, Most High, says:

“Say, ‘All intercession belongs to Allah.’”

And He says:

“Those whom they invoke besides Him have no power of intercession.”

And His words, “Except those who

---

## Page 13365

bear witness to the truth knowingly,” is a disconnected exception, according to the more correct of the two views.

People thus divided into “three groups” regarding them: one group denied their intermediary role in conveying the message and therefore rejected the Scriptures and the messengers. These include the peoples of Noah, Hūd, Sālih, Lot and Shuʿayb, Pharaoh’s people, and others whom Allah describes as having rejected the messengers. They rejected the messengers as a whole; they believe in some of them but not others.

Among these are those who deny prophethood, including the Brahmins, the polytheist philosophers of India, and other polytheists. Whoever rejects the messengers can only be a polytheist. The same applies to whoever rejects some of them but not others, as He, Most High, says:

“Indeed, those who disbelieve in Allah and His messengers, wish to make a distinction between Allah and His messengers, say, ‘We believe in some and disbelieve in others,’ and wish to adopt a way in between—those are truly the disbelievers.”

Thus, whoever rejects Muhammad, the Messiah, David, Solomon, or any other prophet sent after Moses is a disbeliever. He, Most High, says:

“We certainly gave Moses the Scripture and sent messengers after him in succession.”

And He, Most High, says:

“We gave Jesus, son of Mary, clear proofs and strengthened him with the Holy Spirit. Is it that whenever a messenger came to you with what your souls did not desire, you grew arrogant, rejecting some and killing others?”

And He, Most High, says:

“When they are told, ‘Believe in what Allah has revealed,’ they say, ‘We believe in what was revealed to us,’ yet they disbelieve in what came after it, although it is the truth, confirming what they already possess. Say, ‘Why then

---

## Page 13366

did you kill Allah’s prophets in the past, if you were believers?’”

Among the philosophers, heretics and others are those who regard prophethood as a kind of dreaming and hold that its sole purpose is imaginative representation. He, Most High, says:

“Rather, they say, ‘A jumble of dreams! No, he has fabricated it! No, he is a poet!’”

These people deny prophethood. Others hold that the prophets are distinguished by knowledge they acquire through a sacred faculty, without learning. They do not affirm that angels descend with revelation, nor that Allah speaks. Rather, they say that He does not know particulars, and therefore knows neither Moses nor Muhammad nor any other messenger. They say:

“What distinguishes a prophet is this sacred faculty of knowledge, a faculty through which he influences the world and produces extraordinary occurrences, and an imaginative faculty through which realities are represented within him as imagined forms: he sees luminous shapes within himself and hears speech within himself.”

This, in their view, is a prophet. Yet these three faculties are found in many ordinary individuals, to whom the prophets are superior. Although these people are closer to the truth than the preceding group, they are still among those who reject the messengers.

Many adherents of religious innovation acknowledge what the messengers brought, except in matters that conflict with their own opinions. They then give their opinions precedence over what the messengers brought and turn away from it, saying that they do not know what the messengers intended by it, or distorting words from their proper meanings. Such people are found among both the People of the Scripture and those who face the qiblah. For this reason, at the beginning of Sūrat al-Baqarah, Allah mentions the believers and the disbelievers, then mentions the hypocrites and speaks about them at length.

---

## Page 13367

A second group went to extremes concerning the prophets and the righteous, and the angels as well. They made them intermediaries in worship, worshipping them so that they might bring them nearer to Allah. They made images of them and remained in devotion at their graves. This is widespread among Christians and those misguided people who face the qiblah and resemble them. For this reason, Allah mentions this group in the Quran, in Sūrat Āl ʿImrān and Sūrat Barāʾah, within His discussion of the Christians. He, Most High, says:

“It is not for a human being to whom Allah has given the Scripture, sound judgment and prophethood to then tell people, ‘Be worshippers of me rather than Allah.’ Rather, ‘Be devout scholars because of your teaching of the Scripture and your study of it.’ Nor would he command you to take the angels and the prophets as lords. Would he command you to disbelieve after you have become Muslims?”

He, Most High, also says:

“They have taken their rabbis and monks as lords besides Allah, and the Messiah, son of Mary, though they were commanded only to worship one God. There is no deity except Him. Glory be to Him! Exalted is He above what they associate with Him!”

And He, Most High, says:

“Say, ‘O People of the Scripture, come to a common word between us and you: that we worship none but Allah, associate nothing with Him, and do not take one another as lords besides Allah.’ But if they turn away, say, ‘Bear witness that we are Muslims.’”

These words, which Allah commanded him to say to them, are what he wrote to Heraclius, ruler of the Romans. These people may suppose that if they seek their intercession, they will intercede for them, and that whoever approaches a revered angel or prophet and seeks his intercession will have him intercede with Allah on his behalf, just as the close associates of kings intercede with them. Allah has refuted this kind of intercession in more than one

---

## Page 13368

place in the Quran and explained the distinction between Himself and His creation. A created being may intercede with another without the latter’s permission, and the latter accepts the intercession out of desire, fear, love, or something similar. Thus, the intercessor becomes a partner of the one whose favour is sought.

Such intercession has no place with Allah. He, Most High, says:

“Who is it that can intercede with Him except by His permission?”

And He, Most High, says:

“They intercede only for those with whom He is pleased.”

These people make pilgrimages to their graves and invoke them. They may also prostrate themselves before them, make vows to them, and direct other forms of worship to them. These people, too, are polytheists. Most polytheists combine rejection of some of what the messengers brought with associating partners with Allah. They thus have some share of both associating partners with the Creator and rejecting His messengers. Some combine associating partners with Allah with denial: they deny the Creator or some of the names and attributes that are His due.

The Companions of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and those who follow them in righteousness until the Day of Resurrection belong to neither group. Rather, they affirm that the messengers are intermediaries in conveying Allah’s message; they believe in them and love them, but do not make pilgrimages to their graves or take their graves as places of worship. This is the true fulfilment of “the testimony that none has the right to be worshipped but Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah.”

Making them and their message known is part of believing in them. Concealing their graves, so that people are not led astray by them, is part of perfecting belief in Allah’s oneness and worshipping Him alone. The Companions and the community of Muhammad have upheld this.

---

## Page 13369

For this reason, Muslim scholars preserve accounts of people of learning and piety—the Companions, the Successors, and those who came after them, including leading scholars, men of piety, and just rulers—which acquaint people with these individuals, inspire praise and supplication for them, and ensure that they are remembered with honour. These accounts also convey something beneficial: either words from which others may benefit or righteous deeds in which they may follow their example. For scholars are the heirs of the prophets. The purpose concerning the prophets—may Allah Most High be pleased with them all—is to benefit from their words, the truths they conveyed, and their commands, and to follow their example in their deeds—may Allah’s blessings be upon them all.

As for those who have gone astray, such as Christians and the adherents of religious innovations, despite their excesses, their veneration of these figures’ graves and images, and their seeking intercession through them, they possess no accounts of these figures whose truth can be distinguished from falsehood. Rather, the two have become confused. Hardly any of their scholars can distinguish, within the religion they practise, between what came from the Messiah and what came from others, whether other prophets or their own religious teachers. Indeed, they have mixed truth with falsehood.

Likewise, among those who face the qiblah, the people of misguidance and religious innovation venerate a shaykh, a religious leader, or some other figure, associate him with Allah, invoke him besides Allah, seek his aid in distress, make vows to him, and make pilgrimages to his grave. They may even prostrate themselves before him and worship him more than they worship Allah, as Christians do. Yet they are among the most ignorant of his life and circumstances: they transmit loose reports about him with no chain of transmission

---

## Page 13370

and whose truth cannot be distinguished from their falsehood. Rather, most of what they preserve consists of exaggeration and extravagant claims that promote associating him with Allah. Muslims who understand Islam and do not mix it with anything else know Allah and worship Him alone. They know His prophets, affirm what they brought, and follow it. They know the people of learning and piety and benefit from their words and deeds. Those who have gone astray, however, are in darkness: they know neither Allah nor His prophets nor His allies. They do not distinguish between what Allah has commanded and what He has forbidden, or between the allies of the Most Merciful and the allies of Satan.

Undoubtedly, among those who face the qiblah are people who resemble the Jews and Christians in certain respects. Thus, in the two Sahīhs, Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī relates that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“You will surely follow the ways of those before you exactly, so much so that if they entered a lizard’s burrow, you would enter it too.” They asked, “O Messenger of Allah, do you mean the Jews and Christians?” He replied, “Who else?”

In Sahīh al-Bukhārī, Abū Hurayrah relates that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“My nation will surely follow the course of the nations before it, handspan by handspan and cubit by cubit.” They asked, “O Messenger of Allah, do you mean the Persians and Romans?” He replied, “Who else could it be?”

Imitating them in associating partners with Allah at the graves of prophets and righteous people is among the forms of imitation against which he warned his nation before his death, both in health and in sickness. In Sahīh Muslim, Jundub ibn ʿAbd Allāh said:

“I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace

---

## Page 13371

five days before his death, saying:

‘Before Allah, I disavow taking any of you as an intimate friend, for Allah has taken me as an intimate friend just as He took Abraham as an intimate friend. Were I to take anyone from my nation as an intimate friend, I would take Abū Bakr. Beware! Those before you used to take the graves of their prophets and righteous people as places of worship. Do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.’”

As for his cursing those who did this, the two Sahīhs relate from ʿĀ’ishah and Ibn ʿAbbās:

“When death approached the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he began placing a cloak over his face. Whenever it stifled him, he uncovered his face. While in that state, he said, ‘May Allah’s curse be upon the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship,’ warning against what they had done.”

The two Sahīhs also relate that ʿĀ’ishah said:

“During the illness from which he never recovered, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘May Allah curse the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.’”

ʿĀ’ishah said: “Were it not for that, his grave would have been left exposed, but he feared that it would be taken as a place of worship.” Another wording reads: “But he feared—or it was feared.”

Also related in the Sahīh, from Abū Hurayrah, is that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

This is Muslim’s wording. Both Muslim and al-Bukhārī also relate:

“May Allah destroy the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

The two Sahīhs relate from ʿĀ’ishah that Umm Habībah and Umm Salamah mentioned to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, a church they had seen in Abyssinia that contained images. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “Those people, whenever there died among them

---

## Page 13372

a righteous man, they would build a place of worship over his grave and make those images in it. Those will be the worst of creation in Allah’s sight on the Day of Resurrection.”

In the Musnad and the Sahīh of Abū Hātim, Ibn Masʿūd relates that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Among the worst of people are those who are still alive when the Hour overtakes them, and those who take graves as places of worship.”

This is a broad subject whose detailed treatment belongs elsewhere. It has already been discussed at length in refutations of people more distinguished than this man, explaining how they opposed the Book, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus on this subject and others. Since those people were more learned and more distinguished, the response to them was commensurate with their standing. Allah knows best.

A transcription of the four judges’ endorsements on the reverse of the fatwa of Shaykh Taqī al-Dīn Abū al-ʿAbbās Ibn Taymiyyah concerning “Traveling Solely to Visit the Graves of the Prophets”:

The statement transcribed on the inner side in answer to the question—that visiting the prophets is an innovation, or words to that effect, and that one may not avail oneself of concessions when traveling to visit the prophets—is false and is rejected. A number of scholars and leading imams have reported that visiting the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is a meritorious act and a Sunnah upon which there is consensus. The aforementioned mufti should be restrained from issuing such fatwas, which are invalid in the judgment of

---

## Page 13373

the scholars and leading imams. He should be prevented from issuing unconventional fatwas rejected by the four imams, imprisoned if he does not desist, and his case publicized so that people may beware of following him. Written by the servant in need of Allah, Muhammad ibn Ibrāhīm ibn Saʿd ibn Jamāʿah.

Beneath it:

Thus says Ahmad ibn ʿUmar al-Maqdisī al-Hanbalī.

Beneath it:

Muhammad ibn al-Jarīrī al-Hanafī says likewise. However, he must certainly be imprisoned now, unconditionally.

Beneath it:

The servant in need of Allah, Muhammad ibn Abī Bakr al-Mālikī, says likewise, if this is proven against him. Stern measures should be taken against him to the extent necessary to avert this and other harms.

This is a transcription of their handwritten endorsements in Egypt. Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. May Allah bless our master Muhammad, his family, and his Companions, and grant them abundant peace.

Shaykh al-Islam—may Allah grant him a home in Paradise, amen—said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.

There is no strength except through Allah, the Most High, the Magnificent.

Praise be to Allah. We praise Him, seek His help, and ask His forgiveness. We seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray; and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. I bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah alone, with no partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him, his family, and his Companions, and grant them abundant peace.

---

## Page 13374

Section

A response to what was written on a copy of the fatwa response, explaining its invalidity and that, by the consensus of the Muslims, a ruling based upon it is invalid on numerous grounds. These have been set out at length elsewhere. They are fifty grounds demonstrating the invalidity of what was written and of the ruling based upon it.

The first ground is that he attributed to the response something it did not contain, then based his ruling on that false attribution. Such a ruling is invalid by consensus. He alleged that the respondent had said, “Visiting the prophets is a religious innovation,” or had stated something to that effect. Yet the respondent neither said this nor reported it from any scholar. The response merely mentioned the scholars’ views concerning someone who travels solely to visit the graves of the prophets and the righteous: whether such travel is forbidden or permissible, and that both groups agreed that it is not recommended.

Neither group said this about visiting graves in general. Rather, the majority of them say that visiting graves is recommended. This is the correct view, as indicated by the authentic hadiths. They do not, however, say that travelling to visit them is recommended, just as the Muslims agree that attending mosques other than the three mosques is prescribed, and that attending them

---

## Page 13375

may be obligatory or recommended, as when attending them for Friday prayer or congregational prayer. They agree that travelling to mosques other than the three mosques is neither obligatory nor recommended. Likewise, visiting graves in the prescribed manner is recommended and is a sunnah, whereas travelling for that purpose is neither obligatory nor recommended according to either group.

The respondent did not state a view of his own in the response; rather, he reported the views of the Muslim scholars and their evidence. These people attributed to him what he had not said and argued from matters that are not disputed. They erred in what they reported and understood from the words of those who had reported a consensus, and in the evidence they used against him, in very many respects. The point of this ground, however, is that the person who wrote on the response represented the respondent as himself holding this view and as having said, “Visiting the prophets is a religious innovation.” This attribution to him is false, and a ruling based upon a false report is invalid by consensus.

The second ground:

Both groups of Muslim scholars agree that travelling solely to visit graves is neither obligatory nor recommended. Yet these people declared travelling to visit graves to be a sunnah instituted by the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not institute travelling for that purpose for his community, nor did the scholars of his law say that travelling to them is a sunnah. They have therefore issued a ruling contrary to the Sunnah and consensus, and this ruling is invalid by consensus. For the respondent mentioned the two views concerning someone who travels only to the graves without also intending to visit the mosque: the view of those who permitted this but did not deem it recommended

---

## Page 13376

and the view of those who prohibited it.

They did not merely reject one of the two views, for that would not contradict what the respondent had stated. Rather, they said:

“This aforementioned mufti should be restrained from issuing such fatwas, which are invalid in the view of the scholars.”

If what he stated in the response concerning the two views is invalidated, such travel must necessarily be deemed a recommended sunnah. Moreover, they cited reports of a consensus that the very travel concerning which he mentioned the two views is recommended.

The third ground:

They cited reports from scholars stating that visiting the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is an encouraged virtue and a sunnah established by consensus. These scholars reported a consensus concerning visitation, not concerning travel solely to visit the grave. Even if they had reported a consensus concerning travel for visitation, it is well known that Muslims intend to visit both the mosque and the grave; only an ignorant person intends to visit the grave without the mosque. When a visitor intends to visit both the mosque and the grave, the respondent did not mention the two views concerning that situation. He mentioned them only concerning someone who travels solely to visit graves. Nor did the response concern the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, specifically; rather, it concerned graves in general.

They treated that as a consensus concerning travel to all the other prophets’ graves, even though the respondent distinguished between the form of visitation prescribed by the Prophet, which Muslims unanimously agree is recommended, and that which they unanimously agree is not recommended, as well as that concerning which they disagree. Even if, in their view, the consensus they reported did not support what the respondent stated, it would not constitute evidence against him. Yet they treated it as evidence

---

## Page 13377

that the response was invalid. That would follow only if travel were considered recommended without qualification. They thus misrepresented those who reported the consensus, failing to understand their intent, and passed judgment on the basis of this false belief. Such a judgment is invalid by consensus.

The fourth ground:

They treated this report of consensus as contradicting the response, whereas it does not contradict it. Indeed, the mufti mentioned in his response that scholars consider visiting the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, recommended. He did not attribute to anyone the statement that visiting the Prophet’s grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is forbidden. A judgment based on a false report is invalid by consensus.

The fifth ground:

They treated visitation in general as recommended by consensus, without distinguishing between its prescribed and forbidden forms. Yet some forms of visitation are prescribed, while others are forbidden by consensus, as he explained in his response to the request for a fatwa. They rejected this distinction, thereby contradicting the consensus; a judgment based on that rejection is invalid by consensus.

The respondent did not reject travel for visitation whose prescribed status is unanimously agreed upon. Rather, he explained in his response the forms of travel and visitation upon which Muslims agree. This is discussed extensively in many passages of his writings and is well known as his position. He also mentioned the matters concerning which they disagree and those whose prohibition they agree upon. Had these people accepted the distinction, they would not have rejected the response. Thus, when they declared the response invalid in the view of the scholars, it became clear that they had failed to make this distinction.

---

## Page 13378

The sixth ground:

Visitation is of three kinds: one which scholars agree is recommended, one which they agree is forbidden, and one concerning which they disagree. The response mentions all three kinds. These people did not distinguish between matters of consensus and matters over which scholars disagree. Nor did they mention that matters over which scholars disagree must be referred to Allah and the Messenger. Rather, they deemed them rejected merely on their own say-so. This is invalid by consensus, and a judgment based on it is likewise invalid by consensus.

The respondent merely mentioned that both groups agree that travel is not recommended when undertaken solely to visit the grave of one of the prophets or righteous people. This is generally not the case with the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, for anyone acquainted with Islamic law will necessarily intend to visit the mosque along with the grave—especially knowing that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A prayer in this mosque of mine is better than a thousand prayers in any other mosque, except the Sacred Mosque.”

For this reason, a group of scholars cited this hadith as evidence that visiting his grave is recommended. The respondent did not state that there is consensus that this kind of visitation, undertaken by someone who knows Islamic law, is not recommended. How could he say that, when scholars explicitly describe it as recommended?

The seventh ground:

The consensus that visitation is a sunnah and a virtuous act is not a consensus concerning everything called visitation, nor a consensus on this wording. Rather, it is a consensus concerning the rights Allah has prescribed for him in his mosque. There are two views on whether it is disliked to call this a visit to his grave. Much of what is called visiting his grave is disputed or forbidden

---

## Page 13379

by consensus. Yet these people extended the consensus to matters disputed by scholars and invoked it in matters of disagreement. This is an error.

The eighth ground:

Matters disputed by scholars must be referred to Allah and the Messenger. These people did not refer them to Allah or the Messenger. Rather, they declared the statement false and rejected it, without any evidence from the Book of Allah or the Sunnah of His Messenger. This is invalid by consensus.

The ninth ground:

As for those who reported a consensus that travelling solely to visit the grave is recommended, the consensus is in fact only that travelling to his mosque is recommended. Travelling solely for the grave, however, is a matter of well-known disagreement. Disputed matters must be referred to Allah and the Messenger. These people did not refer the matters disputed by scholars to Allah and the Messenger. Rather, they claimed consensus concerning them and misrepresented those from whom they reported that consensus. Rebuking someone for a view on the grounds that it contradicts consensus, when it does not, is itself an error by consensus.

The tenth ground:

There is consensus that matters on which no consensus exists must be referred to Allah and the Messenger. If someone argues concerning such a matter on the basis of the Quran and Sunnah, he is in the right. The response mentions the disagreement and adduces evidence from the Quran and Sunnah concerning the disputed matters. These people rejected this and did not refer it to Allah and the Messenger. Rather, they argued against those who appealed

---

## Page 13380

to the Quran and Sunnah in disputed matters, and issued a ruling endorsing this rejection, which contradicts consensus. Such a ruling is invalid by consensus.

The eleventh ground:

The fatwa sets out matters on which there is consensus, such as the recommended form of visitation, matters scholars unanimously prohibit, and matters on which they disagree. This is the most that can be expected of those who issue fatwas. Yet these people classified it among the fatwas that scholars consider invalid. No Muslim scholar considers this distinction invalid, yet they declared it invalid and ruled accordingly. Such a ruling is invalid by consensus.

The twelfth ground:

No judge is entitled to settle a matter disputed by scholars through a judicial ruling. If no judge is entitled to say, “I have ruled that this view is correct and that the other view is to be rejected,” then, rather, on matters disputed by Muslim scholars or agreed upon by them, a ruler’s opinion is like that of any individual scholar, if he is himself a scholar. If he merely follows scholarly authority, he is no different from ordinary people who do likewise. Office and authority do not turn someone who is not a scholar qualified for independent legal reasoning into one. If the right to speak on knowledge and religion depended on authority and office, the caliph and the sultan would have the greatest right to speak on such matters, and people would have the greatest reason to seek their fatwas and refer to them concerning difficulties in knowledge and religion.

If, then, neither the caliph nor the sultan claims this for himself, nor compels his subjects to accept his ruling in favour of one view over another except on the basis of the Book of Allah and the Sunnah of His Messenger, someone whose authority is beneath that of the sultan has all the more reason not to overstep

---

## Page 13381

his bounds or assume a position that even Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿUthmān, and ʿAlī—the Rightly Guided Caliphs—had no right to occupy, let alone anyone below them. They, may Allah be pleased with them, required people only to follow the Book of their Lord and the Sunnah of their Prophet. ʿUmar, may Allah be pleased with him, used to say:

“I have sent my officials—that is, my deputies—to you only to teach you the Book of your Lord and the Sunnah of your Prophet, and to distribute your public revenues among you.”

Rather, these matters are to be addressed by those Muslim scholars who understand what is established by the evidence of the revealed law: the Book and Sunnah. Whoever has greater knowledge of the Book and Sunnah has a greater right than others to speak on these matters, even if he is not a judge. A judge has no authority to speak on them merely by virtue of being a judge. Rather, if he possesses knowledge, he may speak on them like any individual scholar. These people have therefore ruled on matters in which, by consensus, they have no authority to rule. Such a ruling is invalid by consensus.

The thirteenth ground:

General rulings that apply to Muslims collectively—whether agreed upon or disputed—are not matters for judges to settle by judicial rulings. Rather, a judge who is a scholar is like any individual scholar: he sets out the knowledge he possesses. A judge issues rulings only in specific cases. As for whether a particular act is obligatory, recommended, or forbidden, this belongs to the general rulings in which no one has authority to rule except Allah and His Messenger. Muslim scholars ascertain the ruling of Allah and His Messenger from the evidence bearing on it. These people have issued rulings on such general matters, and their ruling on this is

---

## Page 13382

invalid by consensus.

The fourteenth ground:

Only someone who knows the positions of Muslim scholars on these general questions—what they have agreed upon and what they have disputed—and who knows the Book and Sunnah and how to derive evidence from them may speak on these matters. What these people say reveals that they do not know what Muslim scholars have said about these questions, nor distinguish between matters of scholarly consensus and matters of disagreement. They do not know the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concerning these questions, nor distinguish between what he encouraged and what he forbade and did not prescribe. They do not know the authentic and weak hadiths on this subject. Indeed, they do not even know their own school’s position on these questions, nor do they have any transmitted statements from the four imams or the renowned scholars among their followers supporting what they have said and ruled. Rather, in these matters they are like ordinary students of jurisprudence who ought to seek knowledge of these questions. Indeed, none of them may issue fatwas, engage in debate, or write on these matters, let alone issue judicial rulings.

It is well known that if someone in this position rules on a matter in which he has no authority to rule, his ruling is forbidden by consensus. How much more so if he rules on a matter beyond his authority and rules contrary to consensus! For when a judge rules without either independent juristic reasoning or following scholarly authority, his ruling is forbidden by consensus.

The fifteenth ground:

A judge must be qualified to exercise independent juristic reasoning, according to some

---

## Page 13383

scholars, while others permit him to follow scholarly authority.

Even if these matters fell within their jurisdiction, these men have not followed any of the imams of the Muslims in what they have said, let alone exercised independent juristic reasoning. Rather, they have ruled without either independent juristic reasoning or following scholarly authority. Such a ruling is invalid by consensus, even if it concerned a Jew’s liability for ten specified dirhams. How much more so when they rule against Muslim scholars on general rulings over which, by consensus, they have no jurisdiction!

The sixteenth ground:

Even if they had jurisdiction over these matters and ruled in accordance with the Book, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus, they would not be entitled to issue a ruling until they had heard the statement and argument of the person against whom they were ruling, given him an opportunity to defend himself, and determined whether he had a response. Scholars hold two views on whether judgment may be issued against an absent party in matters involving rights, such as property claims.

Those who permit judgment against him say: “He retains the right to present his argument, which must be heard when he appears.” As for punishments and prescribed penalties, no judgment may be issued against an absent party. Yet these men have issued such a judgment against an absent person without allowing his statement to be heard or his argument to be presented. Even if this had been done to a Jew, the ruling would have been invalid by consensus. Hence all people of knowledge, religious commitment, and sound judgment condemn such a ruling and know that it is unjust.

The seventeenth ground:

If a judge were someone’s adversary in a dispute over a right, he would not be permitted to rule against his adversary, by the consensus of the Muslims. The same applies to “scholarly questions”: if a judge and another scholar disagree over the interpretation of a verse or

---

## Page 13384

a hadith, or over some scholarly question, the judge would not be entitled to rule against him, by consensus, for the two are adversaries in the matter they dispute. A judge may not rule against his own adversary, by consensus.

The eighteenth ground:

These questions are discussed in the books of scholars among the followers of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others. Yet these men have ruled on them contrary to the positions of the four imams, without knowing the positions of their own imams or those of other imams and scholars, or what the Sunnah and transmitted reports indicate. It is well known that such a ruling is invalid by consensus. Whoever among them claims that his ruling represents the scholars’ position should put that claim in his own handwriting and set out what the scholars have stated concerning these questions—the points of consensus and disagreement, and the evidence for them—so that it may become clear that the position he advances in opposition to the mufti’s answer is invalid. Otherwise, it is known that they have ruled contrary to the truth, and this is invalid by consensus.

The nineteenth ground:

Even if one of them knew his own school’s position, he would not be entitled to impose it upon the scholars of the Muslims, nor to say, “You must issue fatwas according to my school, and any school that differs from mine is invalid,” without establishing his school’s position through evidence from the Book and the Sunnah.

Were he to say, “Whoever differs from my school, his position must be rejected, and anyone issuing fatwas according to it must be prohibited from doing so and imprisoned,” his claim would itself be rejected, and he would deserve punishment for it, by consensus. How much more so when his ruling does not represent the position of any of the four imams! Rather, the fatwa issued by the mufti accords with consensus, unlike the position of the one who rejected his statement and contravened consensus.

---

## Page 13385

The twentieth ground:

Even if a scholar who issued many fatwas were to make mistakes in a hundred questions, that would not discredit him. Everyone other than the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is sometimes right and sometimes wrong.

If anyone were to prohibit a scholar from issuing fatwas altogether and order his imprisonment because he had made mistakes in certain questions, that would be invalid by consensus. Thus, the ruling prohibiting him from issuing fatwas and ordering his imprisonment is invalid by consensus. How much more so when the mufti’s answer accords with the Sunnah of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the position of the scholars of his community!

The twenty-first ground:

Suppose a mufti issues fatwas on questions of Islamic law—“questions of legal rulings”—according to one of two positions held by Muslim scholars, adducing evidence from the Book and the Sunnah and explaining that this position, rather than the other, is what the Book and the Sunnah indicate. Whatever the subject—sales, marriage, divorce, pilgrimage, visitation, or anything else—no one is entitled to compel him to adopt the other position without evidence from the Book or the Sunnah. Nor, by consensus, may anyone rule that he must adhere to a particular position or prohibit him from holding a different one. How much more so if someone imposes a blanket prohibition on him and orders his imprisonment! By the consensus of the Muslims, this is among the most invalid of rulings.

The twenty-second ground:

If a judge supposed there to be consensus where none existed, and compelled people to adopt that position because he believed it to be agreed upon, without adducing evidence from the Book or the Sunnah, while there was disagreement of which he was unaware, he would be mistaken in compelling people

---

## Page 13386

to adopt it, according to consensus, unless it was supported by the Book or the Sunnah.

The twenty-third ground:

Whenever a judge contravenes a scriptural text or consensus, his ruling must be overturned, by agreement of the imams. These men’s ruling contravenes both scriptural texts and consensus in many respects, and therefore warrants being overturned by consensus.

The twenty-fourth ground:

This ruling and others like it resemble the rulings repeatedly issued in the past in matters comparable to this case. Each of those rulings is invalid by consensus on numerous grounds, and the same applies to this one.

The twenty-fifth ground:

Besides being invalid by consensus, these rulings stir up strife, divide the hearts of the community, entail aggression against Muslims and those in authority over them, cause them harm, and bring about discord among Muslims. Judging by what Allah has revealed brings well-being in this world and the Hereafter, whereas judging by other than what Allah has revealed brings ruin in this world and the Hereafter. This ruling must therefore be overturned by consensus.

The twenty-sixth ground:

If something that causes harm to Muslims is among the things commanded by Allah and His Messenger, then in doing it they are obeying Allah and His Messenger, and their reward for it rests with Allah, as in the case of jihad. But if what harms them is something that neither Allah nor His Messenger has commanded, it must be rejected by consensus. And rulings such as these, which harm Muslims and those in authority over them

---

## Page 13387

and contravene the Sunnah and consensus, must therefore be rejected by consensus.

The twenty-seventh ground:

They said: “This mufti should be restrained from issuing fatwas like these, which the scholars and leading imams consider invalid.” Yet it is their own statement that the scholars and leading imams consider invalid. Anyone who claims that the position of the scholars and leading imams is what the scholars and leading imams consider invalid has made a statement—and issued a ruling based upon it—that is invalid by consensus.

For this fatwa expresses the position of the scholars and leading imams: it includes the position of Mālik and other leading imams. As for the other view, the scholars and leading imams [have] no position other than what is mentioned in this fatwa. What these men have stated is not known to have been held by any of the scholars and leading imams.

The twenty-eighth ground:

They said that he should be prevented from issuing unusual fatwas rejected by the four imams and other imams of the Muslims. Issuing such a ruling is invalid by consensus, for the four imams agree that a judge’s ruling is to be overturned only when it contravenes the Book, the Sunnah, consensus, or something equivalent. A ruling that accords with the position of some qualified jurists in “matters open to independent juristic judgment” is not to be overturned merely because it differs from the position of the four imams.

Whatever a judge may rule upon, a mufti may also issue a fatwa upon, by consensus. Indeed, issuing a fatwa is subject to less stringent requirements, since a judge’s ruling is binding, whereas a mufti’s fatwa is not. Whatever the four imams permit a judge to rule upon, they permit a mufti to issue a fatwa upon with even greater reason. Anyone who rules that issuing such fatwas must be prohibited has therefore contravened the four imams and all the other imams of the Muslims. Thus, it is their own statement that contravenes the four imams and all the other imams of the Muslims

---

## Page 13388

and is therefore invalid by consensus.

The twenty-ninth ground:

Every school contains positions held by some of its adherents that were not held by its founding imam. Every school also contains positions that differ from those of the four imams. They report these as positions within the school and do not declare them invalid without proof, particularly when a position is derived from the principles of the school’s founding imam and passages from their statements are shown to support it, as their followers do in many questions. The respondent has cited statements of the four imams and earlier scholars whom they revere, as well as statements of their predecessors, which make the positions of Muslim scholars known.

Thus, invalidating a position merely because it differs from those of the four imams itself contradicts the positions of the four imams and their followers. It is therefore invalid by consensus.

The thirtieth ground:

None of the fatwas concerning visitation and divorce to which they objected, issued by the mufti in question, falls outside the four schools. Either his fatwa expresses the position of all the adherents of the four schools—as does his fatwa on this question, “the question of visitation,” for what he stated is the position of all the adherents of the four schools, indeed, of all Muslim scholars, who have set out both what they agree upon and what they dispute—or his fatwa expresses the position of some of the four imams or some of those affiliated with their schools, “as in questions of divorce.” For the disputed questions concerning divorce have been disputed among the adherents of the four schools, and the mufti

---

## Page 13389

in question has issued no fatwa on these matters except in accordance with the position of some of them; indeed, no fatwa on these matters can be issued otherwise. Whoever condemns what he does not know, judges without knowledge, and contradicts the scriptural texts and scholarly consensus—his judgment is invalid by consensus.

The thirty-first ground:

As for their statement, “He is to be imprisoned if he does not desist, and his case is to be publicized so that people guard against following his example”—only someone who openly promotes innovation in the Muslims’ religion, regards it as recommended, calls people to it, and rules that those who enjoin the Sunnah and call to it should be punished deserves such treatment. Travelling to visit graves is precisely the innovation that none of the imams of the Muslims regarded as recommended. Likewise, treating visitation to graves as a single category, without distinguishing between visitation sanctioned by Islamic law and innovated visitation, is an error by the agreement of the Muslims. The same applies to equating “the visitation to the Prophet sanctioned by Islamic law,” in which Muslims travel to the mosque of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with travelling to visit someone else’s grave. All this contradicts the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the consensus of his community.

Thus, someone who enjoins this is more deserving of being restrained and having his error publicized, so that people guard against following his example, than someone who issues fatwas in accordance with the Sunnah and scholarly consensus. Yet it is Allah, glorified be He, who brings this about: He is the One who exposes these people’s error throughout the East and the West, in this age and in the ages to come, just as He has done with all others who introduced innovations into religion and contradicted the revealed law of the foremost of the messengers. For in his response, the mufti set out what Muslims unanimously agree is recommended

---

## Page 13390

and what they unanimously agree is prohibited, as well as the matters over which they disagree. He did not prohibit visitation altogether, either explicitly or implicitly. The consensus they cited agrees with what he stated rather than contradicting it. Indeed, he is among the foremost advocates of recommending the visitation upon which Muslims are unanimously agreed. He does not regard everything called “visitation” as recommended, equating the religion of the Most Merciful with the religion of Satan, as these people have done. They have even condemned those who distinguish between the religion of the Most Merciful and the religion of Satan.

The thirty-second ground:

Accepting the statement of a judge or anyone else without proof, despite its contradicting the Sunnah, is contrary to the consensus of the Muslims. Rather, this is the religion of the Christians, who “took their rabbis and monks as lords besides Allah, and also the Messiah, son of Mary, although they were commanded to worship only one God. There is no deity except Him. Glorified is He above what they associate with Him!” The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“They made unlawful things lawful for them and lawful things unlawful for them, and they obeyed them. That was their worship of them.”

Muslims unanimously agree that whatever they dispute must be referred back to Allah and the Messenger. Yet these people did not refer the matter disputed by Muslims back to Allah and the Messenger; instead, they ruled that it be rejected on the basis of their own statements. This is invalid by the consensus of the Muslims. Moreover, they ruled according to a third position contrary to both positions held by Muslim scholars. Thus, both they and their ruling departed from the consensus of the Muslims. This is invalid by the consensus of the Muslims.

---

## Page 13391

The thirty-third ground:

Their words acknowledge that the mufti’s fatwa represents the position of some Muslim scholars. Whatever Muslims dispute must therefore be referred back to Allah and the Messenger, and nothing but the Book of Allah or the Sunnah of His Prophet may adjudicate it. Yet these people ruled on a matter disputed by Muslims without recourse to the Book of Allah or the Sunnah of His Messenger. Such a ruling is invalid by the consensus of the Muslims. This would apply even if his fatwa represented the position of only some of them. How much more so, then, when he cited the two positions agreed upon by the Muslims? The position they condemned is that of the leading imams, whereas their own position has been transmitted by none of the imams, great or small.

The thirty-fourth ground:

Even supposing that the mufti issued an erroneous fatwa, punishment is not permissible until the proof has been established against him. It is therefore necessary to explain how the Book and the Sunnah demonstrate his error and to answer the evidence he relied upon. The evidence must be presented and the opposing argument answered. Otherwise, if each side has an argument, neither may be declared correct without grounds for preferring one argument over the other. These people did none of this. Thus, even if the mufti had been mistaken, they would not have established the proof against him. How much more so when he is correct and they are mistaken? The ruling of judges such as these is invalid by consensus.

The thirty-fifth ground:

When the scriptural evidence has been made clear to the mufti, either the correct position becomes clear to him, or else he is in the same position as his fellow scholars who hold a less well-supported view. It is well known that such scholars do not deserve punishment, imprisonment, or being barred from

---

## Page 13392

issuing fatwas altogether, by the consensus of the Muslims. This ruling is invalid by the consensus of the Muslims.

The thirty-sixth ground:

Compelling people to observe what Allah and His Messenger have not made binding upon them, and preventing them from following what the Book and the Sunnah teach, is forbidden by the consensus of the Muslims. A ruling to that effect is likewise invalid by their consensus. These people have neither supported their position with evidence from the Book of Allah or the Sunnah of His Messenger, nor answered the argument of the one who cited the Book and the Sunnah. Imposing such an obligation and issuing a ruling to enforce it are invalid by consensus.

The thirty-seventh ground:

When Muslim scholars differ on an issue, holding two positions, those who come after them may not introduce a third position, for that third position would contravene their consensus.

Muslims have differed over travelling to places other than the three mosques, holding two positions: either it is forbidden, or it is permissible but not recommended. To hold that it is recommended is therefore a third position that contravenes the consensus. No Muslim scholar has held that travelling to visit graves is recommended while travelling to mosques is not. Rather, some scholars are reported to have held that travelling to mosques is recommended and becomes obligatory through a vow. As for travelling to graves, none of them held that it is recommended or that it becomes obligatory through a vow.

They all agree that going to mosques is better than going to graves. For where visiting the prophets and the righteous is prescribed, it is not prescribed five times each day and night, whereas attending the mosque is prescribed five times each day and night. Thus, attending the mosque takes precedence over visiting graves, by consensus.

---

## Page 13393

The thirty-eighth ground:

Going to the Mosque of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, making it one’s destination, and travelling there take precedence over going to his grave—were the chamber open—and travelling to it, by the consensus of the Muslims. The Companions used to attend his mosque five times each day and night, yet none of them entered the chamber beside it. They knew that he had forbidden them to take graves as places of worship or to make his grave a site of recurring gatherings or an idol. He had also told them:

“Invoke blessings upon me wherever you may be.”

Likewise, they knew that invoking blessings and peace upon him in the mosque was better than doing so at his grave. Everyone who travels for the visit actually travels to the mosque, whether he intends that or not. Travelling to the mosque is recommended by explicit scriptural evidence and consensus. In his answer, the respondent mentioned both the visitation agreed upon by consensus and that over which there is disagreement. These people, however, have turned away from enjoining what Allah, His Messenger, and the scholars of his ummah have enjoined, and from recommending what Allah, His Messenger, and all the scholars of his ummah love. They have understood the scholars’ words in ways those scholars did not intend.

For al-Qādī ʿIyād, whose words he quoted, explicitly stated what his imam and the majority of his followers explicitly stated: that it is not permissible to travel to places other than the three mosques. He did not say that making the grave, rather than the mosque, one’s destination is recommended. Rather, in reporting the scholars’ statements about the merit of visiting the Messenger, he made his intended meaning clear. He also related that Mālik disapproved of remaining there after offering the greeting of peace. This amounts to his disapproval of visitation as practised by most ordinary people. And these people

---

## Page 13394

have deemed anything termed visitation recommended and have condemned those who distinguish between visitation prescribed by the religion and innovated visitation. He mentioned that it is disliked for the people of Medina to stand at the grave, even if they intend merely to offer the greeting of peace, except when travelling. He also mentioned that making the mosque one’s destination is recommended and that Muslims have always done so. He said:

“Section on the ruling on visiting his grave:

Visiting his grave is a Sunnah unanimously accepted among Muslims and a virtuous deed that is encouraged.”

He said:

“Mālik disapproved of saying, ‘We visited the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.’”

Then he said:

“Ishāq ibn Ibrāhīm, the jurist, said: ‘Those who perform Hajj have always made a practice of passing through Medina and intending to pray in the Mosque of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, seeking blessings through seeing his Rawdah (garden), his pulpit, his grave, the place where he sat, the places his hands touched and his feet trod, and the pillar against which he used to lean, and where Gabriel would descend upon him with revelation; and through the Companions, Successors, and leading scholars of the Muslims who frequented it and made it their destination, while reflecting on all of this.’”

He thus made clear that the consensus they reported includes the intention to pray in his mosque, and that the grave is among the sites associated with him. Yet these people claimed that he reported a consensus on travelling solely to the grave. He mentioned neither that nor anything indicating it; rather, he mentioned several things to the contrary. These people were mistaken about what he reported and did not understand the Sunnah and consensus relating to this matter. This ruling is invalid by consensus.

---

## Page 13395

The thirty-ninth point:

Even supposing that a scholar who issues many fatwas gave rulings on several matters contrary to the established Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and to the practice of the Rightly Guided Caliphs, it would not be permissible to prohibit him altogether from issuing fatwas. Rather, his errors in those matters should be explained to him. In every generation of the Companions, the Successors, and the Muslim scholars after them, there have always been scholars in this position.

Ibn ‘Abbās, may Allah be pleased with them both, held views on “temporary marriage and currency exchange” that contradicted the authentic Sunnah. The Companions objected to those views, but they did not prohibit him altogether from issuing fatwas. Rather, they explained to him the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, which contradicted his position. Thus, ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him, narrated to him that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had forbidden temporary marriage. Abū Sa‘īd al-Khudrī, may Allah be pleased with him, and others narrated to him the Prophet’s prohibition of usury through unequal exchange. They did not reject his fatwas merely on the strength of their own statements and judgments, nor prohibit him altogether from issuing fatwas. There are many such examples.

A blanket prohibition is therefore a judgment contrary to what Allah has revealed, and it is invalid by the agreement of the Muslims—even if his position on the matters they disputed with him had contradicted the Sunnah. How much more so when the Sunnah is on his side—indeed, when the consensus of the Muslim scholars also supports him on the questions of visitation to which they objected! This shows that this judgment is among the most utterly invalid judgments in Islam and among the gravest alterations of the religion of Islam, by the consensus of the Muslims.

The fortieth point:

The Muslim scholars have known these matters from the time of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, until the present, for all Muslims

---

## Page 13396

need them; it is therefore impossible for some people to know the truth concerning them while the early generations and the imams did not.

The respondent has written volumes on these matters, explaining the statements and actions of the Companions and the views of the Muslim scholars: what they agreed upon and what they disputed. He has distinguished the authentic prophetic hadiths from the weak ones, presented the scholars’ discussions of them, and explained the errors of those who opposed him in their writings on the subject, addressing all this at length.

Even if these people had adopted some of the scholars’ opinions, they would not thereby have established any proof against him. How much worse, then, when they have said what contradicts the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the consensus of the Muslim scholars concerning such a momentous matter—one which the Messenger explained to his community and which its scholars have understood, generation after generation, until the present! It is well known that such a judgment is invalid by the consensus of the Muslims.

The forty-first point:

Had they adopted some of the scholars’ opinions, believing them to be undisputed, they would have been a group comparable to those who suppose that the Sunnah for a visitor is to stand at the grave, face it, and offer greetings of peace to its occupant. Someone may suppose that this is a matter of consensus, but he is mistaken. Some scholars did not consider facing the direction of prayer recommended, while others did not consider standing at the grave recommended, as the reports from them have made clear in the relevant passages. These people, however, have issued a judgment based on a view that no Muslim scholar has ever held, and that is invalid by consensus.

The forty-second point:

If a mufti were to say what they have said, he would have to be censured

---

## Page 13397

and barred from doing so, and imprisoned if he did not cease issuing such fatwas, because this contradicts the Sunnah and consensus. How much more so, then, if a judge says this and compels people to abide by it! He is all the more deserving of being restrained and punished for this, like the proponents of religious innovations—the Khawārij, the Rāfidah, and others—and those who introduce an innovation, impose it on people, show hostility towards anyone who disagrees with them about it, and consider it lawful to punish him.

Innovations involving associating partners with Allah, taking graves as idols, making pilgrimages to them, and calling upon and worshipping others besides Allah are among the innovations of the Khawārij and the Rāfidah.

Allah knows best. All praise belongs to Allah alone. May Allah bless Muhammad, his family, and his Companions, and grant them peace.

---

## Page 13398

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.

Allah is sufficient for us, and He is the best Disposer of affairs. All praise belongs to Allah. We seek His help and forgiveness, and we seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whomever Allah guides, none can lead astray; and whomever He leads astray, none can guide. We bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah alone, with no partner, and we bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him and his family and grant him peace.

To proceed: Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah says:

When I learned what the ruler, the sultan—may Allah support him and guide him aright—intended by his directive, I wrote a brief statement at that time because the person present pressed for a prompt reply. This also gives a brief account of the circumstances. If the ruler—may Allah support him and guide him aright—so orders, I will bring before him many Muslim works, both early and recent, containing the words of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—

---

## Page 13399

and of the Companions and the Successors, as well as the statements of the four imams of the Muslims, other imams, and the followers of the four imams, all agreeing with what I wrote in the fatwa. The fatwa is brief and does not allow for detailed exposition. No one can cite anything to the contrary from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—or from the Companions, the Successors, or the imams of the Muslims, whether the four imams or others.

Only those who speak without knowledge have opposed this. They have no transmitted account supporting what they say from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—or from the Companions, the Successors, or the imams of the Muslims. Nor can such a person produce any authoritative book transmitting the teachings of the Muslim imams that supports his claim. He does not know how the Companions and the Successors used to conduct themselves when visiting the grave of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—or other graves.

My handwritten statement of the fatwa I issued is available, and I have many similar statements written in my own hand. This is to be shown to everyone regarded as learned, in the East and the West. Whoever claims to possess knowledge contradicting it should write a detailed response in his own hand, identifying those who expressed his position before him and the evidence on which they relied.

Then, when the ruler, the sultan—may Allah support him—has examined what I have written and what others have written, I know that the truth will be as plain as the sun. Even the humblest of the sultan’s young attendants will recognize it. No sultan like him has been seen in these times; may Allah increase him in knowledge, sound judgment, and support. Everyone can recognize the truth, for the truth with which Allah sent the messengers cannot be confused with anything else

---

## Page 13400

by those who know, just as a skilled assayer cannot confuse pure gold with adulterated gold. Allah Most High has made the proof clear and the path plain through Muhammad, the last of the messengers, the finest of the prophets, and the best of all Allah’s creation.

The scholars are the heirs of the prophets. It is their duty to explain what the Messenger brought and refute whatever contradicts it. A scholar must therefore “first” know what the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—actually said, for fabricated hadiths are numerous. One person associated with religious scholarship has written a work on this question and similar matters, including all manner of false reports attributed to the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and the Companions, by which the ignorant are deceived. He did not deliberately lie; rather, he loves and reveres the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—but lacks the expertise to distinguish truth from falsehood. When he finds that an author writing about the merits of particular places or other subjects has attributed a report to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—or to the Companions, he assumes it is authentic and builds upon it, although that report is weak, or even fabricated, according to those well versed in his Sunnah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Once a scholar has distinguished what the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said from what he did not say, he must understand his intent, grasp the meaning of his words, bring the hadiths together, and consider related reports alongside one another. He must thus bring together what Allah and His Messenger have brought together and distinguish between what Allah and His Messenger have distinguished. This is the knowledge that benefits Muslims and that must be received and accepted. Through it, the imams of the Muslims attained their eminence, including the four imams and others

---

## Page 13401

—may Allah be pleased with them all.

The ruler, the sultan of the Muslims—may Allah strengthen him and guide him aright—has the foremost duty to uphold the religion of Islam and what the Messenger, peace be upon him, brought, and to restrain those who oppose it, speak about religion without knowledge, command what the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—forbade, or seek to extinguish his religion, whether through ignorance or desire.

Allah has declared His Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—free of these two failings. He, Most High, says:

“By the star when it descends! Your companion has neither strayed nor erred. Nor does he speak from his own desire. It is only a revelation sent down.”

And He, Most High, says of those who oppose him:

“They follow nothing but conjecture and what their souls desire, although guidance has already come to them from their Lord.”

They oppose his revealed law and the way of the Companions, the Successors, and the imams of the Muslims who know his Sunnah and his purposes and strive, to the best of their ability, to follow him—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. May Allah be pleased with them all.

Thus, once the matter becomes clear to the ruler, the sultan—may Allah grant him might—he is the one who wields the sword and bears the foremost responsibility for waging jihad in Allah’s cause through force, so that Allah’s word may be supreme, all worship may be devoted to Allah alone, and the testimony that there is no god but Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah may be clearly fulfilled; so that the reality of Allah’s oneness and of the mission of the Messenger whom Allah made the best and last of His messengers may become manifest; and so that the guidance and religion of truth with which he was sent, and the light revealed to him, may become manifest, and all this may be safeguarded

---

## Page 13402

from what ignorant and deceitful people mix into it—those who lie about Allah and His Messenger, are ignorant of His religion, introduce into it innovations resembling those of the polytheists, and belittle the Messenger’s revealed law, his Sunnah, and the affirmation of Allah’s oneness with which he was sent. Belittling his religion, Sunnah, and revealed law entails disparaging and impugning him, for which the perpetrator deserves a corresponding punishment.

The rulers of the Muslims therefore bear the foremost responsibility for supporting Allah and His Messenger, striving in His cause, exalting Allah’s religion, and upholding the revealed law of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. This is the finest of revealed laws, with which Allah sent the last of the messengers and the best of the prophets. They must also uphold its teachings: affirming Allah’s oneness, worshipping Him without partners, and worshipping Him according to what He has commanded and prescribed, not according to desires and innovations.

Whatever favors Allah has granted those in authority, whatever blessings He has bestowed upon them in this world, and whatever blessings they hope to receive from Allah in the Hereafter come only through their following the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and supporting the truth he brought.

The ruler—may Allah strengthen him and guide him aright—has sought the objective intended by what I have written. Its purpose is obedience to Allah, Mighty and Majestic, and His Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and that we worship Allah alone, associating nothing with Him. Worship can only be in accordance with the revealed law of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. This consists of what Allah Most High has made obligatory, such as the five daily prayers, fasting the month of Ramadan, and pilgrimage to the Sacred House; or what He has recommended, such as prayer at night

---

## Page 13403

and travelling to the Mosque of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and al-Aqsā Mosque to pray, recite the Quran, remember Allah, observe devotional retreats, and perform other such acts there. This also includes invoking blessings and peace upon the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—when entering and leaving the mosque and during prayer, and following the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—in his practices in mosques, when visiting graves, and in other matters. For religion consists in obeying his commands and following what he prescribed for his community.

One must therefore not go beyond his Sunnah in the acts of worship he performed, such as going to Qubāʾ Mosque and praying there, visiting the martyrs of Uhud, and visiting the graves of those buried in al-Baqīʿ.

Whatever Allah and His Messenger do not love, and which is not recommended, is not among the acts of worship and obedience through which one draws near to Allah, Mighty and Majestic. This includes the religious practices of innovators among the polytheists, the People of the Book, and those who imitate them. They have forms of worship for which Allah neither sent down a scripture nor sent a messenger: worshipping created beings, such as the stars, angels, or prophets, or worshipping images fashioned in their likeness, as Christians do in their churches, saying that they seek their intercession.

It is reported in al-Sahīh that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—used to say in his sermons:

“The best speech is the speech of Allah, the best guidance is the guidance of Muhammad, the worst matters are those newly introduced, and every innovation is misguidance.”

This means whatever constitutes an innovation according to the revealed law. A practice may be prescribed, yet be called an innovation when performed after his time, as in the words of ʿUmar—may Allah be pleased

---

## Page 13404

with him—concerning the Ramadan night prayers, when he gathered the people behind a single reciter and said:

“What an excellent innovation this is! Yet the prayer they sleep through is better.”

The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—had already established the Ramadan night prayers, saying:

“Allah has made fasting in Ramadan obligatory upon you, and I have established its night prayers as a Sunnah for you.”

During his lifetime—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—they would pray in separate groups: a man would pray alone, while another would lead a group, each group praying separately. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—also led them in congregational prayer on several occasions. He said:

“When a man prays with the imam until the imam finishes, he is credited with a whole night in prayer.”

However, he did not maintain this congregational prayer regularly, as with the five daily prayers, for fear that it would become obligatory upon them. After his death, they were safe from any additional obligation being imposed, so ʿUmar gathered them behind Ubayy ibn Kaʿb.
