We must love the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—so much that he is dearer to us than our own selves, our fathers, our sons, our families, and our wealth. We must honour and revere him, obey him inwardly and outwardly, be loyal to those who are loyal to him, and show enmity towards those who show enmity towards him. We must know that there is no path to Allah except by following him—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. No one can be a friend of Allah, or even a believer, or attain felicity and escape punishment, unless he believes in him and follows him inwardly and outwardly. There is no means of drawing near to Allah, Mighty and Majestic, except through faith in him and obedience to him.

He is the best of all earlier and later generations, the Seal of the Prophets, and the one singled out on the Day of Resurrection for the greatest intercession, by which Allah has distinguished him above all the other prophets. His are the Praiseworthy Station and the raised banner—the Banner of Praise. Adam and those

---

## Page 13405

after him will be under his banner. He will be the first to ask for the gate of Paradise to be opened:

“The keeper will say, ‘Who are you?’ He will reply, ‘I am Muhammad.’ The keeper will say, ‘I was commanded not to open it for anyone before you.’”

He has prescribed obligations for his community and instituted recommended practices for them. Pilgrimage to the House of Allah is obligatory, while travelling to his mosque and al-Aqsā Mosque to pray, recite the Quran, remember Allah, supplicate, and observe devotional retreat is recommended by the agreement of the Muslims. When one comes to his mosque, one greets him with peace and invokes blessings upon him.

Greetings of peace and blessings are also invoked upon him during prayer, for Allah says:

“Indeed, Allah bestows blessings upon the Prophet, and His angels pray for him. O you who believe, invoke blessings upon him and greet him with peace.”

Whoever invokes blessings upon him once, Allah bestows blessings upon him ten times; and whoever greets him with peace, Allah sends peace upon him ten times.

One should also ask Allah to grant him al-wasīlah, as established in the authentic collection, where he said:

“When you hear the caller to prayer, repeat what he says, then invoke blessings upon me. Whoever invokes blessings upon me once, Allah bestows blessings upon him ten times in return. Then ask Allah to grant me al-wasīlah, for it is a rank in Paradise befitting only one of Allah’s servants, and I hope to be that servant. Whoever asks Allah to grant me al-wasīlah will be entitled to my intercession on the Day of Resurrection.”

Narrated by Muslim.

Al-Bukhārī also narrated that he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever says upon hearing the call to prayer, ‘O Allah, Lord of this perfect call and the prayer about to be established, grant Muhammad al-wasīlah and pre-eminence, and raise him to the Praiseworthy Station which You have promised him. Indeed, You never break Your promise,’ will be entitled to my intercession on the Day of Resurrection.”

This is enjoined. And greeting him with peace at

---

## Page 13406

his honoured grave is permissible, for it is reported in the Sunan collections that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“No one greets me with peace but Allah restores my soul to me so that I may return his greeting.”

Wherever a person invokes blessings and peace upon him, in the east or the west, Allah conveys those blessings and greetings to him. For it is reported in the Sunan collections, from Aws ibn Aws, that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Invoke blessings upon me abundantly on Friday and the night preceding it, for your blessings are presented to me.”

They asked, “How will our blessings be presented to you when you have decayed?”—that is, become dust.

He replied, “Allah has forbidden the earth to consume the bodies of the prophets.”

For this reason, he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Do not make my grave an ʿīd (a place of recurring observance). Invoke blessings upon me wherever you are, for your blessings reach me.”

Narrated by Abū Dāwūd and others. Thus, blessings reach him from afar just as they reach him from nearby.

Al-Nasāʾī also reports that he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Allah has angels who travel throughout the earth, conveying to me the greetings of peace from my community.”

Allah has commanded us to invoke blessings upon him. He has prescribed for us in every prayer that we praise Allah with expressions of reverence, then say:

“Peace be upon you, O Prophet, and Allah’s mercy and blessings.”

This greeting reaches him from the east and the west. The same applies when we invoke blessings upon him, saying:

“O Allah, bestow blessings upon Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, as You bestowed blessings upon the family of Abraham. Indeed, You are Praiseworthy, Glorious. And bless Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, as You blessed the family of Abraham. Indeed, You are Praiseworthy, Glorious.”

During his lifetime and the times of Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿUthmān, and ʿAlī, the Muslims used to pray

---

## Page 13407

in his mosque and greet him with peace during prayer. They would likewise greet him with peace when entering and leaving the mosque, without needing to go to the honoured grave or turn towards it and raise their voices in greeting, as some pilgrims do.

Rather, this is an innovation that no scholar has recommended. Indeed, the scholars disapproved of raising one’s voice in his mosque. When ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—saw two men raising their voices in his mosque and recognized that they were not local residents, he said:

“Did you not know that voices must not be raised in the mosque of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace? Had you been residents of this city, I would have given you a painful beating.”

He excused them on account of their ignorance and did not punish them.

When the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—died, he was buried in the chamber of ʿĀʾishah—may Allah be pleased with her. Her chamber and those of his other wives stood to the east and on the qiblah side of the mosque; none of them was within the mosque itself. This remained the case until the era of the Companions in Medina had come to an end.

Then, during the caliphate of al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān, about a year after the pledge of allegiance to him, the mosque was expanded and the chamber was incorporated into it out of necessity. Al-Walīd wrote to his governor, ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, instructing him to purchase the chambers from their owners, the heirs of the Prophet’s wives—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—for all his wives had by then died, may Allah be pleased with them. He instructed him to purchase the chambers and add them to the mosque. He therefore demolished them and incorporated their sites into the mosque, while ʿĀʾishah’s chamber remained as it was. It was kept locked, and no one was allowed to enter to reach the grave of the Prophet

---

## Page 13408

—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—whether to pray beside it, supplicate, or for any other purpose, up to the time when ‘Ā’ishah was alive. She died more than twenty or thirty years before the chamber was incorporated into the mosque, during the caliphate of Mu‘āwiyah. He was succeeded by his son Yazīd, then Ibn al-Zubayr during the civil strife, then ‘Abd al-Malik ibn Marwān, and then his son al-Walīd. Al-Walīd’s reign began after 80 AH, by which time most of the Companions had died. It is said that none remained in Medina except Jābir ibn ‘Abd Allāh—may Allah be pleased with them both—for he was the last of them to die there, in 78 AH, ten years before the chamber was incorporated into the mosque.

During ‘Ā’ishah’s lifetime—may Allah be pleased with her—people would enter her chamber to hear hadith, seek her legal opinions, and visit her. Those who entered would not go to the honoured grave to pray, supplicate, or for any other purpose. Sometimes, however, someone would ask her to show him the graves, and she would do so. They were neither flush with the ground nor raised high, and were covered with pebbles from the courtyard. There is disagreement over whether they were mounded or flat-topped. The account in al-Bukhārī describes them as mounded. Sufyān al-Tammār said that he saw the grave of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and that it was mounded.

Nevertheless, someone entering would greet the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—with peace, because he said:

“No one greets me with peace but Allah restores my soul to me so that I may return his greeting.”

This greeting was prescribed for those who entered the chamber. It is the greeting offered from close by, which the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—returns to the person offering it. As for the general greeting of peace

---

## Page 13409

offered outside the chamber and in every place, it is like greeting him with peace during prayer, and is akin to invoking blessings upon him. Allah bestows ten blessings upon whoever invokes blessings upon him once, and sends peace ten times upon whoever greets him with peace once. This is what Muslims have been commanded to do specifically for the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Greeting him with peace at his grave, by contrast, is something he shares with all believers: every believer may be greeted with peace at his grave, just as he is greeted when met during his lifetime.

As for invoking blessings and peace in every place, and singling out a particular individual for invocations of blessing, this has been commanded only with regard to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It is upon him that people have been commanded to invoke blessings and abundant peace. May Allah bless him and his family and grant them abundant peace.

His wives’ chambers were outside the mosque, on its eastern side and on the side facing the direction of prayer. Thus he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Between my house and my pulpit is a garden from the gardens of Paradise.”

This is the wording of the two Sahīh collections. The wording “my grave” is not found in the Sahīh, for there was no grave at that time. His mosque was granted its distinction only because of him—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—for he was the one who built it and founded it upon piety. It is established in the two Sahīh collections that he said:

“One prayer in this mosque of mine is better than a thousand prayers in any other mosque, except the Sacred Mosque.”

The majority of scholars hold that the Sacred Mosque is the most excellent of mosques, and that a prayer in it is equivalent to one hundred thousand prayers. This was narrated by Ahmad, al-Nasā’ī, and others

---

## Page 13410

with a good chain of transmission. The Sacred Mosque owes its distinction to him and to Abraham, the intimate friend of Allah. Abraham built the House and, at Allah’s command, called people to perform pilgrimage to it, but did not make this obligatory upon them. Thus, Hajj was not obligatory in the early days of Islam; it was prescribed only later. The correct view is that it became obligatory in the year when the surah Āl ‘Imrān was revealed, when the delegation from Najrān arrived, in 9 or 10 AH.

Those who say it became obligatory in 6 AH base their argument solely on Allah’s words:

“Complete the Hajj and ‘umrah for Allah.”

Everyone agrees that this verse was revealed in the year of al-Hudaybiyah. However, it commands the completion of pilgrimage once begun; it does not impose an obligation to undertake it in the first place.

The Sacred House already possessed the distinction of having been built by Abraham, the intimate friend of Allah, who called people to perform pilgrimage to it. It then acquired a second distinction: Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—rescued it from the hands of the polytheists and kept it from their control. He was also the one who made pilgrimage to it obligatory upon everyone able to undertake it. People have come on pilgrimage to it from east and west. Through Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—Allah has thus been worshipped there by many times more people than before, and with greater worship, for Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—is the foremost of Adam’s descendants.

When he died, he was buried in ‘Ā’ishah’s chamber. She said: “During his final illness, the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

‘May Allah curse the Jews and the Christians; they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.’”

He was warning against what they had done. ‘Ā’ishah—may Allah be pleased with her—said: “Were it not for that, his grave would have been left exposed, but he disliked its being made a place of worship.” And in the Sahīh of

---

## Page 13411

Muslim, it is reported that he said five days before his death:

“Those who came before you used to take graves as places of worship. Beware! Do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.”

It is also reported in the Sahīh of Muslim that he said:

“Do not sit on graves, and do not pray towards them.”

He—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—thus forbade taking graves as places of worship and praying towards them. He cursed the Jews and Christians because they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship, for this was the first cause of polytheism among Noah’s people. Allah Most High said of them:

“And they said, ‘Never abandon your gods; never abandon Wadd, Suwā‘, Yaghūth, Ya‘ūq, or Nasr.’ And they have already led many astray.”

Ibn ‘Abbās and others among the early generations said: These were righteous men among Noah’s people. When they died, the people devoted themselves to staying at their graves, then made images of them, and then worshipped them.

Out of his perfect concern for his community, he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—warned them against falling into what the polytheists and the People of the Book had fallen into. He therefore forbade them to take graves as places of worship or to pray towards them, lest they imitate the disbelievers, just as he forbade them to pray at sunrise and sunset lest they imitate the disbelievers.

For this reason, when the chamber was incorporated into his mosque of special merit during the caliphate of al-Walīd ibn ‘Abd al-Malik—as mentioned earlier—they built a wall around it, raised it, and set it at an angle, so that no one would pray towards his noble grave—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

In Mālik’s Muwatta’, it is reported that he said:

“O Allah, do not let my grave become an idol that is worshipped. Allah’s wrath was severe against a people

---

## Page 13412

who took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

Allah answered his supplication, and his grave—praise be to Allah—has not been made an idol, as the graves of others have been. Indeed, no one can enter his chamber now that it has been enclosed. Even before that, they would not allow anyone to enter it to supplicate or pray beside his grave, or to do any of the other things done at the graves of others. Some ignorant people, however, pray towards his chamber, raise their voices, or utter forbidden words. These things are done outside his chamber, not beside his grave.

As for his grave itself, praise be to Allah, Allah answered his supplication: no one has ever been allowed to enter and pray or supplicate beside it, or associate him with Allah in worship, as has happened with others whose graves were made into idols. During ‘Ā’ishah’s lifetime—may Allah be pleased with her—no one entered except to see her, and she would not allow anyone to do anything beside his grave that he had forbidden. After her death, the chamber remained locked until it was incorporated into the mosque; its doorway was then sealed, and another wall was built around it.

All of this was done to safeguard him—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—by preventing his house from becoming a place of recurring gatherings and his grave an idol. It is, after all, well known that all the people of Medina are Muslims, that only Muslims come there, and that they all revere the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Even the graves of individual members of his community throughout the lands are held in honour. Thus, they did not do this to show disrespect for his noble grave. Rather, they did it so that it would not become an idol that is worshipped, so that his house would not become a place of recurring gatherings, and so that it would not be treated as the People of the Book treated the graves of their prophets.

The noble grave within the chamber is covered only with sand—that is, coarse sand. There are no stones or wood over it, nor is it plastered with clay, as has been done with the graves of others.

---

## Page 13413

He—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—prohibited this only to block the means leading to polytheism, just as he prohibited prayer at sunrise and sunset lest it lead to polytheism. He asked Allah, Mighty and Majestic, not to let his grave be made into an idol that is worshipped. Allah answered his supplication—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—so his case was unlike that of those whose graves were taken as places of worship: no one can enter where his grave is at all.

Whenever the communities of the prophets before him introduced a religious innovation, Allah would send a prophet to forbid it. But he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—is the Seal of the Prophets; there is no prophet after him. Allah therefore protected his community from agreeing upon misguidance and protected his honoured grave from being made into an idol. For if that were done—and we seek refuge in Allah from it—there would be no prophet after him to forbid it, and those who did it would have gained ascendancy over the community. Yet he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—has stated that a group of his community will continue to uphold the truth victoriously, unharmed by those who oppose or abandon them, until the Day of Resurrection. Thus, the people of religious innovation have had no way to do to his honoured grave what has been done to the graves of others. May Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Section

I have mentioned in my writings on the pilgrimage rites that travelling to his mosque and visiting his grave—as the leading Muslim scholars discuss in their works on the pilgrimage rites—is a righteous deed

---

## Page 13414

that is recommended. In several of my works on the rites of Hajj, I have explained the Sunnah concerning this: how one should greet him, and whether one should face the chamber or the direction of prayer. There are two views on this. Most scholars, including Mālik, al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad, say that one should face the chamber. Abū Hanīfa says that one should face the direction of prayer, with the chamber to one’s left according to one account of his view, or behind one according to another.

For when the honoured chamber was outside the mosque and the Companions would greet him, no one could face him—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—with their back to the direction of prayer, as became possible after the chamber was incorporated into the mosque. Rather, if a person faced the direction of prayer, the chamber would be to his left. Thus, if they used to face him with their backs to the west, the majority’s view is stronger. But if they used to face the direction of prayer with the chamber to their left, Abū Hanīfa’s view is stronger.

Prayers may be shortened on this recommended journey, by agreement of the leading Muslim scholars. No leading Muslim scholar has said that prayers may not be shortened on this journey. Nor has any of them forbidden travelling to his mosque, even when the traveller also visits his grave—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Rather, this is among the finest righteous deeds. Nothing in my words or those of others prohibits this, or prohibits the prescribed manner of visiting the graves of the prophets and the righteous, or the prescribed manner of visiting other graves. Indeed, I have stated in several places that visiting graves is recommended, just as “the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—used to visit those buried in al-Baqī' and the martyrs of Uhud, and teach his Companions

---

## Page 13415

when they visited graves, to say:

‘Peace be upon you, inhabitants of these dwellings, believers and Muslims. We shall, Allah willing, join you. May Allah have mercy on those among us and you who have gone before and those who are yet to follow. We ask Allah for well-being for ourselves and for you. O Allah, do not deprive us of the reward for their loss, do not subject us to trials after them, and forgive us and them.’”

If visiting the graves of believers in general is prescribed, then visiting the graves of the prophets and the righteous is all the more so. Yet the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—has a distinction not shared by the other prophets and righteous people: we have been commanded to invoke blessings upon him and send him greetings of peace in every prayer. This is particularly emphasized during prayer, at the call to prayer, and in all other supplications. We have also been commanded to invoke blessings upon him and send him greetings of peace when entering a mosque—whether his mosque or any other—and when leaving it. Thus, everyone who enters his mosque must pray there and send him greetings of peace during the prayer.

Travelling to his mosque is prescribed. Nevertheless, the scholars distinguished between him and others, to the extent that Mālik—may Allah have mercy on him—disliked the expression, “I visited the grave of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.” This is because the prescribed purpose of visiting graves is to greet their occupants with peace and supplicate for them. These greetings and supplications are already offered in the fullest manner during prayer, whether in his mosque or elsewhere, when hearing the call to prayer, and with every supplication. Invoking blessings upon him is therefore prescribed with every supplication, for he

“has a greater claim upon the believers than they have upon themselves.”

For this reason, the worshipper greets him with peace during prayer before greeting himself and all the righteous servants of Allah, saying:

“Peace be upon you, O Prophet, and the mercy of Allah

---

## Page 13416

and His blessings. Peace be upon us and upon the righteous servants of Allah.” He also invokes blessings upon him, thus supplicating for him before supplicating for himself. As for anyone else, there is no mosque associated with him to which travel is recommended as it is to the Prophet’s mosque. Rather, visiting his grave is prescribed in the same manner as visiting graves generally. The Prophet himself—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—prescribed travelling to his mosque and forbade anything that might give the impression that the journey was to somewhere other than the three mosques.

A distinction must be made between the prescribed visitation instituted by the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and the innovated visitation which he did not prescribe but rather forbade, such as taking the graves of prophets and righteous people as places of worship, praying towards a grave, and making it an idol.

It is authentically reported from him in the two Sahīh collections that he said:

“Journeys are not to be undertaken except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque, this mosque of mine, and al-Aqsā Mosque.”

Indeed, when Abū Hurayrah travelled to Mount Sinai, where Allah spoke to Moses son of ‘Imrān—peace be upon him—Basrah ibn Abī Basrah al-Ghifārī said to him:

“Had I reached you before you set out, you would not have gone. I heard the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—say: ‘Mounts are not to be ridden on journeys except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque, this mosque of mine, and the mosque of Jerusalem.’”

Travel to these mosques has therefore been prescribed for worshipping Allah in them through prayer, Quran recitation, remembrance, supplication, and devotional retreat. Circumambulation is exclusive to the Sacred Mosque; it is not performed at any other mosque. As for other mosques, if a person goes to them and prays in them without undertaking a journey,

---

## Page 13417

that is among the finest deeds, as it is established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever purifies himself at home and then sets out for the mosque, one of his steps erases a sin and the other raises him a degree. A servant remains in prayer as long as he is waiting for prayer. The angels pray for each of you as long as he remains in the place where he prayed, saying, ‘O Allah, forgive him; O Allah, have mercy on him,’ so long as he does not break his state of ritual purity.”

If someone were to travel from one town to another—for example, from Egypt to Damascus for the sake of its mosque, or vice versa—or travel from a distant town to the Mosque of Qubā’, this would not be prescribed, by agreement of the four imams and others. If he vowed to do so, he should not fulfil his vow, by agreement of the four imams and others, apart from an isolated dissenting view reported from al-Layth ibn Sa‘d concerning mosques. Ibn Maslamah, one of Mālik’s followers, held this view specifically concerning the Mosque of Qubā’.

However, when someone comes to Medina, it is recommended that he visit the Mosque of Qubā’ and pray there, since this does not constitute travel or setting out on a journey. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—used to visit the Mosque of Qubā’ every Saturday, both riding and walking, and pray two rak‘ahs there. He said:

“Whoever purifies himself at home and then comes to the Mosque of Qubā’ will have a reward like that of an ‘umrah.”

This was narrated by al-Tirmidhī and Ibn Abī Shaybah. Sa‘d ibn Abī Waqqās and Ibn ‘Umar said: “A prayer there is like an ‘umrah.”

If someone vows to walk to Mecca for Hajj and ‘umrah, his vow is binding upon him by agreement of the Muslims. If he vows to go to the mosque in Medina or the mosque in Jerusalem, there are two opinions:

---

## Page 13418

The first: he is not obliged to fulfil it. This is the view of Abū Hanīfah and one of al-Shāfi‘ī’s two opinions, because no act of this kind is obligatory under Islamic law.

The second: he is obliged to fulfil it. This is the position of Mālik, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and al-Shāfi‘ī in his other opinion, because this is an act of obedience to Allah. It is established in Sahīh al-Bukhārī that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah, let him obey Him; and whoever vows to disobey Allah, let him not disobey Him.”

If someone vows to travel to places other than mosques, or to travel solely to the grave of a prophet or righteous person, he is not obliged to fulfil his vow, by their agreement, for the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—did not command such travel. Rather, he said:

“No journey should be undertaken except to three mosques: al-Masjid al-Harām, this mosque of mine, and al-Masjid al-Aqsā.”

Only an act of obedience becomes binding through a vow. Mālik and others explicitly stated that if someone vows to travel to the Prophet’s city, intending to pray in the mosque of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—he should fulfil his vow. But if his intention is solely to visit the grave without praying in the mosque, he should not fulfil his vow, because the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Mounts should not be driven on a journey except to three mosques.”

The judge Ismā‘īl ibn Ishāq discussed this issue in al-Mabsūt, and its substance appears in al-Mudawwanah, al-Khilāf, and other works by Mālik’s followers. He states that whoever vows to visit the Prophet’s mosque—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—is obliged to fulfil his vow, because the mosque is only visited

---

## Page 13419

for prayer. If someone vows to visit the Prophet’s city, he should fulfil his vow if his intention is to pray in the mosque. But if he intends something else, such as visiting those buried in al-Baqī‘ or the martyrs of Uhud, he should not fulfil his vow, because such travel is prescribed only to the three mosques. I know of no leading Muslim scholar who has contradicted this position stated by Mālik and others; rather, their statements indicate agreement with it.

The followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad have mentioned two opinions concerning travel to visit graves: prohibition and permissibility. Their early scholars and leading authorities held that it is prohibited, as did the followers of Mālik and others. The disagreement arose only among later scholars, because the Prophet’s words—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—

“No journey should be undertaken except to three mosques”

are declarative in form but convey a prohibition, making such travel unlawful.

Some of them said: “This is not a prohibition. It means only that such travel is not prescribed: it is neither obligatory nor recommended, but permissible, like travelling for trade or other purposes.”

The response to this is: Those journeys are not undertaken as worship, but for a permissible worldly benefit, whereas travel to graves is undertaken as worship. Worship, however, can only consist of what is obligatory or recommended. Since there is agreement that travel to graves is neither obligatory nor recommended, anyone who undertakes it as an act of worship is introducing an innovation and contravening scholarly consensus. Worshipping through an innovation is not permissible. However, someone who does not know that this is an innovation may be excused. Once the Sunnah has been explained to him, he may no longer oppose the Prophet

---

## Page 13420

, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or worship by doing what he has forbidden. Likewise, prayer is not permissible at sunrise or sunset, nor is fasting permissible on either Eid, even though prayer and fasting are among the finest acts of worship. If someone did this before learning the Sunnah, however, he would incur no sin.

The various schools therefore agree that it is not recommended. I know of no leading Muslim scholar who has said that travelling to graves is recommended. Some of their followers may have said so, but none of the leading scholars qualified to exercise independent juristic judgment has said this. Were such a view advanced, it would constitute a third opinion on the matter. Its proponent should then be shown that this view is mistaken and contrary to the Sunnah and the consensus of the Companions. For none of the Companions—may Allah be pleased with them all—travelled to the grave of a prophet or a righteous man during the caliphates of Abū Bakr al-Siddīq, ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī, or under their successors until the generation of the Companions had passed away.

None of the Companions travelled to “the grave of Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, peace be upon him,” in Syria. They would visit Jerusalem and pray there, but would not go to the grave of Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, peace be upon him. It was not visible; rather, it lay within the building erected by Solomon son of David, peace be upon them both.

Nor was “the grave of Joseph the truthful” known. It was only brought to light more than three hundred years after the Hijrah. For this reason, it became a matter of dispute: many scholars deny its identification, and this position has been transmitted from Mālik and others, because the Companions did not visit it so that it might become known. And when control was seized

---

## Page 13421

by the Christians over Syria, they broke an opening through the building over the grave of Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, peace be upon him, and turned the place into a church. Then, when the Muslims conquered the town, it remained open. During the time of the Companions, however, Abraham’s grave was like the grave of our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. None of the Companions would travel to Medina for the sake of the Prophet’s grave. Rather, they would come and pray in his mosque, offering greetings of peace to him during prayer; those who offered such greetings when entering or leaving the mosque would do so then. He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was buried in ‘Ā’ishah’s chamber, may Allah be pleased with her, so they would neither enter the chamber nor stand outside it in the mosque beside its wall.

During the caliphates of Abū Bakr al-Siddīq and ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, reinforcements would arrive from Yemen—the men who conquered Syria and Iraq, concerning whom Allah said:

“Allah will bring forth a people He will love and who will love Him.”

They would pray in his mosque as we have mentioned. No one would go to the grave, enter the chamber, or stand outside it in the mosque. Rather, greetings of peace to him are offered from outside the chamber. Mālik and others rely primarily on the practice of Ibn ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with them both, in this regard.

In any event, even if half the Muslims held this view, it would have the same standing as comparable opinions on disputed matters. But to treat it as the true religion and consider it lawful to punish anyone who disagrees with it, or to declare him an unbeliever, is contrary to the consensus of the Muslims and to what the Quran and Sunnah have brought. For if one who opposes the Messenger

---

## Page 13422

in this matter is an unbeliever, then it is the one who opposes his Sunnah and the consensus of the Companions and the scholars of his community who is the unbeliever. We do not declare any Muslim an unbeliever for making a mistake, whether in these matters or in others. But if it were assumed that someone who errs should be declared an unbeliever, then one who opposes the Quran, the Sunnah, and the consensus—the consensus of the Companions and the scholars—would more justly be judged an unbeliever than one who follows the Quran, the Sunnah, the Companions, and the early generations and leading scholars of the Muslim community.

The leading scholars of the Muslims distinguished, in this matter and others, between what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded and what he forbade. What he commanded is worship, obedience, and a means of drawing near to Allah. What he forbade is the opposite; indeed, it may constitute associating partners with Allah, as in the practices of the misguided among the polytheists, the People of the Book, and those who imitate them. They establish places of worship over the graves of prophets and righteous people, pray towards them, dedicate vows to them, and make pilgrimages to them. They may even regard pilgrimage to the house of a created being as superior to pilgrimage to Allah’s Sacred House, calling it “the Greater Pilgrimage.” Their shaykhs have written works for them on this subject. Thus al-Mufīd ibn al-Nu'mān wrote a book on the rites associated with shrines, which he entitled “Manāsik Hajj al-Mashāhid (Rites of Pilgrimage to Shrines),” likening the house of a created being to the House of the Creator.

The foundation of Islam is that we worship Allah alone and do not make any of His creatures a rival, an equal, or a counterpart to Him.

The Most High says:

“So worship Him and be steadfast in His worship. Do you know of any equal to Him?”

The Most High also says:

“And there is none comparable to Him.”

The Most High also says:

“There is nothing like Him, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing.”

The Most High also says: “So do not set up

---

## Page 13423

rivals to Allah while you know.”

In the two Sahih collections, Ibn Mas'ūd reported:

“I said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, which sin is the greatest?’
He replied, ‘To set up a rival to Allah when He created you.’
I asked, ‘Then which?’
He replied, ‘To kill your child for fear that he will share your food.’
I asked, ‘Then which?’
He replied, ‘To commit adultery with your neighbor’s wife.’”

Allah then revealed the following verse in confirmation of His Messenger:

“And those who do not invoke another god besides Allah, nor kill a soul whom Allah has forbidden to be killed except by right, nor commit adultery. Whoever does so will face punishment.”

The Most High also says:

“Yet among people are those who take others as rivals to Allah, loving them as they love Allah. But those who believe are stronger in their love for Allah.”

Whoever equates the Creator with a created being in love, or in fear and hope, is a polytheist.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade his community from every form of associating partners with Allah, great or small. He even said:

“Whoever swears by anything other than Allah has associated partners with Him.”

This was reported by Abū Dāwūd and others.

A man said to him, “As Allah wills and you will.” He replied:

“Have you made me a rival to Allah? Rather, say: ‘As Allah alone wills.’”

He also said:

“Do not say, ‘As Allah wills and Muhammad wills.’ Rather, say, ‘As Allah wills, then as Muhammad wills.’”

Once, Mu'ādh ibn Jabal came and prostrated before him. He asked, “What is this, Mu'ādh?”

He replied, “O Messenger of Allah, I saw people in Syria prostrating before their bishops.”

He said:

“Mu'ādh, prostration is due only to Allah. Were I to command anyone to prostrate before another, I would command a woman to prostrate before her husband because of the great right he has over her.”

For this reason, a distinction was drawn by

---

## Page 13424

the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, between the grave visitation practiced by those who worship Allah alone and that practiced by those who associate partners with Him. When those who worship Allah alone visit Muslim graves, they greet the deceased with peace and supplicate for them. This is like offering the funeral prayer over them. By contrast, the visitation practiced by those who associate partners with Allah involves likening a created being to the Creator: they make vows to him, prostrate before him, invoke him, and love him as they love the Creator. Thus, they have made him a rival to Allah and equated him with the Lord of all worlds.

Allah has forbidden associating angels, prophets, or anyone else with Him. The Most High says:

“It is not for a human being to whom Allah has given the Scripture, wisdom, and prophethood to then say to people, ‘Be my worshippers rather than Allah’s.’ Rather, ‘Be devoted servants of the Lord through your teaching of the Scripture and through your study of it.’ Nor would he command you to take angels and prophets as lords.”

“Would he command you to disbelieve after you have submitted to Allah?”

The Most High also says:

“Say, ‘Call upon those you claim to be gods besides Him. They have no power to remove harm from you or to shift it elsewhere.’”

“Those whom they invoke themselves seek a means of drawing near to their Lord, striving to be closest to Him. They hope for His mercy and fear His punishment. Indeed, your Lord’s punishment is something to beware of.”

A group of the early generations said: Some people used to invoke prophets such as the Messiah and Ezra, and to invoke the angels. The Most High therefore informed them that these are His servants: they hope for His mercy, fear His punishment, and draw near to Him through their deeds. He, glory be to Him, also forbade drawing analogies between Him and created beings. He must therefore not be likened to a created being who

---

## Page 13425

needs helpers, gatekeepers, and the like. The Most High says:

“When My servants ask you about Me, I am indeed near. I answer the supplicant’s call when he calls upon Me. So let them respond to Me and believe in Me, so that they may be rightly guided.”

The Most High also says:

“Say, ‘Call upon those you claim to be gods besides Allah. They possess not even an atom’s weight in the heavens or on earth, nor do they have any share in either, nor does He have any helper among them.’”

“Intercession before Him is of no benefit except for one whom He permits.”

Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is the foremost of those who intercede before Him. His intercession is the greatest of all intercessions, and his standing before Allah is the highest of all. On the Day of Resurrection, when people seek intercession from Adam, then Noah, then Abraham, then Moses, then Jesus, each will refer them to another. When they come to the Messiah, he will say, “Go to Muhammad, a servant whose past and future sins Allah has forgiven.”

The Prophet said:

“I will go, and when I see my Lord, I will fall down in prostration before Him and praise my Lord with words of praise that He will inspire me to utter, which I do not know now. Then it will be said, ‘O Muhammad, raise your head. Speak, and you will be heard; ask, and you will be given; intercede, and your intercession will be accepted.’” He said: “Then He will set a limit for me, and I will admit them to Paradise.” The hadith continues.

Whoever denies our Prophet’s intercession—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—for those who commit major sins is a misguided innovator, as are the Khawārij and the Mu‘tazilah, who deny it. Whoever says that a created being intercedes before Allah without His permission has contradicted the consensus of the Muslims and the explicit statements of the Quran. The Most High says:

“Who can intercede before Him except with His permission?”

The Most High also says: “Nor

---

## Page 13426

do they intercede except for those with whom He is pleased.” The Most High also says:

“How many angels there are in the heavens whose intercession is of no avail whatsoever, except after Allah has given permission to whom He wills and is pleased with.”

The Most High also says:

“Voices will be humbled before the Most Merciful, and you will hear nothing but a whisper.”

“On that Day, intercession will be of no benefit except from one to whom the Most Merciful has given permission and whose words He approves.”

The Most High also says:

“No one can intercede except after His permission.”

The Most High also says:

“You have no protector or intercessor besides Him.”

There are many such passages in the Quran. Religion consists in following the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: enjoining what he enjoined, forbidding what he forbade, loving the deeds and people whom Allah and His Messenger love, and hating the deeds and people whom Allah and His Messenger hate. Allah, glorified and exalted is He, sent His Messenger Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with the Criterion, thereby distinguishing between these things. No one may therefore conflate what Allah has distinguished.

Thus, whoever travels to the Sacred Mosque, al-Aqsā Mosque, or the Mosque of the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and prays in his mosque, prays in the Mosque of Qubā’, and visits graves in accordance with the established Sunnah of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has indeed performed a righteous deed. Whoever rejects such travel is an unbeliever who must be called upon to repent. If he repents, well and good; otherwise, he is to be put to death.

As for someone who intends to travel solely to visit the grave, without intending to pray in his mosque, and travels to his city but neither prays in his mosque, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor sends greetings of peace upon him during prayer, but instead comes to the grave and then returns, such a person is an innovator

---

## Page 13427

who is astray and acts contrary to the Sunnah of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the consensus of his Companions, and the scholars of his community. It is regarding this that two views have been stated: one holds that it is forbidden; the other holds that he incurs no blame but receives no reward.

What Muslim scholars practise is the prescribed form of visitation: they pray in his mosque, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and send greetings of peace upon him when entering the mosque and during prayer. Muslims agree that this is prescribed. I have mentioned this in my work on pilgrimage rites and in the fatwa, explaining that one sends greetings of peace upon the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and upon his two Companions. This is the matter concerning which I mentioned no disagreement in the fatwa, although disagreement does exist. Some scholars do not consider visiting graves recommended at all, while others consider it disliked altogether, as has been reported from Ibrāhīm al-Nakhaʿī, al-Shaʿbī, and Muhammad ibn Sīrīn, who were among the eminent Successors. This has also been reported from Mālik. Another report from him holds that it is permissible, not recommended. This is also one of the two views within Ahmad’s school.

However, the apparent position of his school, and the position of the majority, is that the prescribed form of visitation is recommended. This consists of visiting believers’ graves to supplicate for them, greeting them with peace and praying for them. The graves of unbelievers are also visited, because doing so reminds one of the Hereafter.

As for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he has a distinction shared by no other created being: what is sought at anyone else’s grave—namely, supplicating for him—is something we are commanded [to do] (1) for the Messenger during the five daily prayers, when entering and leaving mosques, at the call to prayer, and whenever making supplication. He also forbade taking graves as places of prayer

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I did not find it in Safeguarding the Collected Fatwas against Omissions and Textual Corruption.
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — coordinator of the book for al-Maktaba al-Shāmila.

---

## Page 13428

and forbade making his grave a place of recurring celebration, and asked Allah not to make it an idol that is worshipped. Thus, no one was allowed to enter his burial place to visit him as one enters the burial place of another. The invocation of blessings and peace upon him, whether in his mosque or elsewhere, is an honour with which Allah has singled him out and favoured him above others. Through this, Allah has spared him any need for what is done at another person’s grave—even when that is permissible.

As for “taking graves as places of prayer,” this is forbidden at every grave, even if the person praying prays only to Allah and supplicates only to Allah. How much more so if he supplicates to a created being, prostrates to him, makes vows to him, or does other such things practised by the people of polytheism, religious innovation, and misguidance!

If, however, someone were to come to the mosque without praying there, but instead approach the grave and then leave, this is precisely what the leading scholars, such as Mālik and others, condemned. None of the scholars considers this recommended. The disagreement concerns whether it is forbidden or permissible. We know of no Muslim scholar who recommended anything of this kind. Rather, they condemned travelling solely for the grave without intending to pray in the mosque, and regarded this as a forbidden journey.

Nor did any of the early generations do this. Rather, when the Companions travelled to his mosque, they prayed there and met with his caliphs, such as Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿUthmān, and ʿAlī. They sent greetings of peace and invoked blessings upon him during prayer, and those among them who observed this practice also did so when entering and leaving the mosque. And they did not

---

## Page 13429

go to the grave. This is established from them through mass transmission. No one can report from them, or from any one of them, that after praying behind the Rightly Guided Caliphs, he would go, either then or at another time, to stand outside the chamber. As for entering the chamber, that was not possible for them. If, after travelling to his mosque, they followed his prescribed practice of invoking blessings and peace upon him without going to his grave, how could they intend to travel to it? Or make it the purpose of their journey without praying in the mosque?

Whoever says that this is recommended must cite an imam of the Muslims who said so. Even if he produces such a report, the person who said it would have contradicted the statements of the scholars, just as the person who practises it has contradicted the practice of the Muslim community, the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), the consensus of his Companions, and the scholars of his Ummah.

Allah Most High says:

“Whoever opposes the Messenger after guidance has become clear to him and follows a path other than that of the believers, We will leave him to what he has chosen and burn him in Hell—what an evil destination!”

And:

“Actions are judged only by intentions, and each person will have only what he intended.”

In their manuals of pilgrimage rites, Muslim scholars have mentioned that travelling to his mosque is recommended, and have mentioned visiting his honoured grave. I know of no Muslim who has said that someone whose sole intention is to visit the grave undertakes a recommended journey—even if they were to say this concerning someone else’s grave. Yet this was not what some people intended—people unfamiliar with the Sharia and with what the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) commanded and forbade. At most, such a person may be excused because of his ignorance

---

## Page 13430

and Allah may pardon him. As for those who know what Allah and His Messenger have commanded and forbidden, none of them has instructed people to travel solely to visit a grave, whether that of a prophet or anyone else. Indeed, leading scholars among the followers of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad ibn Hanbal and others have explicitly declared such travel forbidden. Only a group of later scholars among the followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad have said that it is permissible rather than forbidden.

They consequently differed over whether someone who travels solely to visit the graves of prophets and righteous people may shorten his prayers. There are two views, as mentioned in the response to the request for a fatwa.

Some of them distinguished between the graves of prophets and those of others, and said that travelling solely to visit graves is forbidden, as is the position of Mālik and his followers and the view of the earlier scholars among the followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad. These scholars hold that someone whose journey itself constitutes disobedience may not shorten his prayers. Thus, according to their view, prayers are not to be shortened on such a journey. However, those who undertake these journeys do not know that this is forbidden, and anyone who knew it was forbidden would not do it, for no Muslim intends to draw nearer to Allah through something forbidden.

Accordingly, if they shorten their prayers during a journey that they did not know was forbidden, this is permissible, and they do not have to repeat those prayers. This is like someone who travels to seek knowledge or to hear hadith from a particular person, only to discover that he is a liar or ignorant: shortening the prayers during such a journey is permissible.

The followers of Ahmad have mentioned four views on whether prayers may be shortened during journeys to visit the graves of prophets and righteous people:

One view is that prayers may not be shortened in any such case.

---

## Page 13431

Another view is that prayers may be shortened in all such cases. A third is that they may be shortened only when travelling to the grave of our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. A fourth is that they may be shortened only when travelling to his honoured grave and the graves of other prophets, but not those of righteous people.

Those who made an exception for the grave of our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, have two grounds for their position:

The first—and it is the correct one—is that the prescribed journey to him is the journey to his mosque. Prayers may be shortened on this journey by the consensus of the Muslims. These scholars considered the journey in general, without distinguishing between one intention and another, since Muslims generally will inevitably pray in his mosque. Thus, whoever travels to his honoured grave has also travelled to his mosque of special merit.

Likewise, some followers of al-Shāfiʿī said that whoever vows to visit the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is to fulfil his vow; whereas, if he vows to visit someone else’s grave, there are two views. Similarly, many scholars speak without qualification of travelling to his honoured grave. They understand this to include travelling to his mosque, since every Muslim who comes to the honoured chamber will inevitably pray in his mosque. In their view, the two are inseparable.

Some of these scholars say that a Muslim will necessarily intend, from the outset of his journey, to pray in his mosque. The prescribed journey is therefore necessarily involved, and these people have not travelled solely for the grave.

Others say that travelling solely to visit the grave is permissible. They supposed that the exception was not based on something unique to him, but on his being a prophet. They therefore held that prayers may be shortened when travelling to the graves of prophets, but not to those of anyone else.

The reality is that praying in his mosque is necessarily part of this journey

---

## Page 13432

so everyone who travels to his honoured grave will inevitably perform an act of obedience and devotion for which he is rewarded by praying in his mosque. As for the intention itself, those knowledgeable about hadith intend to travel to his mosque, although some of them may also intend to travel to the grave if they do not know that this is prohibited. Someone unaware of this may intend only to travel to the grave, but he will inevitably pray in his mosque and be rewarded for doing so. He is not punished for a prohibited act that he committed without knowing it was prohibited. Thus, he receives a reward and bears no sin. This is unlike travelling to anyone else’s grave, where there is nothing to which travel is prescribed. Nevertheless, such a person may perform an act of obedience for which he is rewarded, and be forgiven for what he did not know was forbidden.

Prayer in mosques built over graves is prohibited without exception, unlike prayer in his mosque, where one prayer is worth a thousand prayers. It was founded upon piety, and its sanctity was already established during his lifetime, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the lifetimes of his rightly guided caliphs, before the chamber was incorporated into it. At that time, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the Emigrants and the Helpers prayed there. Worship there then was of greater excellence and merit than worship there after the chamber was incorporated into it. The chamber was incorporated only after the era of the Companions had ended, during the rule of al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, who assumed power sometime in the eighties AH, as mentioned previously.

Some of them supposed that the exception was due to his being a prophet, so they extended it, saying:

“One may travel

---

## Page 13433

to the graves of all the other prophets as well.”

For the same reason, people have differed over whether one may swear by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, despite agreeing that one must not swear by any revered created thing, such as the Throne, the Footstool, the Kaaba, or the angels. The majority of scholars—including Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, Abū Hanīfa, and Ahmad according to one of his two positions—held that one must not swear by the Prophet and that such an oath is not legally binding, just as one must not swear by any created thing. No expiation is required of someone who swears by any of these things and then breaks his oath.

For it is authentically reported that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not swear except by Allah.”

He also said:

“Whoever wishes to swear, let him swear by Allah or remain silent.”

And in the Sunan collections:

“Whoever swears by other than Allah has committed shirk.”

There is another position reported from Ahmad ibn Hanbal permitting an oath by the Prophet alone, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, because belief in him in particular is obligatory, as is mentioning him in the two testimonies of faith and in the call to prayer. Belief in him thus has a distinctive status shared by no one else.

Ibn ‘Aqīl said: “Rather, this is because he is a prophet.” This ruling was then extended to all the other prophets. Yet the correct position, held by the general body of Muslim scholars, both earlier and later, is that one must not swear by any created being: neither a prophet nor anyone else, neither an angel, nor a king, nor a shaykh.

Most of them understand this prohibition as rendering the act unlawful, as in the school of Abū Hanīfa and others. This is also one of the two positions in Ahmad’s school, as mentioned earlier. Indeed, Ibn Mas‘ūd, Ibn ‘Abbās, and others would each say:

“I would rather swear falsely by Allah than

---

## Page 13434

swear truthfully by other than Allah.”

In another wording:

“I would rather swear falsely by Allah than make such a comparison.”

Swearing by other than Allah is shirk, and shirk is graver than lying. At its gravest, lying is likened to shirk, as in the authentic hadith in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“False testimony has been equated with associating partners with Allah.”

He said this twice or three times, then recited the words of Allah Most High:

“And avoid false speech.”

“Devoting yourselves to Allah, associating nothing with Him. Whoever associates partners with Allah—it is as though he had fallen from the sky and been snatched away by birds, or swept by the wind to a distant place.”

Yet this prohibited—indeed, unlawful—act, which the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, regarded as graver than a false oath, has been considered by some scholars to be religiously sanctioned and not prohibited. There are many similar examples. But Allah Most High says:

“Obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you. If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day. That is best and gives the finest outcome.”

Whatever Allah and His Messenger command is the truth. He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited swearing by other than Allah, praying at sunrise and sunset, taking graves as places of prayer, and making his grave a place of recurring observance. He also prohibited undertaking journeys to places other than the three mosques, and similar practices, to ensure that devotion is sincerely for Allah and that Allah alone is worshipped, without any partner. All of this is to safeguard

---

## Page 13435

the oneness of Allah, Mighty and Majestic, and to ensure that all devotion is for Allah: none is worshipped but Him, none is relied upon but Him, none is invoked but Him, and none is held in reverent fear but Him. Prayer and fasting are offered only for Him, vows are made only to Him, oaths are sworn only by Him, and pilgrimage is made only to His House.

The obligatory pilgrimage is only to the greatest and oldest of His houses: the Sacred Mosque. Recommended travel is only to two mosques, because each was built by a prophet. The Prophet’s Mosque in Medina was founded upon piety by the Seal of the Messengers, while the mosque of Jerusalem was already a mosque before Solomon. In the two Sahīh collections, Abū Dharr, may Allah be pleased with him, reported:

“I asked, ‘O Messenger of Allah, which mosque was established first?’

“He replied, ‘The Sacred Mosque.’

“I asked, ‘Then which?’ He replied, ‘Al-Aqsā Mosque.’

“I asked, ‘How much time was there between them?’

“He replied, ‘Forty years. Then, wherever you are when prayer becomes due, pray, for that place is a place of prayer for you.’”

In al-Bukhārī’s wording:

“For therein lies virtue.”

This was the practice of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: he would pray wherever he was when prayer became due. Al-Aqsā Mosque thus existed from the time of Abraham, peace be upon him, but Solomon, peace be upon him, rebuilt it on a magnificent scale.

Each of the three mosques was built by a noble prophet so that he and the people could pray there. Since the prophets—peace be upon them—deliberately sought to pray in these two mosques, travelling to them for prayer and worship was prescribed in emulation of the prophets—peace be upon them—and in following their example. Just as Abraham, the intimate friend of Allah—peace be upon him—

---

## Page 13436

built the House and Allah Most High commanded him to proclaim the pilgrimage to it among the people, they began travelling to it from the time of Abraham, peace be upon him. According to the more correct of the two views, this was not obligatory upon people. Nor was it obligatory in the early days of Islam; Allah only imposed it upon Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, at a later stage, when Surah Āl ‘Imrān was revealed.

In Surah al-Baqarah, He commanded those who had begun Hajj or ‘umrah to complete them. Hence, according to the vast majority of scholars, voluntarily beginning either pilgrimage makes its completion obligatory. It has also been said that the command to complete them makes undertaking them obligatory in the first place, but the first view is correct.

So too, Al-Aqsā Mosque and the Mosque of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, were each built by a noble messenger who called people to travel there for worship. No prophet—peace be upon them—built a mosque and called people to travel there for worship except in the case of these three mosques. They did, however, have mosques in which they prayed without calling people to travel to them. Abraham, peace be upon him, likewise prayed where he was, but called people only to make pilgrimage to the House.

No prophet ever called people to travel to his grave, his house, a place where he had stood, or any other traces of his presence. Rather, they called people to worship Allah alone, without any partner. After mentioning them, Allah Most High said:

“That is the guidance of Allah, by which He guides whom He wills of His servants. Had they associated others with Him, all their deeds would have come to nothing.”

“Those are the ones to whom We gave the Scripture, judgment, and prophethood. If these people disbelieve in them, We have entrusted them to a people who do not disbelieve in them.”

“Those are the ones whom Allah has guided, so follow their guidance.”

---

## Page 13437

For this reason, none of these three mosques may be moved from its location. As for all other mosques, their merit lies in being mosques devoted to Allah and houses in which prayer is performed—a distinction they all share. Although some may be distinguished by the greater amount of worship performed in them, their greater age, or similar qualities, such distinctions are found among mosques generally: some accommodate more worship than others, and some are older than others. If travelling for such reasons were prescribed, this would entail travelling to mosques generally.

Travelling to venerated places is a form of pilgrimage, and every community has its pilgrimage. The Arab polytheists used to make pilgrimage to al-Lāt, al-‘Uzzā, Manāt—the third, the other one—and other idols. Thus, when the learned scholar who foretold the coming of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to Umayya ibn Abī al-Salt, “The time is drawing near when a prophet will be sent, and he belongs to a House to which the Arabs make pilgrimage,” Umayya replied, “We, the people of Thaqīf, have among us a house to which the Arabs make pilgrimage.” The scholar said, “He is not from among you; he is from your brethren of Quraysh.” He thus informed Umayya that the Arabs used to make pilgrimage to al-Lāt.

A number of the early Muslims mentioned that this had been a man who mixed roasted grain meal for the pilgrims and fed it to them. When he died, they devoted themselves to his grave, and he became an idol to whom pilgrimage was made, prayers were offered, and supplications were addressed besides Allah. A number of the early Muslims recited:

“Have you considered al-Lāt?”

with the t doubled, as al-Lātt. Al-Lāt belonged to the people of Tā’if, al-‘Uzzā to the people of Mecca, and Manāt to the people of Medina.

Hence the account: “On the day of Uhud, Abū Sufyān began chanting in verse, saying:

‘Rise high, Hubal!’

---

## Page 13438

so the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Will you not answer him?’

They asked, ‘What should we say?’

He said, ‘Say: Allah is higher and more glorious.’

Abū Sufyān then said, ‘We have al-‘Uzzā, and you have no ‘Uzzā.’

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Will you not answer him?’

They asked, ‘What should we say?’

He said, ‘Say: Allah is our Protector, and you have no protector.’”

Travelling to venerated places is a form of pilgrimage. Polytheists of various nations make pilgrimage to their gods, just as the Arabs used to make pilgrimage to al-Lāt, al-‘Uzzā, and Manāt—the third, the other one. Alongside this, they also made pilgrimage to the House, circumambulated it, and stood at ‘Arafāt. Thus, they sometimes worshipped Allah and sometimes worshipped others besides Him.

In their pilgrimage invocation, they would say: “Here I am at Your service. You have no partner except a partner who belongs to You: You own him and all that he owns.”

For this reason, Allah Most High said:

“He sets forth for you an example drawn from yourselves. Do you have, among the slaves you own, any partners in what We have provided for you, so that you are equal in it and fear them as you fear one another?”

Allah Most High is saying: “If one of you would not accept his slave as an equal partner, how can you make those whom I own partners with Me?” Everything other than Allah—angels, prophets, the righteous, and all other creatures—is owned by Him. Glory be to Him: there is no god but Him. His is the dominion, and His is all praise, and He has power over all things.

For this reason, taking angels and prophets as partners with Allah is deemed disbelief. Allah Most High said:

“Nor would he command you to take the angels and prophets as lords. Would he command you to disbelieve after you have submitted to Allah?”

He also condemned the Christians for associating partners with Him, saying:

---

## Page 13439

“They have taken their rabbis and monks, as well as the Messiah, son of Mary, as lords besides Allah, though they were commanded only to worship one God. There is no god but Him. Glory be to Him above what they associate with Him!”

The polytheists of these times, in India and elsewhere, make pilgrimage to their gods, such as Somnath and their other deities. Likewise, Christians make pilgrimage to Qumāmah and Bethlehem, and to the icon at Saydnāyā—an icon being an image—and to other churches of theirs containing images which they venerate, invoke, and seek as intercessors.

Scholars of Quranic commentary, historical biography, and other disciplines have mentioned Abraha, the king of Abyssinia who led the elephant to Mecca to destroy it, when the Abyssinians had seized Yemen and subdued the Arabs. Afterwards, Sayf ibn Dhī Yazan went to seek aid from Chosroes, the king of Persia, who supported him with an army until he expelled the Abyssinians from Yemen. He was among those who foretold the coming of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

The sign of the elephant, through which Allah Most High manifested the sanctity of the Kaaba, occurred in the year of the birth of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Allah sent against them birds in separate flocks, pelting them with stones of hardened clay. The flocks were separate groups, and the stones were clay that had turned to stone. This event is among the proofs of his prophethood, the signs of his mission, and the evidence for his religious law. And it is to this House that he and his community alone make pilgrimage and towards which they alone pray.

They said:

Abraha had built a church in Yemen and wanted to divert the Arabs’ pilgrimage to it. An Arab man entered the church and relieved himself there, which angered

---

## Page 13440

Abraha, who then set out for the Kaaba to demolish it, until events unfolded as they did.

Allah Most High says:

“Have you not seen how your Lord dealt with the people of the elephant?
Did He not bring their plot to nothing?
And He sent against them birds in flocks,
pelting them with stones of hardened clay,
leaving them like chewed-up straw.”

It is well known among scholars of Quranic commentary, historical biography, and other disciplines that he built a church to which he wanted to divert the Arabs’ pilgrimage. It is also clear that he intended only to perform there what is performed in Christian churches. This shows that travelling to churches among them is of the same kind as pilgrimage among Muslims: it is called pilgrimage, and through it the Sacred House is rivalled. It also shows that anyone who intends to designate a place for worship, to be travelled to as the Sacred Mosque is travelled to, intends an act of worship of the same kind as pilgrimage.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade anyone to make a pilgrimage or travel to any place other than the three mosques. The obligatory pilgrimage, which is what the unqualified word “pilgrimage” denotes, is exclusively to the Sacred Mosque. The other two mosques are destinations for travel to a particular place to worship there. Any other journey to a venerated place is of the same kind as making pilgrimage to it, and that is forbidden.

Likewise, in the report: “When Abū Sufyān met with Umayya ibn Abī al-Salt al-Thaqafī, the latter mentioned a Christian scholar who had told him that a prophet would soon be sent from among the Arabs. Umayya said: ‘I said, “We are Arabs.” He replied, “He comes from the people of a House to which the Arabs make pilgrimage.” I said, “We, the people of Thaqīf, have among us a house to which the Arabs make pilgrimage.”

---

## Page 13441

He replied, “He is not one of you; he is from your brethren, Quraysh.”’” As mentioned earlier.

Thaqīf had among them al-Lāt, mentioned in the Quran in the words of Allah Most High:

“Have you considered al-Lāt and al-‘Uzzā,
and Manāt, the third—the other one?
Is the male for you and the female for Him?”

It is related that this was the place where a man used to mix roasted flour with liquid and serve it to the pilgrims as a drink. When he died, they devoted themselves to his grave, and it became a great idol that was worshipped. They called travelling to it a pilgrimage, as mentioned earlier. This shows that travelling to shrines constitutes pilgrimage to them, as some ordinary people say: “By the right of the Prophet, to whom riding beasts make pilgrimage.”

‘Abd ibn Humayd said in his Quran commentary:

“Qabīsa narrated to us, from Sufyān, from Mansūr, from Mujāhid, concerning ‘Have you considered al-Lāt and al-‘Uzzā?’ He said: ‘There was a man who used to mix roasted flour with liquid. When he died, his grave was made a place of prayer.’”

He also said:

“Sulaymān ibn Dāwūd narrated to us, from Abū al-Ashhab, from Abū al-Jawzā’, from Ibn ‘Abbās, who said: ‘Al-Lāt was a man who used to mix roasted flour with liquid for the pilgrims.’”

Ibn Abī Hātim likewise narrated it from Abū al-Jawzā’, from Ibn ‘Abbās, who said:

“He used to mix roasted flour with liquid on the stone, and no one drank any of it without growing stout, so they worshipped him.”

It is related from al-A‘mash that he said:

“Mujāhid used to recite ‘al-Lātt,’ with a doubled t, and would say: ‘There was a man who used to mix roasted flour with liquid on a rock along the road to al-Tā’if and feed it to the people. He died and was buried, and they devoted themselves to his grave.’”

Sulaymān ibn Harb said:

“Hammād ibn Zayd narrated to us, from ‘Amr ibn Mālik, from Abū al-Jawzā’, who said: ‘Al-Lāt was a stone on which roasted flour was mixed with liquid, and so it was called al-Lāt.’”

And he said:

---

## Page 13442

“‘Ubayd Allāh ibn Mūsā narrated to us, from Isrā’īl, from al-Suddī, from Abū Sālih, who said: ‘Al-Lāt was the man who tended their gods and mixed roasted flour with liquid for them. Al-‘Uzzā was a palm tree on which they hung draperies and coloured wool. Manāt was a stone at Qudayd.’”

A number of the early generations recited al-Lātt, with a doubled t. It has also been said that it is a name formed by altering the name Allah. Al-Khattābī said:

“The polytheists sought to use Allah as a name for some of their idols, but Allah diverted it to al-Lāt, safeguarding and defending this name.”

I say: There is no contradiction between the two explanations or the two readings. There was a man who used to mix roasted flour with liquid on a stone. They devoted themselves to his grave and called him by this name, pronouncing it with a single t. They intended to say, “He is the deity,” just as they used to call idols gods. Thus, the name combined both meanings.

Al-Lāt belonged to the people of al-Tā’if, who called her al-Rabba (the Lady), while al-‘Uzzā belonged to the people of Mecca. Hence, on the day of Uhud, Abū Sufyān said:

“We have al-‘Uzzā, and you have no ‘Uzzā.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, “Will you not answer him?”

They asked, “What should we say?”

He replied, “Say: ‘Allah is our Protector, and you have no protector.’”

The remainder of the hadith has already been cited.

Manāt belonged to the people of Medina. Thus, every city in the Hijaz had a false deity to which its people made pilgrimage, which they took as an intercessor, and which they worshipped. As for the statement of some Quran commentators that al-‘Uzzā belonged to Ghatafān, this was because Ghatafān worshipped her and she lay in the direction of their lands, while the people of Mecca made pilgrimage to her.

---

## Page 13443

for al-‘Uzzā stood in the valley of Nakhla, towards ‘Arafāt. Authentic reports establish that the people of Mecca worshipped al-‘Uzzā, just as mass-transmitted reports establish that al-Lāt belonged to the people of al-Tā’if. Manāt stood near Qudayd, and the people of Medina would enter the state of pilgrimage consecration for her, as is established in the two Sahīh collections from ‘Ā’isha, may Allah be pleased with her.

As for Ma‘mar ibn al-Muthannā’s statement that these three were stone idols inside the Kaaba, it is false by the agreement of those knowledgeable in this field. Rather, it was Hubal that stood inside the Kaaba, for whom Abū Sufyān chanted on the day of Uhud:

“Rise high, Hubal! Rise high, Hubal!”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, “Will you not answer him?”

They asked, “What should we say?”

He replied, “Say: ‘Allah is higher and more majestic.’”

This has already been mentioned. Isāf and Nā’ila stood on al-Safā and al-Marwa, and there were three hundred and sixty idols around the Kaaba.

These three names—al-Lāt, al-‘Uzzā and Manāt—are feminine. In any case, Umayya ibn Abī al-Salt said:

“Among us stands a House to which the Arabs make pilgrimage.”

Abū Sufyān agreed with him on this. This shows that journeying to those sites constitutes a pilgrimage, and pilgrimage is ritual worship. It is therefore a pilgrimage to somewhere other than the House of Allah and ritual worship devoted to other than Allah, just as supplication addressed to them is prayer to other than Allah.

Allah Most High has said:

“Say: ‘My Lord has indeed guided me to a straight path, an upright religion, the way of Abraham, who was devoted to Allah alone and was not one of the polytheists.’”

“Say: ‘Indeed, my prayer, my ritual worship, my living and my dying are for Allah, Lord of all worlds.’”

“He has no partner. This I have been commanded, and I am the first of the Muslims.”

Thus Allah Most High commanded His Prophet

---

## Page 13444

, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to devote his prayer and ritual worship to Allah. Whoever travels to a place other than those houses of Allah to which travel is prescribed, and invokes anyone other than Allah, has devoted his ritual worship and prayer to someone other than Allah, Mighty and Majestic.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited travelling to any mosque other than the three mosques, even though it is a house of Allah, because it has no special distinction warranting such a journey. Neither he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor the prophets before him prescribed travelling to it. The three mosques are different: each was built by a prophet who called people to travel to it. They therefore possess special distinctions that other mosques do not.

If travelling to houses of Allah other than these three is not prescribed, by agreement of the four imams—indeed, the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited it—then what of travelling to the houses of created beings whose graves are made into mosques, idols, and places of celebration, through which partners are associated with Allah and which are invoked besides Him? So much so that many who revere them consider pilgrimage to them superior to pilgrimage to the House of Allah. They thus regard polytheism and idol worship as superior to worshipping Allah alone and worshipping the Most Merciful, as some polytheists do.

Allah Most High said:

“Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating partners with Him, but He forgives anything less than that for whom He wills. Whoever associates partners with Allah has indeed strayed far away.”

“They invoke none besides Him but female deities, and they invoke none but a rebellious devil.”

“Allah has cursed him.”

These idols had devils that spoke to their worshippers and appeared to them.

Ibn ‘Abbās said:

“At every

---

## Page 13445

idol there is a devil who appears to its custodians and speaks to them.”

Ubayy ibn Ka‘b said:
“With every idol there is a female jinn.”

It has also been said that “females” here means inanimate things. Al-Hasan said:
“Everything without life, such as wood and stone, is termed ‘female.’”

Al-Zajjāj said:
“All inanimate things are spoken of in the feminine. Thus, one says in Arabic, ‘The stones please me’ and ‘The dirhams benefit you.’”

This is not confined to inanimate things. Rather, everything other than Allah Most High can be referred to collectively using a feminine form. Thus one says al-malā’ika (“angels”), and those worshipped besides Allah are called āliha (“gods”). Allah Most High said:
