“Say, ‘Whose testimony is greatest?’ Say, ‘Allah is witness between me and you. This Quran has been revealed to me so that I may warn you with it and whoever it reaches. Do you really testify that there are other gods besides Allah?’ Say, ‘I do not so testify.’ Say, ‘He is but One God, and I am free of whatever you associate with Him.’”

Allah Most High also said:

“We brought the Children of Israel across the sea, and they came upon a people devoted to their idols. They said, ‘O Moses, make us a god just as they have gods.’ He said, ‘You are indeed an ignorant people.’”

“What these people are engaged in is doomed to destruction, and all they have been doing is in vain.”

“He said, ‘Shall I seek a god for you other than Allah, when He has favoured you above all peoples?’”

These are idols, and they are referred to in the feminine. Allah Most High said:

“Have you considered those you invoke besides Allah? If Allah intends harm for me, can they remove His harm? Or if He intends mercy for me, can they withhold His mercy? Say, ‘Allah is sufficient for me. In Him those who trust place their trust.’”

All the deities worshipped besides Allah are of this nature, and they are

---

## Page 13446

the idols taken as objects of worship besides Allah. Allah Most High said:

“Nor would he command you to take the angels and prophets as lords. Would he command you to disbelieve after you have become Muslims?”

Joseph the truthful said:

“O my two fellow prisoners! Are many different lords better, or Allah, the One, the All-Prevailing?”

“You worship besides Him nothing but names you and your forefathers have devised, for which Allah has sent down no authority.”

Everyone who worships anything besides Allah worships nothing but names for which Allah has sent down no authority. Moreover, those who worship angels or prophets do not see them; rather, they worship statues fashioned in their likenesses, made of earth, stone, and wood. Thus, they worship lifeless things.

In the Sahīh—Sahīh Muslim—Abū al-Hayyāj al-Asadī reported:

“ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib, may Allah be pleased with him, said to me: ‘Shall I send you on the same mission on which the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, sent me? He sent me to leave no image without effacing it, and no raised grave without leveling it.’”

Allah Most High said:

“Is He who creates like one who does not create? Will you not then take heed?”

“If you tried to count Allah’s blessings, you could never enumerate them. Indeed, Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

“Allah knows what you conceal and what you reveal.”

“Those whom they invoke besides Allah create nothing; they themselves are created.”

“They are dead, not alive, and they do not know when they will be resurrected.”

None of the dead knows when they will be resurrected. No one but Allah, Mighty and Majestic, knows when the Hour will come. It is reported in the Sahīh: “When the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, died, the people were addressed by Abū Bakr

---

## Page 13447

al-Siddīq, who said:

‘Whoever worshipped Muhammad, Muhammad has died; but whoever worships Allah, Allah is Ever-Living and never dies.’ He then recited the words of Allah Most High:

‘Muhammad is no more than a messenger; messengers have passed away before him. If he dies or is killed, will you turn back on your heels? Whoever turns back on his heels will not harm Allah at all, and Allah will reward the grateful.’

It was as though the people had never heard it until Abū Bakr recited it.” There was not a single person who was not reciting it. In times of crisis, people lose sight of the meanings of the Quran, but when reminded of them, they recognize them.

Allah Most High said:

“Indeed, when those who fear Allah are touched by a prompting from Satan, they remember Allah, and then they see clearly.”

“But their brothers draw them deeper into error and never relent.”

As for His words, Most High:

“Are males for you and females for Him?”

“That, then, is an unjust division!”

That is, an unjust, distorted division, since you assign to yourselves what you love—males—while assigning females to Me. This refers to their claim that the angels are Allah’s daughters: they attributed female children to Him, although they themselves hated for any of their children to be female. They are like the Christians, who attribute a child to Allah while considering a senior monk too exalted to have a child.

As for al-Lāt, al-ʿUzzā, and Manāt, the third and last, when Allah Most High said:

“Are males for you and females for Him?”

A group, including al-Kalbī, interpreted this to mean that they used to say, “These idols are Allah’s daughters.” This is the explanation mentioned by a group of later scholars.

---

## Page 13448

But that is not so. They did not call these idols Allah’s daughters; rather, they said this of the angels, as Allah relates of them in His words later in this passage:

“Indeed, those who do not believe in the Hereafter give the angels female names.”

He also said:

“And they have made the angels, who are servants of the Most Merciful, females. Did they witness their creation?”

And He, Most High, said:

“When one of them is given news of what he attributes to the Most Merciful, his face darkens and he is filled with suppressed grief.”

A child resembles its father, just as a partner resembles his partner. They thus set up females as counterparts to Him and made them partners with Allah, glorified is He, treating them as His equals. A partner is like a brother; thus, they attributed female children and female partners to Him, assigning Him daughters and sisters, although none of them liked having either a daughter or a sister. Indeed, if a father disliked having a daughter, he disliked having a sister even more. They did not allow daughters or sisters to inherit. This reveals their extreme ignorance and injustice: they assigned to Allah what they would not accept for themselves. Thus, in their own estimation, they were greater than Allah, glorified is He.

This is like the example He set forth for them when He, Most High, said:

“And they assign to what they do not know a share of what We have provided for them. By Allah, you will surely be questioned about what you used to fabricate.”

“And they attribute daughters to Allah—glorified is He—while they have whatever they desire.”

Through to His words:

“Those who do not believe in the Hereafter have evil attributes, while Allah has the highest attributes. And He is the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

And He, Most High, said:

“He sets forth for you an example drawn from yourselves: do you have, among those whom your right hands possess, any partners in what We have provided for you, so that you are

---

## Page 13449

equal in it, fearing them as you fear one another? Thus We explain the signs in detail for people who reason.”

They would not accept a slave belonging to one of them as his partner, yet they have made the Lord’s slaves partners with Him. Thus, they have assigned to Allah partners and children of a kind they would not accept for themselves. They would not accept their own slaves as partners, yet they have made them partners with Allah. Nor are they content with female offspring: they accept females neither as children nor as equals, yet they have made females Allah’s children and equals. The essential point is that Allah is more majestic, greater, higher and mightier than everything, yet they have assigned to Allah what they would not accept for themselves.

This applies to everyone who attributes to Allah a quality from which a created being is held to be free, such as those who say that He is poor or miserly;

those who say that He is described only by negations, or that He is described neither by negation nor by affirmation;

those who make some created beings equal to Him in any respect—by worshipping them, invoking them, relying upon them, or loving them as they love Him;

those who say that He acts without wisdom, but rather aimlessly;

those who say that He may put things where they do not belong, punishing the best people and honouring the worst;

those who say that He cannot speak by His will;

those who say that He neither hears nor sees;

and those who say that someone other than Him may be loved as He is loved, and likewise invoked and petitioned. They have thus made His slave a rival to Him. There are many similar examples.

---

## Page 13450

The Quran abounds in affirmations of Allah’s oneness and that nothing is like Him. No created thing may be likened to Him in any respect, for nothing is like Him—in His essence, His attributes, His actions, or the worship, love, reliance, obedience, supplication and all other rights that are His due.

Allah Most High says:

“Lord of the heavens and the earth and whatever is between them, so worship Him and persevere in His worship. Do you know of anyone comparable to Him?”

No one is His peer. No one deserves to bear the names that belong exclusively to Him, nor does anyone equal Him in what any of His names signifies—whether the Ever-Living, the All-Knowing, the All-Powerful, or any other name. Nor does anyone equal Him in what is signified by such general names as ‘being’ and ‘existent’. Nor can anyone else be God, Lord or Creator.

Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘He is Allah, the One;
Allah, the Eternal Refuge.
He neither begets nor is born,
and there is none comparable to Him.’”

Thus, no one is comparable to Him in any respect: nothing equals Him, nothing resembles Him, and nothing is His counterpart.

Allah Most High says:

“All praise is for Allah, who created the heavens and the earth and made darkness and light. Yet those who disbelieve set up equals to their Lord.”

And Allah Most High says:

“So they will be flung headlong into it, they and the misguided,
and the hosts of Iblīs, all together.
They will say, as they dispute there,
‘By Allah, we were clearly astray
when we made you equal to the Lord of all worlds.’”

And Allah Most High says:

“They worship besides Allah those who have no power to provide them with any sustenance from the heavens or the earth, nor are they capable of doing so.
So do not draw comparisons with Allah. Indeed, Allah knows and you do not know.”

---

## Page 13451

What we have mentioned—that travelling to revered places, whether graves or other sites, is regarded by its practitioners as Muslims regard Hajj—is well known among earlier and later generations, both in terminology and in substance. Through invoking created beings, humbling themselves before them and pleading with them, they seek the equivalent of what Muslims seek through invoking Allah Most High, humbling themselves before Him and pleading with Him. Yet, as Allah Most High says:

“Among the people are those who take others besides Allah as equals, loving them as they love Allah. But those who believe are stronger in their love for Allah.”

They call this a pilgrimage to those places, and this is well known among their earlier and later generations. Likewise, people of innovation and misguidance among the Muslims, such as the Rāfidah and others, make pilgrimage to shrines and to the graves of their shaykhs and imams, calling this Hajj.

Their herald proclaims, “Set out for the greater pilgrimage!” They display a banner for this pilgrimage, accompanied by a herald calling people to it, just as Muslims raise a banner for Hajj. But the herald of the people of innovation publicly proclaims, in places such as Baghdad, “Set out for the greater pilgrimage!” He means travelling to one of the shrines. Thus, they make the journey to the grave of a created being the greater pilgrimage, while regarding Hajj to the House of Allah as the lesser. Their imams have stated this in their writings.

Some ignorant people say, “By the right of the Prophet, to whom the mounts journey on pilgrimage!”

Since the polytheists offer prayers, invoke created beings and make pilgrimage to their graves, Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘My Lord has guided me to a straight path—a sound religion, the faith of Abraham, who inclined to truth and was not one of the polytheists.’”

“Say, ‘Indeed, my prayer, my rites of worship, my living and my dying

---

## Page 13452

of all worlds. He has no partner. This I have been commanded, and I am the first of those who submit.’”

Allah Most High also says:

“Do not invoke another god alongside Allah.”

In explaining His words, “my rites of worship,” commentators have mentioned sacrificing animals for Allah and making Hajj to the House of Allah. They have also stated that the term encompasses worship in general.

Allah, glorified is He, has made clear in the Quran that both sacrifice and Hajj are rites of worship. He says:

“For every community We have appointed a rite of sacrifice, so that they may mention Allah’s name over the livestock He has provided for them.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever slaughters after the prayer has properly performed the sacrificial rite. Whoever slaughters before the prayer has merely slaughtered a sheep for meat, providing it early for his family; it does not count as a ritual sacrifice at all.”

Allah Most High relates that Abraham and Ishmael said:

“Our Lord, accept this from us. Indeed, You are the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing.

“Our Lord, make us both submissive to You, and from our descendants a community submissive to You. Show us our rites and accept our repentance. Indeed, You are the Accepter of Repentance, the Most Merciful.”

Allah thus showed Abraham and his son Ishmael the places to be visited during Hajj and the acts to be performed there, such as circumambulation, walking between Safā and Marwah, the ritual standing, and casting pebbles, as several of the early generations have explained.

Prayer encompasses invocation both in the sense of worship and in the sense of petition. It combines both. Allah Most High says:

“Your Lord has said, ‘Call upon Me; I will answer you. Indeed, those who are too proud to worship Me will enter Hell, humbled.’”

Calling upon Him has thus been interpreted as asking Him. Allah commanded the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to say:

“Say, ‘Indeed, my prayer, my rites of worship, my living and my dying are for Allah, Lord of

---

## Page 13453

all worlds.’”

Thus, Allah Most High commanded him to devote invocation and prayer to Allah. Mosques must be built only for Allah—not over the grave of any created being, nor for that being’s sake—and no journeys are to be made to the houses of created beings. Indeed, he prohibited making pilgrimage or journeying even to those houses of Allah that do not possess those special distinctions.

This and similar matters are known from the words and Sunnah of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace; the practice of his rightly guided caliphs; the ways of the Companions after him and those who followed them in righteousness; and the statements of the four imams of the Muslims and others. Hence, no one can cite any imam of the Muslims as recommending travel to visit the grave of a prophet or a righteous man. Whoever reports such a statement must provide its source.

Since this is the case, and the fatwa contains nothing beyond what the imams and scholars of the Muslims have stated, whoever opposes it opposes the Muslims’ religion and revealed law, the Sunnah of their Prophet, and the practice of his rightly guided caliphs. He also opposes that with which Allah sent His messengers and revealed His Books: affirming His oneness and worshipping Him alone, without partner, and worshipping Him only through what He has prescribed as obligatory or recommended—not through what He has forbidden and has not prescribed.

Allah, glorified is He, sent Muhammad with guidance and the religion of truth to make it prevail over all religion, and Allah suffices as a witness. He sent him with the religion of Islam, with which He sent all the prophets, for the religion with Allah is Islam:

“Whoever seeks a religion other than Islam, it will never be accepted from him”—whether he belongs to the earlier or later generations.

---

## Page 13454

And all the prophets followed the religion of Islam, as reported in the two Sahīh collections from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“We prophets share one religion; the prophets are brothers with one father and different mothers.”

Allah Most High has stated in the Quran that Noah, Abraham, Israel, the followers of Moses and the Messiah, and others were Muslims, united in worshipping Allah alone, without partner, and worshipping Him as He, glorified and exalted is He, commanded. Thus, none besides Him is to be worshipped, nor is He to be worshipped through a religion He has not prescribed. When the Prophet was commanded in the early days of Islam to pray facing Jerusalem, that was part of the religion of Islam. Then, when that command was abrogated and he was commanded to face the Sacred House, this became part of the religion of Islam, while the abrogated practice was no longer part of it.

Allah Most High has said:

“To each of you We prescribed a law and a way.”

The Torah has its law, the Gospel its law, and the Quran its law. Whoever followed the law of the Torah or the Gospel while it remained unaltered and unabrogated was following the religion of Islam. Such were those who followed the law of the Torah without alteration before the Messiah, peace be upon him, was sent, and those who followed the law of the Gospel without alteration before Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent.

As for anyone who follows a religion that alters what Allah has prescribed, or a religion that has been abrogated, he has departed from the religion of Islam. Such are the Jews who altered the Torah, rejected the Messiah, peace be upon him, and then rejected Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace; and the Christians who altered the Gospel and rejected Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. These people, then, do not adhere to

---

## Page 13455

the religion of Islam which the prophets followed. Rather, they oppose the prophets through the truth they have rejected and the falsehood they have introduced. The same applies to every innovator who opposes the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, rejects some of the truth he brought, and introduces falsehood that the messengers did not prescribe. The Messenger disavows whatever such a person has innovated in opposition to him.

Allah Most High says:

“But if they disobey you, say, ‘I am free of what you do.’”

And Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, those who have divided their religion and become sects—you have nothing to do with them.”

Thus, what is lawful is what Allah and His Messenger have made lawful; what is unlawful is what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden; and religion is what Allah and His Messenger have prescribed.

Allah has condemned the polytheists for declaring things lawful or unlawful and instituting a religion for which Allah has given no permission. Allah Most High says:

“Or do they have partners who have prescribed for them a religion for which Allah has given no permission?”

Allah, blessed and exalted is He, revealed the Meccan surahs concerning the common religion with which all the messengers were sent, including belief in Allah, His angels, His Books, His messengers, and the Last Day. Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is the Seal of the Messengers; there is no prophet after him. His community is the best community brought forth for mankind. Allah sent him with the finest of Books and the finest of revealed laws, perfected the religion for him and his community, completed His favour upon him, and approved Islam as their religion.

He called to the straight path, as Allah Most High says:

“And indeed, you guide to a straight path—the path of Allah, to whom belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. Surely, to Allah all matters return.”

Allah has commanded us to follow

---

## Page 13456

this straight path and not turn aside from it to innovated paths. Allah Most High says:

“And this is My path, which is straight, so follow it; and do not follow other ways, for they will lead you away from His way. This He has commanded you, so that you may be mindful of Him.”

ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd, may Allah be pleased with him, said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, drew a line for us, then drew lines to its right and left. He then said: ‘This is Allah’s way, and these are other ways; on each of them is a devil calling to it.’ Then he recited: ‘And this is My path, which is straight, so follow it; and do not follow other ways, for they will lead you away from His way.’”

For this reason, Allah has commanded us to say in our prayer:

“Guide us to the straight path—the path of those You have blessed, not of those who have incurred Your anger, nor of those who have gone astray.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The Jews have incurred Allah’s anger, and the Christians have gone astray.”

He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not die until he had explained the religion and made the way clear. He said:

“I have left you upon a clear, pure path, whose night is like its day. No one will deviate from it after me except one who is doomed.”

He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also said:

“I have left nothing that brings you closer to Paradise without telling you about it, nor anything that distances you from the Fire without telling you about it.”

And he said:

“Those of you who live after me will see much disagreement. So adhere to my Sunnah and the Sunnah of the rightly guided caliphs who follow me. Hold fast to it and bite firmly onto it with your molar teeth. Beware of newly introduced matters, for every newly introduced matter is an innovation, and every innovation is misguidance.”

Al-Tirmidhī said: “An authentic hadith.”

---

## Page 13457

For this reason, the imams of the Muslims would not declare anything in religion obligatory, recommended, forbidden, or permissible without scriptural evidence from the Quran, the Sunnah, or what these establish. Whatever the Muslims agree upon is truth brought by the Messenger. For his community—praise be to Allah—does not unite upon misguidance, as he himself, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, stated:

“Allah has assured you, through your Prophet, that you will not unite upon misguidance.”

Whatever they disputed, they referred back to the Quran and the Sunnah, as the Most High says:

“O you who believe, obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you. If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day. That is better and leads to the best outcome.”

This was the practice of the early generations. One person might have heard a hadith or grasped a meaning that escaped another. The other is also rewarded for exercising independent judgment and bears no sin for what remained unknown to him despite his efforts.

As related in the two Sahīh collections, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When a judge exercises independent judgment and reaches the correct conclusion, he receives two rewards; when he exercises independent judgment and errs, he receives one reward.”

If four people were to pray facing four different directions when the sky was overcast, each according to his own judgment, all would be obeying Allah, the Almighty and Majestic, and each would have discharged his obligation. Yet only one would have correctly faced the Kaaba, and he would receive two rewards.

The Most High says:

“And remember David and Solomon, when they judged concerning the field into which some people’s sheep had strayed by night. We were witness to their judgment.”

“We gave Solomon understanding of the case, and to each We gave sound judgment and knowledge.”

Thus the Most High praised

---

## Page 13458

both prophets, although He singled out one of them for understanding that particular case. The religion in its entirety is received from the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace; no one after him has the right to change any part of his religion. This is the religion of the Muslims.

The Christians, by contrast, permit their scholars and ascetics to institute laws contrary to Allah’s law. The Most High says:

“They have taken their rabbis and monks as lords besides Allah, and also the Messiah, son of Mary, although they were commanded to worship only one God. There is no god but Him. Glory be to Him, far above whatever they associate with Him!”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“They made lawful for them what was forbidden, and they obeyed them; and they forbade them what was lawful, and they obeyed them. That was how they worshipped them.”

For this reason, the imams of the Muslims would not describe anything as an act of worship, obedience, or drawing near to Allah except on the basis of scriptural evidence and following those who preceded them. They would not speak about religion without knowledge, for Allah has forbidden this in His words:

“Say: My Lord has only forbidden shameful deeds, whether openly or in secret, sin, unjust aggression, associating with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority, and saying about Allah what you do not know.”

The imams of the religion have agreed that travel to the three mosques is prescribed—the Sacred Mosque, the Mosque of the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and al-Aqsā Mosque—in contrast to destinations other than these three. For it is related in the two Sahīh collections that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not undertake a journey except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque, this mosque of mine, and al-Aqsā Mosque.”

---

## Page 13459

The Muslims differed over visiting graves. A group of the early generations held that all such visits were prohibited and that this prohibition had not been abrogated, for al-Bukhārī did not narrate the hadiths establishing its abrogation, and those hadiths had not become widely known. When al-Bukhārī discussed visiting graves, he cited as evidence the hadith of the woman who wept beside a grave.

Ibn Battāl related that al-Shaʿbī said:

“Had the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, not forbidden visiting graves, I would have visited my son’s grave.”

Al-Nakhaʿī said, “They used to disapprove of visiting graves.” A similar statement is related from Ibn Sīrīn.

Ibn Battāl said that Mālik was asked about visiting graves and replied:

“He, peace be upon him, had forbidden it, then permitted it. If someone were to do so and say nothing but good, I would see no harm in it. It is not, however, the practice of the people.”

It is also related that he held such visits in low regard.

The scholars agree that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, initially forbade visiting graves. Some said this was because it leads to associating partners with Allah; others said it was because of the wailing at graves; and others said it was because people used them as a source of mutual boasting. In explaining the words of the Most High,

“Rivalry for more distracts you, until you visit the graves,”

a number of scholars mentioned that people would compete in numbers by counting the graves of their dead.

Among those who mentioned this was Ibn ʿAtiyya in his Quran commentary. He said:

“This is a rebuke for visiting graves excessively—that is, you have made visiting graves an occupation that distracts you from worship and knowledge, seeking to swell your numbers with those who have gone before and to extol their memory. Then the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘I had forbidden you to visit

---

## Page 13460

graves; now visit them, but do not speak offensively.’ His prohibition thus accorded with the meaning of the verse. Later, he permitted visiting them to take heed, not for ostentation and boasting, raising their mounds with marble, colouring them extravagantly, or building mausoleums over them.” These are Ibn ʿAtiyya’s exact words.

The point is that the scholars agree that he had forbidden visiting graves. He also forbade preparing fruit infusions in gourds, earthenware jars, pitch-coated vessels, and tar-coated vessels. They differed, however, over whether these prohibitions had been abrogated.

One group said that they had not been abrogated, because the hadiths establishing their abrogation were not widely known. This is why Abū ʿAbd Allāh al-Bukhārī did not include reports establishing a general abrogation.

Others said that they had indeed been abrogated.

Some of the latter group held that the prohibition had merely been replaced by permission: visiting graves is permissible, not recommended. This is one position within the schools of Mālik and Ahmad. They argued that an imperative following a prohibition conveys only permission, as in the authentic hadith in which he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I had forbidden you to visit graves; now visit them. I had forbidden you to prepare fruit infusions in vessels; now prepare them, but do not drink anything intoxicating.”

Another narration reads:

“Visit them, but do not speak offensively.”

This indicates that the prohibition was intended to block the means leading to the objectionable things people used to say at graves. Similarly, preparing fruit infusions in vessels was initially prohibited because intoxicating strength could develop in them unnoticed, so that someone might drink wine without realizing it.

---

## Page 13461

The majority held that visiting the graves of believers is recommended in order to supplicate for the dead and greet them with peace, as “the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would go out to al-Baqīʿ and supplicate for them.” Likewise, it is authentically reported of him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the two Sahīhs that “he went out to the martyrs of Uhud and prayed over them as he prayed over the dead, as though bidding farewell to the living and the dead.”

It is also authentically reported in the Sahīh that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “used to teach his Companions, when visiting graves, to say: ‘Peace be upon you, believing inhabitants of these abodes. We shall, if Allah wills, join you. May Allah have mercy on those of us and of you who have gone before, and those who come later. We ask Allah for well-being for ourselves and for you. O Allah, do not deprive us of our reward on their account, do not put us to trial after them, and forgive us and them.’”

This concerns visiting the graves of believers. As for visiting a disbeliever’s grave, permission has been given for this as a reminder of the Hereafter, but it is not permissible to ask forgiveness for them. It is authentically reported in the two Sahīhs that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “visited his mother’s grave and wept, causing those around him to weep. He said: ‘I asked my Lord’s permission to visit her grave, and He permitted me. I asked His permission to seek forgiveness for her, but He did not permit me. So visit graves, for they remind you of the Hereafter.’”

The scholars who disagree each appeal to evidence from revelation, and some possess knowledge that others do not—for the scholars are the heirs of the prophets. Allah Most High said:

“And David and Solomon, when they gave judgment concerning the field into which the people’s sheep had strayed by night; We were witness to their judgment.”

“We gave Solomon understanding of the case, and to each We gave sound judgment and knowledge.”

---

## Page 13462

All three views are correct in their respective contexts. If a visit involves something forbidden—such as associating partners with Allah, lying, lamentation, wailing, or offensive speech—then it is forbidden by scholarly consensus. Such are the visits made by those who associate partners with Allah or resent His judgment; their visits are forbidden. For no religion is accepted except Islam, which means submission to His creative will and His commands. One submits to what He has ordained and decreed, and submits to what He commands and loves. We carry out His commands and call others to them; we accept His decree and rely upon Him concerning it. Thus, we are content with Allah as our Lord, Islam as our religion, and Muhammad as our Prophet.

In our prayers, we say:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

This is like His saying, Most High:

“So worship Him and put your trust in Him.”

And His saying, Most High:

“Seek help through patience and prayer. Indeed, Allah is with the patient.”

And His saying, Most High:

“Establish prayer at the two ends of the day and in the early hours of the night. Indeed, good deeds do away with misdeeds. That is a reminder for those who remember.”

“And be patient, for Allah does not let the reward of those who do good go to waste.”

The second type is visiting graves simply out of grief for the deceased because of kinship or friendship. This is permissible, just as weeping over the deceased is permissible without lamentation or wailing. Thus:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, visited his mother’s grave and wept, causing those around him to weep. He said: ‘Visit graves, for they remind you of the Hereafter.’”

He had forbidden such visits because of the wrongful practices they used to engage in. Once they had come to understand Islam, he permitted them, because they serve a beneficial purpose: remembering death. Many people, when they see a relative who is

---

## Page 13463

buried, are reminded of death and prepare for the Hereafter. Yet they may also become distraught, so the two effects conflict. Grief itself is permissible: if one seeks through it to obey Allah, it becomes an act of obedience; if one commits a sin, it becomes sinful.

As for the third type, it is visiting graves to supplicate for those buried there, just as one does in the funeral prayer. This is the recommended form of visiting established by the Sunnah: the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, practised it and taught his Companions what to say when visiting graves.

As for visiting Qubāʾ, anyone who comes to Medina is encouraged to go to Qubāʾ and pray in its mosque. Likewise, according to the majority of scholars, it is recommended that they visit al-Baqīʿ and the martyrs of Uhud, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to do. Visiting graves to supplicate for the deceased is therefore of the same nature as offering funeral prayers: its purpose is to supplicate for them, not to invoke any created being besides Allah. Nor is it permissible to turn graves into mosques, or to visit them on the grounds that supplication at or by them is better than supplication in mosques and homes.

By the agreement of the Muslims, offering funeral prayers is better than supplicating for the dead at their graves. Funeral prayer is prescribed—indeed, it is a communal obligation—established through mass transmission and agreed upon among the Muslims. If someone were to approach the bier of the deceased, invoking him besides Allah and seeking his aid, this would constitute association of partners with Allah, forbidden by the consensus of the Muslims. If someone were to lament him and wail, that too would be forbidden, though less grave than the former.

Thus, whoever cites as evidence the visits of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to those buried in al-Baqīʿ and to the people of

---

## Page 13464

Uhud in justification of the visits practised by those who associate partners with Allah and those who wail over the dead is even more misguided than someone who cites his funeral prayer as evidence that it is permissible to associate the deceased with Allah, invoke him besides Allah, lament him and wail over him. Some people do precisely this: they cite this practice of the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—which is worship of Allah and obedience to Him, for which the person performing it is rewarded, from which the person prayed for benefits, and with which the Lord, Almighty and Majestic, is pleased—as evidence that it is permissible to engage in acts that constitute association of partners with Allah, harm to the deceased and wrongdoing against oneself. Such are the visits of those who associate partners with Allah and those who give way to anguish, who neither devote their religion sincerely to Allah nor submit to what He, glorified and exalted, has decreed.

Every visit that involves doing what He has forbidden and abandoning what He has commanded is therefore prohibited. This includes visits involving anguish, foul speech and a failure to remain patient, or those involving association of partners with Allah, invocation of others besides Him and a failure to devote one’s religion sincerely to Him. This second kind is more sinful than the first.

It is not permissible to pray towards graves, or even beside them. Indeed, this is among the things the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited when he said:

“Do not pray towards graves, and do not sit on them.”

Muslim narrated it in his Sahīh.

Visiting graves thus takes two forms:

One form was prohibited by the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the scholars agree that it is not prescribed: making graves places of prayer, idols, or sites of recurring gatherings. They must not be sought out for the prescribed prayer, worshipped as idols are worshipped, or made sites of recurring gatherings where people assemble at a time

---

## Page 13465

fixed, just as the Muslims gather at ‘Arafah and Minā. As for “visitation prescribed by Islamic law,” most scholars consider it recommended. Others consider it permissible, while others hold that all such visits are prohibited, as mentioned earlier. The scriptural evidence indicates that we should interpret the scholars’ unqualified statements in light of their qualified statements and distinguish three types of visitation: prohibited, permissible and recommended. This is the correct view.

Mālik and others said: “We visit only these sites: the Prophet’s Mosque, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the Mosque of Qubā’, and those buried at al-Baqī‘ and Uhud.” The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would specifically visit only these two mosques and these two cemeteries. He would pray on Friday in his mosque and go to Qubā’ on Saturday, as reported in the two Sahīhs from Ibn ‘Umar—may Allah be pleased with him and his father:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to go to Qubā’ every Saturday, riding or walking, and pray two units of prayer there.”

As for the hadiths conveying the prohibition, they are numerous and well known, appearing in the two Sahīhs and elsewhere. Among them is his statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of prayer.”

‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, said: “Were it not for that, his grave would have been left exposed, but he feared that it would be made a place of prayer.”

Al-Bukhārī and Muslim narrated it.

In Sahīh Muslim, it is reported that five days before his death, he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Those who came before you used to take graves as places of prayer. So do not take graves as places of prayer; I forbid you to do so.”

And in the two Sahīhs, it is reported from ‘Ā’ishah and Ibn ‘Abbās

---

## Page 13466

—may Allah be pleased with them—who said:

“When death came upon the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he began placing a cloak of his over his face. Whenever he felt stifled, he uncovered it. While in that state, he said: ‘May Allah’s curse be upon the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of prayer.’”

He was warning against what they had done.

In the two Sahīhs, Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, narrated that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah destroy the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of prayer.”

Another wording reads:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of prayer.”

In the two Sahīhs, ‘Ā’ishah narrated:

“Umm Habībah and Umm Salamah mentioned a church they had seen in Abyssinia containing images. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: ‘When a righteous man among those people died, they would build a place of prayer over his grave and make those images in it. Those are the worst of creation before Allah on the Day of Resurrection.’”

‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, the Mother of the Believers, to whom the Prophet’s chamber belonged, narrated the hadiths on this subject, as did other Companions, including Ibn ‘Abbās, Abū Hurayrah, Jundub, Ibn Mas‘ūd and others.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, as narrated by Ibn Mas‘ūd:

“Among the worst of people are those who are still alive when the Hour overtakes them, and those who take graves as places of prayer.”

Abū Hātim narrated it in his Sahīh, and Imam Ahmad in his Musnad.

In Sunan Abī Dāwūd, it is reported that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not make my grave a place of recurring observance. Send blessings upon me wherever you may be, for your blessings reach me.”

In Mālik’s Muwatta’, it is reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“O Allah, do not let my grave become an idol that is worshipped. Severe was

---

## Page 13467

Allah’s wrath against a people who took the graves of their prophets as places of prayer.”

In Sunan Sa‘īd ibn Mansūr, it is reported that ‘Abd Allāh ibn Hasan ibn Husayn ibn ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib—one of the Hasanī nobles, indeed the most eminent among them in the generation following the Successors, during the caliphates of al-Mansūr and others—saw a man frequently visiting the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He said:

“Sir, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: ‘Do not make my grave a place of recurring observance. Send blessings upon me wherever you may be, for your blessings reach me.’ Thus, you and a man in al-Andalus are on an equal footing.”

When the imams sought to follow his Sunnah in visiting his honoured grave and greeting him, they looked for a reliable basis in his Sunnah. Imam Ahmad therefore relied on the hadith in the Sunan collections, narrated by Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No one greets me without Allah returning my soul to me so that I may return his greeting.”

Abū Dāwūd took this from Ahmad. He cited no other hadith concerning visiting his honoured grave, placing it under the heading “Chapter on Visiting the Grave.” Yet whether the hadith establishes the intended point is disputed and requires qualification, for Muslims agree that it does not support everything people call a “visit.”

The question remains concerning the greeting mentioned in it: does it mean greeting him at the grave, as those who entered the quarters of ‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, used to greet him? Or does it encompass both this and greeting him from outside the chamber? Those who cited it as evidence understood it to encompass both.

---

## Page 13468

and this was the extent of what they had from him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, on this subject. He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, hears greetings from those nearby, while the angels convey to him blessings and greetings from those far away. As reported by al-Nasā’ī, he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has angels who travel about, conveying to me the greetings of my community.”

In the Sunan collections, Aws ibn Aws, may Allah be pleased with him, reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Send abundant blessings upon me on Friday and the night before it, for your blessings are presented to me.”

They asked, “How will our blessings be presented to you when your body has decayed?”

He replied, “Allah has forbidden the earth to consume the bodies of the prophets.”

May Allah’s blessings and abundant peace be upon him and his family.

Mālik mentioned in his Muwatta’ that ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar would come and say, “Peace be upon you, O Messenger of Allah. Peace be upon you, O Abū Bakr. Peace be upon you, Father.” Then he would leave. Another version states that he would do this upon returning from a journey. Ma‘mar narrated this from Nāfi‘, who reported it from Ibn ‘Umar. This was what Mālik, may Allah have mercy on him, relied upon regarding what should be done at the chamber, since he had no report on the matter other than that of Ibn ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with them both.

As for anything beyond this, such as remaining there to supplicate for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, while sending abundant blessings and greetings upon him, Mālik disapproved of it and said: “It is an innovation that the early generations did not practise. Nothing will reform the latter part of this community except what reformed its first.”

As for travelling to the graves of the prophets and the righteous, this did not exist in Islam during Mālik’s time. It arose only after the first three generations—the generation of

---

## Page 13469

the Companions, the Successors, and their successors. This practice was not prevalent during those generations whom the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, praised. Rather, falsehood and polytheism emerged after them. Thus, when Mālik was asked about a man who had vowed to visit the Prophet’s grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he said:

“If he intended the mosque, let him go there and pray in it. But if he intended the grave, he should not do so, because of the hadith: ‘Do not undertake a journey except to three mosques.’”

Likewise, visiting the graves of the prophets and the righteous to invoke them, ask them to supplicate, or deliberately supplicate at their graves in the belief that supplications are more likely to be answered there was not known in Mālik’s time, whether at the Prophet’s grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or elsewhere. If Mālik, may Allah have mercy on him, disapproved of a man standing for a long time by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to supplicate, then what of someone whose intention is neither to greet him nor to supplicate for him, but only to invoke him and ask him to fulfil his needs, raising his voice in his presence? In doing so, he offends the Messenger, associates partners with Allah, and wrongs himself.

None of the imams—neither the four nor any others—relied on any of the hadiths that some people narrate concerning this, such as their report that he said:

“Whoever visits me after my death is as though he visited me during my lifetime.”

Or the saying:

“Whoever visits me and my father in a single year, I guarantee him Paradise from Allah.”

And other such reports. None of the imams of the Muslims narrated this, nor did they rely

---

## Page 13470

on them. Neither the compilers of the authentic hadith collections nor those of the reliable Sunan collections, such as Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasā’ī, narrated them, for they are weak—indeed, fabricated—as scholars have explained in their discussions of them. Those who visited him during his lifetime, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, were among those who emigrated to him. Were someone who came after them to spend gold equal to Mount Uhud, it would not equal a mudd given by any one of them, or even half of it. Even by fulfilling the obligatory duties, such a person does not equal the Companions. How, then, could he equal them through voluntary acts, through something that is not an act of devotion, or through something that is prohibited?

Mālik, may Allah be pleased with him, disliked someone saying, “I visited the Prophet’s grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.” He disliked this wording because the Sunnah did not use it concerning his grave. Scholars have offered various explanations for this. Others permitted this wording on the basis of the general hadiths concerning visiting graves. Like all other scholars, Mālik recommended travelling to Medina and praying in the Prophet’s mosque, as well as greeting him and his two Companions at their graves, following the example of Ibn ‘Umar. Mālik was among the most knowledgeable people concerning this, for he had seen the Successors who had seen the Companions in Medina. He therefore recommended following the early generations in this matter and disliked anyone introducing an innovation there.

He thus disliked a man standing and supplicating for a long time at the Prophet’s grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, because the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, did not do that.

Mālik also disliked the people of Medina going to the Prophet’s grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, every time they entered the mosque, because the early generations did not do that.

Mālik, may Allah have mercy on him, said:

“Never will

---

## Page 13471

the later generations of this community be set right except by what set its earliest generations right.”

Rather, they would come to his mosque and pray behind Abū Bakr al-Siddīq, ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with them all. These four led the prayers in his mosque, and the Muslims prayed behind them just as they had prayed behind him. During the prayer, they would say:

“Peace be upon you, O Prophet, and Allah’s mercy and blessings.”

They said this just as they had during his lifetime. Then, when they had finished the prayer, they would remain seated or leave. They did not come to the grave to offer greetings of peace, knowing that invoking blessings and peace upon him during the prayer was more complete and better, and was the prescribed practice.

As for entering the area of his grave to invoke blessings and peace upon him there, or to pray and supplicate, he did not prescribe this for them. Rather, he forbade them, saying:

“Do not make my grave an ‘īd. Invoke blessings upon me wherever you may be, for your invocations of blessings reach me.”

He thus explained that invocations of blessings reach him from afar, as do greetings of peace. Whoever invokes blessings upon him once, Allah bestows blessings upon that person ten times in return. Whoever sends greetings of peace upon him once, Allah sends peace upon that person ten times, as related in some hadiths.

Singling out the chamber for invoking blessings and peace makes it an ‘īd, which he had forbidden them to do. He also forbade them to take his grave, or anyone else’s grave, as a place of prayer. He cursed those who did so, that they might beware of incurring a curse like the one that had befallen others.

His Companions were the best generation, the most knowledgeable of this community concerning his Sunnah, and the most obedient to his commands. When they entered his mosque, none of them would go to his grave

---

## Page 13472

either from inside the chamber or from outside it. In their time, the chamber could be entered through its door, since ‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, was living there. It remained accessible afterward, until the other wall was built. Yet despite being able to reach his grave, they did not enter it to greet him with peace, invoke blessings upon him, supplicate for themselves, or ask about a hadith or a matter of knowledge.

Nor did Satan entertain hopes of deceiving them by making them hear speech or greetings of peace, so that they would think that he himself had spoken to them, given them legal opinions, and explained hadiths to them, or that he had returned their greetings in a voice audible from outside. Satan did entertain such hopes concerning others and led them astray at his grave and the graves of others. They came to think that the occupant of the grave was speaking to them in person, giving them legal opinions, and issuing commands and prohibitions; that he would emerge from the grave and they would see him outside it. They imagined that the very bodies of the dead had emerged from their graves to speak to them, and that the deceased person’s soul had taken bodily form before them, so that they saw it just as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, saw them on the night of the Ascension while awake, not in a dream.

The Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, were the best generation of this community, which is the best community ever brought forth for humanity. They received the religion directly from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, without an intermediary. They thus understood his intentions, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, witnessed his actions, and heard directly from him what those who came after them did not. Likewise, they learned from one another things unavailable to later generations. They had broken with all the peoples of the earth and shown them enmity, forsaken all communities and their religions, and waged jihad against them with their lives

---

## Page 13473

and their wealth. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in an authentic hadith:

“Do not revile my Companions. By Him in Whose hand is my soul, if any one of you were to spend gold equal to Mount Uhud, it would not equal a double handful given by one of them, nor even half of it.”

He said this to Khālid ibn al-Walīd when he quarrelled with ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf, because ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf was among the earliest forerunners: those who spent and fought before the victory, meaning the victory at al-Hudaybiyah. Khālid, ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās, and ‘Uthmān ibn Talhah embraced Islam during the truce after al-Hudaybiyah and before the conquest of Mecca. They were therefore among the later Emigrants, not the earliest Emigrants.

As for those who embraced Islam in the year of the conquest of Mecca, they were not Emigrants, for there is no emigration after the conquest. Rather, those inhabitants of Mecca who embraced Islam were called “the freed ones,” because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, released them after overcoming them by force, just as a captive is released.

Those who pledged allegiance to him beneath the tree, together with those who had emigrated to Abyssinia, were the earliest forerunners among the Emigrants and the Helpers. In an authentic report, Jābir ibn ‘Abd Allāh, may Allah be pleased with them both, said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to us on the day of al-Hudaybiyah: ‘You are the best people on earth.’ There were 1,400 of us.”

For this reason, Satan did not hope to achieve among them the misguidance and deception he achieved among those who came after them. None of them would deliberately lie about the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, even if he had other deeds for which he might be reproached. Nor was there anyone among them who belonged to

---

## Page 13474

the well-known sects of innovation, such as the Khawārij, the Rawāfid, the Qadariyyah, the Murji’ah, and the Jahmiyyah. Rather, all these groups arose only among those who came after them.

Nor did Satan hope to appear to any of them in human form and say, “I am al-Khidr,” or “I am Abraham,” or Moses, or Jesus, or the Messiah; or to speak to anyone at a grave so that he would think its occupant had spoken to him. He achieved this only among those who came after them.

He also achieved this among the Christians, when he came to them after the crucifixion and said, “I am the Messiah, and these are the marks of the nails”—he does not say, “I am a devil,” for a devil does not have a body—or words to that effect. This is what the Christians relied upon in believing that he had been crucified, not their having witnessed it. None of them witnessed the crucifixion. Rather, some Jews attended it and knew the man who was crucified, believing him to be the Messiah. For this reason, Allah counted it among their sins, even though they had not crucified him. Nevertheless, they intended this deed and rejoiced in it. Allah Most High says:

“And for their disbelief and their uttering against Mary a tremendous slander,”

“And their saying, ‘We killed the Messiah, Jesus, son of Mary, the Messenger of Allah.’ But they neither killed him nor crucified him; it was only made to appear so to them. Those who differ concerning him are indeed in doubt about him. They have no knowledge of it, following only conjecture. They certainly did not kill him.”

“Rather, Allah raised him to Himself.”

A detailed discussion of this belongs elsewhere. The point is that Satan did not hope to mislead the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, as he misled others among the people of innovation who interpreted the Quran contrary to its true meaning, were ignorant of the Sunnah, or saw and heard extraordinary phenomena and imagined them to be signs like those of

---

## Page 13475

the prophets and the righteous, when in fact they were the work of devils. In this way, too, he misled the Christians and the people of innovation. They follow what is ambiguous and abandon what is clear and definitive. Likewise, they cling to ambiguous rational and sensory evidence: a person hears and sees things and supposes that they come from the Most Merciful, when in fact they are satanic. They abandon the plain truth in which there is no ambiguity.

Likewise, Satan did not hope to appear in the Prophet’s form and come to the aid of someone who appealed to him for help, or to convey to them a voice resembling his, because those who had seen him knew that this was shirk—associating partners with Allah—and was unlawful.

For this reason, too, Satan had no hope that any of them would say to his companions, whether during his lifetime or after his death, “If you have a need, come to my grave and appeal to me for help,” as has happened with many of those who came later.

Nor did Satan hope to come to any of them and say, “I am one of the men of the unseen, or one of the four, seven, or forty Pillars,” or tell him, “You are one of them.” They regarded this as falsehood with no reality to it.

Nor did Satan hope to come to any of them and say, “I am the Messenger of Allah,” or address him at the grave, as happened to many who came after them, at the Prophet’s grave, at other graves, and away from graves. Much the same happens to polytheists and the People of the Book: after the deaths of their revered spiritual leaders, they see them. Thus, the people of India see those whom they revere among their unbelieving spiritual leaders and others. Christians see those whom they revere among the prophets, the disciples, and others.

Those who have gone astray among the people who face the qiblah see those whom they revere: either the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

---

## Page 13476

or another of the prophets, while they themselves are awake. He speaks to them, and they speak to him. They may seek religious rulings from him and ask him about hadiths, and he answers them. Some are made to imagine that the chamber has split open and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has emerged from it, and that he and his two Companions have embraced them. Others are made to imagine that their voices, raised in greeting, have reached a distant place several days’ journey away.

I know a great many people who have experienced this and similar things. Those who have told me of their own experiences in this regard, and of what they related to other truthful people, are too numerous to mention here. Such experiences occur among many people, just as they occur among Christians and polytheists. Yet many people deny them, while many who believe them suppose that they are divine signs and that the person who witnessed them did so because of his righteousness and religious devotion. They do not realize that this comes from Satan, and that Satan misleads a person according to his lack of knowledge.

To someone with less knowledge, Satan says things that the person knows plainly contradict the Sharia. As for someone with some knowledge of it, Satan does not tell him what he knows contradicts the Sharia, nor does he give him anything beneficial to his religion. Rather, he leads him astray from some of what he already knew, for this is what devils do. Even if the person thinks he has gained something, what he has lost of his religion is greater.

For this reason, none of the Companions ever said that al-Khidr, Moses, or Jesus had come to him, or that he had heard the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, return his greeting. Ibn ʿUmar used to offer his greeting upon returning from a journey, but he never said that he heard a reply. The same was true of the Successors and their followers. This only arose among some of the later generations.

---

## Page 13477

Likewise, none of the Companions—may Allah be pleased with them—would come to him at his grave to ask him about matters of knowledge over which they disagreed or which they found unclear, neither his four caliphs nor anyone else. Yet they were the closest people to him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Even with his daughter Fātimah—may Allah be pleased with her—Satan had no hope of persuading her by saying, “Go to his grave and ask him whether his estate may be inherited or not.”

Nor did Satan have any hope of persuading them, when they suffered drought, by saying, “Ask him to pray for rain for you.” Nor did he say, “Ask him to pray for victory for you,” or to seek forgiveness for them. During his lifetime, they would ask him to pray for rain and victory for them, but after his death, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, Satan had no hope of inducing them to ask him for these things. Nor did he have such hopes during the first three generations. These deviations only appeared among those whose knowledge of monotheism and the Sunnah was limited, so Satan led them astray, just as he led the Christians astray in certain matters because of their limited knowledge of what the Messiah and the prophets before him had brought—may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon them.

Likewise, Satan had no hope of carrying any of them through the air or transporting them across great distances in a short time, as happens to many people of later generations. This was because the journeys they undertook were acts of obedience, such as journeys for Hajj, ʿumrah, and jihad. They are rewarded for every step they take on these journeys, and the greater the distance, the greater the reward. It is like someone who leaves his house for the mosque: of the steps he takes, one

---

## Page 13478

raises him a rank, while the other erases a sin. Thus Satan could not deprive them of that reward by carrying them through the air or driving them forcefully across the earth so that they would cover great distances quickly. They knew that Allah, Mighty and Majestic, had taken the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, by night from the Sacred Mosque to the Farthest Mosque only to show him some of His greatest signs. This was one of his unique distinctions. No one after him can have an ascension like this. Satan, however, makes a person imagine satanic ascensions, just as he has done with a number of people of later generations.

As for crossing a great river by walking on water, believers may sometimes need this—for example, when they cannot otherwise cross over to the enemy and complete their jihad. For this reason, Allah would honour those Companions and Successors who needed it with such a gift, as He honoured al-ʿAlāʾ ibn al-Hadramī and his companions, and Abū Muslim al-Khawlānī and his companions. A detailed discussion of this belongs elsewhere, outside this book.

The point, however, is to recognize that the Companions are the best generation and the best of creation after the prophets. Whatever has appeared among those after them, is thought to be a virtue of later generations, and was not found among the Companions, is from Satan: it is a deficiency, not a virtue, whether it concerns knowledge, acts of worship, extraordinary feats and signs, or governance and kingship. Rather, the best people after them are those who follow them most closely. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd, may Allah be pleased with him, said: “Whoever among you wishes to follow an example

---

## Page 13479

should follow those who have died, for the living are not safe from being put to trial. Those were the Companions of Muhammad: the most righteous in heart of this nation, the deepest in knowledge, and the least given to affectation. They were people whom Allah chose to accompany His Prophet and establish His religion. So recognize their due and hold fast to their guidance, for they followed the straight path.” A detailed discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

The point here is that the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, refrained from religious innovations associated with graves, whether his honoured grave or anyone else’s, because he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had forbidden them to do such things, and so that they would not resemble the People of the Book, who made the graves of the prophets into idols. Some of them would, however, come from outside and greet him upon returning from a journey, as Ibn ʿUmar used to do. Indeed, during his lifetime they would greet him and then leave the mosque; they did not come to him at every prayer. Whenever one of them came to greet him, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would return his greeting.

Likewise, when someone greets him at his grave, he returns the greeting. They used to enter ʿĀʾishah’s quarters and greet him as they had greeted him during his lifetime. One of them would say: “Peace be upon the Prophet, and Allah’s mercy and blessings.” This has also been reported generally concerning all the graves of believers: whenever a man passes the grave of someone he knew in this world and greets him, Allah restores the deceased’s soul to him so that he may return the greeting. If returning greetings applies to believers generally, it applies all the more to the best of creation. When a Muslim greets him during prayer, even if he does not return the greeting, Allah sends peace upon that Muslim ten times, as stated in the hadith: “Whoever greets me with peace once, Allah sends peace upon him

---

## Page 13480

ten times.” Thus Allah rewards him for this greeting with something better than receiving a reply, just as whoever invokes blessings upon him once receives ten blessings from Allah in return. Ibn ʿUmar would greet him and then leave, without remaining there to supplicate either for him or for himself. For this reason, Mālik disapproved of going beyond Ibn ʿUmar’s practice by remaining to supplicate for the Prophet or for oneself, because this had not been reported from any of the Companions and was therefore an outright innovation.

Mālik said:
“Nothing will reform the latter part of this nation except what reformed its first.”

Moreover, if the other Companions did not do as Ibn ʿUmar did, his practice can serve only to offer justification for it, as with similar practices observed by some of the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them. As for declaring this act recommended, forbidden, or permissible, that can be established only by evidence from revelation. Obligation, recommendation, permissibility, desirability, disapproval, and prohibition can none of them be established except through such evidence. All such evidence ultimately goes back to him, may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon him. It was he who conveyed the Quran, and he who taught the Sunnah. Through his words, consensus is known to be protected from error. Analogical reasoning is authoritative only when we know that the new case is like the original case and that the effective cause underlying the original ruling is also present in the new case.

He has taught us that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, does not contradict himself: he does not give contradictory rulings concerning two equivalent cases, nor does he apply a ruling on account of an effective cause on one occasion and withhold it on another when that cause is present, unless one of the two cases has a distinguishing feature that warrants different treatment. His law, then, is what he himself, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prescribed, and his Sunnah is what he himself instituted. The words of others are not to be attributed to him

---

## Page 13481

nor are their deeds—even if they are among the finest of people—when his Sunnah has been transmitted. Indeed, nothing may be attributed to him without evidence supporting that attribution.

For this reason, Companions such as Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, and Ibn Masʿūd would give opinions based on their own independent judgment. They might be correct and in agreement with his Sunnah, yet one of them would say:
“I offer my own opinion on this matter. If it is correct, it is from Allah; if it is mistaken, it is from me and from Satan, and Allah and His Messenger are free of it.”

For whatever conflicts with his Sunnah is either an abrogated law or an altered one. Nevertheless, scholars who exercise independent judgment are rewarded even when their opinions are mistaken, and their errors are forgiven.

When any of the Companions wished to supplicate for himself, he would face the direction of prayer and supplicate in the Prophet’s mosque, just as they used to do during his lifetime. They did not deliberately seek to supplicate beside the chamber, nor would any of them enter it to approach the grave. Greeting the Prophet with peace has been prescribed for Muslims in every prayer, and also whenever they enter a mosque, whichever mosque it may be.

As for the first kind, in every prayer the worshipper says:
“Peace be upon you, O Prophet, and Allah’s mercy and blessings.”
Then he says:
“Peace be upon us and upon Allah’s righteous servants.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:
“When you say this, it reaches every righteous servant of Allah in heaven and on earth.”

Thus, Muslims have been instructed in every prayer to greet the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, specifically, and Allah’s righteous servants among the angels, humankind, and jinn generally.

In the two Sahīhs, Ibn Masʿūd is reported to have said:
“We used to say while praying behind the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: ‘Peace be upon so-and-so and so-and-so.’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: ‘Allah is al-Salām (the Source of Peace), so when

---

## Page 13482

one of you sits during the prayer, let him say: “All greetings, prayers, and good things are for Allah. Peace be upon you, O Prophet, and Allah’s mercy and blessings. Peace be upon us and upon Allah’s righteous servants. I bear witness that there is no god but Allah, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger.”’”

Other wordings of the tashahhud (the testimony recited while seated in prayer) have also been transmitted from him, as Muslim narrated from Ibn ʿAbbās, and as Ibn ʿUmar used to teach it to the people. Muslim also narrated it from Abū Mūsā, but that is the same tashahhud as Ibn Masʿūd’s. Al-Bukhārī, however, included only Ibn Masʿūd’s tashahhud. All these wordings are permissible: the Quran was revealed in seven forms, so such variation is all the more permissible in the tashahhud.

The point is that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained that when someone praying says, “Peace be upon us and upon Allah’s righteous servants,” this greeting reaches every righteous servant of Allah in heaven and on earth. This includes the angels and the righteous among humankind and the jinn, as Allah Most High relates the jinn saying:

“Some of us are righteous and some are otherwise; we have followed different paths.”

The second kind is greeting him with peace upon entering a mosque. As reported in the Musnad and the Sunan, Fātimah, daughter of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and may Allah be pleased with him—narrated that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When one of you enters the mosque, let him say: ‘In the name of Allah, and peace be upon the Messenger of Allah. O Allah, forgive me my sins and open for me the gates of Your mercy.’ When he leaves, let him say: ‘In the name of Allah, and peace be upon the Messenger of Allah.

---

## Page 13483

O Allah, forgive me my sins and open for me the gates of Your bounty.’”

Muslim also narrated in his Sahīh the supplication upon entering the mosque, asking Allah to open the gates of His mercy, and upon leaving, asking Allah for His bounty. This supplication is particularly emphasized when entering the Prophet’s Mosque, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Accordingly, scholars mention in their works on pilgrimage rites that those who come to his mosque should say it.

Thus, greeting him with peace is prescribed when entering and leaving the mosque, and within every prayer. This is better, more beneficial, and more constant than greeting him with peace at his grave. It is wholly beneficial, with no harmful consequence to fear. Allah is pleased with it and conveys its benefit to His Messenger and to the believers. It is prescribed in every prayer and when entering and leaving the mosque, unlike greeting him with peace at the grave.

Moreover, ever since he was buried, no one has been allowed to enter where his grave is, whether to visit, pray, supplicate, or for any other purpose. ʿĀ’ishah, however, lived there because it was her house. She remained apart from the graves: the graves were at the front of the chamber, while she was at its rear. The Companions did not enter that area.

During the Companions’ time, the chamber was outside the mosque, adjoining it. It was incorporated into the mosque only during the caliphate of al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān, after the deaths of the four ʿAbd Allāhs: Ibn ʿUmar, Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn al-Zubayr, and Ibn ʿAmr. Indeed, this was after the deaths of all the Companions who had been in Medina, for the last to die there was Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh, a few years after

---

## Page 13484

70 AH. The mosque was expanded sometime in the 80s AH. The Companions would neither enter the area containing the grave nor stand beside it outside, even though they entered his mosque night and day. He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“One prayer in this mosque of mine is better than a thousand prayers in any other mosque, except the Sacred Mosque.”

He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also said:

“Journeys should not be undertaken except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque, this mosque of mine, and al-Aqsā Mosque.”

They would return from their travels to meet the Rightly Guided Caliphs and for other purposes. They would pray in his mosque and send greetings of peace upon him during prayer and when entering and leaving the mosque. They would not come to the grave, since they regarded this as something he had neither commanded them to do nor instituted for them. Rather, he had commanded and instituted for them the sending of blessings and greetings of peace upon him during prayer, when entering mosques, and on other occasions. Ibn ‘Umar, however, would come to his grave and greet him and his two companions upon returning from a journey. Others besides Ibn ‘Umar may also have done this. Accordingly, some scholars considered this permissible, following the example of the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them.

Ibn ‘Umar would offer his greetings and then leave, without lingering. He would say: “Peace be upon you, O Messenger of Allah. Peace be upon you, O Abū Bakr. Peace be upon you, O my father.” Then he would leave. Most of the Companions did not do as Ibn ‘Umar did. Rather, the caliphs and others would travel for Hajj and other purposes, then return without doing that, since this was not

---

## Page 13485

in their view, a practice he had instituted for them. Likewise, during the caliphs’ time and afterward, his wives would travel for Hajj, and then each would return to her home, as he had instructed them.

During the caliphates of Abū Bakr al-Siddīq and ‘Umar, the reinforcements from Yemen—of whom Allah Most High said:

“Allah will bring forth a people whom He loves and who love Him”—

would come in groups from Yemen to fight in Allah’s cause. They would pray behind Abū Bakr and ‘Umar in his mosque, but none would enter the chamber or stand outside it in the mosque to supplicate, pray, offer greetings of peace, or do anything else. They knew his Sunnah as the Companions and their successors had taught them. They also knew that the rights owed to him are inseparable from those owed to Allah, Mighty and Majestic, and that all the duties Allah has commanded and loves, whether owed to Him or to His Messenger, are enjoined upon those responsible for them in every place and land. Thus, sending blessings and greetings of peace upon him at his honoured grave is no more emphatically enjoined than doing so elsewhere.

Rather, a person is commanded to do so wherever he may be, either generally or on occasions that give it particular emphasis, such as prayer, supplication, and the call to prayer. No duty owed to him and no act of worship is more meritorious at his grave than elsewhere. Rather, his mosque itself possesses merit because it is his mosque. To believe that it had no merit before the grave was there—when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the Emigrants and Helpers used to pray in it—and that it acquired its merit only during the caliphate of al-Walīd ibn ‘Abd al-Malik, when he incorporated the chamber into his mosque, is to hold a view that no one would express

---

## Page 13486

except someone profoundly ignorant, or an unbeliever who denies what he brought and deserves to be put to death. The Companions used to supplicate in his mosque just as they had during his lifetime. No new religious law had arisen for them beyond the law he had taught them while alive. He had not instructed them, whenever one of them had a need, to go to the grave of a prophet or righteous person and pray there and call upon him, or supplicate without praying, or ask for his needs to be fulfilled, or ask him to petition his Lord. The Companions—may Allah be pleased with them—had thus learned that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had not commanded them to do any of these things. Nor had he instructed them to single out his grave or chamber for prayer or supplication, whether for him or for themselves. Rather, he had forbidden them to make his house a place of recurring celebration.

He did not tell them, as some ignorant shaykhs tell their followers:

“If you have a need, come to my grave.”
