Rather, his prohibition went further: he forbade them to turn his grave or anyone else’s grave into a mosque in which they would pray to Allah, Mighty and Majestic, thereby blocking the means leading to associating partners with Allah. May Allah bestow blessings and abundant peace upon him and his family, and grant him the finest reward He has ever granted a prophet on behalf of his community. He conveyed the message, discharged the trust, sincerely counselled the community, strove for Allah as He deserves, and worshipped Allah until death came to him from his Lord. Allah’s favour in sending him was the greatest blessing He bestowed upon His servants.

He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, directed them to the best acts of worship and the most excellent places, as related in the two Sahīh collections from Ibn Mas‘ūd, may Allah be pleased with him, who said:

“I said:

---

## Page 13487

‘O Messenger of Allah, which deed is best?’ He replied, ‘Prayer at its appointed times.’ I asked, ‘Then which?’ He replied, ‘Kindness to one’s parents.’ I asked, ‘Then which?’ He replied, ‘Jihad in the cause of Allah.’” He said, “I asked him about these, and had I asked him for more, he would have told me more.”

In the Musnad and Sunan Ibn Mājah, Thawbān reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Remain steadfast, though you will not attain complete steadfastness. Know that the best of your deeds is prayer, and none but a believer consistently maintains his ablution.”

The community has been instructed to establish mosques for prayer. These are the places most beloved to Allah, as is authentically reported from him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in Sahīh Muslim and elsewhere:

“The places most beloved to Allah are the mosques, and the places most hateful to Allah are the marketplaces.”

Nevertheless, during his final illness, he cursed those who turn the graves of prophets and righteous people into mosques, out of sincere concern for the community and eagerness to guide it. This accords with Allah’s description of him:

“There has certainly come to you a Messenger from among yourselves. Grievous to him is what you suffer; he is deeply concerned for you, and kind and merciful to the believers.”

In the two Sahīh collections, ‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, said:

“During the illness from which he never recovered, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘May Allah curse the Jews and Christians: they turned the graves of their prophets into mosques.’”

‘Ā’ishah said, “Were it not for that, his grave would have been left exposed, but he disliked the prospect of its being made into a mosque.”

Another version reads: “But he feared that it would be made into a mosque.” In a version recorded by al-Bukhārī: “Except that I fear it would be made into a mosque.”

‘Ā’ishah and Ibn ‘Abbās said:

“When death came upon the Messenger of Allah

---

## Page 13488

may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he began placing a patterned cloak of his over his face. Whenever he felt stifled, he uncovered his face. In that state he said, ‘May Allah’s curse be upon the Jews and Christians: they turned the graves of their prophets into mosques.’” He was warning against what they had done.

It is part of Allah’s wisdom that ‘Ā’ishah, the Mother of the Believers, whose chamber was where he was buried, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, should narrate these hadiths, having heard them from him herself. Other Companions also narrated them, including Ibn ‘Abbās, Abū Hurayrah, Jundub ibn ‘Abd Allāh and Ibn Mas‘ūd—may Allah Most High be pleased with them.

In the two Sahīh collections, Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, reports that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah destroy the Jews: they turned the graves of their prophets into mosques.”

Also in the two Sahīh collections, ‘Ā’ishah reports that Umm Habībah and Umm Salamah mentioned to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, a church they had seen in Abyssinia that contained images. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When a righteous man among those people died, they would build a mosque over his grave and place those images inside it. Those are the worst of creation in Allah’s sight on the Day of Resurrection.”

In Sahīh Muslim, Jundub ibn ‘Abd Allāh, may Allah be pleased with him, said:

“I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, saying five days before his death, ‘I disavow before Allah having any of you as an intimate friend, for Allah has taken me as an intimate friend just as He took Abraham as an intimate friend. Were I to take anyone from my community as an intimate friend, I would take Abū Bakr as an intimate friend. Take heed: those who

---

## Page 13489

came before you used to take graves as mosques. Take heed: do not take graves as mosques, for I forbid you to do so.’”

In Sahīh Muslim, Abū Marthad al-Ghanawī reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not sit on graves or pray towards them.”

In the Musnad and the Sahīh of Abū Hātim, he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“Among the worst of people are those whom the Hour overtakes while they are still alive, and those who take graves as mosques.”

His prohibition against making his grave a place of recurring observance has already been mentioned.

When the Companions understood that he had forbidden them to make it a place for the obligatory prayers through which they drew near to Allah, Almighty and Majestic, lest they resemble the polytheists who invoke graves, pray to them and make vows to them, the prohibition against invoking them was all the stronger. Likewise, since he forbade them to pray at sunrise and sunset lest they resemble those who prostrate to the sun, the prohibition against prostrating to the sun itself applied with still greater force.

The Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, therefore sought to pray, supplicate and remember Allah in the mosques built for Him, rather than at the graves of the prophets and the righteous, which they had been forbidden to take as mosques. Those graves are only the abodes of created beings. After his death, they continued to do what they had done during his lifetime, may Allah bless him and his family and grant him peace.

Among the evidence supporting what Mālik and other Muslim scholars mentioned concerning their disapproval of the people of Medina deliberately going to the grave when entering or leaving, and similar practices

---

## Page 13490

even if their intention was merely to greet him with peace and invoke blessings upon him, is the fact that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to visit Qubā’ every Saturday, both riding and walking. This is established in the two Sahīhs through the hadith of Ibn ‘Umar, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to visit Qubā’ every Saturday, both riding and walking.”

Ibn ‘Umar used to do likewise. Nāfi‘ added, reporting from Ibn ‘Umar concerning the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“And he would pray two units of prayer there.”

This authentic hadith indicates that he would pray in his mosque on Friday, then go to the mosque of Qubā’ and pray there on Saturday. Both were founded on righteousness. Allah Most High has said:

“A mosque founded on righteousness from the first day is more worthy for you to stand in. In it are men who love to purify themselves, and Allah loves those who purify themselves.”

It has been reported through several routes that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, asked the people of Qubā’ about this purification for which Allah had praised them. They explained that they used water to cleanse themselves after relieving themselves. In Sunan Abī Dāwūd and elsewhere, it is reported:

“This verse was revealed concerning the mosque of the people of Qubā’: ‘In it are men who love to purify themselves.’” He said: “They used water to cleanse themselves after relieving themselves, so this verse was revealed concerning them.”

It is also established in the Sahīh that:

“Sa‘d asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, about the mosque founded on righteousness while he was in the house of one of his wives. He took a handful of pebbles and struck the ground with them, then said: ‘It is this mosque of yours,’ referring to the mosque of Medina.”

It is thus clear that both mosques were founded on righteousness. The mosque of Medina, however, embodies this quality more fully and is therefore more deserving of this designation. The mosque of Qubā’ occasioned the revelation of the verse because it

---

## Page 13491

was adjacent to the mosque built to cause harm, in which he was forbidden to stand in prayer.

The point is that Ibn ‘Umar used to visit Qubā’ every week to pray there, following the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Yet neither Ibn ‘Umar nor anyone else, while residing in Medina, would visit the Prophet’s grave, whether weekly or otherwise. Ibn ‘Umar would only visit the grave upon returning from a journey. Many of the Companions—or most of them—would return from their travels without visiting the grave, whether to offer greetings of peace, supplicate, or do anything else. They would not stand beside it, outside the chamber within the mosque, as Ibn ‘Umar used to do. None of them would enter the chamber for that purpose. Indeed, they would enter it only to visit ‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, while she lived there. At that time, those who entered to visit her would greet the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, just as they used to greet him when in his presence.

As for greetings of peace that he does not hear, Allah grants those who offer them peace tenfold in return. These include greetings offered to him during prayer and when entering or leaving the mosque. Such greetings are prescribed at every time and in every place. They are superior to greetings offered specifically at his grave, for greetings offered specifically at his grave are of the same kind as those offered to other believers, living or dead.

As for unrestricted, general invocations of peace upon him, the command to offer them is among his distinctive privileges, just as the command to invoke blessings upon him is among his distinctive privileges. There is, however, disagreement about invoking blessings and peace upon others generally, and about invoking blessings upon others in particular. Some have extended this to greetings of peace

---

## Page 13492

and deemed them exclusive to him, just as invoking blessings is exclusive to him. This view has been attributed to Abū Muhammad al-Juwaynī. The majority of scholars, however, hold that greetings of peace are not exclusive to him. As for invoking blessings, there is a well-known disagreement.

This is because Allah Most High commanded in His Book that blessings and peace be invoked upon him specifically. He Most High said:

“Indeed, Allah and His angels send blessings upon the Prophet. O you who believe, invoke blessings upon him and greet him with peace.”

Here He both makes a declaration and issues a command. Concerning the believers generally, however, He makes a declaration without issuing a command. He Most High said:

“It is He who sends blessings upon you, as do His angels.”

Thus, when preachers mention this, they say: “Allah has given you a command in which He began with Himself, then mentioned His angels, and then addressed the believers among His creation”—that is, He said, “O you who believe.” For when speaking of His blessings upon the believers, He began with Himself and then mentioned His angels, but did not address the believers among His creation.

It also occurs in the hadith:

“Indeed, Allah and His angels send blessings upon the one who teaches people what is good.”

The Muslims agree that invoking blessings upon him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is prescribed during prayer before supplication, and outside prayer as well. They have differed only over whether invoking blessings upon him is obligatory in the prescribed prayers and in sermons. Al-Shāfiʿī held it to be obligatory, whereas Abū Hanīfah and Mālik did not. Two positions are reported from Imam Ahmad. If it is held to be obligatory, is it an essential pillar, or is its omission through forgetfulness excused? There are two reported positions on this as well.

The strongest view is that invoking blessings upon him is obligatory when supplicating: we should not supplicate until we have first invoked blessings upon him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Greetings of peace upon him are commanded during prayer and form part of the tashahhud, which is

---

## Page 13493

an essential pillar of prayer according to al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, in the well-known position reported from him. Thus, omitting it invalidates the prayer, whether deliberately or through forgetfulness.

The final tashahhud according to Mālik and Abū Hanīfah; and according to Mālik and Ahmad, in the well-known position reported from him: if a person deliberately omits the first tashahhud, his prayer is invalid; if he omits it through forgetfulness, he must perform the prostrations of forgetfulness. Imam Ahmad calls this obligatory, while Mālik’s followers call it an obligatory Sunnah. They say: “An obligatory Sunnah.” There is no substantive disagreement here, given the position that whoever deliberately omits it must repeat the prayer, while whoever omits it through forgetfulness must perform the prostrations of forgetfulness.

Mālik and Ahmad classify the acts of prayer into different categories, like the acts of Hajj. Abū Hanīfah divides them into three categories, but holds that someone who omits an act in the obligatory category has done wrong without being required to repeat the prayer, whether he omitted it deliberately or through forgetfulness. Al-Shāfiʿī, however, holds that an obligatory act in prayer is an essential pillar. This differs from Hajj, in which they all agree that there are obligatory acts whose omission is compensated for by an animal sacrifice, distinct from both essential pillars and recommended acts.

There is no disagreement that he himself, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, may invoke blessings upon others, as Allah Most High said:

“And invoke blessings upon them.”

Likewise, it is established in an authentic hadith that he said:

“O Allah, send blessings upon the family of Abū Awfā.”

It is also reported that he said to a woman:

“May Allah send blessings upon you and your husband.”

She had asked him to invoke blessings upon her and her husband.

There is likewise no disagreement that blessings may be invoked upon his family together with him, as he taught his community to say:

“O Allah, send blessings upon Muhammad and the family of Muhammad as You sent blessings upon Abraham. Indeed, You are Praiseworthy

---

## Page 13494

and Glorious. And bless Muhammad and the family of Muhammad as You blessed the family of Abraham. Indeed, You are Praiseworthy and Glorious.”

As for someone other than the Prophet invoking blessings upon another person independently—for example, saying, “May Allah send blessings upon Abū Bakr, Umar, Uthmān, or Alī”—there are two views.

The first is that this is permissible. Ahmad explicitly stated this on several occasions, citing as evidence that Alī said to Umar, “May Allah send blessings upon you.” This is the position of most of his followers, including Judge Abū Yaʿlā, Ibn Aqīl, and Shaykh Abd al-Qādir, who mentioned no disagreement concerning it.

The second is that this is not permissible, as stated by a group of Mālik’s and al-Shāfiʿī’s followers and also reported from the two imams themselves. This is the position mentioned by our grandfather, Abū al-Barakāt, in his large work; he mentioned no other view. He cited as evidence what a number of narrators reported from Ibn Abbās, who said: “I do not know of anyone upon whom it is appropriate for anyone to invoke blessings other than the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

Those who disallow it say: “As for his invoking blessings upon others, blessings are his due, so he may grant them to others. As for invoking blessings upon others together with him, something may be permissible in a subsidiary capacity that is not permissible in its own right.”

Those who permit it cite the two rightly guided caliphs, Umar and Alī, and the fact that neither the Quran nor the Sunnah contains any prohibition of it. However, invoking blessings upon anyone else is not obligatory as it is in the case of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. His particular distinction therefore lies in its being commanded and obligatory, not in its being permissible and recommended.

They said: “It is also established that

---

## Page 13495

the angels invoke blessings upon the believers, as stated in the authentic report:

‘The angels invoke blessings upon any one of you as long as he remains in his place of prayer.’

If Allah and His angels send blessings upon a believer, why should believers not be permitted to invoke blessings upon him? As for Ibn Abbās’s statement, he made it when the people of innovation began singling out Alī or others for blessings without invoking them upon anyone else. This is an innovation by consensus. They do not invoke blessings upon every Abbasid member of Banū Hāshim, nor upon every descendant of al-Hasan and al-Husayn, nor upon the Prophet’s wives, even though the following is established in the authentic report:

‘O Allah, send blessings upon Muhammad, his wives, and his offspring.’

Thus, there is no evidence to justify singling out certain members of the Prophet’s household for blessings to the exclusion of the rest of his household and all other believers.”

Since Allah Most High commanded that blessings and peace be invoked upon him, and some then held that invoking blessings upon anyone else was prohibited, a group, including Abū Muhammad al-Juwaynī, extended this reasoning and said: “Peace must not be invoked upon anyone else.” This view is not known to have been held by any of the early scholars, and most later scholars rejected it. Greeting others with peace is prescribed: one greets a person upon meeting him. This is either obligatory or strongly recommended; scholars hold two views on the matter, both of which are represented in Ahmad’s school. Returning the greeting is obligatory by consensus, either as an individual or a communal obligation.

When concluding the prayer, the worshipper says: “Peace be upon you; peace be upon you.” The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to

---

## Page 13496

teach his Companions, when visiting graves, to greet their occupants with peace, saying:

“Peace be upon you, inhabitants of these abodes, believers and Muslims.”

Those who regard the invocation of peace as exclusive to him do not prohibit greeting someone who is present. Rather, they say that peace should not be invoked upon someone who is absent. Thus, they regard invoking peace upon him in his absence as exclusive to him. This is correct. However, it is the command to do so, and its being obligatory, that are exclusive to him, as in the tashahhud, the testimony recited in prayer: no specific individual is greeted with peace in it except him. The same applies when entering and leaving the mosque. This supports the view that invoking peace is like invoking blessings: both are obligatory for him, within prayer and outside it. For anyone else, nothing is obligatory except the greeting of peace upon meeting, which is emphatically prescribed by agreement. Whether it is obligatory or recommended is the subject of two well-known views in Ahmad’s school and elsewhere. The scriptural texts indicate that it is obligatory.

Muslim narrated in his Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Five things are owed by one Muslim to another: to greet him when he meets him, visit him when he is ill, accompany his funeral procession when he dies, accept his invitation when he invites him, and—as also narrated—invoke Allah’s mercy upon him when he sneezes.”

Most jurists have held that accepting an invitation is obligatory. Offering the funeral prayer for the deceased is a communal obligation by their consensus, and greeting someone upon meeting him is more emphatically required than accepting his invitation. The same applies to visiting the sick. The harm caused by failing to greet someone upon meeting him or to visit him when he is ill is greater than that caused by declining his invitation. Moreover, greeting someone is easier than accepting an invitation or visiting the sick. These matters are discussed at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 13497

The point here is that greeting someone with peace—whether meeting him during his lifetime or visiting his grave after his death—is prescribed with regard to every Muslim. One should greet any Muslim whom one meets alive or whose grave one visits.

The Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, knew that greeting him at his grave was neither exclusive to him nor a distinction he enjoyed over others. This is the greeting of which he said:

“No one greets me with peace without Allah restoring my soul to me so that I may return his greeting.”

Rather, this is prescribed with regard to every Muslim, living or dead. Every believer returns the greeting of whoever greets him. This is not something sought for its own sake; rather, when one meets him, one greets him. Likewise, when one visits a grave, one greets the deceased. It does not mean that one should undertake the trouble of travelling and seeking a meeting solely for that purpose.

Invoking peace upon him in prayer and when entering and leaving the mosque, however, is exclusive to him. It is among the invocations of peace upon him that Allah commanded in the Quran. Whoever invokes peace upon him, Allah invokes peace upon that person ten times, just as He bestows blessings upon him ten times when he invokes blessings upon the Prophet. This, then, is what is prescribed and commanded: it is better, more beneficial and more complete, and entails no harm. The other involves an effort that is not exclusive to him, and one is not commanded to travel solely for its sake. Rather, deliberately going there with the intention of invoking blessings and peace and offering supplication amounts to making it a place of recurring observance. He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not make my house a place of recurring observance.”

For this reason, the prevailing practice among the Companions—the Rightly Guided Caliphs and the earliest forerunners among the Emigrants and the Helpers—was that they would enter his mosque and invoke blessings upon him

---

## Page 13498

in prayer and invoke peace upon him, as Allah and His Messenger commanded them. During prayer, they would supplicate for themselves, choosing whichever prescribed supplications they wished. As related in the authentic hadith of Ibn Masʿūd, after teaching him the tashahhud, he said:

“Then let him choose whichever supplication he likes best.”

They would not go to the grave, whether from inside the chamber or outside it, to offer supplication, invoke blessings or peace upon him, or fulfil any other duties owed to him that are enjoined everywhere—let alone go there to seek the fulfilment of their needs, as those who practise polytheism and religious innovation do. Such conduct was unknown during the first three generations, whether at his grave or anyone else’s: neither in the time of the Companions, nor that of their Followers, nor that of the generation after them.

When a person endowed with faith and knowledge grasps these matters, he understands what Islam teaches concerning them and distinguishes between those who understand monotheism, the Sunnah and faith, and those who are ignorant of them.

It has thus become clear that the Rightly Guided Caliphs and the majority of the Companions would enter the mosque and invoke blessings upon the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, there. They would not greet him when leaving Medina or returning from a journey. Rather, they would enter the mosque, pray there and invoke peace upon the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, without coming to the grave. The intention of some of them was to offer a greeting.

Furthermore, it is recommended for everyone entering the mosque to invoke peace upon the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, saying:

“In the name of Allah, and peace be upon the Messenger of Allah. O Allah, forgive my sins and open for me the gates of Your mercy.”

Likewise, when leaving, one says:

---

## Page 13499

“In the name of Allah, and peace be upon the Messenger of Allah. O Allah, forgive my sins and open for me the gates of Your bounty.”

Offering this salutation whenever one enters the mosque suffices in place of greeting him at the grave. It is something particular to him and entails no harm. One does this in prayer as well: people invoke blessings and peace upon him during prayer, and invoke blessings upon him when they hear the call to prayer, asking that he be granted al-wasīlah. This is based on the report narrated by Muslim in his Sahīh from ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀs, who said that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When you hear the caller to prayer, repeat what he says, then invoke blessings upon me, for whoever invokes blessings upon me once, Allah will bless him ten times. Then ask Allah to grant me al-wasīlah, for it is a rank in Paradise befitting only one of Allah’s servants, and I hope to be that servant. Whoever asks that I be granted al-wasīlah will be entitled to my intercession on the Day of Resurrection.”

They knew that the salutation recommended at his honoured grave is the greeting offered upon meeting someone, just as it is recommended at the grave of any Muslim and upon meeting him. Thus, others share in this, as he said:

“No man greets me with peace without Allah restoring my soul to me so that I may return his greeting.”

He also said:

“No man passes the grave of a believing brother whom he knew and greets him with peace without the deceased recognising him and returning his greeting.”

When he visited the graveyards, he would say:

“Peace be upon you, inhabitants of these abodes, believers and Muslims. We shall, if Allah wills, join you. You have gone ahead of us, and we shall follow you. I ask Allah for well-being for us and for you.”

He would teach his Companions, when they visited the graves, to say:

---

## Page 13500

“Peace be upon you, inhabitants of these abodes, believers and Muslims.”

Offering salutations of peace upon him during prayer is better than doing so at his grave. It is something particular to him and has been commanded. Allah bestows peace upon the one who offers him salutations, just as He blesses the one who invokes blessings upon him: whoever invokes blessings upon him once, Allah blesses him ten times, and whoever offers him a salutation of peace once, Allah bestows peace upon him ten times.

Their purpose and his purpose in offering him salutations and invoking blessings upon him have thus been fulfilled, whether in his mosque or elsewhere. Going to the grave therefore affords no further benefit to them or to him. This differs from going to the mosque of Qubā, which they would visit every Saturday to pray there, following his example, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, for prayer there is equivalent to an umrah. They would combine this with praying in his mosque on Friday, since neither could take the place of the other; rather, this brought an additional reward.

Likewise, it is good for a person to go out to al-Baqī‘ and those buried at Uhud to supplicate for them, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to do. This brings benefit without harm. They do not supplicate for those people in every prayer, such that one could say, “This makes that unnecessary.” Nevertheless, Mālik is reported to have disapproved of treating this as an established sunnah. In this matter, he did not follow Ibn ‘Umar’s practice, just as he did not follow his practice of rubbing the place where the Prophet sat on the pulpit, nor his view that it was recommended to seek out the places where the Prophet had prayed simply because the time for prayer had overtaken him there. Ibn ‘Umar considered it recommended to seek out those places and pray there, whereas the majority of the Companions did not. Rather, they favoured what he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, himself favoured

---

## Page 13501

namely, to pray wherever the time for prayer overtook him. His father, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, used to forbid people from deliberately seeking out those places to pray there, saying:

“Those before you were ruined precisely because of this: they turned the sites associated with their prophets into places of worship. Whoever happens to be there when prayer falls due should pray; otherwise, he should move on.”

Thus ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb instructed them to follow the practice established for them by the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, was one of the rightly guided caliphs whose sunnah we have been commanded to follow. The Prophet also specifically commanded us to follow his example and that of Abū Bakr, saying:

“Follow the example of the two who come after me: Abū Bakr and ‘Umar.”

The command to follow their example is of a higher order than the command to follow their sunnah, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

Likewise, Mālik is reported to have disapproved of travelling to Jerusalem, fearing that travelling there would be treated as an established sunnah. He disapproved of this when a fixed time was appointed for people to travel there as a group, like the appointed time for Hajj. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not do this at Qubā, at the graves of the martyrs or those buried in al-Baqī‘, or elsewhere, as he did for Hajj, Friday prayers and the Eids. A distinction must therefore be drawn between these practices.

Although he prayed voluntary prayers in congregation on several occasions—during the night, in the forenoon and at other times—gathering for such voluntary prayer at a fixed time was not made an established sunnah like the five daily prayers, the eclipse prayer, the two Eid prayers and Friday prayer.

As for coming to the grave to offer him salutations of peace, they had no need of this, since they offered him salutations during prayer and when entering and leaving the mosque. Coming to it repeatedly after prayer opens the way to its being made a place of recurring observance and an object of worship.

---

## Page 13502

and they had been forbidden to do so. He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is buried in ‘Ā’ishah’s chamber. Her chamber and the other chambers of his wives lay to the east of the mosque and on its qiblah side; they were not part of his mosque. Rather, he would leave the chamber to enter the mosque. During the caliphate of al-Walīd, however, the mosque was enlarged. He was fond of building mosques and undertook building work on the Sacred Mosque, the mosque of Damascus, and others. He therefore ordered his governor, ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz, to purchase the chambers from their owners, who had inherited them from the Prophet’s wives, and incorporate them into the mosque.

It was from that time that the chambers became part of the mosque. This occurred after the deaths of the Companions: after the deaths of Ibn ‘Umar, Ibn ‘Abbās, Abū Sa‘īd al-Khudrī, and ‘Ā’ishah—indeed, after the deaths of the great majority of the Companions, none of whom remained in Medina. It is reported that Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyib disapproved of this. Many of the Companions and their successors had disapproved of ‘Uthmān’s construction of the mosque using stone, plaster, and teak, may Allah be pleased with him; these people were even more averse to what al-Walīd did.

As for ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, he enlarged the mosque but retained its original manner of construction: mud bricks, palm-trunk pillars, and a roof of palm fronds. It is not reported that anyone disapproved of what ‘Umar did. The disagreement concerned only what ‘Uthmān and al-Walīd did.

During the time of the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, anyone wishing to offer the Prophet salutations of peace would approach him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, from the western side of the chamber and offer salutations, either facing the chamber

---

## Page 13503

or facing the qiblah. Now, however, he can approach from the direction of the qiblah.

For this reason, most scholars recommended facing the chamber when offering him salutations of peace. Others held that one should instead face the qiblah when offering him salutations, as was the view of Abū Hanīfah.

Al-Walīd ibn ‘Abd al-Malik assumed the caliphate after the death of his father, ‘Abd al-Malik, sometime in the eighties AH. All these Companions had already died, as had the great majority of the Companions throughout the lands. Only a very few remained in the various cities, such as Anas ibn Mālik in Basra, who died during al-Walīd’s caliphate, sometime in the nineties AH. Jābir ibn ‘Abd Allāh died in Medina in 78 AH and was the last Companion to die there. Al-Walīd incorporated the chamber into the mosque long afterward, some ten years later. The construction of the mosque took place after Jābir’s death, so no Companion remained in Medina.

As for ‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān, may Allah be pleased with him, he enlarged the mosque while many Companions were still alive, but did not incorporate any part of the chamber into it. Rather, he left the Prophet’s chamber as it had been: outside the mosque, adjoining its eastern side, just as it had been during the time of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and during the time of Abū Bakr and ‘Umar. ‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, was living in it.

‘Ā’ishah continued to live there until the latter part of Mu‘āwiyah’s caliphate, and she died after al-Hasan ibn ‘Alī. Al-Hasan had asked her permission to be buried in the chamber, and she had granted it. Others, however, objected, holding that since ‘Uthmān, may Allah be pleased with him, had not been buried there, no one else should be buried there either. Strife nearly broke out. When ‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, was dying, she instructed that she be buried with

---

## Page 13504

her companions in al-Baqīʿ, rather than there. She did this out of humility, not wishing to be accorded special distinction on account of him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Thus, only the Successors, such as Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyib and others like him, discussed whether what al-Walīd had done was permissible or disliked.

Saʿīd was then among the most eminent Successors. Ahmad ibn Hanbal was asked, “Which of the Successors was the most virtuous?”

He replied, “Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyib.”

He was then asked, “What about ʿAlqamah and al-Aswad?”

He replied, “Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyib.”

ʿAlqamah and al-Aswad had both died some time before that. It was from that time onward that the chamber became part of the mosque. Yet the mosque already possessed virtue before the chambers were incorporated into it. Its virtue lay in the Prophet’s having built it for himself and the believers, as a place of prayer for him and for the believers until the Day of Resurrection. Thus, it was distinguished by his having built it.

I say: Mālik said, “It has reached me that Gabriel was the one who established its qiblah for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

Its virtue also lay in his deliberately attending it for Friday and congregational prayers until his death. He never performed Friday prayer anywhere else, whether travelling or residing at home. As for congregational prayer, he would perform it wherever he happened to be when its time arrived.

We are commanded to follow him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, by believing everything he conveyed and obeying him in everything he made obligatory and commanded. Faith in him is not complete without both. This includes following his example in those actions in which we are prescribed to emulate him. Whatever he did as obligatory, recommended, or permissible, we likewise do as obligatory, recommended, or permissible. This is the position of the vast majority of scholars

---

## Page 13505

except for what is established as exclusive to him. When he deliberately performed an act of worship in a particular place, it is prescribed for us to seek to perform that act of worship in that place. Thus, he deliberately travelled to Mecca and worshipped at the Sacred Mosque, praying there, circumambulating it, passing between al-Safā and al-Marwah, ascending al-Safā and al-Marwah, standing at ʿArafah and al-Mashʿar al-Harām, performing the ritual stoning, and standing to supplicate at the first two stoning sites, but not at the third, Jamrat al-ʿAqabah. All of this is prescribed for us, either as obligatory or as recommended.

In Mecca, he did not go anywhere other than the Sacred Mosque. Nor did he journey to the cave where he had stayed during his migration, or ascend to the Cave of Hirāʾ, where he used to devote himself to worship before revelation came to him. This was a devotional practice among the people of Mecca, said to have been instituted for them by ʿAbd al-Muttalib.

He performed two units of prayer after circumambulating the House, but did not perform any prayer after passing between al-Safā and al-Marwah. When he entered the Sacred Mosque, he circumambulated the House; this circumambulation served as the greeting of the mosque. He did not first offer a prayer to greet it, as is done in other mosques.

Likewise, when he reached Minā, he began by stoning Jamrat al-ʿAqabah; that was the act of worship. Afterwards, he slaughtered his sacrificial animals, shaved his head, and then circumambulated the House. Hence, the Sunnah for those at Minā is to perform the stoning and then slaughter their sacrifices. For them, stoning takes the place of the Eid prayer for others. There is no Eid prayer at Minā, nor is there Friday prayer there or at ʿArafah, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not perform an Eid prayer at either place, nor did he perform Friday prayer on the Day of ʿArafah. Nor did he perform Friday or Eid prayers during his travels. For this reason,

---

## Page 13506

scholars generally held that Friday prayer is not performed while travelling, with only an isolated dissenting view on the matter. The majority of scholars likewise hold that Eid prayer is performed only where Friday prayer is held. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not perform Eid prayer while travelling, and during his lifetime the Eid prayer in Medina was held only as a single congregation. No one performed Eid prayer individually. This is the position of the majority of scholars, though there is well-known disagreement concerning it. Thus, following his Sunnah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, Muslims at Minā perform the stoning and then slaughter their sacrificial animals.

Whatever he did to draw closer to Allah is an act of worship to be performed with that same intention. Whatever he declined to do, despite the presence of a reason for doing it, is neither an act of worship nor recommended. Whatever he did as a permissible act, without intending it as worship, is permissible.

Some scholars recommend imitating him in the outward form of such acts, as Ibn ʿUmar used to do. Most scholars, however, say that following him means intending what he intended. Merely resembling him outwardly, without sharing his purpose and intention, does not constitute following him. Thus, when he did something without intending it as worship, performing it as worship is not recommended: that is not following him, but acting contrary to his example.

It is established in the authentic hadith collection that he would pray wherever he happened to be when prayer became due. It is also established in the authentic hadith collection that:

“When Abū Dharr asked him, ‘Which mosque was first established on earth?’ he replied, ‘The Sacred Mosque, then al-Aqsā Mosque. Thereafter, wherever you are when prayer becomes due, pray, for that place is a mosque.’”

It is also related in

---

## Page 13507

the authentic hadith collection:

“For there is merit in it.”

If prayer becomes due while a man and his companions are in one place, but they leave without praying there and go elsewhere because that place contains a trace of one of the prophets, they have acted contrary to the Sunnah.

ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, saw some people repeatedly visiting a place where the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had prayed. He asked, “What is this?”

They replied, “This is a place where the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed.”

He said, “A place where the Messenger of Allah prayed! Do you intend to turn the sites associated with your prophets into places of worship? It was precisely through such things that the Children of Israel were ruined. Whoever happens to be there when prayer becomes due should pray there; otherwise, he should move on.”

As for his mosque, which has special merit, prayer there is recommended because he accorded it preference. How much more so when he said:

“One prayer in this mosque of mine is better than a thousand prayers elsewhere, except in the Sacred Mosque.”

He also said:

“Do not undertake a journey except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque, al-Aqsā Mosque, and this mosque of mine.”

This merit belonged to it before the chamber was incorporated into it. Indeed, those who prayed there at that time were better than those who would pray there thereafter until the Day of Resurrection. It must not be thought that, after the chamber was incorporated into it, the mosque became superior to what it had been during his lifetime and the lifetimes of his rightly guided caliphs. Rather, if merit varies with times and people, then his time and that of the rightly guided caliphs were superior, and the people of those times were superior. Thus, if these differences affect its merit, the mosque was superior then, before the chamber was incorporated into it; and if they do not,

---

## Page 13508

there is no difference. In any case, it must not be thought that incorporating the chamber into the mosque made it better than it had been. Their purpose was not to incorporate the chamber into it, but to enlarge it by including the apartments of the Prophet’s wives, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The chamber was thus necessarily included, despite the objections of those among the early generations who disapproved of this.

The point is that mosques built for Allah derive their merit from Allah alone being worshipped in them, without partner; from the prophets and righteous people who worshipped Allah in them; and from their having been built for that purpose. As Allah Most High says:

“A mosque founded on righteousness from the very first day is more worthy for you to stand in. In it are men who love to purify themselves, and Allah loves those who purify themselves.”

“Is one who founded his building on righteousness and the pursuit of Allah’s pleasure better, or one who founded his building on the edge of a crumbling bank, which collapsed with him into the Fire of Hell? Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people.”

Deeds vary in merit according to the intentions of those who perform them, their obedience to Allah Most High, and the faith in their hearts through their obedience to Allah. As established in the authentic hadith collection, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah does not look at your appearance or your wealth, but rather He looks at your hearts and your deeds.”

For these they are rewarded, while for neglecting what Allah has made obligatory they are punished. Through these, affliction in this world and the Hereafter is averted from them. Whatever calamities befall them are due to their sins. Allah Most High says:

“If you do good, you do good for yourselves; and if you do evil, it is against yourselves.”

And Allah Most High says:

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever befalls you of

---

## Page 13509

evil is from yourself.” The scholars explain: whatever victory, provision, and well-being come to you are among Allah’s blessings upon you, while whatever calamities befall you are due to your sins. As Allah Most High says:

“Whatever calamity befalls you is because of what your own hands have earned, and He pardons much.”

They also all agree that worship must be devoted to Allah alone, that trust must be placed in Him alone, and that reverent fear and piety must be directed to Allah alone. The Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has rights that no one else in the community shares, such as the obligation to obey him in everything he makes obligatory and commands. Allah Most High says:

“Whoever obeys the Messenger has indeed obeyed Allah.”

And Allah Most High says:

“We sent no messenger except to be obeyed by Allah’s permission.”

Thus, pledging allegiance to him was pledging allegiance to Allah. As Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, those who pledge allegiance to you are actually pledging allegiance to Allah.”

For they pledged to obey him in jihad and not to flee, even if they died. This obedience to him is obedience to Allah.

We must love the Messenger more than ourselves, our fathers, our children, our families, and our wealth. As related in the authentic hadith, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“By Him in Whose hand is my soul, none of you believes until I am dearer to him than his father, his child, and all people.”

This was narrated by al-Bukhārī and Muslim. One wording narrated by Muslim includes: “and his family and his wealth.”

In al-Bukhārī, Abd Allāh ibn Hishām said:

“We were with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, while he was holding the hand of Umar ibn al-Khattāb

---

## Page 13510

, and Umar said to him, ‘O Messenger of Allah, you are dearer to me than everything except my own self.’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, replied, ‘No, by Him in Whose hand is my soul, not until I am dearer to you than your own self.’ Umar then said to him, ‘Now, by Allah, you are indeed dearer to me than my own self.’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Now, Umar.’”

Allah Most High has said:

“Say: If your fathers, your sons, your brothers, your spouses, your relatives, the wealth you have acquired, the trade whose decline you fear, and the homes you cherish are dearer to you than Allah and His Messenger and striving in His cause, then wait until Allah brings about His command. Allah does not guide the defiantly disobedient people.”

Allah Most High has also said:

“The Prophet has a greater claim on the believers than they have on themselves.”

In the two Sahīh collections, he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I have a greater claim on every believer than he has on himself.”

This is because no one can escape Allah’s punishment or attain His mercy except through the Messenger: by believing in him, loving him, being loyal to him, and following him. It is through him that Allah saves a person from punishment in this world and the Hereafter, and it is he who leads that person to the good of this world and the Hereafter. The greatest and most beneficial blessing is therefore the blessing of faith, which can only be attained through him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He is more devoted to each person’s welfare, and more beneficial to him, than his own self and wealth. For it is through him that Allah brings a person out of darkness into light; there is no other way for him. As for his own self and his family, they cannot avail him at all against Allah.

He called creation to Allah by Allah’s permission, as Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, We have sent you

---

## Page 13511

as a witness, a bearer of good tidings and a warner, and as one who calls to Allah by His permission, and as an illuminating lamp.”

Whoever opposes him calls to others besides Allah without Allah’s permission. Whoever follows the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, calls only to Allah and His Messenger. Allah’s words, “by His permission,” mean by His command and through the knowledge He has revealed. As Allah Most High says:

“Say: This is my way: I call to Allah with clear insight—I and those who follow me.”

Whoever follows the Messenger calls to Allah with clear insight—that is, with clear evidence and knowledge, calling to Him on the basis of revelation from Allah, unlike someone who commands what he does not know or what Allah has not revealed. As Allah Most High says:

“They worship besides Allah that for which He has sent down no authority, and of which they have no knowledge. The wrongdoers will have no helper.”

Whatever Allah has commanded or recommended concerning the rights owed to him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is not confined to his chamber, whether inside or outside it. Rather, it is to be performed wherever it has been prescribed.

Fulfilling any of the rights owed to him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—such as believing in him, loving him, being loyal to him, conveying the knowledge received from him, striving for what he brought, showing loyalty to his allies and enmity towards his enemies, invoking blessings and peace upon him, and doing everything Allah loves and through which one draws near to Him—is no better at his chamber than far away from it. This applies both to invoking blessings and peace upon him and to all his other rights. Indeed, he himself, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade making his house a place of recurring observance. He thus forbade seeking out his house for the special performance of any of these acts. Thus, anyone whose intention or belief is that

---

## Page 13512

performing these acts at the chamber is better is acting contrary to him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. This concerns acts that are themselves prescribed, such as believing in him, testifying that he is the Messenger of Allah, and invoking blessings and peace upon him.

As for what Allah has not prescribed and for which He has sent down no authority to him—indeed, what he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has forbidden—such as supplicating to and worshipping any created beings besides Allah, whether angels, prophets or others, and making pilgrimage to created beings and their graves: only those who have neither knowledge nor revelation from Allah to support these practices command them. They resemble those who worship besides Allah that for which He has sent down no authority and of which they have no knowledge—or they themselves are a category of such people.

Allah has distinguished between His own right and the right of the Messenger in such statements as:

“Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger, stands in awe of Allah and fears Him.”

Obedience is owed to Allah and the Messenger, whereas awe is for Allah alone, and fear is for Allah alone. No created being is to be held in awe or feared—neither an angel nor a prophet nor anyone else.

Allah Most High says:

“Allah has said: Do not take two gods. He is only One God, so fear Me alone.”

“To Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth, and to Him belongs perpetual devotion. Will you then fear anyone other than Allah?”

Allah Most High also says:

“Only those who believe in Allah and the Last Day, establish prayer, give zakah and fear none but Allah maintain Allah’s mosques. It is they who may be among the rightly guided.”

And Allah Most High says:

“So do not fear people, but fear Me, and do not exchange My signs for a small price.”

Likewise, He distinguishes between the two kinds of rights in His words, Most High:

“Had they been content with what

---

## Page 13513

Allah and His Messenger had given them and had said, ‘Allah is sufficient for us. Allah will give us of His bounty, and so will His Messenger. To Allah alone we turn in hope.’”

Concerning giving, He said:

“Allah and His Messenger had given them,”

because the Messenger is the intermediary between us and Allah in conveying His commands and prohibitions, what He makes lawful and unlawful, and His promises and warnings. Thus, what is lawful is what Allah and His Messenger have made lawful; what is forbidden is what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden; and religion is what Allah and His Messenger have prescribed.

Allah Most High says:

“Whatever the Messenger gives you, take it, and whatever he forbids you, refrain from it.”

For this reason, Allah Most High said:

“Had they been content with what Allah and His Messenger had given them and had said, ‘Allah is sufficient for us.’”

Here He did not say, “and His Messenger,” because Allah alone is sufficient for all His believing servants. As He, Most High, says:

“O Prophet, Allah is sufficient for you and for the believers who follow you.”

That is, He is sufficient for you and for the believers who follow you.

Allah Most High also says:

“Indeed, my Protector is Allah, who sent down the Book, and He protects the righteous.”

He mentioned this after His words:

“Indeed, those whom you call upon besides Allah are servants like yourselves,”

through to His words:

“Say, ‘Call upon your partners, then plot against me and give me no respite.’”

“Indeed, my Protector is Allah, who sent down the Book, and He protects the righteous.”

Ibn ʿAbbās said: “They are those who do not set up equals to Allah. He therefore protects and supports them, and the hostility of those who oppose them does not harm them.”

As Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, We will support Our messengers and those who believe, both in the life of this world and on the Day when the witnesses stand forth.”

Then Allah Most High said, among the words He commands them to say:

“Allah will give us of His bounty, and so will His Messenger. To Allah alone we turn in hope.”

Thus He commanded them to

---

## Page 13514

direct their hopes to Allah alone, as He, Most High, says:

“So when you have finished, strive diligently, and turn to your Lord in hope.”

This is because no created being has the power to benefit or harm another. This applies to all the inhabitants of the heavens and the earth. Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘Call upon those whom you claim to be gods besides Him. They have no power to remove harm from you or divert it.’

“Those whom they invoke themselves seek a means of nearness to their Lord, each seeking to be nearest. They hope for His mercy and fear His punishment. Indeed, your Lord’s punishment is something to beware of.”

A group of the early generations, including Ibn ‘Abbās and others, said: This verse concerns those who worshipped the angels and the prophets, such as the Messiah and Ezra.

‘Abd Allah ibn Mas‘ūd said: Some humans used to worship some jinn. The jinn embraced Islam, but those humans continued worshipping them.

Thus, the verse encompasses everyone who invokes, besides Allah, any angel, human, or jinn who is righteous in Allah’s sight. Allah Most High says that these whom you have invoked

“have no power to remove harm from you or divert it.”

“Those whom they invoke themselves seek a means of nearness to their Lord, each seeking to be nearest. They hope for His mercy and fear His punishment. Indeed, your Lord’s punishment is something to beware of.”

Abū Muhammad ‘Abd al-Haqq ibn ‘Atiyyah said in his Quran commentary: Allah Most High states that these beings who are worshipped seek to draw near to Him and gain His favour, and that this is their true condition. The pronoun in “their Lord” refers either to those seeking nearness or to all of them.

Wasīlah means nearness and a means of attaining one’s objective. The expression tawassala al-rajul means that a man sought to draw near and attain a particular end. An example of this is the saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

---

## Page 13515

“Whoever asks Allah to grant me al-wasīlah”—and so on in the hadith.

The other Quran commentators offered explanations similar to his, but he surpassed them in his treatment, saying:

“‘Which of them’ is the subject, and ‘is nearest’ is its predicate. ‘Those’ refers to the beings worshipped; it is the subject, and ‘seek’ is its predicate. The pronoun in ‘they invoke’ refers to the unbelievers, while that in ‘they seek’ refers to the beings worshipped. The implied meaning is: what they consider and discuss is ‘which of them is nearest.’

“This resembles what ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, said in the report about the banner at Khaybar: ‘The people spent that night eagerly discussing which of them would be given it’—that is, competing in their pursuit of nearness.”

He further said, may Allah have mercy on him: “Al-Zajjāj fell short in his treatment of this passage, so reflect upon it.”

He was indeed right. Al-Zajjāj proposed two interpretations of “which of them is nearest,” both fundamentally unsound. Ibn al-Jawzī and others related these interpretations from him, and al-Mahdawī, al-Baghawī, and others followed him. Ibn ‘Atiyyah, however, was more firmly grounded than these scholars in Arabic and its meanings, and more familiar with the school of Sībawayh and the Basran grammarians. He therefore recognized where al-Zajjāj had fallen short, despite the latter’s learning in Arabic, his distinction, and his understanding of meaning and expression—may Allah have mercy on him. Those scholars possessed mastery and merit in matters in which they surpassed Ibn ‘Atiyyah. Yet he was more knowledgeable about what words convey according to Arabic usage, even if they were more knowledgeable about other matters, whether transmitted reports or otherwise.

Allah, glorified and exalted, has made it clear that although the Messiah was a noble messenger, he worshipped Allah. Thus, whoever worships him worships one who can neither benefit nor harm him. The Most High says:

“Indeed,

---

## Page 13516

those who say, ‘Allah is the Messiah, son of Mary,’ have disbelieved. But the Messiah said, ‘O Children of Israel, worship Allah, my Lord and your Lord. Whoever associates partners with Allah, Allah has forbidden Paradise to him, and his refuge is the Fire. The wrongdoers will have no helpers.’”

“Those who say, ‘Allah is the third of three,’ have certainly disbelieved. There is no god except one God. If they do not desist from what they say, a painful punishment will surely afflict those among them who disbelieve.”

“Will they not repent to Allah and seek His forgiveness? Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

“The Messiah, son of Mary, was only a messenger; messengers had passed away before him. His mother was a woman of truth. They both ate food. See how We make the signs clear to them; then see how they are turned away!”

“Say, ‘Do you worship besides Allah that which has no power to harm or benefit you, while Allah is the All-Hearing, All-Knowing?’”

The Most High commanded the best of creation to say that he had no power to bring himself harm or benefit, nor to bring others harm or right guidance. The Most High said:

“Say, ‘I have no power to benefit or harm myself, except as Allah wills.’”

And He said:

“Say, ‘I have no power to bring you harm or right guidance.’”

“Say, ‘No one can protect me from Allah, nor will I find any refuge besides Him.’”

“Except for conveying from Allah and His messages.”

He is saying: No one can protect me from Allah if I disobey Him, as the Most High said:

“Say, ‘I fear, if I disobey my Lord, the punishment of a tremendous Day.’”

“Nor will I find any refuge besides Him”—that is, any shelter to which I can turn.

“Except for conveying from Allah and His messages”—that is, no one can protect me from Him; only obedience to Him can do so, by conveying to you what I have been sent with. Through that, protection and safety are attained.

It has also been interpreted as follows: “I do not

---

## Page 13517

have power to bring you harm or right guidance”—that is, I have no power except to convey what I have been sent with from Him. There are many such passages in the Quran.

It is thus clear that safety from Allah’s punishment and the attainment of happiness come only through obedience to Him, the Most High, for He says:

“Why would Allah punish you if you are grateful and believe?”

The Most High also says:

“Say, ‘My Lord would not care for you were it not for your supplication.’”

That is, if you did not call upon Him as He commanded, obeying Him, worshipping Him and obeying His messengers, He would pay you no heed whatsoever.

This is the means of drawing near to Him that Allah commanded people to seek. The Most High said:

“O you who believe, fear Allah and seek the means of drawing near to Him.”

Quran commentators generally, including Ibn ‘Abbās, Mujāhid, ‘Atā’ and al-Farrā’, said: “The means is drawing near.”

Qatādah said: “Draw near to Allah through what pleases Him.”

Abū ‘Ubaydah said: “‘I sought a means of approach to him’ means ‘I drew near to him.’”

‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Zayd said: “Seek Allah’s love.”

Seeking His love and drawing near to Him are achieved only through obedience to His Messenger. Faith in the Messenger and obedience to him are the means by which creation draws near to Allah. They have absolutely no means of drawing near to Him except faith in His Messenger and obedience to him. No created being has any means of drawing near to Allah, Blessed and Exalted, except through faith in this noble Messenger and obedience to him.

A person is commanded to do this wherever they may be and at all times. As for acts of worship assigned to a particular place, such as Hajj, or a particular time, such as fasting and the Friday prayer, each is to be observed in its appointed place and time. The interior of the chamber itself—let alone its outer wall—has no special status whatsoever with respect to the prescription of

---

## Page 13518

acts of worship or the performance of any of them. By the consensus of the Muslims, being near to Allah is better than being far from Him. The mosque was endowed with special merit during his lifetime, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, before the grave existed; its merit was therefore not due to the grave. Neither he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor any of his Companions or the scholars of his community recommended that anyone reside beside a grave or remain there in devotion—whether his honoured grave or anyone else’s—or deliberately choose to live near any grave whatsoever.

Living in the Prophet’s city is preferable for someone whose obedience to Allah and His Messenger is more frequent there. Such was the case when people were commanded to emigrate to it. Emigrating to it and residing there were then better than doing so anywhere else, including Mecca. Indeed, this was obligatory, among the greatest obligations. But when Mecca was conquered, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There is no emigration after the conquest, but jihad and intention remain.”

When someone from Mecca or elsewhere came to emigrate and settle in Medina, he would instruct him to return to his own town, rather than tell him to reside there. Likewise, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, would instruct people to return to their homelands after Hajj so that they would not crowd the people of Mecca.

During the period of emigration, the Messenger would also instruct many of his Companions to travel elsewhere, to govern a region or for other purposes. Obeying the Messenger by travelling away from Medina was better than remaining with him there even when it was the destination of emigration. How much more so, then, after that?

---

## Page 13519

For what benefits people is obedience to Allah and His Messenger. Nothing else benefits them—neither kinship, nor proximity, nor anything else—as is established in the authentic hadith in which he said:

“O Fātimah, daughter of Muhammad, I can be of no avail to you against Allah. O Safiyyah, aunt of Allah’s Messenger, I can be of no avail to you against Allah. O ‘Abbās, uncle of Allah’s Messenger, I can be of no avail to you against Allah.”

He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The family of so-and-so are not my allies. My only allies are Allah and the righteous believers.”

He also said:

“My allies are those who fear Allah, wherever they may be and whoever they may be.”

Allah Most High has said:

“Indeed, Allah defends those who believe.”

He, Blessed and Exalted, therefore defends the believers wherever they may be. Allah is the One who wards off harm, and faith is the means by which this happens.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to say in his sermon:

“Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger has followed the right path. Whoever disobeys them harms only himself and will not harm Allah at all.”

Allah Most High said:

“Whoever obeys Allah and the Messenger will be with those whom Allah has blessed: the prophets, the utterly truthful, the martyrs and the righteous. What excellent companions they are!”

As for the belief held by some people that calamity is averted from the inhabitants of a town or region through the prophets and righteous people buried among them—for example, some believe that calamity is averted from the people of Baghdad because of the graves of three men: Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Bishr al-Hāfī and Mansūr ibn ‘Ammār; others believe that calamity is averted from

---

## Page 13520

the people of Syria because of the graves of prophets among them—Abraham, Allah’s close friend, and others, peace be upon them; some believe that calamity is averted from the people of Egypt through Nafīsah or others, or from the people of the Hijāz through the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and those buried in al-Baqī‘ or elsewhere—all of this is excess that contradicts the religion of Islam, the Quran, the Sunnah and scholarly consensus.

Jerusalem had as many graves of prophets and righteous people as Allah willed. Yet when its people disobeyed the prophets and acted contrary to what Allah and His messengers had commanded, He gave power over them to those who exacted retribution upon them. The messengers who have died were responsible only for conveying the message clearly, and they conveyed their Lord’s message.

The same applies to our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Allah Most High said concerning him:

“Your duty is only to convey the message.”

And He, Most High, said:

“The Messenger’s duty is only to convey the message clearly.”

Allah has guaranteed guidance and support to everyone who obeys the Messenger. Whoever disobeys the Messenger’s command deserves punishment, and no one can avail him in any way against Allah. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“O ‘Abbās, uncle of Allah’s Messenger, I can be of no avail to you against Allah. O Safiyyah, aunt of Allah’s Messenger, I can be of no avail to you against Allah. O Fātimah, daughter of Allah’s Messenger, I can be of no avail to you against Allah.”

He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also said to those of his Companions whom he appointed to positions of authority:

“Let me not find any of you coming on the Day of Resurrection with a camel grunting upon his neck, saying, ‘O Messenger of Allah, help me!’ I will say, ‘I can do nothing for you against Allah. I have conveyed the message to you.’”

The people of Medina, during the caliphate of

---

## Page 13521

Abū Bakr and ‘Umar and the early part of ‘Uthmān’s caliphate, were in the best of circumstances in this world and the Hereafter because they adhered to obedience to the Messenger. They then changed somewhat with the killing of ‘Uthmān, may Allah be pleased with him. The prophetic caliphate passed from their midst, and they became subjects of others. They then changed somewhat again, and in the year of al-Harrah they suffered killing, plunder and other calamities unlike anything they had previously experienced.

Although the one who inflicted this upon them was an unjust aggressor, he was no more unjust than those who had done what they did to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions. Allah Most High said:

“Why, when a calamity befell you, although you had inflicted twice as much, did you say, ‘Where has this come from?’ Say, ‘It is from your own selves.’”

Yet the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the earliest forerunners in faith were buried in Medina. Likewise, the people of Syria enjoyed well-being in worldly and religious affairs in the early days of Islam. Then turmoil arose, and sovereignty passed out of their hands. Thereafter, because of their sins, heretical hypocrites and Christians were given power over them. They seized Jerusalem and the grave of Abraham, Allah’s close friend, opened up the structure built over it, and turned it into a church. Then the people’s religious conduct improved, so Allah granted them might and victory over their enemies when they obeyed Allah and His Messenger and followed what had been sent down to them from their Lord. Obedience to Allah and His Messenger is the axis around which happiness revolves:

“Whoever obeys Allah and the Messenger will be with those whom Allah has blessed: the prophets, those steadfast in truth, the martyrs and the righteous. What excellent companions they are!”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to say in his sermons:

“Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger has followed right guidance, but whoever disobeys them harms none but

---

## Page 13522

himself, and does not harm Allah in the least.”

Even in Mecca itself, calamity is not warded off from its people, nor is provision brought to them, except through their obedience to Allah and His Messenger. As Abraham, Allah’s close friend, peace be upon him, said:

“Our Lord, I have settled some of my descendants in an uncultivated valley near Your Sacred House, our Lord, so that they may establish prayer. So make hearts among the people incline toward them, and provide them with fruits, so that they may be grateful.”

During the pre-Islamic age of ignorance, they revered the sanctity of the Sacred Precinct, performed pilgrimage and circumambulated the House. They were better than other polytheists, and Allah does not wrong anyone even by an atom’s weight. They were accorded honors that others were not accorded and given what others were not given, because they adhered more closely to Abraham’s religion than others did. Under Islam, if they are better than others, their recompense is proportionate to their excellence; and if their deeds are worse than those of others, their recompense is proportionate to their evil deeds.

The merit of mosques and sacred sites benefits only those who act within them in obedience to Allah, Mighty and Majestic. Otherwise, places in themselves bring neither reward nor punishment. Rather, reward and punishment depend on deeds that are commanded or forbidden.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had established a bond of brotherhood between Salmān al-Fārisī and Abū al-Dardā’. Abū al-Dardā’ was in Damascus, while Salmān al-Fārisī was in Iraq. Abū al-Dardā’ wrote to Salmān:

“Come to the Holy Land.”

Salmān wrote back:

“Land does not sanctify anyone; rather, it is a person’s deeds that sanctify him.”

Residing at the frontier posts for jihad is better than living in the Two Sacred Sanctuaries, by agreement of the scholars.

---

## Page 13523

And for this reason, it was better for the Companions to reside in Medina, for the sake of migration and jihad.

Allah Most High is the One who created all beings, and He is the One who guides them, provides for them, and grants them victory. None besides Him has any power over these things, as He Most High says:

“Say, ‘Call upon those whom you claim besides Allah. They possess not an atom’s weight in the heavens or on earth, nor do they have any share in either, nor does He have any helper among them.’”

“Intercession does not benefit with Him except for those to whom He gives permission.”

They have explained this to mean that permission is given both to the intercessor and to the one on whose behalf intercession is made. For Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the foremost of the intercessors on the Day of Resurrection, said concerning his wish to intercede:

“When I see my Lord, I will fall down in prostration before Him and praise Him with words of praise that He will inspire me with, which I do not know how to offer now. Then I will be told: ‘Raise your head. Speak, and you will be heard; ask, and you will be given; intercede, and your intercession will be accepted.’” He said: “Then He will set a limit for me, and I will admit them into Paradise.”

He mentioned the same regarding the second and third times. For this reason, He Most High says:

“Those whom they invoke besides Him have no power of intercession, except those who bear witness to the truth knowingly.”

He thus declares that no one besides Allah has power over intercession. His words, “except those who bear witness to the truth knowingly,” constitute a disjunctive exception. That is, those who knowingly bear witness to the truth are the people involved in intercession: some are intercessors, and others are those on whose behalf intercession is made.

It is established in the Sahīh that Abū Hurayrah asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Who will be most blessed by your intercession, O Messenger of Allah?”

He replied:

“O Abū Hurayrah, I thought that no one would ask me about this hadith before you

---

## Page 13524

because of the eagerness for hadith I have seen in you. The one most blessed by my intercession on the Day of Resurrection is whoever says, ‘There is no god but Allah,’ sincerely from his heart.” Narrated by al-Bukhārī. Thus, he identified those most perfect in sincerity as those most blessed by his intercession.

He also said in an authentic hadith:

“When you hear the caller to prayer, repeat what he says, then invoke blessings upon me, for whoever invokes blessings upon me once, Allah will bless him tenfold in return. Then ask Allah to grant me al-wasīlah, for it is a rank in Paradise befitting only one of Allah’s servants, and I hope to be that servant. Whoever asks Allah to grant me al-wasīlah will be entitled to my intercession on the Day of Resurrection.”

Recompense thus corresponds to the deed. He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained that whoever invokes blessings upon him once, Allah will bless him tenfold in return, and whoever asks Allah to grant him al-wasīlah will be entitled to his intercession on the Day of Resurrection.

He did not say that such a person would be the one most blessed by his intercession. Rather, he said:

“The one most blessed by my intercession on the Day of Resurrection is whoever says, ‘There is no god but Allah,’ sincerely from his heart.”

It is therefore clear that what a servant gains through monotheism and sincerity, including the Messenger’s intercession and other benefits, cannot be gained through other deeds, even righteous ones such as asking Allah to grant the Messenger al-wasīlah. What, then, of deeds that he did not command, but rather forbade? Through such deeds a person gains no good, either in this world or in the Hereafter—as with the Christians’ excessive exaltation of the Messiah, peace be upon him, which harms them and does not benefit them.

Similar to this is his saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, reported in the two Sahīh collections:

“Every prophet has a supplication that is answered, and I have reserved my supplication as intercession for my community on the Day of Resurrection. Allah willing, it will reach whoever dies without associating

---

## Page 13525

anything with Allah.”

Likewise, all the hadiths concerning intercession show that he intercedes only for those who worship Allah alone. A servant merits the honour of intercession and other blessings in proportion to his worship of Allah alone and his sincere devotion to Him. Allah—glorified is He—has made His promises and warnings, reward and punishment, and praise and blame dependent upon faith in Him, worshipping Him alone, and obeying Him. Whoever is more perfect in these respects is more deserving of Allah’s protective care and the good of this world and the Hereafter. Moreover, He alone provides for all His servants, Muslims and disbelievers alike; He alone wards off harm from them, and it is to Him that they turn in times of calamity.

The Most High says:

“Whatever blessings you have are from Allah. Then, when hardship touches you, it is to Him that you cry for help.”

The Most High also says:

“Say, ‘Who guards you by night and day in place of the Most Merciful?’”

That is, instead of the Most Merciful. This is the more correct of the two interpretations, as in His saying:

“If We willed, We could have put angels in your place, succeeding you on earth.”

That is, We could have put them in your stead, as the commentators generally explain. The same usage occurs in the poet’s words:

“Would that, instead of Zamzam’s water, we had a draught,
Cooled through the night upon al-Tahyān.”

That is, instead of Zamzam’s water. Thus, none but Allah watches over creation by night and day, protecting them and warding off harm from them.

The Most High says:

“Who could possibly be an army for you, helping you besides the Most Merciful? The disbelievers are in nothing but delusion.”

“Who could possibly provide for you if He withheld His provision? Yet they persist in defiance and aversion.”

---

## Page 13526

Whoever thinks that a particular land protects its inhabitants from calamity in all circumstances, because of its special status or because it contains the graves of prophets and righteous people, is mistaken. The best of all places is Mecca, yet Allah inflicted a severe punishment on its people. The Most High says:

“Allah sets forth the example of a town that was safe and secure, receiving its provision in abundance from every direction. Yet it was ungrateful for Allah’s blessings, so Allah made it taste hunger and fear that enveloped it because of what its people used to do.”

“A messenger from among themselves certainly came to them, but they denied him, so punishment overtook them while they were wrongdoers.”

Section

Those in authority have the foremost duty to support the religion of the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and the guidance and religion of truth he brought, and to denounce what he forbade, as well as the lies and religious innovations falsely attributed to him, whether through the transmitter’s ignorance or deliberate intent. For the foundation of religion is enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong.
