Foremost among what is right is worshipping Allah alone, and foremost among what is wrong is associating partners with Him.

Allah sent Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—with guidance and the religion of truth. Through him, Allah distinguished between worshipping Him alone and associating partners with Him, between truth and falsehood, between guidance and misguidance, between sound judgment and error, and between right and wrong. Whoever seeks to enjoin what he forbade, forbid what he commanded, and alter his law and religion—whether through ignorance and lack of knowledge or for some ulterior purpose or personal desire—the ruler has the foremost duty to restrain him by the means Allah and His Messenger have commanded. And he himself has the foremost duty

---

## Page 13527

to make manifest the guidance and religion of truth brought by the Messenger. For Allah, glorified is He, will surely support His Messenger and those who believe, both in this worldly life and on the Day when the witnesses stand forth. Whoever is the means through which this support comes will attain happiness in this world and the Hereafter. Otherwise, Allah will bring about that support through someone else and recompense each people for their deeds. Your Lord is never unjust to His servants.

Allah, glorified is He, has promised that this religion will continue to prevail, that it will prevail only through truth, and that whoever holds back from upholding the truth will be replaced by those who uphold it. The Most High says:

“O you who believe, what is the matter with you that, when you are told to go forth in the cause of Allah, you cling heavily to the earth? Are you content with the life of this world rather than the Hereafter? Yet the enjoyment of this worldly life is but little compared with the Hereafter.”

“If you do not go forth, He will punish you with a painful punishment and replace you with another people. You cannot harm Him at all, and Allah has power over all things.”

The Most High also says:

“O you who believe, whoever among you turns back from his religion—Allah will bring forth a people whom He loves and who love Him, humble toward the believers and firm toward the disbelievers. They strive in the cause of Allah and do not fear the blame of any critic. That is Allah’s bounty, which He grants to whom He wills. Allah is All-Bountiful, All-Knowing.”

Allah has shown people, within themselves and in the world around them, that which has enabled them to recognize the truth of what He has foretold, fulfilling His words:

“We will show them Our signs throughout the horizons and within themselves until it becomes clear to them that it is the truth. Is it not enough that your Lord is a witness over all things?”

Allah knows best. All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

---

## Page 13528

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

As for the graves of the prophets, the grave whose location scholars agree upon is that of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Knowledge of his grave has been transmitted through numerous independent channels, as has knowledge of the graves of his two companions.

As for the grave of Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, most people hold that the well-known site is indeed his grave, although some have denied this. Such a denial has been attributed to Mālik, who reportedly said, “There is no prophet’s grave anywhere in the world whose location is known except the grave of our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.” Nevertheless, most people hold that this is Abraham’s grave, and there is abundant evidence for it. The People of the Book likewise recognize it as his grave.

However, there is no religious benefit in knowing the precise locations of the prophets’ graves, nor is preserving this knowledge part of the religion. Had it been part of the religion, Allah would have preserved it as He has preserved the rest of the religion. Most people who ask about this seek only to pray at these graves, offer supplications there, and engage in similar forbidden religious innovations.

As for someone whose intention is to invoke blessings and peace upon the prophets, believe in them, and keep their memory alive, he can do so even if he does not

---

## Page 13529

know where their graves are—may Allah Most High be pleased with them all.

It has already been mentioned that “the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, cursed the Jews and Christians who took the graves of their prophets as places of worship,” along with other hadiths to this effect.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about the “graves of the prophets,” peace and blessings be upon them:

Are they the graves that people visit today, such as the graves of Noah, Abraham—Allah’s intimate friend—Isaac, Jacob, Joseph, Jonah, Elijah, Elisha, Shu‘ayb, Moses, and Zechariah, whose grave is in the mosque of Damascus? And where is the grave of ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib? Are any of these graves authentically identified or not?

He answered:

All praise is due to Allah. The grave whose location is agreed upon is that of our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. There is disagreement concerning the grave of Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, but the correct view, held by the majority, is that it is indeed his grave. As for Jonah, Elijah, Shu‘ayb, and Zechariah, the locations of their graves are unknown. The grave of ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib is at the governor’s palace in Kūfah, and the grave of Mu‘āwiyah is the one that the common people say is the grave of Hūd. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 13530

He was also asked:

Are the shrines bearing the names of ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib and his son al-Husayn—may Allah be pleased with them both—authentic or not? And where has ‘Alī’s grave been reliably established to be?

He answered:

Some of these well-known shrines are unquestionably falsely attributed, such as the shrine outside Damascus attributed to Ubayy ibn Ka‘b, the shrine outside that city attributed to Uways al-Qaranī, and the shrine in Egypt attributed to al-Husayn—may Allah be pleased with him. There are many other such shrines in Syria, Iraq, Egypt, and elsewhere, too numerous to list. Indeed, a group of scholars, including ‘Abd al-‘Azīz al-Kinānī, stated: “None of these graves attributed to the prophets is authentically identified except the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.” Other scholars have also affirmed the location of the grave of Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, peace be upon him.

As for the “shrine of ‘Alī,” the overwhelming majority of scholars hold that it is not his grave. It has even been said that it is the grave of al-Mughīrah ibn Shu‘bah. This is because it was only made known some three hundred years after ‘Alī’s death, during the rule of Banū Buwayh. They mentioned that the basis for this was a story

---

## Page 13531

that had reached them concerning al-Rashīd: that he went to that place and began apologizing to its occupant for what had taken place between him and ‘Alī’s descendants. Nothing can be established on the basis of such a story, for al-Rashīd himself had no knowledge of the matter. Perhaps, if this story about him is authentic, he had merely been told this, just as others had.

Most knowledgeable authorities say that ‘Alī was buried in the governor’s palace in Kufa or nearby. This accords with the Sunnah, for transporting a deceased person from Kufa to a distant place possessing no special merit is not prescribed. It should therefore not be supposed that ‘Alī’s family—may Allah be pleased with him—did this to him. Nor should it be supposed that this remained unknown to his household and the Muslims for three hundred years, until some ignorant non-Arabs driven by their desires revealed it.

Likewise, it has been said that the “grave of Mu‘āwiyah” outside Damascus is not his grave, and that his grave is by the wall of the mosque of Damascus, at what is said to be the “grave of Hūd.” The underlying problem is that most accounts concerning these graves and shrines are confused and fabricated. Reliable knowledge can scarcely be obtained about any but a few of them, and only after painstaking investigation.

This is because identifying them and building mosques over them are not part of Islamic law. Nor does this fall within the scope of the Reminder that Allah has undertaken to preserve, as He says:

“Indeed, We have sent down the Reminder, and We will surely preserve it.”

On the contrary, a prohibition has been issued by

---

## Page 13532

the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) against the practices of those who introduce religious innovations at these sites, as in the report narrated by Muslim in his Sahīh from Jundub ibn ‘Abd Allāh, who said:

“I heard the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), five days before his death, saying: ‘Those before you used to take graves as places of prayer. Beware! Do not take graves as places of prayer, for I forbid you to do so.’”

He also said:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of prayer.”

The leading scholars of Islam have agreed that building these shrines over graves is not prescribed, nor is taking them as places of prayer, praying at them, or going to them specifically to worship there through prayer, devotional retreat, seeking aid, fervent supplication, or similar acts. They disapproved of praying at them; moreover, many of them held that prayer offered there is invalid because the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) prohibited it.

Rather, the Sunnah for someone visiting the grave of a deceased Muslim—whether a prophet, a righteous man, or anyone else—is to greet him with peace and supplicate for him, just as one does when offering his funeral prayer. Allah linked these acts when He said concerning the hypocrites:

“And never pray over any of them who dies, nor stand at his grave.”

The implication is that funeral prayers are to be offered for believers and that one should stand at their graves. It is reported in the Sunan collections: “When the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace)

---

## Page 13533

buried one of his Companions, he would stand at his grave and say: ‘Ask that he be granted steadfastness, for he is now being questioned.’”

It is reported in the Sahīh that he used to teach his Companions to say when visiting graves:

“Peace be upon you, inhabitants of this abode of believers. We shall, if Allah wills, join you. May Allah have mercy on those of us and you who have gone before and those who follow. We ask Allah for well-being for ourselves and for you. O Allah, do not deprive us of our reward on their account, do not subject us to trials after them, and forgive us and them.”

Rather, Allah’s religion calls for venerating the houses of Allah alone, who has no partner: the mosques, where prayer—both congregational and individual—devotional retreat, and all other bodily acts of worship and acts of worship of the heart are prescribed, including recitation, remembrance of Allah, and supplication to Him.

Allah Most High says:

“The mosques belong to Allah, so do not invoke anyone alongside Allah.”

He Most High also says:

“Say, ‘My Lord has commanded justice. Direct your faces to Him at every place of worship.’”

He Most High also says:

“The mosques of Allah are maintained only by those who believe in Allah and the Last Day, establish prayer, give zakah, and fear none but Allah. It is they who may be among the rightly guided.”

He Most High also says:

“In houses which Allah has ordered to be raised and in which His name is to be mentioned, men glorify Him morning and evening—men whom neither trade nor sale distracts from the remembrance of Allah, establishing prayer, and giving zakah. They fear a Day when hearts and eyes will be turned about, so that Allah may reward them according to the best of what they did and grant them still more out of His bounty. Allah provides for whomever He wills without measure.”

This is the religion of the Muslims, who worship Allah with sincere devotion to Him alone.

---

## Page 13534

As for taking graves as idols, this is the religion of the polytheists, which the master of the messengers forbade. May Allah Most High set right the affairs of all Muslims. Praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds, and may Allah bless Muhammad.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—was asked about the shrine attributed to al-Husayn, may Allah be pleased with him, in the city of Cairo:

Is this attribution authentic or not? Was al-Husayn’s head taken to Damascus and then to Egypt, or was it taken from Iraq to Medina? Is there any truth to what some people relate concerning the shrine that was in Ascalon? Who has mentioned the matter of al-Husayn’s head and its transfer to Medina, the Prophet’s city, rather than to Syria or Egypt? Which earlier and later scholars have stated categorically that the shrines of Ascalon and Cairo are falsely attributed and not authentic? They are requested to discuss this matter in detail, given the pressing necessity and need for it

---

## Page 13535

and may they be recompensed and rewarded, if Allah Most High wills.

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Rather, the attribution of the shrine in Cairo to al-Husayn ibn ʿAlī—may Allah be pleased with them both—is a fabricated lie. There is no disagreement about this among the recognized scholars to whom Muslims turn in such matters because of their knowledge and truthfulness.

No named scholar known for learning and truthfulness is reported to have said that this shrine’s attribution is authentic. Some people merely repeat it as a statement attributed to an unknown person, in the manner of those who recount the claims of the Rāfidah and other such purveyors of falsehood. They transmit reports and stories and recount doctrines and opinions. Yet when you ask them who said these things and transmitted them, they have no secure basis to fall back on. They name no one known for truthfulness in what he transmits or knowledge of what he says.

Rather, their ultimate basis is to say, “The true group has reached a consensus.” They consider themselves the true group and regard themselves as the believers, while regarding the rest of the Muslim community as unbelievers.

They say that they are upon the truth only because the infallible imam is among them. According to the Twelver Imāmī Rāfidah, this infallible one is the person whom they claim went into

---

## Page 13536

the cellar in Samarra after the death of his father, al-Hasan ibn ʿAlī al-ʿAskarī, in the year 260 AH. He remains absent to this day: nothing has been heard of him, and no one has seen him or found any trace of him.

Those knowledgeable about the genealogies of the Prophet’s household say that al-Hasan ibn ʿAlī al-ʿAskarī had neither children nor descendants.

There is no doubt that all rational people regard such a claim as the height of folly. Only the most foolish, misguided, and ignorant of people would accept the belief that such a person is an imam and is infallible. A detailed refutation of their claims belongs elsewhere.

The purpose here is to illustrate the kinds of assertions and reports circulated among people of ignorance and misguidance. For these people claim among the ignorant and misguided that this awaited one was either two, three, or five years old when his father died; they disagree among themselves about this.

It is established by the explicit texts of the Quran, the mass-transmitted Sunnah, and the consensus of the Muslim community that a child of this age must be under another person’s guardianship, both personally and with respect to his property. He would therefore himself be in another’s custody and care, under someone entitled to assume responsibility for his person and property and to oversee and look after him, whether a dhimmī (non-Muslim under Muslim protection) or otherwise. Before the age of seven, he is a child who is not commanded

---

## Page 13537

to pray. When he reaches ten, if he does not pray, he is disciplined to make him do so. How, then, can someone like this be an infallible imam who knows the whole religion, and without believing in whom no one can enter Paradise?

Furthermore, even assuming his existence, his imamate, and his infallibility, people are only obliged to obey someone present among them, commanding them to do what Allah and His Messenger have commanded and forbidding them from what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden. If they neither see him nor hear his words, they have no way of knowing what he commands or forbids. They therefore cannot be required to obey him, since he has given them no command that they have heard and understood. Obeying someone who issues no commands is inherently impossible.

Even supposing that he gives them commands, but those commands do not reach them and they have no means of learning them, they would be incapable of knowing what they have been commanded to do. The ability to learn what is commanded is a prerequisite for obedience, especially according to the later Shia. They are among those who most strongly reject imposing obligations beyond a person’s capacity, since they agree with the Muʿtazilah concerning divine decree, as they do concerning the divine attributes.

If it is said, “This is because of their sins, for they have made him afraid to appear,” the reply is:

Granted that his enemies have frightened him, what sin have his supporters and those who love him committed? What benefit do they gain from believing in him when he teaches them nothing and commands them to do nothing? Moreover, how could it be permissible for him—despite his obligation to call people to the truth—to withdraw into this

---

## Page 13538

occultation, which has now lasted more than four hundred and fifty years?

What made this occultation permissible for him, unlike his forefathers, who were present among people before their deaths: ʿAlī, al-Hasan, al-Husayn, ʿAlī ibn al-Husayn, Muhammad ibn ʿAlī, Jaʿfar ibn Muhammad, Mūsā ibn Jaʿfar, ʿAlī ibn Mūsā, Muhammad ibn ʿAlī, ʿAlī ibn Muhammad, and al-Hasan ibn ʿAlī al-ʿAskarī? These men were present among people and met with them. Knowledge was transmitted from ʿAlī, al-Hasan, al-Husayn, ʿAlī ibn al-Husayn, Muhammad ibn ʿAlī, and Jaʿfar ibn Muhammad, as is well known to those versed in it. The others have well-known biographies and publicly known accounts of their lives. How, then, could he consider it lawful to remain hidden for this lengthy period—more than four hundred years—when he is the imam of the Muslim community? Indeed, according to their claim, he is its guide, its caller, and its infallible leader, in whom it is obliged to believe; anyone who does not believe in him is, in their view, not a believer.

If they say, “Fear,” the reply is:

His forefathers faced greater danger, and there is no disagreement among the scholars about this. Some of them were imprisoned, and some were killed. Moreover, he would only have cause for fear if he waged war. If, however, he did as his predecessors did—sitting with the Muslims and teaching them—he would have nothing to fear.

---

## Page 13539

A full account of these people’s misguidance would be lengthy. The point here is simply that they make this the foundation of their religion. Then they say: “If the true sect is divided between two opinions, the proponent of one being known and the proponent of the other unknown, then the opinion whose proponent is unknown is the truth.” This is how I found it in the books of their leading scholars. They justified it by saying that the infallible imam would be among those holding the opinion whose proponent is unknown. This is the height of ignorance and misguidance.

The same applies to everything they transmit on this subject. They relate biographies, stories, and hadiths, but if you ask them for their chains of transmission, they do not refer you to anyone known for truthfulness. Rather, it is enough for one of them to have heard it from someone like himself, or to have read it in a book containing no known chain of transmission. If they do name someone, he is among those notorious for lying and fabrication. It is inconceivable that they should ever transmit anything unknown to the scholars of the Sunnah except through an unidentified person or someone known for lying.

One example is the claim that this grave in Cairo is “the shrine of al-Husayn,” may Allah be pleased with him. Indeed, other shrines are likewise identified as the grave of al-Husayn, may Allah be pleased with him. It is known by universal agreement that this shrine was built sometime between 543 and 549 AH, that it was transferred from a shrine in Ascalon, and that the latter

---

## Page 13540

shrine in Ascalon had been established after 490 AH.

Thus, this Cairene shrine has its origin in that shrine in Ascalon. The shrine in Ascalon was established more than 430 years after al-Husayn’s killing, and the Cairene shrine nearly 500 years after his killing. There has been no disagreement about this among the various scholars who have addressed this subject, including hadith scholars, compilers of accounts of Cairo, and historians. Scholars have transmitted this from generation to generation, and such matters are widely known among them.

This is well known among them and established by mass transmission, regardless of whether the shrine’s attribution to al-Husayn is considered true or false. They have not disputed that it was transferred from Ascalon during the final years of the Ubaydī dynasty.

Since this Cairene shrine derives from the shrine in Ascalon, as is universally agreed and established by mass transmission, it is clear that the claim that the shrine in Ascalon was built over the head of al-Husayn, may Allah be pleased with him, has no evidence whatsoever. None of the scholars whose concern it is to transmit such accounts has reported this: neither hadith scholars, nor scholars of historical accounts and chronicles, nor scholars who have written on genealogy—the genealogy of Quraysh, Banū Hāshim, and the like.

---

## Page 13541

That shrine in Ascalon was established at the end of the fifth century AH; it was not ancient. Before it, there had been no site there, or anything similar, associated with al-Husayn, nor any inscribed stone or anything else that might be cited as evidence of such an association. It is thus clear that attributing such a place to al-Husayn is a claim made without any knowledge whatsoever. Whoever makes this claim has nothing that could serve as a basis for it—not a sound report, nor even a weak one. Indeed, this is no different from someone coming to some graves in a Muslim city and claiming that one of them contains al-Husayn’s head, or that a particular grave belongs to one of the prophets, or making similar claims of the kind often advanced by people of falsehood and misguidance. It is well known, and Muslims agree, that no such claim has been transmitted.

What such people generally rely on is a claim to have had a dream, or to have found at the grave some sign indicating the righteousness of its occupant: a pleasant fragrance, an imagined occurrence beyond the ordinary, or something similar. Alternatively, they rely on an account that someone used to venerate that grave.

As for dreams, many—indeed, most—are fabricated. In our own time, we have known people in Egypt, Syria, and Iraq who claim to have had dreams concerning certain places, to the effect that a particular place is a prophet’s grave, or contains a trace of a prophet, or something similar, yet the claimant is lying.

---

## Page 13542

and this is widespread.

Those who report such dreams are often lying. Even assuming they are truthful, the one who told them this may have been a devil. There is agreement that a dream alone, without evidence supporting its truth, cannot establish anything. For it is established in the Sahīh that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“Dreams are of three kinds: a dream from Allah, a dream arising from a person’s own thoughts, and a dream from Satan.”

Since dreams fall into three categories, each must be distinguished from the others. Some people—even shaykhs who outwardly possess knowledge and ascetic piety—base their claims in such matters on a story they relate from an unknown person. Some even say, “My brother al-Khidr told me that al-Khidr’s grave is in such-and-such a place.” It is well known, as we have explained elsewhere, that all such claims—whether someone claims to have seen al-Khidr, to have seen someone who saw al-Khidr, or to have heard someone who saw al-Khidr, or whether the observer merely supposed that the person he saw was al-Khidr—can gain credence only among ignorant purveyors of superstition who have no share of knowledge, reason, or religion. Rather, they are among those who neither understand nor reason.

As for reports of a pleasant fragrance, an occurrence beyond the ordinary, or similar things associated with a grave, none of this establishes the identity of its occupant—that he is this or that particular person. Rather,

---

## Page 13543

the most this can show—if substantiated—is that the person buried there was righteous, and that it is the grave of a righteous man or a prophet. That fragrance may, however, have been produced by some common people, for some of them engage in such practices. One of our companions told me that two men appeared on the banks of the Euphrates. One had established a grave to which money was brought by misguided people who visited it and made vows to it. The other then turned to a grave and claimed to have seen in a dream that it was the grave of ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn ʿAwf. He placed various perfumes inside it, producing a powerful fragrance.

People living near the grave in Mount Lebanon, in the Biqāʿ, which is said to be Noah’s grave, told me about it. It had only recently become known, during the seventh century AH. Its origin was that they had noticed a pleasant fragrance coming from a grave and found large bones. They said, “These indicate someone of a large physical build.” Then, on mere conjecture, they said, “This is Noah’s grave.” Yet many people of the same kind were buried at that site.

Likewise, a number of people have stated that this shrine in Ascalon is the grave of one of the disciples or another follower of Jesus, son of Mary. Things of the same kind as those found at the graves of believers may also be found at the graves of idolaters. Indeed, the claim that it is al-Husayn’s grave is mere supposition and guesswork. Among the shaykhs renowned for knowledge and piety was

---

## Page 13544

a man in Cairo who reportedly said, “It is the grave of a Christian.”

Likewise, on the eastern side of Damascus there is a shrine said to be the grave of Ubayy ibn Kaʿb. The scholars agree that Ubayy never came to Damascus; rather, he died in Medina. Some people therefore used to say, “It is the grave of a Christian.” This is not implausible, for the Jews and Christians were the first to venerate graves and shrines. Hence the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said in the hadith recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

He was warning against what they had done.

Christians go to greater extremes in this than Jews, as related in Sahīh al-Bukhārī and Sahīh Muslim on the authority of ʿĀ’ishah:

“Umm Habībah and Umm Salamah, may Allah be pleased with them both, mentioned to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, a church in Abyssinia, describing its beauty and the images inside it. He said, ‘When a righteous man among those people died, they would build a place of worship over his grave and make those images within it. Those are the worst of creation in Allah’s sight on the Day of Resurrection.’”

Christians often venerate the relics of their saints. It is therefore not implausible that they suggested to some ignorant Muslims that this was the grave of someone whom Muslims venerate, so that they would join them in venerating it. How could this be unlikely, when they have already led astray many of

---

## Page 13545

the ignorant Muslims, to the point that they began having their children baptized, claiming that this would ensure a long life for the child. They even induced them to visit the churches and houses of worship they venerate. Many ignorant Muslims have begun dedicating vowed offerings to places venerated by Christians, just as many of them now visit Christian churches and seek blessings from their priests, monks, and others like them.

Those who venerate graves and shrines bear a striking resemblance to Christians. Indeed, when I arrived in Cairo, one of their highly regarded monks met with me and debated with me about the Messiah and the Christian religion. I demonstrated to him the falsity of those beliefs and answered what he claimed constituted proof. Later, I learned that he had written a book seeking to refute the Muslims and disprove the prophethood of Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him. A Muslim brought it to me and began reading it aloud so that I could answer the Christians’ arguments and expose their flaws.

Among the last things I said to that Christian was, “You are polytheists.” I explained some of their practices that constitute associating partners with Allah: their devotion to statues and graves, their worship of them, and their appeals to them for aid.

He said to me, “We do not associate them as partners with Allah or worship them. We only seek access to Allah through them, just as Muslims do when they come to the grave of a righteous man and cling to the grille that

---

## Page 13546

encloses it, and do other such things.”

I said to him, “This too is polytheism. It is not part of the Muslims’ religion, even if ignorant people practise it.” He acknowledged that it was polytheism. Indeed, a priest who was present during this discussion said upon hearing it, “Yes, on that understanding, we are polytheists.”

Some Christians would say to some Muslims, “We have a lord and a lady, and you have a lord and a lady. Our lord is the Messiah and our lady is Mary; your lord is al-Husayn and your lady is Nafīsa.”

Christians therefore rejoice in those practices of religious innovators and ignorant Muslims that accord with their own religion and resemble their practices. They want such practices to grow stronger and become more widespread. They also want to place their monks on a par with devout Muslim worshippers, and their priests on a par with Muslim scholars. They imitate Muslims, for the rational among them do not deny the validity of Islam. Rather, they say, “This is a path to Allah, and that is a path to Allah.”

This is why many hypocrites among their converts find it easy to profess Islam outwardly. In their view, Muslims and Christians are like the adherents of different schools within Islam; indeed, they call religions ‘schools.’ Yet it is well known that the adherents of the various schools—the Hanafīs, Mālikīs, Shāfiʿīs, and Hanbalīs—share one religion. By the consensus of Muslims, any of them who obeys Allah and His Messenger to the best of his ability is a believer destined for felicity.

---

## Page 13547

Thus, if Christians hold such a view of religions, one of them leaving his religion becomes like a person moving from one school to another. People frequently do this out of desire or fear. If his relatives and friends remain in the first school, he does not object; rather, he continues inwardly to love and cherish them. For a school is like a homeland, and the soul longs for its homeland unless it believes that remaining there is forbidden or entails harm and worldly loss.

For this reason, many among the People of the Book who outwardly profess Islam make no distinction between Muslims and the People of the Book. Some incline more towards Muslims, while others incline more towards their former religion. Others are drawn to their former community by temperament and habit, or by ties of ethnicity, kinship, homeland, cooperation in pursuing their aims, and the like.

Likewise, the philosophers and those who follow their path, such as the Qarmatians, the Ittihādiyya, and others like them, consider it permissible for a person to follow the religion of the Muslims, the Jews, or the Christians. It is well known that all of this constitutes disbelief by the consensus of Muslims. Whoever does not acknowledge, inwardly and outwardly, that Allah accepts no religion other than Islam is not a Muslim.

---

## Page 13548

And whoever does not acknowledge that, after Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—was sent, no one can be a Muslim unless he believes in him and follows him inwardly and outwardly, is not a Muslim. Whoever does not consider it forbidden, after his mission—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—to profess the religion of the Jews or Christians—indeed, whoever does not regard them as disbelievers and hate them—is not a Muslim, by the consensus of Muslims.

The point here is that Christians like Muslims to have practices resembling their own, so that their religion may thereby be strengthened and Muslims may not be repelled by them or their religion. For this reason, Islamic law requires differing from the Jews and Christians, as we have explained at length in our book, “The Requirement of the Straight Path to Differ from the People of Hell.” Christians have obtained much of what they seek from ignorant Muslims, particularly extremists among the Shia, ignorant ascetics, and those who go to extremes in venerating shaykhs. These people closely resemble Christians in their excesses, innovations in worship, and similar matters. Thus, Christians mislead Muslims about graves belonging to their own community, so that ignorant people imagine them to be the graves of righteous Muslims and venerate them.

If some have said that the shrine at Ascalon is the grave of a Christian or one of the disciples—and we have no evidence that it is the grave of a Muslim, let alone a grave containing al-Husayn’s head—then the claim that it is the grave of

---

## Page 13549

a Muslim—al-Husayn or anyone else—is a falsehood and a lie, to be rejected. This alone is sufficient grounds for refusing to call it “the shrine of al-Husayn.”

Section

We go further: we know with certainty that it does not contain al-Husayn’s head, and that the shrine at Ascalon was never a shrine of al-Husayn, for several reasons. Among them is that, had al-Husayn’s head been there, its discovery and disclosure would not have been delayed until more than four hundred years after his death. The Umayyad dynasty had ended more than three hundred and fifty-odd years before that discovery. The Abbasid caliphate had succeeded it, and during its rule shrines appeared in Iraq and elsewhere, many of which were falsely attributed.

They had built a shrine at the site where al-Husayn was killed in Karbala. Powerful rulers used to frequent it, so much so that the imams censured them for this. Indeed, when certain matters were brought before al-Mutawakkil, he is said to have gone too far in opposing this and exceeded what was required.

This is to say nothing of the Abbasid caliphate in its early days and while it remained upright, for at that time they did not venerate shrines, whether genuine or spurious, as

---

## Page 13550

happened in later times, for Islam was then still strong and vigorous. During the time of the Companions, their successors, and the generation after them, none of this existed in the Muslim lands—not in the Hijaz, Yemen, Syria, Iraq, Egypt, Khurāsān, or the Maghreb. No shrine whatsoever had been erected over the grave of a prophet, a Companion, a member of the Prophet’s household, or a righteous person. Rather, these shrines were generally established later. They emerged and spread when the Abbasid caliphate weakened, the Muslim community became divided, heretics who deceived Muslims grew numerous among them, and the teachings of the people of religious innovation became widespread. This began during the reign of al-Muqtadir, toward the end of the third century AH. It was then that the ‘Ubaydī-Qaddāhī Qarmatians emerged in the Maghreb. They subsequently came to Egypt.

It is said that unlawful levies were introduced in the Muslim lands around that time. The Buyids also emerged around then, and many of them harbored heresy and grave religious innovations.

During their rule, the descendants of ‘Ubayd al-Qaddāh grew powerful in Egypt. It was also during their rule that the shrine attributed to ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him, near Najaf was made known. Before that, no one had said that ‘Alī’s grave was there. Rather, ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him, was buried in the governor’s palace in Kufa. All that they mentioned was that one of them related that al-Rashīd had come to a spot there and begun apologizing to the person buried in it. They said that this was ‘Alī and that he had apologized to him for what he had done to his descendants. Thus they said, “This is ‘Alī’s grave.” Some people have said

---

## Page 13551

that it is the grave of al-Mughīrah ibn Shu‘bah. This matter is discussed at length elsewhere.

If we consider the Buyids and the descendants of ‘Ubayd, both groups were marked by extreme Shiism. Indeed, during their rule in Baghdad, they publicly displayed unprecedented Rāfidī rites on the day of ‘Āshūrā’, such as hanging sackcloth over doorways and sending women into the marketplaces to wail in mourning. This frequently led to fighting that the rulers were unable to stop. It was for this reason that al-Khiraqī, author of the compendium on jurisprudence, left Baghdad when reviling the early generations became widespread there. The Qarmatians in the East at that time went so far as to seize the Black Stone, keeping it with them for a considerable period, and to kill the pilgrims and throw them into the well of Zamzam.

Thus, if, despite all this, not even a shrine to al-Husayn appeared in Ascalon—although, had his head been there, the earlier generations of these people would have known this better than the later ones—then its failure to appear, despite ample eagerness and motivation, opportunity and power, establishes that the claim is false and fabricated.

This is like someone claiming noble descent from ‘Alī when it is known that none of his ancestors ever made such a claim, despite how eager they would have been to do so had it been true. This establishes that the claimant is lying. By the same reasoning, we know the falsehood of those who claim an explicit designation of ‘Alī as caliph, or anything else that people would have had abundant motivation and incentive to transmit, yet which was not transmitted.

---

## Page 13552

The second point:

None of those who compiled accounts of al-Husayn and his killing, such as Abū Bakr ibn Abī al-Dunyā, Abū al-Qāsim al-Baghawī and others, mentioned that his head was taken to Ascalon or Cairo. Abū al-Khattāb ibn Dihyah made a similar statement in his book entitled al-‘Ilm al-Mashhūr fī Fadā’il al-Ayyām wa-l-Shuhūr (Well-Known Knowledge concerning the Merits of Days and Months). He stated that those who wrote about al-Husayn’s killing unanimously agreed that the head had not been taken far afield. He mentioned this after stating that the shrine in Cairo was a fabricated falsehood with no basis. He discussed this at length and also addressed related matters in his discussion of the day of ‘Āshūrā’.

The third point:

The account given by reliable scholars and historians is that the head was taken to Medina and buried beside his brother al-Hasan.

It is well known that al-Zubayr ibn Bakkār, author of the Book of Genealogies, Muhammad ibn Sa‘d, al-Wāqidī’s secretary and author of al-Tabaqāt, and others renowned for their learning, trustworthiness and extensive knowledge were more knowledgeable about this subject and more truthful in what they transmitted than the ignorant, the liars, and certain writers of history whose knowledge and truthfulness cannot be trusted. Indeed, a man may be truthful yet lack the expertise in chains of transmission needed to distinguish acceptable reports from rejected ones. Or he may have a poor memory, or be suspected of lying or embellishing reports, as is the case with many chroniclers and historians

---

## Page 13553

especially someone such as Abū Mikhnaf Lūt ibn Yahyā and others like him. It is well known that al-Wāqidī himself is regarded more favorably by scholars than the likes of Hishām ibn al-Kalbī, his father Muhammad ibn al-Sā’ib, and others like them. Yet the scholars’ criticisms of al-Wāqidī are well known. Reports transmitted by him and others like him may serve only as corroboration and supplementary support; relying on them alone to establish knowledge is not sound.

Thus, when reliable authorities state that al-Husayn’s head was buried in Medina, while others claim that it was either returned to his body and buried with it at Karbala, or buried in Aleppo, Damascus, or elsewhere—claims that have no foundation—and no reliable authority mentions that it is in Ascalon, it becomes clear that this claim is false. For in matters of transmitted history, which are learned only from knowledgeable and truthful people, not from the ignorant and dishonest, it is impossible that the knowledgeable and truthful should be wrong while the ignorant and dishonest are right.

The fourth point:

What is established in Sahīh al-Bukhārī is:

“The head was brought before ‘Ubayd Allāh ibn Ziyād, who began tapping its front teeth with a rod in the presence of Anas ibn Mālik.”

And in al-Musnad:

“This took place in the presence of Abū Barzah al-Aslamī.”

Some people, however, have related through a disconnected chain of transmission:

“This tapping took place in the presence of Yazīd ibn Mu‘āwiyah.”

This is false. For Abū Barzah and Anas

---

## Page 13554

ibn Mālik were in Iraq, not Syria, while Yazīd ibn Mu‘āwiyah was in Syria, not Iraq, when al-Husayn was killed. Thus, anyone who reports that his front teeth were tapped with a rod in the presence of Anas and Abū Barzah before Yazīd is unquestionably lying, as is established by mass-transmitted reports.

It is also established by mass-transmitted reports that ‘Ubayd Allāh ibn Ziyād was the governor of Iraq when al-Husayn was killed. Authentic reports establish that he was the one who dispatched ‘Umar ibn Sa‘d ibn Abī Waqqās to command the force that fought al-Husayn. ‘Umar had initially refused, but Ibn Ziyād used inducements and threats until he did what he did.

Scholarly authors have related, through acceptable chains of transmission, that the people of Iraq wrote to al-Husayn while he was in the Hijaz, asking him to come to them. They said, “The Sunnah has been put to death and religious innovation revived,” and made other such claims. It is even said that they sent him enough letters to fill a chest, or more. His wise and devoted friends advised him, but he did not accept their counsel. As the saying goes:

Not every wise man offers you sincere advice,
Nor is everyone who offers sincere advice wise.

Men such as ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Abbās, ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar, and others advised him not to go to them. His brother al-Hasan had also held this view—and they all agreed that there was no benefit in this course and that these Iraqis were lying

---

## Page 13555

to him and would abandon him, for they were the quickest to plunge into turmoil and the least capable of remaining steadfast amid it; and that his father had been superior to him, had commanded greater obedience among the people, and had enjoyed the support of the majority. Despite this, they opposed and abandoned him to an extent known only to Allah, until he began seeking peace after having called for war. By the time he died, his aversion to them had become intense beyond measure. He prayed against them and grew weary of them.

When al-Husayn, may Allah be pleased with him, set out, his cousin Muslim ibn ‘Aqīl was sent to them, and a group followed him. But when ‘Ubayd Allāh ibn Ziyād arrived in Kufa, they sided with Ibn Ziyād, and Muslim ibn ‘Aqīl, Hāni’ ibn ‘Urwah, and others were killed. When news of this reached al-Husayn, he wanted to return, but ‘Umar ibn Sa‘d’s detachment intercepted him and demanded that he surrender himself as their prisoner. He refused and asked that they let him go to his cousin Yazīd and place his hand in his, return whence he had come, or go to one of the frontier regions. In their oppression, injustice, and aggression, they refused to grant his request. Among those most vehement in inciting them against him was Shimr ibn Dhī al-Jawshan. A group from among them joined al-Husayn.

Fighting ensued until Allah honoured al-Husayn and those of his household whom He so favoured with martyrdom—may Allah be pleased with them and grant them His good pleasure. He disgraced those whom He disgraced through their oppression, injustice, and aggression in violating the sanctity of these people and treating their blood as lawful to shed.

“Whomever Allah humiliates, none can honour. Indeed, Allah does whatever He wills.”

This was among Allah’s blessings upon al-Husayn and the honour He bestowed upon him, so that he might attain the ranks of the martyrs, since in the early days of Islam he had not been subjected to the trials

---

## Page 13556

and ordeals to which the other members of his household had been subjected, such as his grandfather, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, his father, his uncle, and his father’s uncle—may Allah be pleased with them. For Banū Hāshim are the best of Quraysh, Quraysh are the best of the Arabs, and the Arabs are the best of the descendants of Adam. This is authentically reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as in his statement in the authentic hadith:

“Allah chose the descendants of Ishmael from among the descendants of Abraham, chose Kinānah from among the descendants of Ishmael, chose Quraysh from Kinānah, chose Banū Hāshim from Quraysh, and chose me from Banū Hāshim.”

Sahīh Muslim also reports that he said on the day of Ghadīr Khumm:

“I remind you of Allah concerning the members of my household. I remind you of Allah concerning the members of my household. I remind you of Allah concerning the members of my household.”

The Sunan collections relate that al-‘Abbās complained to him that some of Quraysh were belittling them. He said:

“By Him in whose hand is my soul, they will not enter Paradise until they love you for Allah’s sake and because of your kinship with me.”

If they are the best of creation, then there is no doubt that their deeds are the best of deeds. The best of them was Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who has no equal among humankind. Thus, a virtuous man among them is superior to every virtuous man from the other tribes of Quraysh and the Arabs—indeed, even from the Children of Israel and others. Moreover, ‘Alī, Hamzah, Ja‘far, and ‘Ubaydah ibn al-Hārith were among the earliest forerunners among the Emigrants. They are therefore superior to those of the second rank from the other tribes. For this reason,

---

## Page 13557

“on the day of Badr, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ordered them to engage in single combat when ‘Utbah ibn Rabī‘ah, Shaybah ibn Rabī‘ah, and al-Walīd ibn ‘Utbah stepped forward. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Rise, Hamzah! Rise, ‘Ubaydah! Rise, ‘Alī!’” Thus, three men from Banū Hāshim went forward to face those three.

It is established in an authentic report that Allah’s words were revealed concerning them:

“These are two opposing parties who have disputed concerning their Lord”—and the rest of the verse.

The verse nevertheless has a general application.

Since al-Hasan and al-Husayn were the leaders of the young men of Paradise, yet had been born after the Hijrah, when Islam was strong, and had not experienced the harm and tribulation suffered by their righteous predecessors, Allah honored them with trials to raise their ranks. This reflected their honored standing with Him, not their insignificance in His sight, just as He honored Hamzah, ‘Alī, Ja‘far, ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān, and others with martyrdom.

In al-Musnad and elsewhere, Fātimah bint al-Husayn narrates from her father, al-Husayn, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No Muslim suffers a calamity and later remembers it—even long afterward—and again declares, ‘Indeed, we belong to Allah, and to Him we shall return,’ without Allah granting him a reward like the reward he received on the day it befell him.”

This hadith was narrated by al-Husayn, and from him by his daughter Fātimah, who witnessed his killing. Allah knew that the calamity that befell him would be remembered throughout the ages. Thus, when this calamity and others like it are remembered, what is prescribed is to say:

“Indeed, we belong to Allah, and to Him we

---

## Page 13558

shall return.”

“O Allah, reward us in our calamity and grant us something better in its place.”

Allah Most High says:

“And give glad tidings to the patient—those who, when a calamity befalls them, say, ‘Indeed, we belong to Allah, and to Him we shall return.’”

Allah Most High says:

“Those are the ones upon whom are blessings from their Lord and mercy, and those are the rightly guided.”

Discussing the circumstances of kings in detail is difficult or impossible. In general, however, we should understand that they and other people who have good and evil deeds fall, by virtue of those deeds, within the scope of the texts promising reward or threatening punishment.

For the texts promising reward to apply to an individual, his deeds must be done sincerely for Allah’s sake and in accordance with the Sunnah. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was asked:

“A man fights out of bravery, out of partisan zeal, or to gain renown. Which of these is in Allah’s cause?”

He replied:

“Whoever fights so that Allah’s word may be supreme is fighting in Allah’s cause.”

Likewise, for the texts threatening punishment to apply to an individual, he must not have been acting on an interpretation or have erred in exercising independent judgment. Allah has pardoned this community for mistakes and forgetfulness. Many of the interpretations adopted by earlier generations, and the doubts that arose for them in connection with those interpretations, are well known; these give rise to personal inclination and desire. Thus, they do what they do under the influence of both doubt and desire.

The evil deeds committed by sinners are erased through repentance. They may also be erased through good deeds that wipe them away and calamities that expiate them. They may also be erased through the Muslims’ funeral prayer for the deceased and through the intercession of

---

## Page 13559

the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, on the Day of Resurrection for those guilty of major sins. For this reason, regarding those known for wrongdoing and the like who are nevertheless Muslims and outwardly have righteous deeds—such as al-Hajjāj ibn Yūsuf and others like him—the scholars preferred not to curse any of them individually. Rather, they say as Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, Allah’s curse is upon the wrongdoers.”

Thus, they curse in general terms those whom Allah and His Messenger have cursed, as in the Prophet’s saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Allah has cursed wine, the one who presses it, the one who has it pressed, its seller, its buyer, the one who serves it, the one who drinks it, the one who carries it, the one to whom it is carried, and the one who consumes its proceeds.”

But they do not curse a particular individual. As is established in Sahīh al-Bukhārī and elsewhere:

“There was a man called Himār who used to drink wine, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to flog him. Once, when he was brought before him, a man cursed him. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Do not curse him, for he loves Allah and His Messenger.’”

This is because a curse falls within the category of threats of punishment, and a general threat cannot be judged with certainty to apply to a particular individual, owing to any of the reasons already mentioned: repentance, good deeds that erase sins, calamities that expiate them, accepted intercession, or other such reasons.

Some scholars curse particular individuals, such as Yazīd. Another group, taking the opposite position, say: “Rather, we love him because of the faith he possesses, on account of which Allah has commanded us to maintain bonds of loyalty, since he is not a disbeliever.”

The position preferred by the Muslim community is that we neither curse a particular individual unconditionally nor love a particular individual unconditionally

---

## Page 13560

for a servant may possess grounds for both when love of both things is combined within him. Among the principles by which Ahl al-Sunnah differ from the Khawārij is that one person may combine good deeds and evil deeds: he is rewarded for his good deeds and punished for his evil deeds, praised for his good deeds and blamed for his evil deeds. In one respect he is approved of and loved, while in another he is hated and regarded with displeasure. This, therefore, is the judgment concerning those who commit wrongful acts.

As for those acting solely on an admissible interpretation, they are people who exercise independent judgment but err. Their errors are forgiven, and they are rewarded for what they have done well through their good intentions and their efforts to seek and follow the truth. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When a judge exercises independent judgment and is correct, he receives two rewards; when he exercises independent judgment and errs, he receives one reward.”

Accordingly, this judgment governs discussion of the earliest forerunners and those whom the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, testified would enter Paradise, such as ʿUthmān, ʿAlī, Talhah, al-Zubayr, and others like them. Indeed, it also applies to those below these eminent figures in rank: those present at al-Hudaybiyah who pledged allegiance beneath the tree. They numbered more than one thousand four hundred.

It is established in the Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

---

## Page 13561

“No one who pledged allegiance beneath the tree will enter the Fire.”

Concerning these men and others like them, we therefore say of the disputes that arose among them: the conduct of any one of them was either a praiseworthy effort, a forgiven sin, or an exercise of independent judgment whose error was pardoned.

For this reason, one of the principles upheld by scholars is that no one should be allowed to speak of these men in a manner that impugns their uprightness and religious integrity. Rather, it must be recognized that they are upright and worthy of approval, and that Allah is pleased with them—especially since the grave allegations reported against them are fabricated lies. For example, a group of ʿUthmān’s supporters accused ʿAlī of ordering ʿUthmān’s killing or helping to bring it about. Some of those who fought against him believed this about him, and it was among the misconceptions on which they based their fighting against ʿAlī. Yet it was a baseless misconception. ʿAlī would swear—and he was truthful and true to his oath—“I did not kill ʿUthmān, nor did I assist in his killing.” He would also say: “O Allah, scatter ʿUthmān’s killers across land and sea, plain and mountain.”

They also used his refusal to hand over ʿUthmān’s killers as grounds for their suspicions in this matter. Yet he was not in a position to carry out everything he wished concerning the enforcement of prescribed punishments and similar matters. The people were divided over him, and his army and its commanders did not obey every order he gave them. Division and discord give rise to causes of evil, corruption, and the suspension of legal rulings, as is understood by scholars familiar with the scriptural texts concerning the virtue of unity and Islam.

---

## Page 13562

Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiyah committed reprehensible acts, including the incident of al-Harrah. An authentic hadith transmitted from ʿAlī, may Allah be pleased with him, reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Medina is a sanctuary from ʿAyr to such-and-such a place. Whoever commits an offense within it or shelters an offender incurs the curse of Allah, the angels, and all mankind. Neither atonement nor ransom will be accepted from him.”

He also said:

“Whoever intends harm against the people of Medina, Allah will dissolve him as salt dissolves in water.”

For this reason, Imam Ahmad was asked, “Would you record hadith on Yazīd’s authority?” He replied, “No, certainly not! Was he not the one who did what he did to the people of al-Harrah?”

He was also asked—according to what people relate—“Do you not love Yazīd?” He replied, “Would anyone who believes in Allah and the Last Day love Yazīd?” He was then asked, “Why, then, do you not curse him?” He replied, “When have you ever seen your father curse anyone?”

The position of Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamāʿah is that they do not declare those who face the qiblah in prayer to be unbelievers merely because of sins or mistaken interpretations. Rather, when a person has both good and evil deeds, his affair rests with Allah. What we have related concerning the killing of al-Husayn, may Allah be pleased with him, is the account on which people agree.

---

## Page 13563

Additional details have also been reported: some are authentic, some weak, and some fabricated lies.

Hadith scholars who have written on this subject, such as al-Baghawī, Ibn Abī al-Dunyā and others, are like hadith scholars who compile other transmitted accounts: they are more knowledgeable and more truthful in these matters, a point on which scholars do not disagree. They either supply chains of transmission for what they relate from trustworthy narrators, or transmit without complete chains from authorities whose reports of this kind approach authenticity. By contrast, much of what chroniclers transmit with chains comes from liars or unknown narrators. As for what they transmit without chains, it is darkness upon darkness. Yet these chroniclers, by my life, do at least transmit from others, whether with chains or without them.

As for those who follow their own desires and others like them, they rely on reports whose original sources are entirely unknown, let alone trustworthy or dependable. They think nothing of fabricating lies. Even the most learned among them bases his accounts on no reliable authority, but on hearsay from ignorant people and liars, and reports from those who perpetrate blatant falsehood.

It has thus become clear that they lied in the account in which they mention al-Husayn’s head being taken to Yazīd and his tapping it with a rod. Even if the head was taken to Ibn Ziyād—as the account establishes—no report with a known chain of transmission states that it was brought before Yazīd. I have seen only a disconnected chain of transmission for this, contradicted by other reports that are

---

## Page 13564

more firmly established and clearer—reports relating that when news of al-Husayn’s killing reached Yazīd, he expressed grief and said:

“May Allah curse the people of Iraq! I would have been content with their obedience without their doing this.”

He said of Ibn Ziyād:

“Indeed, had there been any kinship between him and al-Husayn, he would not have killed him.”

They also relate that lamentation arose in his household over al-Husayn’s killing; that when al-Husayn’s family came to him and the women met, they wept together; and that he gave al-Husayn’s son ʿAlī the choice between staying with him and travelling to Medina. He chose to travel to Medina, so Yazīd provided him well for the journey.

These and similar accounts, transmitted through chains more authentic and firmly established than that unknown, disconnected chain, show that Yazīd did not express approval of al-Husayn’s killing, but expressed grief over it. Allah knows best what was in his heart. It is known that he did not initially order his killing. Nevertheless, he neither exacted retribution from his killers nor punished them for what they had done, since they had killed him to preserve Yazīd’s rule, which he feared al-Husayn and the Prophet’s household might threaten. May Allah be pleased with them all.

The point here is that there is no basis for the claim that al-Husayn’s head was taken to Syria in Yazīd’s time. How much less, then, for its being taken there after Yazīd’s time?

What is established is that it was taken from Karbala to the governor of Iraq, ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Ziyād, in Kufa. The scholars have stated that it was buried in Medina.

---

## Page 13565

As for the accounts related by those who have neither the sense to discern what they are saying nor any familiarity with transmitted reports—that the Prophet’s household were reviled, that they were carried on Bactrian camels, and that these camels grew two humps from that time onward—these are blatant lies that disgrace those who tell them. Bactrian camels had two humps from the day Allah created them, long before those events, just as other kinds of animals had their characteristic forms. Nor do Bactrian camels conceal a woman. No one took the Prophet’s household captive, nor was any member of it taken captive.

Rather, this is like their claim that al-Hajjāj killed them. All scholars of transmitted reports know that al-Hajjāj did not kill anyone from Banū Hāshim, in accordance with the instructions of his caliph, ʿAbd al-Malik. When he married the daughter of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar, this distressed the Umayyads and others of Quraysh, who considered him an unsuitable match for her. They kept pressing the matter until they separated him from her. Indeed, the Marwānids never killed anyone from Banū Hāshim, whether from the family of ʿAlī or the family of al-ʿAbbās, except Zayd ibn ʿAlī, who was crucified at Kunāsat al-Kūfa, and his son Yahyā.

The fourth point: Even supposing that his head had been taken to Yazīd, what purpose would they have had in burying it in Ascalon, which was then a frontier town where men were stationed to guard the border? If their purpose was to conceal its whereabouts, a place like Ascalon would have made them known, because so many people visited it for frontier service.

And if their concern was the blessedness of the site, how could such an aim be entertained by someone said to have been his enemy, who considered it lawful to shed his blood and sought to kill him?

---

## Page 13566

Moreover, it is well known that burying it nearby, beside his mother and brother in al-Baqīʿ, would have been better for him.

The fifth point: Burial in al-Baqīʿ is what their customary practice supports. During times of civil strife, when they killed a man who was not one of their own, they would hand his head and body over to his family. This is what al-Hajjāj did with Ibn al-Zubayr: after killing him and crucifying him, he handed his body over to his mother.

It is well known that al-Hajjāj’s efforts to kill Ibn al-Zubayr, and the warfare between them, were far greater than what took place between al-Husayn and his opponents. Ibn al-Zubayr claimed the caliphate after al-Husayn’s death, and most people pledged allegiance to him. Yazīd fought him until his own death, with his army still fighting Ibn al-Zubayr after the Battle of al-Harra. Then, when ʿAbd al-Malik came to power, he wrested Iraq from Ibn al-Zubayr, adding it to Syria. He then sent al-Hajjāj ibn Yūsuf against him, who subjected him to the well-known siege until he was killed. He then crucified him before handing his body over to his mother.

Al-Husayn’s body was buried where he fell at Karbalāʾ; it was neither exhumed nor mutilated. They would therefore not have refused to hand his head over to his family, just as they handed Ibn al-Zubayr’s body over to his family. And if his family received his head, they would not have forgone burying it with them in Medina the Radiant, beside his uncle, mother, and brother, and near his grandfather, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, only to bury it in Syria, where at that time no one would support them against

---

## Page 13567

their adversaries? Indeed, many of them hated him and his father. No one would do such a thing.

The dome over al-ʿAbbās’s grave in al-Baqīʿ is said to contain, alongside al-ʿAbbās, al-Hasan, ʿAlī ibn al-Husayn, Abū Jaʿfar Muhammad ibn ʿAlī, and Jaʿfar ibn Muhammad. It is also said that Fātima lies buried beneath the wall or nearby, and that al-Husayn’s head is there as well.

The sixth point: It has never been known that anyone, whether from Ahl al-Sunnah or the Shia, frequented the area of Ascalon for the sake of al-Husayn’s head. They neither visited it nor went there. Likewise, people did not formerly frequent the places now associated with the head, such as a site in Aleppo.

Since people neither frequented those places nor made them their destination, but instead frequented Karbalāʾ because the body was there, this was evidence that people in earlier times had not known the head to be in any of these places. Rather, what they knew and believed was that the body was in Karbalāʾ. They would visit it in the time of Ahmad and others. Indeed, Ahmad’s recorded responsa even include questions concerning practices at al-Husayn’s grave, which Abū Bakr al-Khallāl recorded in his al-Jāmiʿ al-kabīr (The Great Compendium), in the section on visiting shrines. No scholar has mentioned that they believed the head to be in any of these places other than Medina.

---

## Page 13568

It is thus clear that, had this been true, the earlier generations would have known better. Had they believed it, they would have acted according to their customary practice, made it known, and spoken about it as they spoke about comparable matters. Since nothing has been reported from the earlier generations—in either word or deed—indicating that the head was in these places, it is clear that this claim is false. And Allah knows best.

The seventh point:

Scholars in every age have consistently stated that the attribution of this Cairene shrine to al-Husayn is a lie and a falsehood, just as they have said of other falsely attributed shrines. Examples include the shrines in Damascus attributed to Ubayy ibn Kaʿb, Uways al-Qaranī, Hūd, Noah, or others; the shrine in Harrān attributed to Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh; those in al-Jazīra attributed to ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn ʿAwf, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar, and others like them; and those in Iraq attributed to ʿAlī, may Allah be pleased with him, and others. The same applies to shrines attributed to the prophets, apart from the graves of our Prophet Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and Abraham, the close friend of Allah, peace be upon him.

Since many shrines were falsely attributed fabrications, scholars in every age recognized these claims as lies and inventions. The well-known books and writings of scholars knowledgeable about this subject are full of such examples. Anyone who seeks them out and examines them will discover this.

---

## Page 13569

People have continued to make it known in their writings and communications that this Cairene shrine is a fabrication. They have recorded this in their works, including scholars knowledgeable about the matter who lived in that city. Abū al-Khattāb ibn Dihya devoted a section to this shrine in his book al-ʿIlm al-mashhūr (Well-Known Knowledge), alongside the established and unestablished reports he related concerning the killing of al-Husayn. Despite this, he stated that the shrine’s attribution was unanimously regarded as false. He explained that it had been transferred from Ascalon towards the end of ʿUbaydī rule and had been contrived for corrupt purposes, and that shortly afterwards Allah brought that dynasty to an end and punished it with the opposite of what it had intended.

This has remained well known among scholars, including those in our Egyptian lands—Cairo and its surroundings. A group of trustworthy people related this to me from Shaykh Abū ʿAbd Allāh Muhammad ibn ʿAlī al-Qushayrī, known as Ibn Daqīq al-ʿĪd; another group from Shaykh Abū Muhammad ʿAbd al-Muʾmin ibn Khalaf al-Dimyātī; another from Shaykh Abū Muhammad ibn al-Qastallānī; another from Shaykh Abū ʿAbd Allāh Muhammad al-Qurtubī, the author of the Quran commentary and the exposition of Allah’s Most Beautiful Names; and another from Shaykh ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz al-Dīrīnī.

For each of these scholars, the person who reported to me from him was someone whose truthfulness I do not doubt, and a great many people reported to me from some of them. Each informant related that the scholar from whom he transmitted would reject the claims concerning this shrine and say:

---

## Page 13570

“It is a lie; neither al-Husayn nor anyone else is buried there.”

Those who related this to me from Ibn al-Qastallānī reported that he said: “A Christian is buried there.” Indeed, both al-Qurtubī and al-Qastallānī discussed the falsity of the claims concerning this shrine in their writings, explaining that they were fabricated, just as Abū al-Khattāb ibn Dihya had done. It was for Ibn Dihya that al-Kāmil built the Kāmiliyya school of hadith. Abū Amr ibn al-Salāh and others like him learned from him much of what they knew about the correct pronunciation of names and words. Our reliance here is not on any one individual, but on the consensus of these scholars. It is well known that in these lands there was no one whose judgment could be relied upon in such matters who was more knowledgeable or more meticulous than these scholars and their peers.

Since all these scholars agree that this is a lie and a fabrication, it is clear that Allah has absolved al-Husayn of any connection with it.

Trustworthy informants also told me that some of these scholars would instruct their companions not to disclose this view on their authority, fearing harm from the common people in these lands because of the wrongdoing and corruption among them. For they had originally been propagandists for the Qarmatian esotericists, who ruled these lands for two hundred years. They thus instilled in them the traits of heretical hypocrites, ignorant innovators, and lying oppressors—traits that could only be uprooted over time. For these lands were conquered—through the overthrow of ʿUbaydī rule—by the people of faith

---

## Page 13571

and the Sunnah under the rule of Nūr al-Dīn and Saladin. Muslims who followed the Sunnah settled there, and the cause of faith and the Sunnah gained a measure of ascendancy. Yet much hypocrisy and religious innovation remained concealed there. In every age, Allah brings forth there manifestations of faith and the Sunnah previously unmentioned, while the hypocrisy and ignorance already notorious there grow rampant.

We ask Allah to make manifest throughout all lands the guidance and rectitude that He loves and approves; to increase the good bestowed upon His servants through the ascendancy of Islam and the Sunnah; and to fulfil His promise in the Quran that His word will reign supreme and the people of faith will prevail.

Many people have adopted beliefs and character traits that originate with the disbelievers and hypocrites, without being aware of this. Likewise, many participate with Christians in their festivals and venerate the places, times, and practices that Christians venerate. A person may do so without intending to venerate disbelief, or even knowing that these things are distinctive to them. Once he learns this, he desists and repents.

Similarly, many people have acquired some of the traits of the hypocrites without knowing that these are hypocritical traits. Once a person learns this, he repents to Allah. May Allah accept our repentance and his, and that of all sinners

---

## Page 13572

among the believers. All of this concerns the invalidity and falsehood of the claim that the head of al-Husayn, may Allah be pleased with him, is in Cairo or Ascalon.

We further say: whether this claim is true or false, building mosques over graves is not part of the religion of the Muslims. Rather, it is prohibited by texts authentically transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and by the agreement of the leading religious scholars. Indeed, it is impermissible to take graves as places of worship, whether by building mosques over them or by deliberately seeking to pray beside them. The leading religious scholars agree that this is prohibited and that no one may deliberately seek to pray beside anyone’s grave, whether that of a prophet or anyone else.

Anyone who says that deliberately seeking to pray beside someone’s grave, at a mosque built over a grave, at a shrine, or elsewhere is prescribed—such that it is recommended and better than praying in a mosque containing no grave—has departed from the religion and opposed the consensus of the Muslims. Anyone who says or believes this must be called upon to repent. If he repents, so be it; otherwise, he is to be executed.

Indeed, no one may pray in mosques built over graves, even without intending to pray beside those graves. Such prayer is not acceptable, whether incidental or deliberately sought, because it entails imitating the polytheists and opens the way to associating partners with Allah, and because of the obligation to warn against this

---

## Page 13573

and other matters, as the leading scholars of Islam from the four legal schools and others have expressly stated. Some explicitly declared it forbidden, while others used the term “disliked.” They do not regard this as the same issue as praying in a public cemetery. Regarding the latter, some explain the prohibition by the impurity of the soil, while others explain it by the resemblance to the polytheists. As for mosques built over graves, they prohibited prayer in them for fear that venerating a created being would lead people astray, as al-Shāfiʿī and other leading Muslim scholars have mentioned.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited prayer at sunrise, at sunset, and when the sun is at its zenith, and said that these are times when the disbelievers prostrate to it.

He prohibited this because it involves resembling them, even if the worshipper intends to prostrate only to the One God. What, then, of prayer in mosques built to venerate graves? We have discussed this issue at length elsewhere.

The purpose here was only to establish the whereabouts of the head of al-Husayn, may Allah be pleased with him, and to explain that the claims concerning the places popularly known in Egypt and Syria as al-Husayn’s shrine—that his head is there—are lies and fabrications, falsehoods and slander. And Allah knows best.

Written by Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah.

---

## Page 13574

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked about visiting the grave of al-Husayn and that of Lady Nafīsah, and praying at the tomb:

“If someone says that Lady Nafīsah frees prisoners, protects the fearful, and is the gateway for presenting one’s needs to Allah, is this permissible or not?”

He replied:

As for al-Husayn, his head was not brought to Egypt, by agreement of the scholars. Nor was it brought to Syria. Whoever says that a deceased person, whether Nafīsah or anyone else, protects the fearful, frees prisoners, and is the gateway for the fulfillment of needs is astray and a polytheist.

For Allah, glorified be He, is the One who grants protection, and no one can grant protection against Him. The gateway for presenting one’s needs to Allah is to call upon Him earnestly and with sincere devotion, as He, Most High, says:

“When My servants ask you concerning Me, I am indeed near. I answer the call of the supplicant when he calls upon Me.”

And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 13575

He—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

As for “the daughter of Yazīd ibn al-Sakan,” she died in Syria, so it is possible that this is her grave. As for “Bilāl’s grave,” this is possible, for he was buried at Bāb al-Saghīr in Damascus, so it is known that he was buried there. Identifying his precise grave with certainty, however, is open to question, for it is said that those graves were ploughed over.

Among these is the grave attributed to “Uways al-Qaranī,” west of Damascus. Uways did not come to Syria; rather, he went to Iraq.

Another is the grave attributed to “Hūd, peace be upon him,” in the congregational mosque of Damascus. This attribution is false by agreement of the scholars, for Hūd did not come to Syria. Rather, he was sent as a prophet in Yemen and migrated to Mecca. It is said that he died in Yemen, and it is also said that he died in Mecca. That site is simply opposite “the grave of Mu‘āwiyah ibn Abī Sufyān.”

As for the grave outside Bāb al-Saghīr that is said to be Mu‘āwiyah’s grave, it is actually that of Mu‘āwiyah ibn Yazīd ibn Mu‘āwiyah, who held the caliphate for a short time and then died without appointing a successor. He was a man of religious devotion and righteousness.

---

## Page 13576

Another is “the grave of Khālid” in Homs. It is said to be the grave of Khālid ibn Yazīd ibn Mu‘āwiyah, the brother of the Mu‘āwiyah just mentioned. However, when it became widely known as Khālid’s grave, and since the Khālid familiar to the common people was Khālid ibn al-Walīd, they assumed it was his. There is disagreement over whether it is his grave or that of Khālid ibn Yazīd. Abū ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-Barr mentioned in al-Istī‘āb that Khālid ibn al-Walīd died in Homs—though Medina is also mentioned—in the year 21 or 22 AH, during the caliphate of ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, whom he appointed as his executor. And Allah knows best.

Another is “the grave of Abū Muslim al-Khawlānī” in Dārayyā, whose attribution is disputed. Another is “the grave of ‘Alī ibn al-Husayn” in Egypt. This attribution is certainly false, for everyone agrees that ‘Alī ibn al-Husayn died in Medina and was buried in al-Baqī‘.

Another is “the Shrine of the Head” in Cairo. Those who have written about the killing of al-Husayn agree that the head is not in Egypt, and they know this attribution to be false. The story originated with its transfer from a shrine in Ascalon. That shrine was built about sixty years before this one, toward the end of the fifth century AH, whereas this one was built during the sixth century AH, some five hundred years after al-Husayn’s killing. Cairo itself was founded some three hundred years after al-Husayn’s killing.

Ibn Dihyah demonstrated in al-‘Ilm al-Mashhūr that this shrine’s attribution is false and that the head was buried in Medina, as al-Zubayr ibn Bakkār reported. What is authentically established concerning the transportation of the head is what al-Bukhārī related in his Sahīh: that it was brought to ‘Ubayd Allah ibn Ziyād, and he began

---

## Page 13577

tapping his front teeth with a rod. Anas ibn Mālik witnessed this—or, according to another report, Abū Barzah al-Aslamī. Both were in Iraq. A report with a broken or unknown chain of transmission states that the head was brought to Yazīd, who began tapping its front teeth with a rod, and that Abū Barzah was present and condemned this.
